https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/advanced_probability/full, advanced probability and martingales, this is a very useful summary where I learned many simplifications to measure theory.
	- How does one invent the notion of a sigma-algebra? (Hint: Need to eventually obtain the Borel $\sigma$-algebra, which is needed for uniqueness when containing a topology. A $\sigma$-algebra serves as the parameter space for a measure; visualize it as sets or "blobs" where the complement of a blob is in the $\sigma$-algebra, the empty set is in the $\sigma$-algebra, and arbitrary countable unions of blobs are in the $\sigma$-algebra. A measure is a map assigning a non-negative extended real number to each set in a $\sigma$-algebra such that the empty set has measure zero and countable unions of disjoint sets satisfy countable additivity $\mu(\bigcup E_i) = \sum \mu(E_i)$. Disjointness prevents overlap. The key mental image is blobs, complements of blobs, and countable unions.)
	- How does one invent measurable functions? (Hint: A function is measurable if one can take preimages at the level of $\sigma$-algebras for maps between measurable/topological spaces, analogous to continuous functions in topology where preimages of open sets are open.)
	- How does one invent the integral with respect to a measure? (Hint: Note that the existence of the integral is a theorem summarized by the monotone convergence theorem. Given a measure space, assign non-negative measurable functions to integrals that are linear and satisfy the monotone convergence theorem, meaning if a monotone increasing sequence of functions converges pointwise, then their integrals converge to the integral of the limit. Lastly, integrating against an indicator function $\mathbf{1}_A$ recovers the measure $\mu(A)$. The key intuition is linear, non-interacting decomposition into a basis similar to Fourier analysis.)
	- How does one invent simple functions? (Hint: Define them as finite linear combinations of indicator functions taking on finitely many values, and approximate measurable functions using monotone convergence. The key image is finiteness and step approximations.)
	- How does one invent the notion of "almost everywhere"? (Hint: Measurable functions are considered equal if they agree up to a set of measure zero. The key image is gluing together boundaries of measure zero.)
	- How does one invent the notion of Fatou's lemma? (Hint: If a sequence of non-negative functions is not monotone, one gets an inequality for the limit inferior: the integral of the limit inferior is less than or equal to the limit inferior of the integrals, i.e., $\int \liminf f_n \, d\mu \le \liminf \int f_n \, d\mu$. This is proven by applying the monotone convergence theorem to the sequence of infimums. The key image is visualizing expectation and the lower bound of the integral of a shifting wave or sine curve.)
	- How does one invent integrable functions? (Hint: Define the space $L^1(E)$ of functions where $\int |f| \, d\mu < \infty$. Extend the integral to general functions by taking the difference $\int f^+ \, d\mu - \int f^- \, d\mu$, using Carathéodory's extension theorem where applicable.)
	- How does one invent and prove the dominated convergence theorem? (Hint: Let $f_n$ be a sequence in a measure space such that $f_n \to f$ almost everywhere. If there exists an integrable dominating function $g$ such that $|f_n| \le g$ almost everywhere, then $\lim \int f_n \, d\mu = \int f \, d\mu$. Prove this by applying Fatou's lemma to $g - f_n$ and $g + f_n$. The easiest intuition is a bounded envelope; domination acts as a compactness condition analogous to sequential weak compactness in $L^p$ spaces.)
	- How does one invent product sigma-algebras? (Hint: Take Cartesian products analogously to product topologies, generating the product $\sigma$-algebra from cylinder sets, and show the existence of a unique product measure that decomposes into the composition of marginal measures.)
	- How does one invent Fubini's theorem? (Hint: Map $(x_1, x_2)$ to iterated integrals across the tensor product of two $\sigma$-algebras, showing that under integrability conditions one can interchange the order of integration for $f(x_1, x_2) \in \mathcal{L}^1(E_1 \times E_2)$.)
	- How does one first encounter conditional probability theory? (Hint: Note the notation change and scale the probability measure first: $P(A \mid B) = P(A \cap B) / P(B)$, assuming event $B$ has occurred by taking the intersection. Next, allow $B$ to vary across disjoint events in a partition, define linear combinations integrating against the partition elements, and think of the probability space as being partitioned into compartments. Testing against random variable $X$ across these countably many disjoint events yields the discrete conditional expectation.)
	- How does one find the characterizing property of conditional expectation? (Hint: Given a sub-$\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{G}$, the conditional expectation $Y = \mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]$ is uniquely characterized by being $\mathcal{G}$-measurable, integrable, and satisfying $\mathbb{E}[Y \mathbf{1}_G] = \mathbb{E}[X \mathbf{1}_G]$ for all $G \in \mathcal{G}$. Prove this by computing expectations and using monotone convergence to swap expectation and infinite sums.)
	- How does one prove the existence and uniqueness of conditional expectation? (Hint: Prove uniqueness up to measure zero via functional analysis on Hilbert spaces. For $X \in L^2$, define $Y$ as the orthogonal projection of $X$ onto the closed subspace $L^2(\mathcal{G})$, using $X - Y \perp L^2(\mathcal{G})$. Extend to non-negative $X \in \mathcal{m}\mathcal{E}_+$ using monotone convergence of $X \wedge n$ and indicator tests. Finally, for arbitrary $X \in L^1$, decompose $X = X^+ - X^-$ and use linearity.)
	- What key properties does one want to prove about conditional expectation? (Hint: Total expectation $\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]] = \mathbb{E}[X]$, positivity $X \ge 0 \implies \mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}] \ge 0$, independence $\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}] = \mathbb{E}[X]$ if $X$ is independent of $\mathcal{G}$, linearity, monotone convergence, Fatou's lemma, dominated convergence theorem, Jensen's inequality, and the tower property $\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}_2] \mid \mathcal{G}_1] = \mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}_1]$ for $\mathcal{G}_1 \subseteq \mathcal{G}_2$.)
	- How does one prove that for $X \in L^1$, the family of random variables $Y_\mathcal{G} = \mathbb{E}[X \mid \mathcal{G}]$ across all sub-$\sigma$-algebras $\mathcal{G} \subseteq \mathcal{F}$ is uniformly integrable? (Hint: Fix $\varepsilon > 0$. Apply Jensen's inequality to obtain $\mathbb{E}[|Y|] \le \mathbb{E}[|X|]$, then use Markov's inequality and the $\mathcal{G}$-measurability of $\mathbf{1}_{\{|Y| > \lambda\}}$ to bound $\mathbb{E}[|Y| \mathbf{1}_{\{|Y| > \lambda\}}] = \mathbb{E}[|X| \mathbf{1}_{\{|Y| > \lambda\}}] < \varepsilon$ uniformly for sufficiently large $\lambda$.)
	- How does one invent filtrations? (Hint: Define an increasing family of $\sigma$-algebras $(\mathcal{F}_n)_{n \ge 0}$ representing information accumulated up to time $n$.)
	- How does one invent stochastic processes? (Hint: Equip a filtered measure space with a collection or sequence of random variables $(X_n)$, and consider the information generated by their trajectories.)
	- How does one invent natural filtrations? (Hint: Define the minimal filtration $\mathcal{F}_n^X = \sigma(X_0, X_1, \dots, X_n)$ generated by the sequence of random variables up to time $n$.)
	- How does one invent adapted processes? (Hint: Require each random variable $X_n$ to be measurable with respect to $\mathcal{F}_n$, so that its value is fully determined by the information available at time $n$.)
	- How does one invent integrable processes? (Hint: Require that $\mathbb{E}[|X_n|] < \infty$ ($X_n \in L^1$) for all $n \ge 0$.)
	- How does one invent martingales? (Hint: Model a fair game where the best prediction of future values given all current and past information is the present value, i.e., $\mathbb{E}[X_{n+1} \mid \mathcal{F}_n] = X_n$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of a stopping time? (Hint: Define a random variable $T$ taking values in $\mathbb{N} \cup \{\infty\}$ such that the event $\{T \le n\} \in \mathcal{F}_n$ for all $n$, meaning whether the event has occurred can be decided using only information up to time $n$.)
	- How does one invent the optional stopping theorem? (Hint: Formulate conditions—such as bounded stopping times, bounded increments, or uniform integrability—under which the expected value of a stopped martingale equals its initial expectation, $\mathbb{E}[X_T] = \mathbb{E}[X_0]$.)

https://zb260.user.srcf.net/notes/III/alggrp.pdf, topics in algebraic groups
	- How does one invent quasi-compact schemes? (Hint: Generalize quasi-compactness from topological spaces to schemes, requiring that every open cover has a finite subcover.)
	- How does one invent quasi-separated schemes? (Hint: Require that the intersection of any two quasi-compact open subschemes is quasi-compact, or equivalently that the diagonal morphism is quasi-compact. Non-quasi-separated schemes arise by gluing infinite-dimensional affine spaces along non-quasi-compact subsets.)
	- How does one invent the relative notion of qcqs? (Hint: Formulate quasi-compact and quasi-separated properties for morphisms $f: X \to Y$ by requiring the preimage of every affine open subset to be quasi-compact and quasi-separated.)
	- How does one invent Noetherian schemes? (Hint: Require a scheme to be locally Noetherian and quasi-compact, since the Noetherian property guarantees finiteness of affine coverings and ideal stabilization.)
	- How does one invent Kähler differentials? (Hint: Impose the sum rule and Leibniz product rule taking values in a $B$-module $\Omega_{B/A}$. Construct this universally via the free $B$-module generated by $d(b)$ modulo the relations $d(a)=0$, $d(b_1+b_2)=db_1+db_2$, and $d(b_1 b_2)=b_1 db_2 + b_2 db_1$, establishing the natural isomorphism $\operatorname{Hom}_B(\Omega_{B/A}, M) \cong \operatorname{Der}_A(B, M)$.)
	- How does one prove that for $B' = B \otimes_A A'$, the Kähler differentials satisfy $\Omega_{B'/A'} \cong \Omega_{B/A} \otimes_B B'$ under base change? (Hint: Use the universal property of derivations and tensor products to establish the natural isomorphism immediately.)
	- What is the geometric motivation for Kähler differentials? (Hint: On a smooth manifold $X$, the tangent space $T_x X$ is the space of $\mathbb{R}$-derivations on $C^\infty(X)$. Dualizing to cotangent vectors and generalizing algebraically yields Kähler differentials as algebraic cotangent modules.)
	- How does one invent cotangent spaces by base changing the Kähler differential $\Omega_{A/k} \otimes_A k$ for a local $k$-algebra $(A, \mathfrak{m})$? (Hint: Show that $\Omega_{A/k} \otimes_A A/\mathfrak{m} \cong \mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^2$ by establishing a surjection from $\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^2 \to \Omega_{A/k} \otimes_A k$ using $\Omega_{k/k} = 0$, and using the universal derivation $A \to \mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^2$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/algebraic_topology_iii/full, algebraic topology
	- Why are maps always assumed to be continuous, i.e., inverse images of open sets are open? (Hint: To preserve topological invariants and topological structure.)
	- How does one invent homotopy and homotopy equivalence? (Hint: Define continuous deformation by taking the cylinder $X \times [0, 1]$ and declaring two maps homotopic if there exists a continuous map extending both boundary inclusions.)
	- How is homotopy equivalence straightforward? (Hint: It defines an equivalence relation that is compatible with composition of continuous maps.)
	- Why are dimension and cardinality insufficient topological invariants up to homotopy? (Hint: Contractibility of $\mathbb{R}^n$ shows spaces of different dimensions can have identical homotopy types.)
	- How does one invent the genus of a surface? (Hint: Holes and handles are preserved under homeomorphism and homotopy; algebraic topology assigns abelian groups to detect these holes algebraically.)
	- How does one invent the notion of a chain complex? (Hint: Define a sequence of abelian groups and boundary homomorphisms $(C_n, \partial_n)$ satisfying the algebraic boundary condition $\partial_n \circ \partial_{n+1} = 0$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of a cochain complex? (Hint: Dualize chain complexes by applying $\operatorname{Hom}(-, G)$ with coboundary maps $d^n = \partial_{n+1}^*$ satisfying $d^{n+1} \circ d^n = 0$.)
	- How does one invent homology? (Hint: Since $\partial^2 = 0$, the image of $\partial_{n+1}$ is contained in the kernel of $\partial_n$. Define the homology group as the quotient $H_n = \ker(\partial_n) / \operatorname{im}(\partial_{n+1})$ to measure non-bounding cycles.)
	- How does one invent the notion of a homology class? (Hint: Represent homology groups as equivalence classes of cycles modulo boundaries $[z] \in \ker \partial / \operatorname{im} \partial$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of cycles? (Hint: Cycles are elements in the kernel of $\partial_n$, algebraically capturing closed paths or loops with no boundary.)
	- How does one invent the notion of boundaries? (Hint: Boundaries are elements in the image of $\partial_{n+1}$, representing cycles that bound higher-dimensional geometric simplices.)
	- How does one invent chain maps? (Hint: Define collections of homomorphisms $f_\# : C_n \to C_n'$ commuting with boundary operators $f_\# \circ \partial = \partial' \circ f_\#$, acting as morphisms in the category of chain complexes.)
	- Why is singular homology both advantageous and disadvantageous? (Hint: It is functorial and does not depend on a choice of triangulation, but singular chain groups are uncountably infinite and impossible to compute directly from the definition without structural tools like Mayer-Vietoris.)
	- Why does one introduce cohomology in addition to homology? (Hint: Cohomology carries a natural ring structure via the cup product, providing finer algebraic invariants.)
	- How does one invent singular $n$-simplices? (Hint: Define them as continuous maps $\sigma: \Delta^n \to X$ from the standard geometric $n$-simplex $\Delta^n$ into the topological space $X$.)
	- How does one invent singular chain complexes? (Hint: Form the free abelian group $C_n(X)$ generated by all singular $n$-simplices with integer coefficients.)
	- Why does one insert alternating signs in the boundary formula? (Hint: Alternating signs in $\partial \sigma = \sum_{i=0}^n (-1)^i \sigma \circ d^i$ ensure cancellation so that $\partial^2 = 0$.)
	- How does one check that the singular chain complex satisfies $\partial^2 = 0$? (Hint: Compute face map compositions $d_j \circ d_i = d_i \circ d_{j-1}$ for $i < j$ and verify that all terms in the double sum cancel in pairs.)
	- How does one compute singular homology? (Hint: Compute the kernel of the $n$-th boundary operator and quotient out the image of the $(n+1)$-th boundary operator.)
	- How does one compute singular cohomology? (Hint: Construct the dual cochain complex $C^n(X; G) = \operatorname{Hom}(C_n(X), G)$ and compute $H^n(X; G) = \ker(d^n) / \operatorname{im}(d^{n-1})$.)
	- How does one prove that a homeomorphism induces an isomorphism of homology groups? (Hint: Show that singular homology is a functor from the category of topological spaces to abelian groups, so it preserves isomorphisms.)
	- How does singular homology work geometrically? (Hint: Cycles detect potential holes, and quotienting out boundaries identifies cycles that surround the same topological void.)
	- How does one compute the singular homology of a one-point space? (Hint: There is a unique singular $n$-simplex $\sigma_n$ for each $n$; the boundary map is an isomorphism for even $n$ and zero for odd $n$, yielding $H_0(\mathrm{pt}) \cong \mathbb{Z}$ and $H_n(\mathrm{pt}) = 0$ for $n > 0$.)
	- How does one compute the homology of a disjoint union of path components? (Hint: Every singular simplex has a connected domain and must lie in a single path component, so $C_n(\bigsqcup X_\alpha) \cong \bigoplus C_n(X_\alpha)$ and $H_n(\bigsqcup X_\alpha) \cong \bigoplus H_n(X_\alpha)$.)
	- How does one compute the zeroth homology of a nonempty path-connected space? (Hint: Define an augmentation map $\varepsilon: C_0(X) \to \mathbb{Z}$ by $\sum n_i x_i \mapsto \sum n_i$; path-connectedness ensures $\ker \varepsilon = \operatorname{im} \partial_1$, so $H_0(X) \cong \mathbb{Z}$.)
	- How does one compute homology using homotopy invariance? (Hint: Homotopic maps induce identical homomorphisms on homology; since $\mathbb{R}^n$ is contractible to a point, $H_n(\mathbb{R}^n) \cong H_n(\mathrm{pt})$.)
	- How does one compute homology using the Mayer-Vietoris sequence? (Hint: Decompose $X = A \cup B$ into open subsets and use the short exact sequence of chain complexes to obtain the long exact sequence $\dots \to H_n(A \cap B) \to H_n(A) \oplus H_n(B) \to H_n(X) \xrightarrow{\partial_*} H_{n-1}(A \cap B) \to \dots$.)
	- How does one invent relative homology and excision? (Hint: Define the relative chain complex $C_n(X, A) = C_n(X)/C_n(A)$ to ignore features in $A$. Excision allows removing a subset $Z \subset A$ whose closure is in the interior of $A$ without changing relative homology.)
	- How does one construct relative homology? (Hint: The boundary operator descends to the quotient complex $C_n(X, A) = C_n(X) / C_n(A)$, and relative homology is defined as $H_n(X, A) = H_n(C_\bullet(X, A))$.)
	- How does one apply the excision theorem? (Hint: If $\overline{Z} \subseteq \operatorname{int}(A)$, the inclusion $(X \setminus Z, A \setminus Z) \hookrightarrow (X, A)$ induces an isomorphism $H_n(X \setminus Z, A \setminus Z) \cong H_n(X, A)$.)
	- How does one compute the homology of spheres $S^n$? (Hint: Decompose $S^n$ into two contractible hemispheres with intersection homotopy equivalent to $S^{n-1}$ and apply induction via Mayer-Vietoris, yielding $H_0(S^n) \cong \mathbb{Z}$, $H_n(S^n) \cong \mathbb{Z}$, and $0$ otherwise.)
	- How does one compute the degree of a map $f: S^n \to S^n$? (Hint: The induced map $f_*: H_n(S^n) \to H_n(S^n)$ on $H_n(S^n) \cong \mathbb{Z}$ is multiplication by a unique integer $\deg(f)$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of the degree of a map? (Hint: Generalize the winding number of closed curves in the plane to higher-dimensional spheres.)
	- What are the applications of the degree of a map? (Hint: Proving the Brouwer fixed-point theorem, the hairy ball theorem, and the fundamental theorem of algebra.)
	- How does one prove that every continuous map $f: D^n \to D^n$ has a fixed point? (Hint: Assume $f(x) \ne x$ for all $x$, construct a retraction $r: D^n \to \partial D^n = S^{n-1}$ by shooting rays from $f(x)$ through $x$, which induces a retraction on homology giving $0 \cong H_{n-1}(D^n) \to H_{n-1}(S^{n-1}) \cong \mathbb{Z}$, a contradiction.)
	- How does one prove the long exact sequence of relative homology? (Hint: Apply the Snake Lemma to the short exact sequence of chain complexes $0 \to C_\bullet(A) \to C_\bullet(X) \to C_\bullet(X, A) \to 0$ and verify well-definedness of the connecting homomorphism $\partial_*$.)
	- How does one prove the Five Lemma? (Hint: Perform diagram chasing on a commutative diagram with exact rows.)
	- How does one prove that relative homology forms an isomorphism under excision? (Hint: Combine the long exact sequence of pairs with the Five Lemma and the small simplex theorem.)
	- How does one prove homotopy invariance of homology? (Hint: Construct an explicit prism operator $P: C_n(X) \to C_{n+1}(Y)$ giving a chain homotopy $\partial P + P \partial = g_\# - f_\#$.)
	- How does one prove excision and Mayer-Vietoris together? (Hint: Use the small simplex theorem via barycentric subdivision to subdivide chains until each simplex lies entirely inside an element of an open cover $\mathcal{U}$, showing $C_\bullet^\mathcal{U}(X) \hookrightarrow C_\bullet(X)$ is a quasi-isomorphism.)
	- How does one prove the small simplices theorem? (Hint: Perform iterated barycentric subdivisions on simplices, bounded by diameter factor $(n/(n+1))^k$, and apply the Lebesgue Number Lemma to ensure every subdivided piece falls inside the open cover.)
	- How does one define reduced homology? (Hint: Augment the chain complex by $\epsilon: C_0(X) \to \mathbb{Z}$ so that $\widetilde{H}_0(X)$ removes one copy of $\mathbb{Z}$, setting $\widetilde{H}_n(\mathrm{pt}) = 0$ for all $n$.)
	- How does one invent good pairs and reduced homology? (Hint: For a good pair $(X, A)$ where $A$ is a deformation retract of a neighborhood in $X$, prove $H_n(X, A) \cong \widetilde{H}_n(X/A)$.)
	- How does one define cell complexes (CW complexes)? (Hint: Construct topological spaces inductively by attaching $n$-cells $D^n$ along their boundaries $S^{n-1}$ to the $(n-1)$-skeleton $X^{n-1}$, equipped with the weak topology.)
	- Why is cellular homology advantageous for computing homology and cohomology? (Hint: The cellular chain groups $C_n^{\mathrm{CW}}(X)$ are free abelian groups generated by the $n$-cells, making them finite-dimensional for finite CW complexes.)
	- How does one discover the Universal Coefficient Theorem? (Hint: Relate homology with coefficients to integral homology via short exact sequences involving $\operatorname{Tor}(H_{n-1}(X), G)$ and $\operatorname{Ext}(H_{n-1}(X), G)$.)
	- Why does one study cohomology with coefficients? (Hint: Changing coefficients to fields like $\mathbb{Q}$ or $\mathbb{F}_p$ simplifies computations and detects torsion phenomena.)
	- Why is the Euler characteristic invariant under cell decomposition? (Hint: The alternating sum of cell counts $\sum (-1)^n c_n$ equals the alternating sum of Betti numbers $\sum (-1)^n \operatorname{rank}(H_n(X))$.)
	- How does one invent the cup product in cohomology? (Hint: For cochains $\phi \in C^k(X; R)$ and $\psi \in C^l(X; R)$, define $(\phi \smile \psi)(\sigma) = \phi(\sigma|_{[v_0, \dots, v_k]}) \psi(\sigma|_{[v_k, \dots, v_{k+l}]})$ to induce a graded ring structure on $H^*(X; R)$.)
	- How does one compute differentials for the cup product? (Hint: Verify the graded Leibniz rule $d(\phi \smile \psi) = d\phi \smile \psi + (-1)^k \phi \smile d\psi$ by direct evaluation on simplices.)
	- Why does the cup product induce a well-defined graded-commutative ring? (Hint: Show that the cup product preserves cocycles and coboundaries, and is graded-commutative: $\alpha \smile \beta = (-1)^{kl} \beta \smile \alpha$ for $\alpha \in H^k, \beta \in H^l$.)
	- How does one prove that the cup product is natural? (Hint: Check that for any continuous map $f: X \to Y$, $f^*(\alpha \smile \beta) = f^*(\alpha) \smile f^*(\beta)$ at the cochain level.)
	- How does one define the cross product? (Hint: Define $\times: H^k(X) \otimes H^l(Y) \to H^{k+l}(X \times Y)$ by $\alpha \times \beta = p_X^*(\alpha) \smile p_Y^*(\beta)$.)
	- How does one invent the relative cup product? (Hint: Extend the cup product to pairs: $H^k(X, A) \times H^l(X, B) \to H^{k+l}(X, A \cup B)$.)
	- How does one prove the full Künneth formula? (Hint: Decompose chain complexes of product spaces via the algebraic Künneth theorem with $\operatorname{Tor}$ terms, and verify using induction on CW cells.)
	- How does one prove the Universal Coefficient Theorem most simply? (Hint: Over a PID $R$, split the free chain complex $0 \to Z_n \to C_n \to B_{n-1} \to 0$ and apply $\operatorname{Hom}_R(-, M)$ and $-\otimes_R M$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of vector bundles? (Hint: Associate a vector space $E_x \cong \mathbb{R}^k$ to each point $x \in M$ such that $E = \bigsqcup E_x$ is locally trivial, $E|_U \cong U \times \mathbb{R}^k$.)
	- How does one define sections of vector bundles? (Hint: Define smooth maps $s: M \to E$ such that $\pi \circ s = \operatorname{id}_M$.)
	- How does one define the pullback of a vector bundle? (Hint: For $f: N \to M$, construct $f^*E = \{(n, e) \in N \times E \mid f(n) = \pi(e)\}$.)
	- How does one compute the Whitney sum of vector bundles? (Hint: Take fiberwise direct sums $E \oplus F$ over the same base manifold $M$.)
	- How does one invent quotient bundles? (Hint: For a subbundle $E \subset F$, construct the fiberwise quotient bundle $F/E$.)
	- How does one invent Grassmannian manifolds? (Hint: Define $\operatorname{Gr}(k, \mathbb{R}^n)$ as the moduli space of all $k$-dimensional linear subspaces of $\mathbb{R}^n$.)
	- How does one invent the Gysin sequence? (Hint: Construct the long exact sequence in cohomology associated with an oriented sphere bundle $S^{k-1} \to E_0 \to M$ using the Euler class.)
	- How does one prove Poincaré duality for a compact oriented manifold? (Hint: Induct on open covers using Mayer-Vietoris, showing $H_c^k(M) \cong H_{n-k}(M)$ via cap product with the fundamental class $[M]$.)
	- How does one relate compactness and discreteness? (Hint: Compactness guarantees finite subcovers; a discrete space is compact if and only if it is finite.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/modular_forms_and_l_functions/full, modular forms and L-functions
	- What is the Langlands program and why is it important? (Hint: It posits deep connections between Galois representations, automorphic forms, and $L$-functions associated with arithmetic objects like elliptic curves over $\mathbb{Q}$.)
	- What is the Dedekind eta function? (Hint: Define $\eta(z) = q^{1/24} \prod_{n=1}^\infty (1 - q^n)$ with $q = e^{2\pi i z}$, satisfying the functional equations $\eta(z+1) = e^{\pi i / 12} \eta(z)$ and $\eta(-1/z) = \sqrt{z/i} \, \eta(z)$.)
	- Why does the Dedekind eta function appear in bosonic string theory? (Hint: It calculates the partition function of free transverse oscillator modes on the string worldsheet in 26 dimensions.)
	- How does one invent $L$-series? (Hint: Generalize the Riemann zeta function by weighting Dirichlet series coefficients with arithmetic characters or automorphic eigenvalues.)
	- How does one study $L$-series? (Hint: Classically using complex analytic continuation and contour integration, or cohomologically via class field theory and étale cohomology.)
	- How does one invent the Fourier transform? (Hint: Expand functions in an orthonormal basis of characters $x \mapsto e^{2\pi i \xi x}$ on abelian groups.)
	- How does one study $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$? (Hint: As abelian topological groups whose character groups are continuous homomorphisms into the circle group $U(1)$.)
	- How does one invent characters? (Hint: Define continuous group homomorphisms $\chi: G \to U(1)$.)
	- How does one invent trivial characters, products of characters, and conjugate characters? (Hint: Use pointwise multiplication, inversion, and complex conjugation in $\mathbb{C}^\times$.)
	- How does one invent the character group? (Hint: Use Pontryagin duality to study $\widehat{G} = \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathrm{cont}}(G, U(1))$ as a dual topological abelian group.)
	- How does one compute the character group for $G = \mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$, and $\mathbb{R}$? (Hint: $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}} \cong U(1)$, $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}} \cong \mu_n$, $\widehat{\mathbb{R}} \cong \mathbb{R}$, and $\widehat{\mathbb{R}^\times} \cong \{\pm 1\} \times \mathbb{R}$.)
	- What is Pontryagin duality and how does one invent it? (Hint: For locally compact abelian groups $G$, the canonical evaluation map $G \to \widehat{\widehat{G}}$ is an isomorphism of topological groups.)
	- Why is there no canonical isomorphism between a finite abelian group and its dual? (Hint: An isomorphism $G \cong \widehat{G}$ depends on a choice of generators/roots of unity, while $G \cong \widehat{\widehat{G}}$ is canonical.)
	- How does one invent the Fourier transform on locally compact abelian groups? (Hint: Integrate against inverse characters: $\widehat{f}(\chi) = \int_G f(g) \overline{\chi(g)} \, dg$.)
	- What is the conceptual way to think about the Fourier transform? (Hint: As spectral decomposition of the translation operator on $L^2(G)$ into character eigencomponents.)
	- How does one invent the Schwartz space? (Hint: Define the space of smooth functions with rapid decay at infinity along with all their derivatives, ensuring stability under Fourier transformation.)
	- How does one invent the Fourier inversion formula? (Hint: Show that applying the dual Fourier transform twice recovers the original function up to reflection: $\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{F}(f))(x) = f(-x)$.)
	- How does one invent the Fourier inversion formula for $G = \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$? (Hint: Reconstruct periodic functions from their discrete Fourier series coefficients via $\sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} \widehat{f}(n) e^{2\pi i n x}$.)
	- How does one prove the Fourier inversion formula for $f: \mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z} \to \mathbb{C}$? (Hint: Use orthogonality of roots of unity $\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x \in \mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}} e^{2\pi i k x / N} = \delta_{k, 0}$.)
	- What is a Haar measure and how does one invent it? (Hint: A non-trivial translation-invariant Borel measure on a locally compact group that is finite on compact sets and positive on non-empty open sets.)
	- Why does one care about Haar measures? (Hint: Haar measure exists uniquely up to a positive scalar multiple on any locally compact group, enabling integration and harmonic analysis.)
	- Why is local compactness necessary for the existence of a Haar measure? (Hint: In infinite-dimensional spaces, infinitely many disjoint translated open sets would force total measure to be either zero or infinite.)
	- How does one compute the Haar measure of $\mathbb{R}$, a discrete group $G$, and $\mathbb{R}_{>0}^\times$? (Hint: Lebesgue measure $dx$ on $\mathbb{R}$, counting measure on discrete $G$, and $dx/x$ on $\mathbb{R}_{>0}^\times$.)
	- How does one normalize the Fourier inversion formula using Haar measure? (Hint: Select dual Haar measures on $G$ and $\widehat{G}$ such that the inversion constant equals 1.)
	- How does one define the Fourier transform using Haar measure? (Hint: Set $\widehat{f}(\xi) = \int_G f(g) \overline{\xi(g)} \, dg$ for $f \in L^1(G)$.)
	- How does one prove the Fourier inversion theorem for locally compact abelian groups? (Hint: Prove it first on Schwartz-Bruhat functions and extend to $L^2(G)$ via Plancherel's theorem.)
	- How does one invent the Poisson summation formula? (Hint: Periodize a Schwartz function $g(x) = \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} f(x+n)$, compute its Fourier series, and evaluate at zero to get $\sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} f(n) = \sum_{m \in \mathbb{Z}} \widehat{f}(m)$.)
	- How does one invent the Mellin transform? (Hint: Integrate against multiplicative Haar measure: $\mathcal{M}(f)(s) = \int_0^\infty f(y) y^{s-1} \, dy = \int_0^\infty f(y) y^s \frac{dy}{y}$.)
	- How does one invent the Gamma function? (Hint: Take the Mellin transform of $e^{-y}$, yielding $\Gamma(s) = \int_0^\infty y^{s-1} e^{-y} \, dy$, which continuously interpolates the factorial $(n-1)!$.)
	- How do Mellin transforms serve as a technique in number theory? (Hint: Relate Dirichlet series $\sum a_n n^{-s}$ to the Mellin transform of their generating functions $\sum a_n e^{-ny}$.)
	- How does one invent the Riemann zeta function? (Hint: Sum $n^{-s}$ over all positive integers: $\zeta(s) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty n^{-s}$ for $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 1$.)
	- How does one invent the Euler product formula? (Hint: Expand geometric series $\prod_p (1 - p^{-s})^{-1} = \prod_p \sum_{k=0}^\infty p^{-ks}$ and use unique prime factorization in $\mathbb{Z}$.)
	- How does one express the Riemann zeta function as a Mellin transform? (Hint: Show $\pi^{-s/2} \Gamma(s/2) \zeta(s) = \int_0^\infty \frac{1}{e^{\pi y} - 1} y^{s/2} \frac{dy}{y}$.)
	- How does one prove that $\zeta(s)$ has a meromorphic continuation to $\mathbb{C}$ with a single simple pole at $s = 1$? (Hint: Use the Jacobi theta function identity and split the Mellin integral $\int_0^1 + \int_1^\infty$.)
	- How does one prove that there are infinitely many primes using the zeta function? (Hint: If there were finitely many primes, the Euler product would remain bounded as $s \to 1^+$, contradicting the divergence of the harmonic series.)
	- How does one compute the values of $\zeta(s)$ at negative integers? (Hint: Use the functional equation relating $\zeta(1-s)$ to $\zeta(s)$ and express values via Bernoulli numbers: $\zeta(1-k) = -B_k / k$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of a Dirichlet $L$-function? (Hint: Associate an $L$-series $L(s, \chi) = \sum_{n=1}^\infty \chi(n) n^{-s}$ to a Dirichlet character $\chi: (\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z})^\times \to \mathbb{C}^\times$.)
	- How does one define primitive Dirichlet characters? (Hint: A character mod $N$ is primitive if it does not factor through $(\mathbb{Z}/M\mathbb{Z})^\times$ for any proper divisor $M \mid N$.)
	- How does one define equivalent Dirichlet characters? (Hint: Characters are equivalent if they are induced by the same primitive character modulo their conductors.)
	- How does one define the conductor of a Dirichlet character $\chi$? (Hint: The smallest modulus $M \mid N$ such that $\chi$ factors through $(\mathbb{Z}/M\mathbb{Z})^\times$.)
	- Why is a Dirichlet series with bounded coefficients $|\chi(n)| \le 1$ convergent for $\operatorname{Re}(s) > 1$? (Hint: Dominated by the convergent series $\sum n^{-\sigma}$ for $\sigma > 1$.)
	- How does one derive the Euler product for Dirichlet $L$-functions? (Hint: Complete multiplicativity $\chi(mn) = \chi(m)\chi(n)$ yields $L(s, \chi) = \prod_p (1 - \chi(p)p^{-s})^{-1}$.)
	- How does one prove that Dirichlet $L$-functions have meromorphic continuation to $\mathbb{C}$? (Hint: Express $L(s, \chi)$ as a linear combination of Hurwitz zeta functions or via the Mellin transform of periodic theta functions.)
	- How does one prove the non-vanishing of Dirichlet $L$-functions at $s = 1$ for non-trivial characters? (Hint: Consider the product $\prod_\chi L(s, \chi)$ and use positivity of coefficients to prevent multiple zeros from cancelling the pole of $\zeta(s)$ at $s = 1$.)
	- How does one prove Dirichlet's theorem on primes in arithmetic progressions? (Hint: Use character orthogonality to isolate primes $p \equiv a \pmod N$ via $\sum_\chi \overline{\chi(a)} \log L(s, \chi)$ and apply non-vanishing $L(1, \chi) \ne 0$.)
	- How does one invent modular forms? (Hint: Study holomorphic functions on the upper half-plane $\mathbb{H}$ that transform with weight $k$ under the action of $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{Z})$ and are holomorphic at cusps.)
	- What is the action of $\mathrm{GL}_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ on the upper half-plane? (Hint: Via Möbius transformations $z \mapsto \frac{az+b}{cz+d}$, with kernel given by scalar matrices $\mathbb{R}_{>0}^\times I$.)
	- Why is the action of $\mathrm{GL}_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ on $\mathbb{H}$ not faithful? (Hint: Positive scalar matrices act as the identity.)
	- How does one invent $\mathrm{PGL}_2^+(\mathbb{R})$ and $\mathrm{PSL}_2(\mathbb{R})$? (Hint: Quotient by the center to obtain faithful projective group actions on $\mathbb{H}$.)
	- What is the Iwasawa decomposition of $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})$? (Hint: Decompose $G = KAN$ into compact $K = \mathrm{SO}(2)$, diagonal $A$, and strictly upper triangular $N$.)
	- How does one compute the Iwasawa decomposition? (Hint: Apply Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization to the column vectors of $g \in \mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})$.)
	- What is the fundamental domain for the modular group $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{Z})$ acting on $\mathbb{H}$? (Hint: The standard region $\mathcal{F} = \{z \in \mathbb{H} \mid |z| \ge 1, |\operatorname{Re}(z)| \le 1/2\}$.)
	- How does one prove that $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{Z}) \backslash \mathbb{H}$ has finite invariant hyperbolic volume? (Hint: Integrate the hyperbolic volume form $y^{-2} \, dx \, dy$ over the fundamental domain $\mathcal{F}$ to get $\pi / 3$.)
	- How does one compute the $q$-expansion of a modular form? (Hint: Invariance under $z \mapsto z+1$ makes $f(z)$ periodic, yielding a Fourier series in $q = e^{2\pi i z}$.)
	- How does one define a cusp form? (Hint: A modular form whose $q$-expansion has vanishing constant term $a_0 = 0$, so that it vanishes at all cusps.)
	- Why are cusp forms important? (Hint: Cusp forms decay exponentially as $y \to \infty$, making them square-integrable with respect to the Petersson inner product.)
	- What is a weakly modular form? (Hint: A function satisfying the modular transformation law that is meromorphic at the cusps.)
	- What are Eisenstein series and why are they fundamental? (Hint: Average $1/(cz+d)^k$ over coprime pairs $(c, d)$ to form holomorphic modular forms that do not vanish at cusps.)
	- How does one generalize Eisenstein series to congruence subgroups? (Hint: Sum over cosets of $\Gamma_0(N)$, $\Gamma_1(N)$, or $\Gamma(N)$ modulo parabolic stabilizers.)
	- How does one invent theta functions as modular forms? (Hint: Define $\Theta(z) = \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} q^{n^2}$, which yields modular forms of weight $1/2$ by the Poisson summation formula.)
	- How does Hecke theory work for $\Gamma_0(N)$? (Hint: Hecke operators $T_n$ act on spaces of modular forms; common eigenforms have multiplicative Fourier coefficients and satisfy multiplicity one.)
	- Why is the arithmetic of the Ramanujan Delta function $\Delta(z) = q \prod_{n=1}^\infty (1-q^n)^{24} = \sum \tau(n) q^n$ significant? (Hint: Deligne proved the Ramanujan conjecture $|\tau(p)| \le 2 p^{11/2}$ using the Weil conjectures, connecting modular forms to $\ell$-adic Galois representations.)
	- Why is the space of modular forms $M_k(\Gamma)$ finite-dimensional? (Hint: Apply the valence formula / Riemann-Roch theorem to the compactified modular curve.)
	- What is the Hecke algebra? (Hint: The commutative algebra generated by Hecke operators $T_n$ and diamond operators acting on $M_k(\Gamma)$.)
	- How does one invent the modular slash operator? (Hint: Define $(f|_k \gamma)(z) = (\det \gamma)^{k/2} (cz+d)^{-k} f(\gamma z)$ to make the modular transformation law a right group action.)
	- What is the Atkin-Lehner theory of newforms? (Hint: Decomposes spaces of cusp forms into oldforms coming from lower levels and newforms that satisfy strong multiplicity one.)
	- What is an automorphic representation? (Hint: An irreducible representation of a reductive algebraic group $G(\mathbb{A})$ over the adeles occurring in the space of square-integrable functions $L^2(G(\mathbb{Q}) \backslash G(\mathbb{A}))$.)
	- How does one invent Maass forms? (Hint: Non-holomorphic smooth modular functions on $\mathbb{H}$ that are eigenfunctions of the hyperbolic Laplacian $\Delta = -y^2(\partial_x^2 + \partial_y^2)$ with polynomial growth at cusps.)
	- How does one invent the Casimir operator? (Hint: The central quadratic element in the universal enveloping algebra $U(\mathfrak{g})$ of a Lie algebra, acting as a Laplacian on automorphic forms.)
	- How does one define general automorphic forms? (Hint: Smooth functions on $G(\mathbb{Q}) \backslash G(\mathbb{A})$ that are $K$-finite, $\mathcal{Z}(\mathfrak{g})$-finite, and satisfy moderate growth conditions.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IV_L/topics_in_number_theory/full, topics in number theory, this is basically number theory
	- How does one invent the notion of a separable closure? (Hint: Construct the maximal algebraic extension $K^{\mathrm{sep}} / K$ such that every element has a separable minimal polynomial, enabling infinite Galois theory with the Krull topology.)
	- How does one invent absolute Galois groups? (Hint: Define $G_K = \operatorname{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)$ as a profinite group, establishing a Galois correspondence between closed subgroups and intermediate field extensions.)
	- How does one define the maximal abelian extension $K^{\mathrm{ab}}$? (Hint: Take the fixed field of the commutator subgroup $[G_K, G_K]$, giving $\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{ab}}/K) \cong G_K^{\mathrm{ab}}$.)
	- What is the absolute Galois group defined up to? (Hint: Up to inner automorphisms (conjugation), while its abelianization $G_K^{\mathrm{ab}}$ is canonically defined.)
	- How does one invent non-archimedean local fields? (Hint: Complete discrete valuation fields with finite residue fields, classifying them as finite extensions of $\mathbb{Q}_p$ or $\mathbb{F}_p((t))$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of a valuation ring? (Hint: Define $\mathcal{O}_K = \{x \in K \mid v(x) \ge 0\}$ with unique maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m} = \{x \in K \mid v(x) \ge 1\}$.)
	- How does one invent the notion of a uniformizer? (Hint: A generator $\pi \in \mathfrak{m}$ with $v(\pi) = 1$, generating the maximal ideal.)
	- How does one invent residue fields? (Hint: Form the quotient $k = \mathcal{O}_K / \mathfrak{m}$, which is a finite field $\mathbb{F}_q$.)
	- How does one invent the maximal unramified extension $K^{\mathrm{nr}}$? (Hint: The composite of all unramified extensions, with Galois group isomorphic to $\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}$ generated by Frobenius.)
	- How does one invent the inertia subgroup $I_K$? (Hint: The kernel of the restriction map $\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K) \to \operatorname{Gal}(k^{\mathrm{sep}}/k)$, measuring ramification.)
	- How does one invent the wild inertia group? (Hint: The unique Sylow $p$-subgroup $P_K \subset I_K$, where $I_K / P_K$ is the tame inertia group.)
	- How does one invent arithmetic and geometric Frobenius elements? (Hint: The arithmetic Frobenius is $x \mapsto x^q$ on residue fields; geometric Frobenius is its inverse $\operatorname{Frob}^{-1}$, acting compatibly on $\ell$-adic étale cohomology.)
	- Why is the tame character $\tau_n$ important? (Hint: Describes the action of tame inertia on $n$-th roots of uniformizers $\pi^{1/n}$, forming a 1-cocycle.)
	- What is the statement of local class field theory? (Hint: The local Artin map establishes an isomorphism $\widehat{K^\times} \cong \operatorname{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{ab}}/K)$, identifying finite abelian extensions with open subgroups of finite index in $K^\times$.)
	- How does one invent the Weil group $W_K$? (Hint: The dense subgroup of $\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{sep}}/K)$ whose elements act on the residue field by integer powers of Frobenius, giving an exact isomorphism $W_K^{\mathrm{ab}} \cong K^\times$.)
	- How does one invent the relative Weil group $W_{L/K}$? (Hint: Form the extension of $W_K$ by $L^\times$ classified by the fundamental class in $H^2(W(L/K), L^\times)$.)
	- How does one prove local class field theory? (Hint: Via Galois cohomology and the Brauer group $\operatorname{Br}(K) \cong \mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}$, Lubin-Tate formal group laws, or Neukirch's explicit construction.)
	- How does one invent global fields? (Hint: Algebraic number fields (finite extensions of $\mathbb{Q}$) or global function fields (finite extensions of $\mathbb{F}_q(t)$).)
	- How does one invent the notion of a place of a global field? (Hint: Equivalence classes of non-trivial valuations on $K$, partitioned into finite places (prime ideals) and infinite places (archimedean embeddings).)
	- How does one define normalized absolute values on a global field? (Hint: Set $|x|_v = q_v^{-v(x)}$ for finite places, $|x|_v = |x|_\mathbb{R}$ for real places, and $|x|_v = |x|_\mathbb{C}^2$ for complex places, ensuring the product formula $\prod_v |x|_v = 1$.)
	- How does one invent the ring of adeles $\mathbb{A}_K$ and group of ideles $\mathbb{I}_K$? (Hint: The restricted topological product of all completions $K_v$ with respect to the integers $\mathcal{O}_v$.)
	- Why is the idele class group $C_K = \mathbb{I}_K / K^\times$ fundamental? (Hint: Global class field theory provides a canonical isomorphism $C_K / D_K \cong \operatorname{Gal}(K^{\mathrm{ab}}/K)$, encoding class group finiteness and Dirichlet's unit theorem.)
	- What is the local Langlands correspondence for $\mathrm{GL}_1$? (Hint: It is local class field theory: 1-dimensional representations of the Weil group $W_K$ correspond to continuous characters of $K^\times = \mathrm{GL}_1(K)$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/notes/III_L/positivity_in_algebraic_geometry_trim.pdf, positivity in algebraic geometry

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/theoretical_physics_of_soft_condensed_matter/full, condensed matter
	- Why does one ignore quantum fluctuations in soft matter physics? (Hint: The thermal energy scale $k_B T$ greatly exceeds the quantum energy scale $\hbar / \tau_Q$, so setting $\hbar \to 0$ is valid.)
	- Why are thermal fluctuations essential in soft matter systems? (Hint: Elastic shear moduli are of order $k_B T \cdot N/V \sim 10^2 - 10^7 \, \mathrm{Pa}$, which makes thermal energies comparable to structural deformation energies.)
	- Why does one coarse-grain a soft matter system? (Hint: To eliminate microscopic molecular degrees of freedom and focus on macroscopic continuous field variables.)
	- Why is mean field theory used and where does it fail? (Hint: It computes dominant free energy minima but neglects large-scale fluctuation effects near critical points.)
	- Why are hydrodynamic equations used for soft condensed matter? (Hint: They describe the slow, long-wavelength dissipative relaxation of conserved order parameters via free energy functionals.)
	- Why is random thermal noise added to hydrodynamic equations? (Hint: To satisfy the fluctuation-dissipation theorem, yielding stochastic PDEs such as the Landau-Lifshitz equations.)
	- How does one modify the Navier-Stokes equations for soft matter? (Hint: Incorporate stress tensors derived from free energy functionals, incompressibility constraints, stochastic noise, and internal orientational order parameters.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/algebras/full, algebras
	- Why the definition of a $k$-algebra? (Hint: A $k$-vector space $A$ equipped with a bilinear associative multiplication map $m: A \otimes_k A \to A$ and a unit map $u: k \to A$.)
	- Why is a finite field extension $K/k$ a $k$-algebra? (Hint: Field multiplication provides the bilinear $k$-algebra multiplication map.)
	- Why does one study division algebras and skew fields? (Hint: They serve as the noncommutative building blocks in Wedderburn's structure theorems.)
	- Why do we care about ideals in algebra? (Hint: Two-sided ideals form kernels of algebra homomorphisms and define quotient algebras; left/right ideals define submodules.)
	- What is a group algebra $k[G]$? (Hint: The free $k$-vector space on basis elements $g \in G$ with multiplication extended linearly from the group product.)
	- What are Artinian algebras? (Hint: Algebras satisfying the descending chain condition on left/right ideals.)
	- What does the Artin-Wedderburn theorem state? (Hint: Every semisimple left-Artinian algebra is isomorphic to a finite direct sum of matrix rings over division algebras $\bigoplus_{i=1}^r M_{n_i}(D_i)$.)
	- What is Maschke's theorem? (Hint: If $G$ is a finite group and the characteristic of $k$ does not divide $|G|$, then the group algebra $k[G]$ is semisimple.)
	- Why are finite-dimensional algebras Noetherian? (Hint: Any ascending chain of ideals must stabilize by vector space dimension constraints.)
	- Why are polynomial algebras and power series rings Noetherian? (Hint: By the Hilbert Basis Theorem.)
	- What is the universal enveloping algebra $U(\mathfrak{g})$? (Hint: The universal unital associative algebra turning Lie brackets into commutators $[X, Y] = XY - YX$.)
	- What is the Weyl algebra? (Hint: The noncommutative Noetherian algebra generated by $X$ and $\partial / \partial X$ satisfying $[ \partial/\partial X, X ] = 1$.)
	- Why do we study $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ in commutative algebra? (Hint: It provides the geometric foundation for algebraic geometry by viewing commutative rings as rings of functions on prime spectra.)
	- What is Goldie's theorem? (Hint: It provides noncommutative analogues of fractions, embedding semiprime Noetherian rings into semisimple Artinian rings of quotients.)
	- How can noncommutative Noetherian algebras be viewed as deformations of commutative algebras? (Hint: The first Weyl algebra $A_1$ deforms the polynomial ring $k[X, Y]$ by replacing $[X, Y] = 0$ with $[X, Y] = 1$.)
	- How does one invent Hochschild cohomology? (Hint: Study associative deformations of noncommutative algebras over $k[[t]]$; obstructions and infinitesimal deformations lie in Hochschild cohomology groups $H\!H^2(A, A)$.)
	- What is an example of a ring that is right-Artinian but not left-Artinian? (Hint: The upper triangular matrix ring $\begin{pmatrix} \mathbb{Q} & \mathbb{R} \\ 0 & \mathbb{R} \end{pmatrix}$ over $\mathbb{Q}$.)
	- How does one define modules over an algebra? (Hint: An abelian group $M$ with a $k$-bilinear action $A \times M \to M$ satisfying module associativity.)
	- How does one define bimodules? (Hint: An abelian group equipped with commuting left $A$-action and right $B$-action: $(a \cdot m) \cdot b = a \cdot (m \cdot b)$.)
	- What is a prime ideal in a noncommutative ring? (Hint: An ideal $P$ such that if $I J \subseteq P$ for two-sided ideals $I, J$, then $I \subseteq P$ or $J \subseteq P$.)
	- How does one define the annihilator of a module? (Hint: $\operatorname{Ann}_A(M) = \{a \in A \mid a \cdot m = 0 \text{ for all } m \in M\}$.)
	- How does one define a simple (irreducible) module? (Hint: A non-zero module whose only submodules are $0$ and $M$.)
	- How does one define a maximal left ideal? (Hint: A proper left ideal $I \subsetneq A$ such that $A/I$ is a simple left $A$-module.)
	- How does one prove the existence of a maximal proper submodule in a finitely generated module? (Hint: Apply Zorn's lemma to the family of proper submodules.)
	- How does one define the Jacobson radical $J(A)$? (Hint: The intersection of all maximal left ideals of $A$, measuring the obstruction to semisimplicity.)
	- What is Nakayama's lemma? (Hint: For a finitely generated left $A$-module $M$, if $J(A) M = M$, then $M = 0$.)
	- How does one decompose a semisimple left $A$-module? (Hint: As a direct sum of simple submodules using idempotent orthogonal decompositions.)
	- Why are completely reducible modules closed under direct sums, submodules, and quotients? (Hint: Every submodule has a complementary summand.)
	- How does the radical of a module generalize the Jacobson radical? (Hint: $\operatorname{Rad}(M)$ is the intersection of all maximal submodules of $M$; for left Artinian $A$, $J(A)M = \operatorname{Rad}(M)$.)
	- What is Schur's lemma? (Hint: If $M$ and $N$ are simple $A$-modules, every non-zero homomorphism $f: M \to N$ is an isomorphism; thus $\operatorname{End}_A(M)$ is a division algebra.)
	- How does one characterize semisimple Artinian rings using the Jacobson radical? (Hint: An Artinian ring $A$ is semisimple if and only if $J(A) = 0$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/II_L/statistical_physics/full, statistical physics
	- What is the network analogy for Bose-Einstein condensation? (Hint: In the Bianconi-Barabási model, node fitness corresponds to energy levels; high-fitness nodes capture a macroscopic fraction of links in a condensation phase.)
	- What is the classification of classical and quantum statistics? (Hint: Maxwell-Boltzmann (distinguishable, no occupancy limit), Fermi-Dirac (indistinguishable, Pauli exclusion with max occupancy 1), and Bose-Einstein (indistinguishable, no occupancy limit).)
	- How does one define a microstate? (Hint: A complete microscopic specification of the quantum state or classical phase space coordinates of every constituent particle.)
	- How does one compute expectation values using the density matrix? (Hint: $\langle A \rangle = \operatorname{Tr}(\rho A)$, where $\rho = \sum p_i |\psi_i\rangle \langle\psi_i|$ represents the mixed state.)
	- What is the fundamental postulate of statistical mechanics? (Hint: An isolated system in thermodynamic equilibrium is equally likely to be found in any of its accessible microstates.)
	- How does one construct the microcanonical ensemble? (Hint: The statistical ensemble of an isolated system with fixed energy in the interval $[E, E + \delta E]$, particle number $N$, and volume $V$.)
	- What is the microcanonical probability distribution? (Hint: Uniform probability $P_n = 1/\Omega(E)$ for all accessible microstates, and $P_n = 0$ otherwise.)
	- How does one define Boltzmann entropy? (Hint: $S = k_B \ln \Omega(E)$, which is extensive and additive for non-interacting subsystems.)
	- How does one define thermodynamic temperature? (Hint: $\frac{1}{T} = \frac{\partial S}{\partial E}$, matching equilibrium conditions when two systems exchange heat.)
	- How does one prove that heat flows from high temperature to low temperature? (Hint: The second law of thermodynamics requires $dS = (1/T_1 - 1/T_2) dE_1 \ge 0$, forcing $dE_1 > 0$ when $T_2 > T_1$.)
	- How does one define the heat capacity of a system? (Hint: $C = \frac{dE}{dT} = T \frac{\partial S}{\partial T}$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/advanced_quantum_field_theory/full, advanced quantum field theory, notes covering functional methods, renormalization, and gauge theories.
	- Why is quantum field theory important? (Hint: It unifies special relativity and quantum mechanics, describing fundamental interactions via path integrals, gauge symmetries, and quantized fields.)
	- What is a Wilson loop? (Hint: A gauge-invariant observable defined by the trace of the path-ordered exponential of a gauge field along a closed loop: $W_C = \operatorname{Tr} \mathcal{P} \exp(i g \oint_C A_\mu dx^\mu)$.)
	- What are phonons? (Hint: Quantized collective vibrational modes (quasiparticles) in a crystalline lattice.)
	- How does spacetime geometry affect field theory? (Hint: Setting the background metric: Minkowski space $(\mathbb{R}^{1,3}, \eta)$ for particle physics, Riemann surfaces for string theory, or general Riemannian manifolds for topological QFT.)
	- How does one define scalar fields on manifolds? (Hint: Smooth sections of trivial bundles $\phi: M \to N$, where $N$ is the target space manifold.)
	- What is the path integral measure $\mathcal{D}\phi$? (Hint: The formal measure over the infinite-dimensional configuration space of fields, regularized via lattice discretization or Faddeev-Popov gauge fixing.)
	- Why are Calabi-Yau manifolds chosen for compactification in string theory? (Hint: They are Ricci-flat Kähler manifolds with $\mathrm{SU}(3)$ holonomy, preserving $\mathcal{N}=1$ supersymmetry in 4D.)
	- Why must field actions be local? (Hint: To preserve causality and ensure equations of motion are local differential equations.)
	- Why does Euclidean path integration introduce a negative exponential $\exp(-S_E / \hbar)$? (Hint: Wick rotation $t \to -i\tau$ turns the oscillating phase $e^{i S / \hbar}$ into a damping factor $e^{-S_E / \hbar}$, establishing a rigorous link with statistical mechanics.)
	- How does one define the partition function in Functorial Quantum Field Theory (FQFT)? (Hint: A symmetric monoidal functor from the cobordism category to the category of vector spaces, where a cylinder corresponds to $e^{-tH}$ and gluing into a torus gives $\operatorname{Tr}(e^{-\beta H})$.)
	- Why study 0-dimensional quantum field theory? (Hint: It reduces functional integrals to ordinary finite-dimensional integrals, allowing exact non-perturbative and perturbative analysis.)
	- How does one compute correlation functions in 0D field theory? (Hint: $\langle f(\phi) \rangle = \frac{1}{Z} \int_\mathbb{R} f(\phi) e^{-S(\phi)/\hbar} \, d\phi$.)
	- How does Wick's theorem work in 0D free field theory? (Hint: For Gaussian actions $S(\phi) = \frac{1}{2} M \phi^2$, $\langle \phi^{2k} \rangle = (2k-1)!! \, M^{-k}$, reducing moments to sums over all pairings.)
	- What is the Isserlis theorem in probability? (Hint: It computes higher moments of multivariate normal distributions as sums of products of covariance matrices across all 2-pairings.)
	- How do Feynman diagrams emerge in perturbation theory? (Hint: Expanding non-quadratic terms in the path integral as power series in the coupling constant produces vertices, while Gaussian integrals connect them via propagators.)
	- What are tachyonic instabilities? (Hint: Negative mass squared $m^2 < 0$ in the potential signals an unstable maximum at $\phi=0$, triggering spontaneous symmetry breaking to the true vacuum.)
	- How does one compute effective actions by integrating out heavy fields? (Hint: Perform the path integral over the high-energy field $\chi$ to generate an effective action $S_{\mathrm{eff}}[\phi]$ for the light field with modified couplings.)
	- How does one integrate over Grassmann variables for fermions? (Hint: Using the Berezin integral with rules $\int d\theta = 0$ and $\int \theta \, d\theta = 1$, yielding the Pfaffian/determinant for Gaussian fermionic integrals.)
	- How is quantum mechanics realized as 1D quantum field theory? (Hint: The path integral $\int \mathcal{D}x(t) \exp\left(-\frac{1}{\hbar}\int (\frac{1}{2}\dot{x}^2 + V(x)) dt\right)$ computes the heat kernel matrix element $\langle x_f | e^{-t H / \hbar} | x_i \rangle$.)
	- What is the Wiener measure? (Hint: The rigorous probability measure on continuous Brownian motion paths underlying Euclidean quantum mechanics.)
	- How does Noether's theorem connect symmetries to conserved currents? (Hint: Continuous global symmetries of the action $\delta S = 0$ lead to conserved currents $\partial_\mu j^\mu = 0$ and time-independent charges $Q = \int j^0 d^3x$.)
	- What is the Ward-Takahashi identity? (Hint: The quantum analogue of Noether's theorem, relating correlation functions to symmetry transformations and insertion of conserved currents.)
	- What is Wilsonian renormalization? (Hint: Successively integrating out high-energy momentum shells $\Lambda - \delta\Lambda \le |k| \le \Lambda$ to produce effective actions with running coupling constants at lower energies.)
	- What are relevant, marginal, and irrelevant operators? (Hint: Under renormalization group flow towards the IR, relevant operators grow, irrelevant operators decay, and marginal operators stay constant to first order.)
	- What is the Callan-Symanzik equation? (Hint: A differential equation $\left[ \mu \frac{\partial}{\partial \mu} + \beta(g) \frac{\partial}{\partial g} + n \gamma \right] G^{(n)} = 0$ expressing the independence of physical observables from the arbitrary renormalization scale $\mu$.)
	- What is the beta function $\beta(g)$? (Hint: $\beta(g) = \frac{\partial g}{\partial \ln \mu}$, encoding the scale dependence of the running coupling constant under RG flow.)
	- What is the Wilson-Fisher fixed point? (Hint: An interacting non-trivial RG fixed point in $d = 4-\varepsilon$ dimensions governing the universal critical exponents of the 3D Ising model.)
	- How does one define principal $G$-bundles in gauge theory? (Hint: A fiber bundle $P \to M$ with structure group $G$ acting freely and transitively on the fibers, where gauge fields correspond to principal connections.)
	- Why is quantum field theory important? (Hint: It provides path integrals and functional methods to study quantum gauge theory. Gauge theories generalise electrodynamics everywhere, transforming fields by Lie groups and producing infinitely redundant descriptions; quantising removes the redundancy but introduces ghost fields and BRST symmetry. Importantly, quantum field theory is a technique.)
	- What is the motivation behind the Wilson loop? (Hint: It is an observable constructed from a gauge connection, used to test how the gauge field behaves around closed curves; more generally, the Wilson loop is a fundamental observable in gauge theory constructions.)
	- What is the motivation behind phonons? (Hint: Crystal vibrations, when quantised, become phonons, i.e. wave–particle duality is general and applies not only to quantum mechanics but also to collective modes.)
	- Why does one need to decide/choose a space? (Hint: It depends on the domain: for particle physics one picks a pseudo-Riemannian manifold, e.g. $(M,g) = (\mathbb{R}^4, \eta)$ with $\eta$ the Minkowski metric, or $(\mathbb{R}^3, \delta)$ with $\delta$ the flat Euclidean metric; in string theory one picks a Riemann surface, such as a sphere or a torus, and only needs a conformal equivalence class of metrics, i.e. a metric up to a scalar factor. For the QFT of knots one chooses an oriented $3$-manifold with no metric. $\mathbb{R}^d$ is noncompact, so it is bad.)
	- Why must one pick a scalar field? (Hint: Field theory is about fields, and a field is a map $M \to N$ whose choice is tasted by a parametrisation of the configuration. The field can be a scalar obtained by choosing an isomorphism on a principal $G$-bundle (or in an infinity-bundle), but one can choose something more general: a target space. In the course, $N$ is a manifold, but ultimately it can be anything that behaves like a manifold, i.e. a sheaf. Basically a field is a value assigned to each parameter point.)
	- What is the entropy of the field? (Hint: It is the measure $D\phi$; it does not actually exist in general. It is the measure used in the partition function, and what it really is is not fully understood in the path integral. It is integrated over all field configurations, weighted by $\exp(-S[\phi]/\hbar)$. It requires the Faddeev–Popov  determinant to correct redundancy in the measure and lattice regularisation to give finite results. There is no compactness, or even local compactness, here; this is a subject of research. Strangely, on compactness is not required, just discretisation.)
	- What does one pick for the field? (Hint: Usually the domain is taken to be $M = [0,T]$ with $M$ an interval, called time (the most typical case), and the field is a map $M \to \mathbb{R}^3$, or paths in $\mathbb{R}^3$. Instead of $\mathbb{R}^3$ one can pick a Calabi–Yau manifold, or pick fields with nonzero spin, e.g. connections on principal $G$-bundles with a nontrivial representation of the Lorentz group.)
	- What is the next step after one picks a field? (Hint: one picks $C$ to be the space of all field configurations, such as some moduli space; this is infinite-dimensional and that makes it hard.)
	- Why does one pick a Calabi–Yau manifold as the target space for a scalar field? (Hint: $SU(5)$ subgroup of the holonomy $SU(3)$ allows a compactification to 4D, and $SU(3)$-holonomy allows quantum supersymmetry to survive compactification, together with solving the vacuum Einstein field equations with zero net expansion or contraction, i.e. Ricci flatness. Hodge numbers determine particle families, charges, and gauge forces in the $1D$ world, so one does a Kähler theory on the Calabi–Yau manifold.)
	- Why does one pick an action? (Hint: One wants a value/statistics for the field, and one wants the action to be local sometimes, so there are no action-at-a-distance effects by terms it but this is a very suspicious assumption; see Bell's theorem and “spooky action at a distance.” One also wants to apply some version of Noether's theorem to it.)
	- Why does one compute the partition function and why is there a minus sign? (Hint: This is where harmonic analysis enters. Euclidean signature gives the minus sign; otherwise there is an $i$. The partition function allows one to do harmonic analysis, e.g $Z = \int D\phi\ e^{-S[\phi]/\hbar}$. The exponential factor suppresses wild contributions using analysis; the problem is that $D\phi$ is not a well-defined measure (no compactness tools), and one fixes this with the lattice regularisation and Faddeev–Popov ghosts. This is the hard part of quantum field theory: finding a working measure. This is the same as the partition function, but in an imaginary time.)
	- How does one compute the partition function of a topological four-functor field theory? (Hint: A functor from the category of $d$-dimensional cobordisms; the partition function is the value of the functor on a $d$-torus, i.e. $Z(T)$. The partition function is the trace over the value of the QFT on the cylinder given by cutting, so the name comes from the cylinder cobordism of the manifold. On Riemannian cobordisms, the value of the QFT on a cylinder of length $t$ is a linear operator of the form $\exp(-\hbar)\, H$; here $H$ is the Hamiltonian (energy). The torus is cut open, then built by gluing the end of the Cylinder, and gluing the ends corresponds to taking the trace of the evolution operator, hence the $d$-torus is mapped to a number.)
	- What is important about the measure $D\phi$ on $C$, where $C$ is the space of all configurations, i.e. a point in $C$ represents a field across all of $M$? (Hint: It does not actually exist, and to invent such a measure is not obvious; constructing a working measure is hard.)
	- Why does one study quantum field theory in dimension $0$? (Hint: Because it is fully rigorous and completely solved.)
	- For a $0$-dimensional quantum field theory, what happens if the universe is assumed to be connected? (Hint: It becomes a single point.)
	- Compute the Lorentz group $O(1,3)$-symmetries for a connected $0$-dimensional universe. (Hint: It is trivial: the manifold is one point, the configuration space is $\mathbb{R}$, and a field is a real variable from the point to $\mathbb{R}$; this is not infinite-dimensional, it has only one dimension.)
	- Compute the scalar field for a connected $0$-dimensional universe. (Hint: It is a map $pt \to \mathbb{R}$, i.e. it assigns a real number to the point.)
	- Compute the action and the partition function of a $0$-dimensional scalar field, and the path integral measure for the partition function. (Hint: The action is e.g. $S(\phi) = \frac{m^{2}}{2}\phi^{2} + V(\phi)$, and $Z = \int D\phi e^{-S(\phi)/\hbar}$; in $d=0$ the path integral measure $D\phi$ is the Lebesgue measure $d\phi$ on $\mathbb{R}$.)
	- Compute the correlation functions in dimension $0$. (Hint: One gets a measure distribution on $\mathbb{R}$; the object of QFT is the expectation value $\langle f\rangle$, i.e. $\langle f\rangle = \frac{1}{Z}\int d\phi\, f(\phi) e^{-S(\phi)/\hbar}$, and this is the expectation of $f$ with respect to that distribution.)
	- Why does one compute the series expansion in $\phi$ of the action? (Hint: One mainly needs the action to be even, so the exponential gives a symmetric decay on both sides of the integral; the partition function itself does not depend on the field, because it is integrated over all field values.)
	- How does one compute the simplest (free) QFT on a $0$-dimensional universe? (Hint: Take the equations of motion to be linear, so superposition holds and particles do not interact; here the partition function becomes a Gaussian integral. This is based on probability theory, and the easiest proof is by Wilsonian renormalisation.)
	- How does one compute the partition function of the simplest QFT of a $0$-dimensional universe? (Hint: It is a Gaussian integral. The matrix is symmetric, so take an orthogonal transformation that diagonalises it (which exists by symmetry), and the Euclidean volume factor $d^n\phi$ is invariant under orthogonal transformations. In terms of eigenvectors, using representation theory, it reduces to a product of $1$-dim Gaussian integrals because the modes are decoupled; one then uses Wick's theorem, i.e. the computation of correlation-function integrals reduces to the combinatorics of counting the number of pairings.)
	- Why does the asymptotic expansion of partition functions come in? (The reminder: The naive Taylor expansion diverges, so one needs an asymptotic series instead; on step one uses Laplace's method / Watson's lemma. The leading term is the semiclassical term, and the remaining terms are quantum corrections.)
	- Why does one want to compute correlation functions or the partition function in QFT? (Hint: The partition function encodes the entire theory, and correlation functions are the observable quantities that give the physical content one measures.)
	- What terms decide the partition function in QFT? (Hint: $Z = Z(m^2, g_n)$, where in the series expansion, $\frac12 m^2 \phi^2$ is the quadratic term and $g_n\phi^n/n!$ are the higher interaction terms; the $g_n$ are the coupling constants, and the expansion in the field looks like a/parametric series.)
	- How does one introduce correlation functions in an $0$-dimensional QFT? (Hint: Pick any function $f$ whose expectation you want; a very good one, however, is a weight of the form $\exp(-S(\phi)/\hbar)$, so that these expectations become convergent integrals. This then motivates the use of more complicated $f$ later; the point is to ask how the field at different points is correlated.) Later, $\langle f\rangle$, or the correlation, depends on $m^2$, $g_n$, and other quantities, but nothing depends on the individual field value since the integral is over all possible fields $\mathbb{R}$.)
	- What is the classical interpretation of free QFTs? (Hint: The action is at most quadratic in $\phi$, the equations of motion are linear, superposition implies no interaction between particles; this is analogous to reducing to the single-particle case, which means there are no interactions among particles.)
	- What does one compute for a $0$-dimensional free QFT? (Hint: Take a positive definite symmetric matrix for simplicity, denoted $M$. Its inverse $M^{-1}$ is the propagator, and it appears in the action formula through the quadratic term $\frac12 M_{ab} \phi^{a}\phi^{b}$. The partition function is a Gaussian integral: $\int d^n\phi\, e^{-\frac{1}{2\hbar}\phi^{T}M\phi} = \frac{(2\pi\hbar)^{n/2}}{\sqrt{\det M}}$. Diagonalise $M$ (the action matrix), since the Lebesgue measure is invariant under an orthogonal map $O$, the integral factorises into $n$ independent $1$-dimensional Gaussian integrals over the eigenvectors; this reduces free QFT to classical probability theory.)
	- How does one generalise the free QFT? (Hint: Add a term $J(\phi)$ to the quadratic action, where $J$ is a source; the details do not matter, and this will be used later to compute correlation functions by taking derivatives with respect of $J$.)
	- How does one compute correlation functions for a $0$-dimensional free QFT and thereby motivate Wick's theorem? (Hint: Proceed as above and use linearity to reduce the polynomial in the average to monomials, handling the odd case since odd correlation functions are vanish. For $m = 2k$, the result is a sum over all pairings of the legs $(1,\dots,2k)$, with each pair $(i,j)$ contributing $M^{-1}_{ij}$; the pairing/edge-count $(2k-1)!!$ in a cheap version of Wick's theorem, and $M^{-1}$ is the propagator appearing in the exponential.)
	- What is Wick's probability theorem (Isserlis' theorem)? (Hint: For a zero-mean multivariate normal random vector $X_1,\dots X_{2k}$, the expectation $\mathbb{E}(X_1X_2\cdots X_{2k})$ is a sum over all complete pairings, each term a product of the $2k$ covariance entries, e.g. $\sum_{\text{pairings}}\prod_{(a,b)\text{pair}} \mathbb{E}(X_aX_b)$. Odd moments vanish, and Isserlis proves the even case by induction using combinatorics. This gives a exact method of Gaussian expectations/characteristic functions.)
	- What is the purpose of the propagator in a $0$-dimensional QFT? (Hint: It reduces the calculation of correlation-function integrals to the combinatorics of pairing indices; note that the number of pairings is $(2k-1)!!$, related to Krened/Connes–Kreimen interpretations. The propagator assigns a weight: one uses it to compute amplitudes by counting the number of ways of pairing up. For interactions, a vertex is of even degree (e.g. 4 legs); pair up legs using Feynman diagrams. Graph enumeration, symmetry factors, and permutations of the dots(lines) are done with orbit-stabiliser arguments. Feynman diagrams are the graph basis, the coproduct the diagram into its divergent subgraphs, and the Hopf algebra and antipode computes the counterterms of BPHZ renormalisation.)
	- What does it mean for a theory to have interactions? (Hint: The action is nonquadratic in the field $\phi$ of Fu.)
	- How does one solve/analyse the interactions? (Hint: Approximate the partition function by an asymptotic series; if the coupling is small (even if $\hbar$ needs to be treated correctly), now use the asymptotic expansion of $Z(\hbar)$ via Laplace's method, i.e. expand the exponent around the peak of the classical configuration; expand $g(t)$ and $h(t)$ in the integral $g(t)e^{h(t)}$, the leading asymptotic term is the semiclassical term, the remaining terms are quantum corrections.)
	- What is quantum field theory about? (Hint: It is about working with the quantum-correction terms, i.e. terms $\propto A\hbar + B\hbar^2$, which appear for interacting theories.)
	- How does one find the asymptotic series expansion of $Z(\hbar, m, \lambda)$? (Hint: Use Gamma functions and their integrals; later the diagram expansion will. See that value $\lambda$ is the coupling constant.)
	- What are tachyons? (Hint: $m^2 < 0$, in which the quadratic term has opposite sign; the action has instability with its minimum at a nonzero field value, this gives symmetry breaking. You choose a minimum and expand the Taylor series around it; the arbitrary choice of the minimum breaks the symmetry.)
	- How does one invent Feynman diagrams? (Hint: Consider the powers of the combination $(\hbar \lambda / m^4)^n$; the power is fixed by dimensional analysis, but the combinatorial coefficient is hard. Rewriting the path integral and using Wick's theorem, the factors arise from the number of ways to pair up $4n$ legs of $n$ vertices. Feynman diagrams then are ways to group quadruple vertices (each of valence 4) together; for example, connecting the two lines of the diagram gives a single point with two loops. Use the orbit-stabiliser theorem—and some counting of connected graphs—to simplify the process.)
	- How does that expand the partition function into different diagrams? (Hint: Expand the partition function as a sum over the various terms, i.e. over the Feynman diagrams one obtains.)
	- Why is the double-scalar field (phi/chi) example important? (Hint: the light field $\phi$ is low energy, and $\chi$ is a high-energy field that is inaccessible; one is interested in correlation functions involving $\phi$ alone. One integrates out $\chi$; then an only fields the effective action for $\phi$. This is a useful way of viewing equal representation of high-energy particles through lower-energy fields, generating an infinite series of new interactions of $\phi$ with a mass shift.)
	- How does one invent the quantity $\log(Z/Z_0)$? (Hint: The Hamiltonian path integral is a good measure. The matter with counting all graphs is tedious, and taking the log selects the sum of all connected graphs.)
	- What is the problem with field-independent terms? (Hint: They play no role in correlation functions because they do not depend on $\phi$, but they cannot be discarded; they contribute to the cosmological constant.)
	- How does one invent supersymmetric theories? (Hint: Arrange that the quantum corrections cancel each other in the asymptotic series of the partition function.)
	- How does one invent effective actions and produce new couplings that have tiny effects? (Hint: Integrate out the high-energy/heavy modes to define the effective action for the low-energy fields; the new couplings are suppressed by negative powers of the heavy mass scale and thus have tiny effects.)
	- How does one compare bosons and fermions? (Hint: bosons obey Bose–Einstein statistics (commutation) and fermions obey Fermi–Dirac statistics (anticommutation); in particular, fermionic fields are Grassmann-odd.)
	- How does one study fermions in a $0$-dimensional QFT? (Hint: One cannot use ordinary path integrals, since these commute; treat the fields as formal symbols and define the path integration subject to the relation $\theta_i\theta_j + \theta_j\theta_i = 0$. Then the exponential allows a power-series expansion, and for a single degree of freedom (the polynomial $a + b\theta$) the Berezin integral gives $\int d\theta\,(a+b\theta) = b$.)
	- Why must one imagine the fields to be complex? (Hint: To get the correct mass term, one needs a field $\phi$ and its complex conjugate $\bar\phi$ (e.g. $m^2|\bar\phi|^2$); for masses/analysis purposes, one works over the complex fields.)
	- How does one construct linear functionals when the fields are formal/Grassmann symbols? (Hint: Since the Grassmann variables cannot be integrated as numbers, the “integral” is a a linear functional from the space of polynomials to the reals, acting as an integral, which gives the Berezin integral.)
	- How does one find the standard choice of action for the QFT in $1$ dimension? (Hint: The standard action is $S[x] = \int dt\big( \gamma and the derivative $\dot x^2``) with a potential $V(x)$, i.e. $\frac{1}{2}g(\dot x,\dot x) + V(x)$, with $\dot x$ the time derivative; nonlinear and $g(\dot x,\dot x)$ may be complicated if the metric is not flat.)
	- How does one quantise / get quantum mechanics? (Hint: Start with a Hilbert space, typically $L^2(M)$, and then take the Hamiltonian operator $H= -\frac12\Delta+V$, where $\Delta$ is the Laplacian (e.g. $\Delta = \frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\partial_\mu \sqrt{g} g^{\mu\rho}\partial_\rho$). The Euclidean amplitude is then the heat kernel $e^{-HT}$; to obtain quantum mechanics, replace the Euclidean time $T$ to $it$, connecting to ordinary QM and the Schrödinger equation.)
	- How does one derive the concatenation property of the heat kernel? (Hint: Since Gaussians convolve, compute the integral $\int d^n z\; K_{t_2}(y,z) K_{t_1}(z,x)$; this concatenation property is $K_{t_1+t_2}(y,x)$. Set a small step $\delta \tau = T/N$; the heat kernel becomes a $N$-step integral of the form $\langle y|e^{-H T}|x\rangle$, which in the limit $N\to\infty$ is the path integral—this is where the path measure comes from.)
	- What is the problem with path integrals arising when writing the heat kernel as a path measure? (Hint: The path integral measure has to be defined; naively this fails, because one would need a Lebesgue measure on an infinite-dimensional space. One assumes the paths are at least continuously condition differentiability; this is not available in general.)
	- What do states mean when evaluating the propagator? (Hint: A state is associated to a codimension-one subspace (e.g. a time slice $N\subset M$ of dimension one less than $\dim M$). Propagator $U$ is a map from a state on one slice to a state on another; the space of states attached to each such subspace is the Hilbert space.)
	- How does one arrive at canonical quantisation? (Hint: Go to the Hamiltonian formulation, hold onto the formal structure (like symmetries) of the classical theory, and quantise only the dynamics of some degrees of freedom while leaving other fields (e.g. second-quantised fields) fixed; this gives the distinction between first and second quantisation.)
	- What does the partition function mean in statistical physics? (Hint: It is $\operatorname{tr} \:e^{-\beta}$$, the trace of the heat kernel; on the cylinder the partition function attaches to a torus—the path integral is integrating over closed loops.)
	- How does one invent local operators? (Hint: The simplest choice for correlation functions in a $1D$ QFT: you just take an operator that depends on the value of the field and finitely many derivatives at a single point; even simpler, no derivatives, just observe the $N\to\mathbb{R}$ function pulled back to $O(x)$.)
	- Why is the order of the local operators in a correlation function important? (Hint: The product of operators is noncommutative and this gives time-ordering; this is already present in QFT perturbation theory, where interactions between different points are given by the propagators at the kinetic terms of last Lagrangian.)
	- What is the big problem with regularisation? (Hint: All mentioned two limiting procedures for the path measure and the action.
