In QFT one struggles with lattice regularisation of the path integral; one would like to decompose the field into Fourier modes (linear combinations of exponentials), take a high-energy cutoff, but the limit does not exist because there is no Lebesgue measure on an infinite-dimensional space.)
	- What are the relevant properties of the Lebesgue measure on a finite/infinite-dimensional inner-product space? (Hint: The volume is positive on nonempty open sets, translation-invariant, and every point $x$ is inside at least one open neighbourhood of finite volume—so no infinite-volume sets hide you.)
	- How does one prove that Lebesgue measure fails on an infinite-dimensional inner-product space? (Hint: Take an open hypercube $C(L)$ and cut it into $2^D$ pieces; each cube has side $L/2$ and the shell contains infinitely many $C(L/2)-copies. If $\operatorname{vol}(C(L/2))$ is nonzero, then $\operatorname{vol}(C(L))$ is infinite; for any $L>0$ all open sets have infinite measure, so the construction is impossible.)
	- What is really needed for the measure, and what new problem arises? (Hint: One only needs $D\phi e^{-S[\phi]}$, and the measure that appears is no longer translation-invariant—this is the Wiener measure. However, for the $4D$ Yang–Mills theory, its existence is a Millennium problem.)
	- What problem does the Wiener measure solve in the QM? (Hint: It gives a rigorous meaning to the functional integral $\int D x \, e^{-S[x]}$; it solves the problem the path in continuous time, and the measure exists. In contrast, that measure is not available for QED and the standard model is open, but it may exist for Yang–Mills only in four dimensions.)
	- Why is regularisation needed? (Hint: In the continuum naive path integral the difference vanishes; but in perturbation theory the path integral is sensitive to time-ordering, and it is not defined when two evaluation times cross; one needs a regularisation with a finite-time lattice, and — something subtle happens at the crossing.)
	- How do you invent the Feynman rules? (Hint: Do a $1D QFT$ on the circle $S^1$, write the path integral, and compute the correlators of the free theory. Move to momentum space and use the orthogonality relations of the Fourier modes; the free theory is then a product of infinitely many $0$-dim fields, one per mode; interactions just couple the modes, so one computes the correlators with Feynman diagrams. For a noncompact space $S^1$ one gets the same as a continuous Fourier transform.)
	- Why does one care about effective field theories in $1$ dimension? (Hint: In dimension $1$ the dynamics is quantum-mechanics, so one has the same separation-of-scales logic as in higher by, and low-energy anomalies can be obtained by integrating out high modes; this is the simplest expansion in which effective actions can be (exactly) computed.)
	- Why is $\log\det = \operatorname{tr}\log$? (Hint: $\det M$ is the product of the eigenvalues of $M$, and the trace is the sum; applied to the log put $\operatorname{tr}\log M = \sum \log \lambda\ =\log(\prod \lambda) `= \log\det M$.)
	- Why is nonlocality bad in physics? (Hint: If the action is nonlocal, the equations to be integral–differential, describing a system that needs to know what is happening all over space/time (e.g. from far away), which is undesirable and leads to acausality.)
	- What is so good about low-energy effective theories? (Hint: The low-energy approximation is that irrelevant operators are suppressed, so only a finite number of couplings matter and the expansion is controllable by powers $E/\Lambda$.)
	- Why do negative mass dimensions appear? (Hint: Low energy means the series is truncated than early; ignoring higher interactions can make it nonunitary. For example, the weak-interaction coupling $G_{\text{weak}}$ has negative mass dimension, so that $G_\text{weak}$ approximates the W-boson propagator or beta decay, and $4$-Fermi theory effective low-en becomes nonunitary; general relativity also has running negatives mass dimension.)
	- How does one introduce the cosmological constant? (Hint: In quantum gravity one sums/all fields an the lattice has objects the graphs over the manifolds, including the singular geometric modes; the result is a superestly partition function, $S_T[x]$, that contains $\frac12\int\dot x^2\, dt$ and the term $\frac{m^2}{2}\int x^2\, dt$ that acts as a cosmological term. The integrals over the $T$-edges which arise in the $1D QFT$ that reproduces the graph integrals.)
	- What is classical field theory? (Hint: Classical field theory relies on the calculus of variations and Noether's theorem to relate symmetries to conservation laws.)
	- How does one derive conservation of charge using classical field theory? (Hint: Start with a local variation $\delta \phi$, generated by a vector/parameter $\epsilon(x)$. If the action $S[\phi]$ is invariant under $x\to x+\xi$ for constant $\xi$, then varying with an arbitrary $\xi(x)$ and using compact support gives $\delta S = 0$ and a conserved Noether current $J$%^mu}. Define a charge $Q = \int_N \star J$, with $N$ a codimension-$1$ hypersurface normal to a time slice; then apply $\nabla_mu J^\mu = 0$, and Stokes' theorem yields conservation of charge. Key ingredients: local $\phi$-variation with compact support, $\delta S=0$, the Noether current, coupled to a time slice with Stoke's theorem.)
	- What is the problem when in Noether's theorem applied in quantum theory? (Hint: The symmetry just to preserve $S[\phi]$, but then one must also act with the measure $D\phi$. Problem: their lattice discretisation of $D\phi$ is necessarily invariant under such the symmetry only restored in the limit. Actually no one has produced an invariant path-integral measure.)
	- Why does one care about correlation functions? (Hint: Noether currents are nice, but true observables are invariant quantities; symmetries manifest themselves in correlations of fields.)
	- Give a simple example of the correlator manifest. (Hint: A complex free field $\phi$: the transformation $\phi\to e^{i\alpha}\phi$, with $\alpha\in \mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}$, and the conjugate field transforms adjointly. If the transformation leaves action and measure invariant, the symmetry forces correlator identity: each propagator joins a $\phi$ and a $\bar{\phi}$; if there are different numbers of fields and $\bar{phi}$, the correlator vanishes.)
	- How does one derive Ward's identity? (Hint: Suppose the action is invariant under an infinitesimal transformation; consider the correlation function of local fields. The zero-order term cancels by symmetry; the variation of the insertions terms gives the Ward identity, which states that the Noether current is transverse except at the insertion points $x_i$, where it carries charges. If $M$ is compact without boundary one gets the global Ward identity by integrating it and using a Stokes theorem: the divergence term vanishes.)
	- How does one invent renormalisation? (Hint: One wants a QFT that is not tested at high energies. We introduce a UV cutoff by integrating out degrees of freedom above the scale. On a compact universe the Laplacian has discrete eigenvalues—e.g. for a flat torus the eigenvalues lie on a lattice via Fourier/Pontryagin duality, so a cutoff keeps a finite number of modes (thus also regularised)–this fails on noncompact manifolds, where the spectra is continuous and the two limits are delicate.)
	- How does one invent the notion of relevant operators? (Hint: QFT starts with from scales; under the Wilsonian RG, infinitely many couplings occur but operators divide into relevant, irrelevant, marginal. At low energies, only finitely many relevant couplings matter; marginal ones have dimension equal to (see below) and matter higher order in αβ; this /RG tells you their behaviour.)
	- How does one deal with infrared divergences? (Hint: For a zero-mass field, e.g. the photon, there is no energy gap; soft photon emissions have arbitrarily low energy: repeated emission indistinguishable from no emission, since a detector with finite resolution cannot see them. Summing over them yields infrared-finite observables. Thus they are a physical feature of massless gauge fields.)
	- How does one split the field into low and high-energy modes by the cutoff $\Gamma_0$? (Hint: Split the Fourier integral at $\Gamma_0$; call the low energy part $\phi$ and the high part $\chi$, so $\phi_{\mathrm{total}} = \phi + \chi$; then define the scale-$\Gamma$ effective action by integrating out the $\chi$ mode.)
	- Perturbatively the Wilsonian RG equation is: for any cutoffs $\Gamma_0$, the RG action is; for any $\epsilon$, take the action at high scale $\Gamma_0$ and produce the effective action at scale $\Gamma_0-\epsilon$; these transformations (law of) central a enubes a one-parameter semigroup (a "renormalisation") acting on the space of actions.)
	- What happens to the coupling constants under the RG action? (Hint: The coupling constants must transform and shift; the field gets rescaled/renormalised.)
	- How is it that the Gaussian free theory is a critical point? (Hint: If all couplings vanish (in particular the mass), integrating out modes nothing happens, and we are at a trivial fixed point; the beta-function zero and quantum corrections zero.)
	- How does one introduce the renormalisation wavefunction renormalization? (Hint: When you integrate out the high modes, the kinetic term for $\phi$ receives corrections; one introduces a factor $Z(\phi)$ that multiplies $( \partial\phi)^2$ to make canonised the kinetic term.)
	- What happens when you differentiate the renormalisation equation with respect to $\ln$? (Hint: You get the Callan–Symanzik equation for the renormalised correlation functions.)
	- How does one obtain the beta functions of the coupling? (Hint: Couplings change with energy scale after the renormalisation flow. Requiring that physical observables large independent of the cutoff gives a $\beta$-function: $\beta(g) = \partial g/\partial \log \mu$, depending on $g$ through the implicit dependence of $g$ on $\mu$; if the $\mu$ fixes the scale, relation holds at fixed energy.)
	- What is the anomalous dimension? (Hint: In the RG/Callan–Symanzik equation the term that gives the anomalous dimension $\gamma(\phi)$ of a field; it quantifies how the quantum corrections change the effective scaling dimension of the field/operators from classically.)
	- What are coupling constants? Hints: In general $g_n$ = the coefficients multiplying the interaction monomials $\mathcal{O}_n$, e.g. the coupling in $\frac{g_n}{n!}\phi^n$; they we quantify the strength of the interaction, and they are running constants that scale with the RG flow.)
	- How to get the Callan–Symanzik equation for correlation functions? (Hint: Differentiate the renormalised $n$-point functions $G^{(n)}(x_1\dots x_n)$ with respect to the cutoff/RG parameter $\ln \mu$, using the beta and anomalous functions; this gives a linear PDE for $G^{(n)}$ in the couplings and fields.)
	- What renormalisation group flow and how does one invent it? (Hint: flow to low scales, integrating out high-energy fluctuations, parametrised by $\Gamma$. As $\Gamma\to0$, move toward the infrared; one follows the couplings as they run with the scale.)
	- What is the purpose of the beta function? (Hint: It measures the dependence of a coupling parameter on energy scale; it is itself independent of the scale. The scale is self- but predicts scale: under a flow, $g(\kappa) = G^{-1}\left((\kappa/\mu)^d G(g(\mu))\right)$. If the beta-function vanish at $g_*$, the theory is scale-invariant—this is a fixed point and in many cases gives rise to a CFT.)
	- How does one compute couplings and masses? (Hint: One computes/completes them: e.g., by matching the computed predictions to experimentally the results, or by requiring that the mass be the physical pole of the operator at some momentum.)
	- How / why does one choose the UV cutoff and the renormalisation scale? (Hint: The objective is to absorb divergences more broadly the couplings and to make the coefficient nonzero (e.g. retain the more relevant part) of some operators. The renormalisation scale $\zeta)$ is arbitrary; it gives the running of couplings.)
	- How would does one invent the renormalisation? / What theory does a renormalisation choose? (Hint: one keeps the low-energy scale fixed and watches the theory as the UV cutoff is taken to infinity; choose field/mass/coupling, renormalisation = define procedure.) One introduces wavefunction renormalisation: scale the fields so the kinetic term has the same normalisation; then the new Lagrangian reproduces the same low-energy IR physics. The flow gives the Callan–Symanzik beta functions, i.e. the $\beta = \partial g/\partial \log \mu$.
	- How does one solve the beta functions? (Hint: Solve the Callan–Symanzik equation for $g(\mu)$ and write the RG flow as $\ln(\zeta)= \int^{g} \frac{dg}{\beta(g)}$; this shows the explicit running and gives the relation of $g$ at different scales.)
	- What is the simplest nontrivial example of performing RG flow? (Hint: Take a Gaussian/Lagrangian $\frac{1}{2}\langle \partial\phi)^2$ free field: split it into $\phi_{\rm IR}$ and $\phi_{UV}$. They do not couple; integrating out $\phi_{UV}$ gives only a constant in the partition function. Rescale the field/coordinates so low modes keep the same kinetic form. Then add a perturbingly tiny mass $m^2\\phi^2$, which is a relevant coupling: $g(\zeta) = g_0 \zeta^{-2}$ flows to zero in the IR, $\beta(g)= -2g$, and the fixed point $g=0$ is the Gaussian fixed point. Analogously for a $\phi^4$ theory near $d=4$.)
	- What are non-fractic examples of fixed points? (Hint: the Wilson–Fisher fixed point describes scaling of self-avoiding random walks and for polymers in good solutions—experimental universality,… since all long chains behaving the same. Bacterial complexity there are fixed points in the KPZ equation for interfaces; financial/Levy examples are other.)
	- What (and what kind) nonperturbative computation does the Wilsonian RG provide? (Hint: Lower the cutoff step by step, use the integration over the high momentum shell at each step, and repeat; the effective action is generated directly without relying solely on Feynman graphs, and one inspects the long-distance physics within universality.)
	- How does one interpret probability theory as a trivial QFT? (Hint: A $0$-dimensional field theory: the expectation $\langle f\rangle = \int dx f(x)P(x)$; the "action" has $S(x)=-\log P(x)$, real. The partition function is the normalisation; the generating function, cumulants are the connected $n$-point parts of $x$. This is the "trivial" field theory.)
	- How does RG flow prove the central limit theorem? (Hint: Under the RG, computing independent variables (the sum) gives the Gaussian as the fixed point; with the central limit theorem is a special case of the Wilsonian renormalisation-group flow.)
	- How does one invent perturbation/effective theory field? (Hint: one defines a Wilson/expansodian flow in the space of all effective Lagrangians; exactly as in lattice field theory, one constructs by successive integration and these flows reduce to low-terms.)
	- What are the cases called with an IR and may be an ultra? UV fixed points? (Hint: the UV and IR fixed points are the fixed points of the RG flow at high or low bond energy.)  (This bullet may be redundant; keep if present.)
	- How does one define a "critical point"? (Hint: In the $0$-dim (probability) case, the starting point uses the Gaussian (no interactions) as behaviour. A critical point is a choice of the couplings such that all beta functions vanish: $\beta_i(g_*)=0$.)
	- How does one compute the renormalisation group equation for a two point function? (Hint: Write the two-point function $\phi$ at a fixed point $g_*$, and "kill" the beta function (set $\beta=0$), so the formal RG derivative gives the simple scaling of a field propagating in a CFT.)
	- How does one move away from a Gaussian universe? (Hint: Start near the critical surface but not at the critical point; the essential move is a Taylor expansion of $\beta$-functions around the fixed point: the flows are controlled by the linearisation, so there are linear $\beta$-functions = leading derivatives; nonlinear bra decret traffic. This is the "linearisation" of the infinitesimal RG around the Gaussian peak.)
	- How does one construct the Lévy-flight RG-LRG? (Hint: The mass/renormalisation point: the variable renormalisation becomes $\mathrm{L}(x)$, $x' = x/2^{1/\alpha}$, with $2\to\alpha$ not fixed at 2. Take $a$ arbitrary, the general (non-Gaussian) term is not Gaussian; use the Fourier transform to compute the beta of the Gaussian coupling: $\beta(g) = (\alpha-2)g$. For $\alpha>2$ Gaussian is stable; for $\alpha<2$, $\beta<0$, so set $\beta(g)=0$ gives a *non*Gaussian fixed point, the Lévy (stable) fixed point. The characteristic function is $\exp(-\mathrm{const}|k|^\alpha)$, non-analytic in $k$; this is an exact fixed point for a "Lévy flight": long jumps as well as short steps, and self-similarity across scales.)
	- How does one enter critical surface? (Hint: By linearizing the RG as above near the fixed point, one of the eigenvectors of the matrix $M_{ij} = \partial \beta_i/\partial g_j$ become stable directions; the irrelevant ones form a stable surface—the critical surface—made of all irrelevant modes; it is infinite in the IR limit, of finite codimension due to relevant modes.)
	- Why can we do physics? (Hint: Starting at the scale $\Gamma_0$, with arbitrary relevant and irrelevant modes, the flow ends at a low; infrared critical trajectory. Although below an infinite number of couplings can be present, at low energies only finitely many of them (the relevant ones/certain marginal) need to be known.)
	- What are marginal operators? (Hint: By stable dimension: the coupling constants considered with dimension/$d=4$; Δ is ordinary marginal if $\Delta_i = d$, i.e. the coupling is dimensionless. At tree level they appear “unchanged” under the RG flow and so can become marginal-running; they are never exponentially suppressed, so they often govern the IR-1D phenomena (as in QED and QCD).)
	- How does one find the high-energy theory from the low-energy? (Hint: The data of the UV at $\Gamma_0=\infty$ cannot be determined by low energies; one must make an assumption and see if $\Gamma_0\to\infty$ holds after renormalisation.)
	- Compute the RG flow for a local-potential approximation (Hint: For $d>2$ one restricts to a Lagrangian of the form $\frac{1}{2}(\partial\phi)^2 + V(\phi)$; the local potential approximation—ignore higher derivative terms—and split into low/high mode fields as before, then integrate the high modes).
	- What does the infrared fixed point (IR) have do with the quasi-integrable? (Hint: Infrared: the fields h) are almost constant; the low-energy $\phi$ can be taken as a constant global mode, and the high modes $\chi$ have nonzero momenta.)
	- How does one keep the $\chi$-path integral well defined/finite? (Hint: Operate on a compact space, e.g., a torus $T^d$L with length $L$; the eigenvalues are discrete and there are finitely many modes below the cutoff, so the path integral is just a finite product Gaussian integrals.)
	- What is exactly mass free theory (=free–free)? (Hint: Only coupling=$\to $ point with no vertices, no impurity above the gradient terms: the Gaussian fixed point. The local couplings are all zero, so there is no interactions; free fields exist as fixed.)
	- Wilson–Fisher fixed point: how does one that argument? (Hint: In $d=4-\epsilon$ dimensions, the natural coupling $u$ in $\phi^4$ has positive classical scaling, but dimensional regularization combined with the one-loop beta function $\beta(u) = -d (rcover) u + O(u^2)$ shows the existence of one extra nontrivial fixed point the WF fixed point would not be in $d=4$.)
	- What is perturbative renormalisation in traditional QFT? (Hint: One works around asymptotic $\hbar$ with loop integrals, made the UV divergent, then defines a subtract at the diagrams/operators; the counterterms remove the initial divergences.)
	- What is a renormalisation scheme? (Hint: Choosing the counter term finite parts: for each coupling $\delta\lambda$ the interaction coefficient is defined to be $m^2 \phi^2,Z_{1}$ etc; method describes how to specify the arbitrariness.  The scheme is one selected such choice, e.g. minimally subtracting.)
	- What is the on-shell renormalisation scheme? (Hint: One fixses the counter terms until propagators have a pole at $k^2 = -m_{\mathrm{phys}}^2$, where $m_{phys}$ is the physical mass, and the residue of that pole is the identity/residue $=1$.)
	- What is important in a U(1) gauge theory? (Hint: a QED in four spacetime naturally: the propagator of a massless vector boson gives the phenomenon; the field can be changed by altogether local rephasing $e^{i\lambda(x)}$, the soldering A$ fields with $U(1)$ transform.)
	- How do you use dimensional regularisation perturbatively? *Hint:* Do the theory in $D=4+O(\epsilon)$; evaluate loops as analytic functions of $\epsilon$ (using conjucture: e.g. Gamma functions), subtract the poles $\propto \epsilon^{-n}$; the result is finite for the analytic continuation. It fails there is no-integer symmetric when $\epsilon$ must be analytically continued appropriately.
	- How does one invent non-abelian gauge theory? (Hint: Start from a manifold; since the fields should be glued from frame vector spaces as vector bundles and a trivial on a local chart, all a trivial locally. Physics introduces by wanting local bundles, and the difference appears: $\mathfrak{g}$ can see no. One has to pick a basis at each point; frames introduce gauge indices and this relates to the Lie group $G$.)
	- *Definition of gauge of the field:* (Hint: In a vector bundle $E$, every fiber should negative be the same vector space, but there is no **canonical** choice of identification; making such identification is a *gauge choice*. The field, twisting of the fiber: fields are sections $\phi$ of some vector become $E$.)
	- Why do we need tangent/cotangent bundles? *Answer:* They be combine via direct sums and tensor products into general tensor-product bundles; (vector) tensor fields are sections.)
	- How does one get $GL$-bundles/G-bundles? (Hint: If the fiber is $V=\mathbb{R}^n with some inner product $g$, one wants $g$ to be arrival: trivialisation is not just a linear isomorphism but/is orthogonal, so that changed between charts are transitions in $O(n)$. More generally, a $G$-bundle $E$ is a vector bundle with all transition functions taking values in a Lie subgroup $G\subseteq GL(V)$; specify transitions functions satisfying the cocycle condition $g_{kj}g_{ji}=g_{ki}$.)
	- How define principal $G$-bundle? (Hint: Instead of a vector space $V$, take the group $G$ itself as the model fibre on which $G$ acts; the right action is free, the transition functions are the usual left-multiplication and sections.")
	- How does final one specify a quantum gauge theory? (Hint: One picks a spacetime $M$, a gauge group $G$—e.g. the gauge group $U(1)$ for QED, $SU(3)$ ones QCD, $SU(3)\times SU(2)\times U(1)$ for the standard model—and obtain a principal $G$-bundle $P$ above $M$. Then pick a category representation $\mathbf{\rho}$ of $G$; the associated bundle is for an associated vector bundle; fields are sections of associated bundle. Examples: $U(1)$-Maxwell with a complex scalar field.)
	- Prove associated bundle is canonical: (Hint: The principal $G$-bundle by transition; the associated bundle is (and only) via these; other covers give different but equivalent cocycles (same in sheaf cohomology) leading to isomorphic bundles.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/differential_geometry/full, differential geometry
	- How does one define differentiability on a manifold? (Hint: Use coordinate charts $(U, \varphi)$ to transfer calculus from $\mathbb{R}^n$ to the manifold locally.)
	- How does one define a chart on a topological space? (Hint: A homeomorphism $\varphi: U \to \varphi(U) \subseteq \mathbb{R}^n$ from an open subset $U \subseteq M$ onto an open subset of $\mathbb{R}^n$.)
	- What is a smooth atlas? (Hint: A collection of charts $\{(U_\alpha, \varphi_\alpha)\}$ covering $M$ such that all transition maps $\varphi_\beta \circ \varphi_\alpha^{-1}$ are smooth diffeomorphisms on their domains.)
	- How does one define a smooth manifold? (Hint: A second-countable Hausdorff topological space equipped with a maximal smooth atlas.)
	- Why is the second-countable condition required for manifolds? (Hint: To ensure the existence of partitions of unity and exclude pathological spaces like the long line.)
	- How does one define smooth maps between manifolds? (Hint: A map $F: M \to N$ is smooth if for all charts $(U, \varphi)$ on $M$ and $(V, \psi)$ on $N$, the composition $\psi \circ F \circ \varphi^{-1}$ is smooth on $\mathbb{R}^n$.)
	- How does one define tangent vectors via derivations? (Hint: Tangent vectors at $p \in M$ are $\mathbb{R}$-linear derivations $v: C^\infty(M) \to \mathbb{R}$ satisfying the Leibniz rule $v(fg) = v(f)g(p) + f(p)v(g)$.)
	- How does one define the differential of a smooth map? (Hint: The linear map $dF_p: T_p M \to T_{F(p)} N$ defined by $(dF_p(v))(g) = v(g \circ F)$ for $g \in C^\infty(N)$.)
	- What is a partition of unity subordinate to an open cover? (Hint: A family of smooth functions $\rho_\alpha: M \to [0, 1]$ with $\operatorname{supp}(\rho_\alpha) \subseteq U_\alpha$, locally finite supports, and $\sum_\alpha \rho_\alpha = 1$.)
	- How does one define embedded submanifolds? (Hint: A subset $S \subset M$ that locally admits slice coordinate charts where $S \cap U = \{x_{k+1} = \dots = x_n = 0\}$.)
	- What is the regular value theorem? (Hint: If $c \in N$ is a regular value of $F: M \to N$, then $F^{-1}(c)$ is an embedded submanifold of dimension $\dim M - \dim N$.)
	- What is a smooth vector field? (Hint: A smooth section of the tangent bundle $\pi: TM \to M$, or equivalently a derivation of the algebra $C^\infty(M)$.)
	- How does one define the Lie bracket of vector fields? (Hint: Define $[X, Y](f) = X(Y(f)) - Y(X(f))$, which satisfies bilinearity, antisymmetry, and the Jacobi identity.)
	- What are integral curves and why do they exist? (Hint: Curves $\gamma(t)$ satisfying $\dot{\gamma}(t) = X(\gamma(t))$; existence and uniqueness follow from the Picard-Lindelöf theorem on ODEs.)
	- What is a complete vector field? (Hint: A vector field whose integral curves are defined for all $t \in \mathbb{R}$, generating a global 1-parameter group of diffeomorphisms.)
	- How does one define the Lie derivative? (Hint: Differentiate a tensor field along the local flow $\Phi_t$ of a vector field $X$: $\mathcal{L}_X Y = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{(\Phi_{-t})_* Y - Y}{t} = [X, Y]$.)
	- What is a Lie group? (Hint: A smooth manifold $G$ equipped with a group structure such that multiplication and inversion are smooth maps.)
	- What is the Lie algebra of a Lie group? (Hint: The Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g} \cong T_e G$ of left-invariant vector fields under the Lie bracket.)
	- How does one define differential forms? (Hint: Smooth sections of the exterior algebra bundle $\Lambda^k(T^*M)$, acting as alternating multilinear forms on tangent vectors.)
	- How does one define the exterior derivative $d: \Omega^k(M) \to \Omega^{k+1}(M)$? (Hint: The unique $\mathbb{R}$-linear operator satisfying $d^2 = 0$, $d(\omega \wedge \eta) = d\omega \wedge \eta + (-1)^k \omega \wedge d\eta$, and $df(X) = X(f)$ for 0-forms.)
	- What is de Rham cohomology? (Hint: The cohomology groups $H_{\mathrm{dR}}^k(M) = \ker(d^k) / \operatorname{im}(d^{k-1})$ measuring closed forms that are not exact.)
	- What is the Poincaré lemma? (Hint: Every closed differential form on a star-shaped or contractible open set is exact for $k \ge 1$.)
	- What is Stokes' theorem for manifolds with boundary? (Hint: $\int_M d\omega = \int_{\partial M} \omega$ for any compactly supported $(n-1)$-form on an oriented $n$-dimensional manifold with boundary.)
	- What is a Koszul connection on a vector bundle $E \to M$? (Hint: A linear map $\nabla: \Gamma(E) \to \Gamma(T^*M \otimes E)$ satisfying the Leibniz rule $\nabla(f s) = df \otimes s + f \nabla s$.)
	- How does one define parallel transport and geodesics? (Hint: A section $s(t)$ along a curve $\gamma$ is parallel if $\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}} s = 0$; a curve is a geodesic if its velocity vector is parallel: $\nabla_{\dot{\gamma}} \dot{\gamma} = 0$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/riemannian_geometry/full, Riemannian geometry
	- How does one write vector and tensor fields in local coordinates? (Hint: Express $X = \sum X^i \frac{\partial}{\partial x^i}$ and tensors $T = \sum T_{j_1 \dots j_l}^{i_1 \dots i_k} \frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i_1}} \otimes \dots \otimes dx^{j_l}$.)
	- What is Einstein summation convention? (Hint: Automatic summation over repeated upper and lower indices.)
	- What is a Riemannian metric? (Hint: A smooth, symmetric, positive-definite $(0, 2)$-tensor field $g \in \Gamma(\operatorname{Sym}^2 T^*M)$ defining an inner product on each tangent space $T_p M$.)
	- How does the Whitney embedding theorem provide an isometric immersion? (Hint: Any smooth $n$-manifold embeds into $\mathbb{R}^{2n}$; pulling back the Euclidean metric $g = F^* g_{\mathrm{Eucl}}$ induces a Riemannian metric on $M$.)
	- What is an isometry between Riemannian manifolds? (Hint: A diffeomorphism $f: (M, g) \to (N, h)$ preserving metrics via pullback: $f^* h = g$.)
	- What is a bi-invariant metric on a Lie group? (Hint: A Riemannian metric on $G$ that is invariant under both left and right translations; exists on any compact Lie group by Haar averaging.)
	- What is the Levi-Civita connection? (Hint: The unique affine connection $\nabla$ on $(M, g)$ that is metric-compatible ($\nabla g = 0$) and torsion-free ($\nabla_X Y - \nabla_Y X = [X, Y]$).)
	- How does one compute Christoffel symbols for the Levi-Civita connection? (Hint: $\Gamma_{ij}^k = \frac{1}{2} g^{kl} \left( \partial_i g_{jl} + \partial_j g_{il} - \partial_l g_{ij} \right)$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/symplectic_geometry/full, symplectic geometry
	- What is a symplectic vector space? (Hint: A real vector space $V$ equipped with a non-degenerate, skew-symmetric bilinear form $\Omega: V \times V \to \mathbb{R}$.)
	- What is the standard form theorem for symplectic vector spaces? (Hint: Every finite-dimensional symplectic vector space has even dimension $2n$ and admits a symplectic basis where $\Omega = \sum e_i^* \wedge f_i^*$.)
	- What are isotropic, coisotropic, and Lagrangian subspaces? (Hint: A subspace $W \subseteq V$ is isotropic if $W \subseteq W^\Omega$, coisotropic if $W^\Omega \subseteq W$, and Lagrangian if $W = W^\Omega$ ($\dim W = \frac{1}{2} \dim V$).)
	- What is a symplectic manifold? (Hint: A smooth $2n$-dimensional manifold $M$ equipped with a closed, non-degenerate differential 2-form $\omega$ ($d\omega = 0$, $\omega^n \ne 0$).)
	- Why is the volume of a compact symplectic manifold non-zero in de Rham cohomology? (Hint: $\omega^n$ is a non-vanishing volume form, so $\int_M \omega^n > 0$, implying $[\omega] \ne 0 \in H_{\mathrm{dR}}^2(M)$.)
	- What is a symplectomorphism? (Hint: A diffeomorphism $f: M \to M$ preserving the symplectic form: $f^* \omega = \omega$.)
	- What is Moser's stability theorem? (Hint: If $\omega_t$ is a smooth family of symplectic forms on a compact manifold with constant cohomology class $[\omega_t]$, then there exists a family of diffeomorphisms $\psi_t$ such that $\psi_t^* \omega_t = \omega_0$.)
	- What is Darboux's theorem? (Hint: Every symplectic manifold is locally symplectomorphic to standard $\mathbb{R}^{2n}$ with coordinates $(x_1, \dots, x_n, y_1, \dots, y_n)$ where $\omega = \sum_{i=1}^n dx_i \wedge dy_i$.)
	- What is the canonical symplectic structure on a cotangent bundle $T^*X$? (Hint: The canonical 1-form $\alpha = \sum p_i dq_i$ gives the symplectic form $\omega = -d\alpha = \sum dq_i \wedge dp_i$.)
	- What is a Lagrangian submanifold? (Hint: An $n$-dimensional submanifold $L \subset M^{2n}$ such that the pullback of the symplectic form vanishes: $\iota^* \omega = 0$.)
	- What is the Lagrangian neighborhood theorem? (Hint: A tubular neighborhood of a Lagrangian submanifold $L \subset M$ is symplectomorphic to a neighborhood of the zero section in $T^*L$.)
	- What is an almost complex structure $J$? (Hint: A smooth bundle endomorphism $J: TM \to TM$ satisfying $J^2 = -\operatorname{id}$.)
	- When is an almost complex structure compatible with a symplectic form? (Hint: When $g(u, v) = \omega(u, Jv)$ defines a Riemannian metric and $\omega(Ju, Jv) = \omega(u, v)$.)
	- What is the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem? (Hint: An almost complex structure $J$ is integrable (induced by complex charts) if and only if its Nijenhuis tensor $N_J$ vanishes identically.)
	- What is a Kähler manifold? (Hint: A manifold equipped with a Riemannian metric $g$, a symplectic form $\omega$, and an integrable complex structure $J$ that are mutually compatible.)
	- What is the Hodge decomposition on a compact Kähler manifold? (Hint: Decomposes de Rham cohomology into Dolbeault cohomology groups $H^k(M, \mathbb{C}) \cong \bigoplus_{p+q=k} H^{p,q}(M)$ with Hodge symmetry $H^{p,q} \cong \overline{H^{q,p}}$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/the_standard_model/full, standard model, notes on gauge theory and the electroweak-strong interactions.
	- What gauge groups mediate the fundamental forces in the Standard Model? (Hint: The gauge group is $\mathrm{SU}(3)_C \times \mathrm{SU}(2)_L \times \mathrm{U}(1)_Y$, mediating strong, weak, and hypercharge interactions via gluons, $W^\pm/Z^0$ bosons, and photons.)
	- How are gauge fields represented geometrically? (Hint: As connection 1-forms $A = A_\mu^a T^a dx^\mu$ on principal $G$-bundles with curvature given by the field strength $F = dA + g A \wedge A$.)
	- What are the exact symmetries of the Standard Model? (Hint: Color $\mathrm{SU}(3)_C$ and electromagnetism $\mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{EM}}$ are unbroken exact gauge symmetries.)
	- What are gauge anomalies? (Hint: Classical symmetries of an action that are broken by quantum loop effects in the path integral measure.)
	- How does spontaneous symmetry breaking occur in the Standard Model? (Hint: The Higgs scalar field develops a non-zero vacuum expectation value $\langle \Phi \rangle = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \begin{pmatrix} 0 \\ v \end{pmatrix}$, breaking $\mathrm{SU}(2)_L \times \mathrm{U}(1)_Y \to \mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{EM}}$.)
	- What is Goldstone's theorem? (Hint: Spontaneous breaking of continuous global symmetries produces massless scalar particles (Nambu-Goldstone bosons).)
	- What is the Higgs mechanism? (Hint: In gauge theories, Goldstone bosons are "eaten" to become the longitudinal polarization states of gauge bosons, giving mass to $W^\pm$ and $Z^0$ while keeping the photon massless.)
	- What are Dirac and chiral representations of spinors? (Hint: Dirac spinors transform under the Clifford algebra $\{\gamma^\mu, \gamma^\nu\} = 2\eta^{\mu\nu}$; chiral projectors $P_{L,R} = \frac{1}{2}(1 \mp \gamma^5)$ separate left- and right-handed Weyl fermions.)
	- What is the CPT theorem? (Hint: Any local, Lorentz-invariant unitary quantum field theory is invariant under the combined operations of Charge conjugation, Parity inversion, and Time reversal.)
	- What are the Sakharov conditions for baryogenesis? (Hint: Baryon number violation, C and CP violation, and departure from thermal equilibrium.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/quantum_computation/full, quantum computation
	- Why is the halting problem undecidable? (Hint: Diagonalization proves that no Turing machine can determine whether an arbitrary program halts on a given input.)
	- How does one define a formal language in computational complexity? (Hint: A subset of bit strings $L \subseteq \{0, 1\}^*$, with input size measured by string length $n$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/modern_statistical_methods/full, modern statistical methods
	- Why are modern statistical methods needed for high-dimensional data? (Hint: To handle the $p \gg n$ regime where the number of parameters exceeds the sample size.)
	- When can ordinary least squares (OLS) be used? (Hint: When the design matrix $X$ has full column rank, making $X^T X$ invertible.)
	- What does the Gauss-Markov theorem state? (Hint: The OLS estimator $\widehat{\beta}_{\mathrm{OLS}} = (X^T X)^{-1} X^T Y$ is the Best Linear Unbiased Estimator (BLUE), minimizing variance.)
	- How does one define the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE)? (Hint: $\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{MLE}} = \arg\max_\theta \ell(\theta; Y) = \arg\max_\theta \sum \ln f(y_i; \theta)$.)
	- What is the Cramér-Rao lower bound? (Hint: The variance of any unbiased estimator is lower bounded by the inverse of the Fisher information matrix: $\operatorname{Var}(\widehat{\theta}) \ge I(\theta)^{-1}$.)
	- Why does one use biased estimators like ridge regression? (Hint: Introducing small bias reduces variance significantly, lowering the overall Mean Squared Error (MSE) via the bias-variance tradeoff.)
	- How does ridge regression resolve singularity? (Hint: Adding a penalty $\lambda I$ makes $X^T X + \lambda I$ invertible for any $\lambda > 0$.)
	- How does singular value decomposition (SVD) analyze ridge regression? (Hint: Decomposing $X = U \Sigma V^T$ shrinks singular components by factors of $\frac{\sigma_i^2}{\sigma_i^2 + \lambda}$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/ramsey_theory/full, Ramsey theory
	- What is the fundamental principle of Ramsey theory? (Hint: Complete disorder is impossible: any sufficiently large structure, when partitioned into finitely many pieces, must contain a monochromatic substructure.)
	- What is Ramsey's theorem for graphs? (Hint: For any integers $k, l$, there exists a minimal $R(k, l)$ such that every 2-coloring of the edges of $K_N$ contains a monochromatic $K_k$ or $K_l$.)
	- What does Rado's theorem state? (Hint: Characterizes partition-regular systems of linear equations $A x = 0$ over $\mathbb{N}$ using the columns property.)
	- How does one define an $r$-set? (Hint: A subset containing exactly $r$ distinct elements.)
	- How does one define a hypergraph? (Hint: A pair $(V, E)$ where edges $E \subseteq \mathcal{P}(V)$ are subsets of vertices, or $E \subseteq V^{(r)}$ for $r$-uniform hypergraphs.)
	- How does one define a monochromatic coloring? (Hint: A subset $Y \subseteq X$ is monochromatic under $c: X \to [k]$ if $c|_Y$ is constant.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_L/logic/full, logic
	- Who formalized the first mathematical notion of proof? (Hint: Kurt Gödel, motivated by the completeness and incompleteness theorems.)
	- What is natural deduction? (Hint: A proof system introduced by Gentzen based on introduction and elimination rules for logical connectives.)
	- How does one write proofs in natural deduction? (Hint: Construct proof trees where assumptions are discharged using introduction rules and premises are broken down using elimination rules.)
	- What is the implication elimination rule (Modus Ponens)? (Hint: From $A$ and $A \to B$, deduce $B$.)
	- What is the principle of explosion (ex falso quodlibet)? (Hint: From a contradiction or falsehood $\bot$, any proposition $C$ can be derived.)
	- How does one define negation constructively? (Hint: Define $\neg A$ as $A \to \bot$.)
	- How does classical logic differ from intuitionistic logic? (Hint: Classical logic adds the Law of the Excluded Middle ($A \lor \neg A$) or Double Negation Elimination ($\neg\neg A \to A$).)
	- How does Russell's paradox arise in naive set theory? (Hint: Defining $R = \{x \mid x \notin x\}$ yields $R \in R \iff R \notin R$, proving inconsistency in unconstrained comprehension.)
	- What are the combinators K and S? (Hint: Axioms in combinatory logic that axiomatize the implicational fragment of intuitionistic logic: $K: A \to (B \to A)$ and $S: (A \to B \to C) \to (A \to B) \to (A \to C)$.)
	- What is Peirce's law? (Hint: $((A \to B) \to A) \to A$, which holds classically but is unprovable in intuitionistic propositional logic.)
	- What is linear logic? (Hint: A resource-sensitive logic where assumptions cannot be duplicated or discarded arbitrarily, eliminating weakening and contraction.)
	- What is proof-theoretic harmony? (Hint: A balance where elimination rules extract no more and no less information than what was put in by the introduction rules.)
	- What is the Curry-Howard correspondence? (Hint: The isomorphism between constructive logic and typed lambda calculus: propositions are types, and proofs are programs.)
	- How does Kripke semantics model intuitionistic logic? (Hint: Uses partially ordered sets of possible worlds where truth is monotonic: $w \le w'$ and $w \Vdash A \implies w' \Vdash A$.)
	- What are Heyting algebras? (Hint: Bounded lattices equipped with a relative pseudo-complement that provide algebraic models for intuitionistic logic, generalizing Boolean algebras.)
	- What is the Gödel-Gentzen negative translation? (Hint: A mapping that embeds classical logic into intuitionistic logic by inserting double negations on subformulas.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/symmetries_fields_and_particles/full, symmetries in physics, a lot of this stuff is better covered in the differential geometry notes, so not covered here. The most important part of these notes are root systems.
	- Why are symmetry groups used in physics? (Hint: Symmetries leave physical laws invariant; continuous global symmetries yield conserved charges via Noether's theorem.)
	- Why are Lie algebras studied instead of Lie groups? (Hint: Lie algebras linearize the group near the identity, reducing non-linear group manifolds to linear algebra.)
	- What is a matrix Lie group? (Hint: A closed subgroup of $\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{C})$, such as $\mathrm{SO}(n)$, $\mathrm{SU}(n)$, or $\mathrm{Sp}(n)$.)
	- What is the orthogonal group $\mathrm{O}(n)$? (Hint: The group of real matrices preserving the Euclidean inner product $M^T M = I$, with $\det M = \pm 1$.)
	- What is the unitary group $\mathrm{U}(n)$? (Hint: The group of complex matrices preserving the Hermitian inner product $U^\dagger U = I$.)
	- Why is the Lorentz group $\mathrm{O}(3, 1)$ non-compact? (Hint: It preserves the indefinite Minkowski metric $\eta = \operatorname{diag}(1, -1, -1, -1)$, allowing unbounded boost parameters.)
	- How does one compute the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{su}(n)$? (Hint: Traceless anti-Hermitian $n \times n$ matrices: $\mathfrak{su}(n) = \{X \in M_n(\mathbb{C}) \mid X^\dagger = -X, \operatorname{Tr}(X) = 0\}$.)
	- Why is $\mathrm{SU}(2)$ the universal covering group of $\mathrm{SO}(3)$? (Hint: $\mathrm{SU}(2) \cong S^3$ is simply connected and has a $2:1$ covering homomorphism onto $\mathrm{SO}(3) \cong \mathbb{RP}^3$.)
	- What is the Poincaré-Hopf theorem? (Hint: The sum of indices of zeros of a smooth vector field on a compact manifold equals its Euler characteristic: $\sum \operatorname{ind}(X) = \chi(M)$.)
	- What is the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula? (Hint: Expresses $\ln(e^X e^Y) = X + Y + \frac{1}{2}[X, Y] + \frac{1}{12}[X, [X, Y]] - \frac{1}{12}[Y, [X, Y]] + \dots$ purely in terms of Lie brackets.)
	- Why is the exponential map not always globally surjective for Lie groups? (Hint: For non-compact or disconnected groups, the exponential image may not cover the entire connected component (e.g., $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})$).)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IB_E/optimisation, optimisation
	- What is the general standard form of a linear program? (Hint: Minimize $c^T x$ subject to $A x \ge b$ and $x \ge 0$.)
	- Why are convex optimization problems computationally tractable? (Hint: Any local minimum of a convex function on a convex set is automatically a global minimum.)
	- When is a twice-differentiable function convex? (Hint: If and only if its Hessian matrix $\nabla^2 f(x)$ is positive semidefinite for all $x$.)
	- What is the role of Morse homology in non-convex optimization? (Hint: Morse theory classifies non-convex landscapes by counting critical points graded by their Morse index (number of negative Hessian eigenvalues).)
	- How do Lagrange multipliers solve constrained optimization problems? (Hint: The gradient of the objective function must be orthogonal to the tangent space of constraints: $\nabla f(x) = \sum \lambda_i \nabla g_i(x)$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IB_M/linear_algebra/full, linear algebra
	- How does one define a vector space? (Hint: A set $V$ equipped with vector addition and scalar multiplication over a field $\mathbb{F}$ satisfying the vector space axioms.)
	- What is the standard basis of $\mathbb{F}^n$? (Hint: The set of unit vectors $\{e_1, \dots, e_n\}$ where $e_i$ has a 1 in the $i$-th entry and 0 elsewhere.)
	- Why does the concept of dimension fail to be unique for general modules over rings? (Hint: Modules over arbitrary rings may lack bases or have invariant basis number failures.)
	- How does one define the span of a subset? (Hint: The set of all finite linear combinations $\operatorname{span}(S) = \{\sum_{i=1}^k c_i v_i \mid c_i \in \mathbb{F}, v_i \in S\}$.)
	- Why are infinite sums not allowed in purely algebraic spans? (Hint: Algebraic vector spaces do not possess topologies to define limits and convergence.)
	- How does one define linear independence? (Hint: A set $\{v_1, \dots, v_k\}$ is linearly independent if $\sum c_i v_i = 0$ implies $c_1 = \dots = c_k = 0$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IB_M/markov_chains/full, Markov chains
	- What is the Markov property? (Hint: The conditional probability of future states depends only on the present state, not on the sequence of past states: $P(X_{n+1} = j \mid X_n = i, \dots, X_0 = i_0) = P(X_{n+1} = j \mid X_n = i)$.)
	- What is a time-homogeneous Markov chain? (Hint: Transition probabilities $P_{ij} = P(X_{n+1} = j \mid X_n = i)$ are independent of time $n$.)
	- What two ingredients uniquely specify a Markov chain? (Hint: The initial probability distribution $\pi_0$ and the transition probability matrix $P$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IB_M/methods/full, methods
	- Why are physical systems frequently linearized? (Hint: Small perturbations around equilibrium obey linear differential equations that can be solved using superposition and spectral decomposition.)
	- Why do function spaces form vector spaces? (Hint: Linear combinations of functions satisfy vector space axioms, enabling Fourier series and orthogonal expansions.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/hydrodynamic_stability/full, hydrodynamic stability
	- What is the goal of hydrodynamic stability theory? (Hint: To determine whether small perturbations to a laminar fluid flow grow exponentially in time, leading to turbulence.)
	- When are inviscid and irrotational assumptions valid? (Hint: In potential flow where $\nabla \times u = 0$, allowing velocity to be expressed as the gradient of a potential $u = \nabla \phi$ satisfying $\nabla^2 \phi = 0$.)
	- How is Bernoulli's theorem applied at a free fluid interface? (Hint: Matches unsteady dynamic pressure across the boundary to balance surface tension and gravity.)
	- What is the Rayleigh-Taylor instability? (Hint: An instability that occurs when a heavy fluid is placed on top of a lighter fluid in a gravitational field.)
	- What is the Boussinesq approximation? (Hint: Density differences are neglected in inertial terms and retained only in the buoyancy gravitational term $g \Delta\rho$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IB_E/variational_principles/full, variational principles, very close to variation principles.
	- What is the Euler-Lagrange equation? (Hint: Necessary condition for a path $y(x)$ to be a stationary point of $S[y] = \int L(x, y, y') \, dx$: $\frac{\partial L}{\partial y} - \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial y'}\right) = 0$.)
	- What is the geometric meaning of stationary points? (Hint: Points where the first variation $\delta S = 0$, meaning directional derivatives vanish in all directions.)
	- What is the Legendre transform? (Hint: Transforms a convex Lagrangian $L(q, \dot{q})$ into a Hamiltonian $H(q, p) = p\dot{q} - L$ where $p = \frac{\partial L}{\partial \dot{q}}$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IA_L/analysis_i/full, analysis I
	- What is the definition of a field? (Hint: A set equipped with addition and multiplication satisfying associativity, commutativity, distributivity, identities, and inverses.)
	- What is a totally ordered field? (Hint: An ordered field where the ordering is total and compatible with field addition and multiplication.)
	- How does one define the supremum and infimum? (Hint: The least upper bound and greatest lower bound of a bounded non-empty set.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/IB_M/analysis_ii/full, analysis II
	- How does one define pointwise convergence of functions? (Hint: $f_n \to f$ pointwise if $\lim_{n \to \infty} f_n(x) = f(x)$ for every $x \in D$.)
	- What is the limitation of pointwise convergence? (Hint: Pointwise limits of continuous functions need not be continuous, e.g., $f_n(x) = x^{2n+1}$ on $[-1, 1]$.)
	- How does one define uniform convergence? (Hint: $f_n \to f$ uniformly if for all $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $N$ such that for all $n > N$ and all $x \in D$, $|f_n(x) - f(x)| < \varepsilon$.)
	- What is a uniformly Cauchy sequence? (Hint: For all $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $N$ such that for all $n, m > N$ and all $x$, $|f_n(x) - f_m(x)| < \varepsilon$; equivalent to uniform convergence in complete spaces.)
	- When does the uniform limit of differentiable functions have a differentiable limit? (Hint: When $f_n(x_0)$ converges at one point and the derivatives $f_n'$ converge uniformly on $[a, b]$.)
	- What is absolute convergence of a series? (Hint: A series $\sum a_n$ converges absolutely if $\sum |a_n| < \infty$.)
	- What is the Weierstrass M-test? (Hint: If $|f_n(x)| \le M_n$ for all $x$ and $\sum M_n < \infty$, then $\sum f_n(x)$ converges uniformly and absolutely.)
	- How does one define uniform continuity? (Hint: A function $f$ is uniformly continuous if for all $\varepsilon > 0$, there exists $\delta > 0$ such that for all $x, y$ with $|x - y| < \delta$, $|f(x) - f(y)| < \varepsilon$.)
	- Why is every continuous function on a compact interval uniformly continuous? (Hint: By the Heine-Cantor theorem / Bolzano-Weierstrass property on compact metric spaces.)
	- What is Lebesgue's criterion for Riemann integrability? (Hint: A bounded function on a compact interval is Riemann integrable if and only if its set of discontinuities has Lebesgue measure zero.)
	- What is a normed vector space? (Hint: A vector space equipped with a function $\|\cdot\|: V \to [0, \infty)$ satisfying positive definiteness, absolute scalability, and the triangle inequality.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/III_M/analysis_of_partial_differential_equations/full, partial differential equations
	- How does one study modern PDEs? (Hint: Prove existence, uniqueness, and regularity of weak solutions using functional analysis on Sobolev spaces.)
	- What does it mean for a PDE to be well-posed in the sense of Hadamard? (Hint: A solution exists, is unique, and depends continuously on initial/boundary data.)
	- What is the transport equation? (Hint: $\partial_t u + b \cdot \nabla u = 0$, describing the advection of a density along characteristics.)
	- What are Laplace's, heat, and wave equations? (Hint: Prototypical elliptic ($\Delta u = 0$), parabolic ($\partial_t u - \Delta u = 0$), and hyperbolic ($\partial_{tt} u - \Delta u = 0$) PDEs.)
	- How are second-order linear PDEs classified? (Hint: By the eigenvalues of the principal coefficient matrix: all same sign (elliptic), one zero (parabolic), one opposite sign (hyperbolic).)
	- What does the Picard-Lindelöf theorem state? (Hint: Guarantees local existence and uniqueness of solutions to ODE initial value problems with Lipschitz continuous vector fields via Banach fixed-point theorem.)
	- What is the Cauchy-Kovalevskaya theorem? (Hint: Guarantees the local existence and uniqueness of real analytic solutions to non-characteristic analytic Cauchy problems.)
	- What is a non-characteristic hypersurface? (Hint: A surface $\Phi(x) = 0$ where the principal symbol of the differential operator does not vanish on the normal vector $\nabla \Phi$.)
	- What is Hölder space $C^{k, \gamma}(\Omega)$? (Hint: The Banach space of $k$-times continuously differentiable functions whose $k$-th partial derivatives are Hölder continuous with exponent $\gamma \in (0, 1]$.)
	- What is $L^p(\Omega)$ space? (Hint: The Banach space of equivalence classes of measurable functions satisfying $\|u\|_{L^p} = \left(\int_\Omega |u|^p \, dx\right)^{1/p} < \infty$.)
	- What is a weak (distributional) derivative? (Hint: A locally integrable function $v = D^\alpha u$ satisfying $\int_\Omega u D^\alpha \phi \, dx = (-1)^{|\alpha|} \int_\Omega v \phi \, dx$ for all test functions $\phi \in C_c^\infty(\Omega)$.)
	- What is a Sobolev space $W^{k, p}(\Omega)$? (Hint: The Banach space of functions in $L^p(\Omega)$ whose weak derivatives up to order $k$ exist and belong to $L^p(\Omega)$; denoted $H^k(\Omega)$ for $p=2$.)
	- What are standard mollifiers? (Hint: Smooth, compactly supported bump functions $\eta_\varepsilon(x) = \varepsilon^{-n} \eta(x/\varepsilon)$ used to approximate $L^p$ and Sobolev functions by smooth functions via convolution $u_\varepsilon = u * \eta_\varepsilon$.)
	- What are Sobolev inequalities? (Hint: Embedding theorems (Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev, Morrey) that trade weak differentiability for higher integrability or Hölder continuity.)
	- What is uniform ellipticity? (Hint: There exists $\theta > 0$ such that $\sum_{i, j} a_{ij}(x) \xi_i \xi_j \ge \theta |\xi|^2$ for all $\xi \in \mathbb{R}^n$.)
	- How does one formulate weak solutions for elliptic boundary value problems? (Hint: Multiply by test functions, integrate by parts to reduce derivative orders, and apply the Lax-Milgram theorem in $H_0^1(\Omega)$.)

https://dec41.user.srcf.net/h/II_M/galois_theory/full, Galois theory
	- What problem was Galois theory originally invented to solve? (Hint: Determining whether polynomial equations are solvable by radicals via the algebraic structure of their Galois groups.)

https://www.preschema.com/papers/stacksncts.pdf, Adeel Khan's stacks, in 76 pages
    - (Page 2) Imagine the moduli space of 1-dimensional linear subspaces in $\mathbb{C}^{n+1}$. What is this object? (Hint: Think about lines through the origin in projective space $\mathbb{P}^n$.)
    - (Page 2) Imagine the moduli space of $k$-dimensional linear subspaces of a vector space and show that it is equivalently the $(k-1)$-dimensional linear subspaces of the projectivization of the vector space. What is this object? (Hint: The Grassmannian $\operatorname{Gr}(k, V) \cong \operatorname{Gr}(k-1, \mathbb{P}(V))$.)
    - (Page 2) Imagine a family or fibration of sets parametrized by commutative algebras. What is this object? What happens if you imagine a fibration of groupoids parameterized by a suitable commutative algebra instead? (Hint: A functor of points/presheaf of sets vs. a fibration in groupoids/stack.)
    - (Page 2) Compute the moduli space of vector bundles on an algebraic variety $X$, and compute the moduli space of principal $G$-bundles on $X$.
    - (Page 2) Compute the moduli space of coherent sheaves on an algebraic variety by removing an equivalence relation.
    - (Page 3) Consider the example in Theorem 0.0.6. How does one specify complex projective $n$-space as a scheme? (Hint: Compute its functor of points: these are $A$-linear surjections $A^{n+1} \twoheadrightarrow L$, where $L$ is a locally free $A$-module of rank 1.)
    - (Page 3) Compute the diagram of a groupoid as a category. What are the objects of a groupoid for an equivalence relation $R \subseteq X \times X$? (Hint: Objects are elements of $X$, and morphisms are elements of $R$.)
    - (Page 4) Compute for the case of $\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{Bun}_G}(C)$ taking a commutative algebra $A$ to the groupoid of principal $G$-bundles on the scheme $C \otimes_k A$ under base change. What do you get for $G = \mathrm{GL}_n$? (Hint: One gets the moduli stack $\operatorname{Bun}_n(C)$ of rank $n$ vector bundles on $C$.)
    - (Page 5) What class of functors does homological algebra allow us to derive, and what do $\infty$-categories let us derive? (Hint: Homological algebra derives functors between abelian categories, whereas $\infty$-categories allow deriving non-abelian functors such as the tangent bundle and non-linear moduli.)
    - (Page 6) Compute the classical moduli stack $\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cl}}$ of vector bundles (respectively, coherent sheaves and principal $G$-bundles). There is a closed immersion $\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{cl}} \hookrightarrow \mathcal{M}$ which is an isomorphism if and only if $X$ is a curve (if and only if $\mathcal{M}$ is smooth). What happens when $\dim X \ge 2$? (Hint: It retains hidden/homotopical smoothness when formulated as a derived stack.)
    - (Page 7) Describe the simplex category $\Delta$ whose objects are finite non-empty ordinals $[n] = \{0, 1, \dots, n\}$ with order-preserving maps. Construct a simplicial set $X: \Delta^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{Set}$ with $X_n := X([n])$, and describe its face and degeneracy maps.
    - (Page 7) Construct the injective coface maps $d^i: [n-1] \to [n]$ skipping vertex $i$ and surjective codegeneracy maps $s^i: [n+1] \to [n]$ doubling vertex $i$, then compute the induced face maps $d_i$ and degeneracy maps $s_i$ satisfying the simplicial identities.
    - (Page 8) Construct the standard $n$-simplex $\Delta^n$ using hom-sets out of $[k]$. What is this object? (Hint: $\Delta_k^n = \operatorname{Hom}_\Delta([k], [n])$ via the Yoneda embedding.)
    - (Page 8) Compute the union of faces of $\Delta^n$ excluding the $k$-th face to define the $k$-th horn $\Lambda_k^n$. (Hint: $\Lambda_k^n = \bigcup_{j \ne k} \partial_j \Delta^n \subset \partial \Delta^n$.)
    - (Page 8) Construct the boundary $\partial \Delta^n$ of the standard $n$-simplex as the union of all its faces.
    - (Page 8) Construct the morphism $\sigma: \Delta^n \to X$ corresponding to an $n$-simplex. What lemma is required for this identification? (Hint: The Yoneda lemma.)
    - (Page 8) Construct the functor category $\operatorname{Fun}([n], \mathcal{C})$ for a small category $\mathcal{C}$, and define the nerve $N(\mathcal{C})$ as a simplicial set whose $n$-simplices are strings of $n$ composable morphisms.
    - (Page 8, Exercise 1.2.3.) Take the left Kan extension along the inclusion $\Delta \hookrightarrow \mathbf{Cat}$ to construct the left adjoint of the nerve functor $N: \mathbf{Cat} \to \mathbf{sSet}$. Why is the nerve fully faithful? (Hint: 0-simplices are objects, 1-simplices are morphisms, and 2-simplices uniquely determine composition.)
    - (Page 9, Remark 1.2.6.) Construct the composition of two morphisms as a horn filling $\Lambda_1^2 \to \Delta^2$ or as a pushout of simplicial sets $\Delta^1 \cup_{\Delta^0} \Delta^1 \cong \Lambda_1^2$.
    - (Page 10, Proposition 1.2.8.) Prove that a simplicial set $X$ is isomorphic to the nerve of a 1-category if and only if the inner horn restriction maps $X_n = \operatorname{Hom}(\Delta^n, X) \to \operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda_k^n, X)$ are bijections for all $0 < k < n$ and $n \ge 2$ (Grothendieck-Segal theorem).
    - (Page 10, Definition 1.3.2.) What condition defines a Kan complex? (Hint: The restriction maps $\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta^n, X) \to \operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda_k^n, X)$ are surjective for all $0 \le k \le n$ and $n \ge 1$, ensuring all horns have fillers.)
    - (Page 10, Definition 1.3.2.) Why is the nerve of an $\infty$-groupoid (or 1-groupoid) a Kan complex? (Hint: Invertibility of all morphisms guarantees that outer horns also admit fillers.)
    - (Page 10) Describe Milnor's theorem relating the homotopy categories of CW complexes and Kan complexes via the singular simplicial set functor $\operatorname{Sing}(X)$ and geometric realization $|K|$.
    - (Page 10) Compute the $n$-simplices of the singular simplicial set $\operatorname{Sing}(X)$ using continuous maps $|\Delta^n| \to X$.
    - (Page 11) Why are Kan complexes regarded as algebraic models of spaces? (Hint: 0-simplices correspond to points, 1-simplices to paths, 2-simplices to homotopies between paths, and composition is unique up to coherent homotopy.)
    - (Page 11, Definition 1.4.1) Define a quasi-category (weak Kan complex / $(\infty, 1)$-category) via inner horn filling. (Hint: The restriction maps $\operatorname{Hom}(\Delta^n, X) \to \operatorname{Hom}(\Lambda_k^n, X)$ are surjective for all inner horns $0 < k < n$.)
    - (Page 11, Construction 1.4.2) Construct a diagram describing homotopies between morphisms as 2-simplices, and define composition up to coherent homotopy in an $\infty$-category.
    - (Page 12) Describe the Duskin nerve of a 2-category as a simplicial set whose 0-simplices are objects, 1-simplices are 1-morphisms, and 2-simplices are 2-morphisms with coherence data.
    - (Page 12) Compute the compatibility relations satisfied by 3-simplices in the Duskin nerve.
    - (Page 13) Describe how a 1-groupoid is viewed as a $(2, 1)$-category by discarding non-invertible 2-morphisms.
    - (Page 13) Describe the large simplicial set $\mathbf{Kan}$ whose vertices are Kan complexes and $n$-simplices are coherent diagrams of maps.
    - (Page 14) What conditions define a Grothendieck topology on a category $\mathcal{C}$? (Hint: Existence of fiber products, stable covers under base change, and stability under composition.)
    - (Page 14) Construct the prototypical site: the category of open subsets $\operatorname{Open}(X)$ of a topological space with covering families given by open covers.
    - (Page 14/15) Construct the étale site of a scheme with coverings given by étale surjections (or faithfully flat morphisms).
    - (Page 17) Construct the Čech nerve $\check{C}(U \to X)$ of a covering morphism. What is its $n$-th term, and how does it define an $\infty$-sheaf? (Hint: $U_n = U \times_X \dots \times_X U$, and the sheaf condition is given by a limit/descent condition.)
    - (Page 18) Compute the limit of a cosimplicial diagram in a 1-category as an equalizer. (Hint: $\lim X^\bullet \cong \operatorname{eq}(X^0 \rightrightarrows X^1)$.)
    - (Page 18) Define a stack as a sheaf of groupoids satisfying descent with respect to covering sieves.
    - (Page 19) Prove the equivalence of categories between $\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}, \mathbf{Cat})$ and Cartesian fibrations over $\mathcal{C}$ via the Grothendieck construction.
    - (Page 19) Compare the étale topology (flat, unramified, locally of finite presentation) and the fpqc topology (faithfully flat, quasi-compact). Which topology is finer?
    - (Page 20) Describe augmented simplicial diagrams $X_\bullet \to X_{-1}$ and their totalizations/limits as descent conditions.
    - (Page 20) Prove that the module functor $R \mapsto \operatorname{Mod}_R$ satisfies faithfully flat descent on the category of commutative rings.
    - (Page 22, Lemma 3.2.9) Prove that the pullback functor $f^*$ on quasi-coherent sheaves is conservative when $f: X \to Y$ is faithfully flat and quasi-compact.
    - (Page 23) Compute the right Kan extension of $\mathbf{Aff}^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{Cat}$ along $\mathbf{Aff} \hookrightarrow \mathbf{Sch}$ to construct quasi-coherent sheaves on arbitrary schemes and stacks.

https://www.math.uni-bonn.de/people/ja/alggeoI/notes.pdf, Scholze's algebraic geometry notes
    - (Page 4) Define an affine algebraic set $V(S) \subseteq \mathbb{A}^n(k)$ as the zero locus of a subset of polynomials $S \subseteq k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$ over an algebraically closed field $k$.
    - (Page 4) Use Hilbert's Basis Theorem to prove that every ideal $\mathfrak{a} \subseteq k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$ is finitely generated, and hence $V(S) = V(f_1, \dots, f_m)$ for a finite set of polynomials.
    - (Page 4) Prove that every ascending chain of ideals in $k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$ stabilizes since $k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$ is Noetherian.
    - (Page 4, Example 1.3) Show that every algebraic subset of $\mathbb{A}^1(k)$ is either a finite set of points or the entire affine line.
    - (Page 4) Compute the zero set $V(f)$ for $f \in k[X]$ using its factorization into linear factors in a PID.
    - (Page 4) Compute the geometric zero loci in $\mathbb{A}^2(\mathbb{C})$ for $x + y = 0$, $x^2 + y^2 = 1$, and $x^2 + y^2 = -1$.
    - (Page 5) Show that a non-singular cubic curve (elliptic curve) forms an affine algebraic set in $\mathbb{A}^2(k)$.
    - (Page 5) Describe the zero locus of $x^2 = y^2$ in $\mathbb{A}^2(k)$ under the Zariski topology as the union of two lines.
    - (Page 5) Verify the axioms of the Zariski topology: $V(\emptyset) = \mathbb{A}^n$, $V(1) = \emptyset$, $\bigcap V(S_i) = V(\bigcup S_i)$, and $V(I) \cup V(J) = V(I J)$.
    - (Page 5) Prove that $V(I) \cup V(J) = V(I \cap J) = V(I J)$ for any two ideals $I, J \subseteq k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$.
    - (Page 5) Define the radical of an ideal $\sqrt{I} = \{f \in R \mid f^m \in I \text{ for some } m \ge 1\}$ and show $V(I) = V(\sqrt{I})$.
    - (Page 6) State Hilbert's Nullstellensatz: for an algebraically closed field $k$ and ideal $I \subseteq k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$, $I(V(I)) = \sqrt{I}$.
    - (Page 6) State the Weak Nullstellensatz: if $I \subsetneq k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$ is a proper ideal, then $V(I) \ne \emptyset$.
    - (Page 6) Prove using Noether normalization that every maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m} \subset k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$ is of the form $(X_1 - a_1, \dots, X_n - a_n)$ for $a_i \in k$.
    - (Page 7) Prove the bijection between radical ideals in $k[X_1, \dots, X_n]$ and algebraic sets in $\mathbb{A}^n(k)$.
    - (Page 7) Use the Rabinowitsch trick to deduce the Strong Nullstellensatz from the Weak Nullstellensatz by adjoining an auxiliary variable $Y$ and considering $(I, 1 - Y f)$.
    - (Page 8) Show that an affine coordinate ring $A(V) = k[X_1, \dots, X_n] / I(V)$ is reduced ($\operatorname{nil}(A(V)) = 0$).
    - (Page 10) Define the coordinate ring $\mathcal{O}(V)$ as the ring of polynomial functions on an algebraic set $V \subseteq \mathbb{A}^n(k)$.
    - (Page 10) Identify $V(I)$ with $\operatorname{Hom}_{k\text{-alg}}(k[X_1, \dots, X_n]/I, k)$.
    - (Page 10) Define the prime spectrum $\operatorname{Spec}(A)$ of a commutative ring $A$ as the set of all prime ideals of $A$.
    - (Page 10) Prove that the kernel of any ring homomorphism $f: A \to K$ into a field $K$ is a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Spec}(A)$.
    - (Page 11) Define the residue field $\kappa(\mathfrak{p}) = \operatorname{Frac}(A/\mathfrak{p})$ at a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Spec}(A)$.
    - (Page 11) Compute $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})$, identifying its closed points $(p)$ and generic point $(0)$.
    - (Page 11) Define the maximal spectrum $\operatorname{Spec}_{\max}(A)$ as the set of maximal ideals of $A$.
    - (Page 12) Compute the continuous map $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Q}) \to \operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})$ induced by the inclusion $\mathbb{Z} \hookrightarrow \mathbb{Q}$.
    - (Page 12) Prove that for any ring homomorphism $f: A \to B$, the preimage $f^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})$ of a prime ideal $\mathfrak{q} \subseteq B$ is a prime ideal in $A$.
    - (Page 18) Define a presheaf and a sheaf of rings on a topological space $X$, stating the locality and gluing axioms.
    - (Page 18) Verify that the assignment $U \mapsto C^0(U, \mathbb{R})$ (and $U \mapsto C^\infty(U, \mathbb{R})$) defines a sheaf of rings.
    - (Page 18) Express the sheaf condition for $\mathcal{F}(U)$ as the equalizer of $\prod \mathcal{F}(U_i) \rightrightarrows \prod \mathcal{F}(U_i \cap U_j)$.
    - (Page 19) Show that for any sheaf $\mathcal{F}$, $\mathcal{F}(\emptyset) = 0$ is the terminal object.
    - (Page 26) Define the localization $A_\mathfrak{p} = S^{-1} A$ at a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$ where $S = A \setminus \mathfrak{p}$, and show that $A_\mathfrak{p}$ is a local ring with maximal ideal $\mathfrak{p} A_\mathfrak{p}$.
    - (Page 27) Define a locally ringed space $(X, \mathcal{O}_X)$ as a ringed space whose stalks $\mathcal{O}_{X, x}$ are local rings.
    - (Page 27) Define the structure sheaf $\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Spec}(A)}$ on the spectrum of a ring $A$, with stalks $\mathcal{O}_{\operatorname{Spec}(A), \mathfrak{p}} \cong A_\mathfrak{p}$.
    - (Page 42) Define quasi-coherent $\mathcal{O}_X$-modules as sheaves that locally admit presentations $\mathcal{O}_X^{\oplus I} \to \mathcal{O}_X^{\oplus J} \to \mathcal{F} \to 0$, or $\widetilde{M}$ on affine schemes.
    - (Page 55) Use Noether normalization to define the Krull dimension of an affine variety as the transcendence degree of its function field.
    - (Page 62) Prove that the Segre embedding $\mathbb{P}^n \times \mathbb{P}^m \hookrightarrow \mathbb{P}^{(n+1)(m+1)-1}$ is a closed immersion, showing products of projective schemes are projective.
    - (Page 62) Prove that closed immersions of schemes are stable under composition and base change.
    - (Page 63) Construct the affine line with a doubled origin by gluing two copies of $\mathbb{A}^1$ along $\mathbb{A}^1 \setminus \{0\}$, demonstrating a non-separated scheme.
    - (Page 73) Define a valuation ring $A \subset K$ of a field $K$ by the property that for every $x \in K^\times$, either $x \in A$ or $x^{-1} \in A$.
    - (Page 80) Define the normalization of an integral scheme $X$ as the scheme $\widetilde{X}$ whose affine charts are the integral closures of $\mathcal{O}_X(U)$ in $\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{O}_X(U))$.
    - (Page 103) Define the divisor group $\operatorname{Div}(C)$ of a smooth algebraic curve $C$ as the free abelian group on closed points $D = \sum n_x [x]$.
    - (Page 103) Define effective divisors ($n_x \ge 0$) and the associated invertible sheaf $\mathcal{O}_C(D)(U) = \{f \in K(C)^\times \mid \operatorname{div}(f)|_U + D|_U \ge 0\} \cup \{0\}$.
    - (Page 104) Prove the natural isomorphism $\mathcal{O}_C(D) \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_C} \mathcal{O}_C(D') \cong \mathcal{O}_C(D + D')$.
    - (Page 107) Prove that the degree of any principal divisor $\operatorname{div}(f)$ on a proper smooth curve is zero: $\deg(\operatorname{div}(f)) = 0$.
    - (Page 108) State the Riemann-Roch theorem for a smooth projective curve $C$: $\dim H^0(C, \mathcal{L}) - \dim H^1(C, \mathcal{L}) = \deg(\mathcal{L}) + 1 - g$.
    - (Page 108) Prove that the degree of the canonical divisor $K_C$ is $2g - 2$ by setting $\mathcal{L} = \omega_C$ in the Riemann-Roch formula.
    - (Page 109) Use the Riemann-Roch theorem to prove that any smooth projective curve of genus 1 with a rational point is isomorphic to a plane cubic in Weierstrass form $y^2 = x^3 + a x + b$.
    - (Page 109) Prove the group law on an elliptic curve $E$: three points $P, Q, R$ sum to zero if and only if they are collinear in $\mathbb{P}^2$.

	- (Page 4) Define the Fourier coefficients $c_n(f)$ for a periodic function $f$ on $\mathbb{T} = \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z} \cong [0,1)$. What is the integral kernel of the Fourier transform in this compact setting, and why is the integration domain restricted to $[0, 1]$? Explain how this representation is used to solve the 1D wave equation (vibrating string) and the heat equation.
	- (Page 4) Write down the continuous Fourier transform $\widehat{f}(\xi)$ and the inversion formula for a non-periodic function $f \in L^1(\mathbb{R}^d)$. How does the integration domain differ from the periodic case?
	- (Page 5) For a function $f$ on a group $G$ and $x \in G$, define the translation operator $\tau_x f(y)$.
	- (Page 5) Let $T: E \to E$ be a linear operator on a translation-invariant function space $E$. What does it mean for $T$ to be translation-invariant? Express $\tau_x(Tf)$ in terms of $T$ and $\tau_x f$.
	- (Page 6) Let $P(D) = \sum_{|\alpha| \le m} a_\alpha D^\alpha$ be a linear differential operator. Show that $P(D)$ is translation-invariant (time-invariant) if and only if its coefficients $a_\alpha$ are constant. Compute the symbol/characteristic polynomial $P(\xi)$ associated with $P(D)$.
	- (Page 6) Let $T$ be a translation-invariant operator and let $\chi$ be a character of $G$. Show that $\tau_x(T\chi)(y) = \chi(-x)(T\chi)(y)$.
	- (Page 6) Using the group law $\tau_x \tau_y = \tau_{x+y}$, prove that every character $\chi: G \to \mathbb{C}^\times$ is a group homomorphism satisfying $\chi(x+y) = \chi(x)\chi(y)$ and $\chi(-x) = \chi(x)^{-1}$.
	- (Page 6) Let $f = T\chi$ for a translation-invariant operator $T$ and a character $\chi$. By evaluating at $y = 0$, prove that $f(x) = \lambda \chi(x)$ where $\lambda = (T\chi)(0)$, and conclude that $\chi$ is an eigenfunction of $T$.
	- (Page 6) Identify the codomain and the target set of all complex characters $\chi \in \operatorname{Hom}(G, \mathbb{C}^\times)$ on a locally compact abelian group $G$.

	- (Page 7) State the conditions under which the characters of a locally compact abelian group $G$ serve as simultaneous eigenfunctions for all bounded translation-invariant linear operators $T$.
	- (Page 7) Simplify $(T\chi)(y - x)$ using the translation invariance of $T$. By evaluating at $y = 0$, deduce the eigenvalue equation $(T\chi)(x) = m(\chi)\chi(x)$ with eigenvalue $m(\chi) = (T\chi)(0)$, and explain why characters diagonalize translation-invariant operators.
	- (Page 8) Let $\chi: \mathbb{R} \to \mathbb{C}^\times$ be a continuous character. Using the identity $\int_x^{x+h} \chi(z)\,dz = \chi(x) \int_0^h \chi(y)\,dy$, prove that $\chi$ is smooth and satisfies the differential equation $\chi'(x) = \chi'(0)\chi(x)$. Solve this ODE to find the general functional form of $\chi(x)$.
	- (Page 8) Find the general form of an unbounded continuous character $\chi$ on $\mathbb{R}^d$ in terms of a vector $\alpha \in \mathbb{C}^d$.

	- (Page 9) For the cyclic group $G = \mathbb{Z}_N = \mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}$, define the primitive root of unity $\omega_N = e^{2\pi i / N}$ and compute the complete set of characters $\chi_k(n) = \omega_N^{kn}$.

	- (Page 10) For $f \in L^1(G)$ and a unitary character $\chi \in \widehat{G}$, define the Fourier transform $\widehat{f}(\chi) = \langle f, \chi \rangle$ as an integral pairing with respect to the Haar measure $\mu$ on $G$.
