	- (Page 36) Solve the Dirichlet problem for Laplace's equation $\Delta u = 0$ on the unit disk $\mathbb{D} = \{z \in \mathbb{C} : |z| < 1\}$ with boundary data $f(e^{2\pi i \theta}) = e^{2\pi i n \theta}$.
	- (Page 37) Derive the Poisson kernel on the unit disk $P_r(\theta) = \sum_{n=-\infty}^\infty r^{|n|} e^{2\pi i n \theta} = \frac{1 - r^2}{1 - 2r\cos(2\pi\theta) + r^2}$ for $0 \le r < 1$, and express the harmonic extension of $f \in L^1(\mathbb{T})$ as $u(r e^{2\pi i \theta}) = (f * P_r)(\theta)$.
	- (Page 41) Characterize bounded translation-invariant linear operators $T$ on $L^p(\mathbb{T}^d)$ for the endpoint cases $p = 1$ (convolution with measures $\mu \in M(\mathbb{T}^d)$) and $p = 2$ (multipliers with bounded sequences $(m_n) \in \ell^\infty(\mathbb{Z}^d)$).
	- (Page 42) Compute the multi-dimensional Fourier series for $f \in L^1(\mathbb{T}^d)$ as $f(x) \sim \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}^d} c_n(f) e^{2\pi i n \cdot x}$ with $c_n(f) = \int_{\mathbb{T}^d} f(t) e^{-2\pi i n \cdot t} \, dt$.
	- (Page 46) For $f \in L^p(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and $\lambda > 0$, define the dilation operator $D_\lambda f(x) = f(\lambda x)$. Compute the adjoint operator $D_\lambda^*$ with respect to the $L^2$-inner product, and compute the Fourier transform $\widehat{D_\lambda f}(\xi)$.
	- (Page 47) Let $P(D) = \sum_{|\alpha| \le m} a_\alpha D^\alpha$ with $D = \frac{1}{2\pi i} \nabla$. Express the action of $P(D)$ on a test function in terms of its polynomial symbol $P(\xi) = \sum a_\alpha \xi^\alpha$.
	- (Page 47) Show that the Fourier multiplier of $P(D)$ is $m(\xi) = P(\xi)$, so that $\widehat{P(D)f}(\xi) = P(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)$.
	- (Page 48) Let $T$ be a linear operator commuting with rigid motions (translations and rotations $R \in \mathrm{SO}(d)$). Show that $T(f \circ R) = (Tf) \circ R$, and deduce that its Fourier multiplier is radial: $m(\xi) = m_0(|\xi|)$.
	- (Page 48) Compute the Fourier multiplier of the Laplacian $\Delta = \sum_{j=1}^d \frac{\partial^2}{\partial x_j^2}$, and prove that any rotation-invariant polynomial differential operator must be a polynomial in $\Delta$.
	- (Page 49) Describe the geometric shape and decay behavior of the multi-dimensional Dirichlet kernel on $\mathbb{R}^d$.
	- (Page 49) Express $f(x)$ as an oscillatory integral pairing against the Dirichlet kernel cut-offs.
	- (Page 49) Define the cubic partial sum operator $(S_R^c f)(x) = \int_{[-R, R]^d} \widehat{f}(\xi) e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi} \, d\xi$ as a convolution $f * D_R^c$, and analyze its integrability.
	- (Page 49) Define the spherical partial sum operator $(S_R^s f)(x) = \int_{B(0, R)} \widehat{f}(\xi) e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi} \, d\xi = (f * D_R^s)(x)$, where $D_R^s(x)$ is the spherical Dirichlet kernel.
	- (Page 50) Formulate the smoothed Dirichlet summability means $S_\Phi^R f(x) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \Phi(\xi/R) \widehat{f}(\xi) e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi} \, d\xi = (f * \Phi_R)(x)$, and state conditions on $\Phi$ under which $\Phi_R$ is an approximate identity.
	- (Page 51) Define the Poisson means $f_P(\varepsilon, x) = (f * P_\varepsilon)(x)$ for $\Phi(\xi) = e^{-2\pi |\xi|}$.
	- (Page 51) Compute the inverse Fourier transform of $\Phi(\varepsilon \xi)$ and $e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}\Phi(\varepsilon \xi)$, and show that $f_\Phi(\varepsilon, x) = (f * \phi_\varepsilon)(x)$ where $\phi = \mathcal{F}^{-1}\Phi$.
	- (Page 51) State the necessary conditions on $\Phi \in C_0(\mathbb{R}^d)$ with $\Phi(0) = 1$ such that $\phi_\varepsilon(x) = \varepsilon^{-d}\phi(x/\varepsilon)$ forms an approximate identity.
	- (Page 52) For the Gaussian profile $\Phi(\xi) = e^{-\pi |\xi|^2}$, compute its Fourier transform $\phi(x) = \mathcal{F}^{-1}\Phi(x) = e^{-\pi |x|^2}$. Prove the Unicity Theorem and the Inversion Theorem for the Fourier transform on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, and define the Fourier cotransform $\mathcal{F}^* f(x) = \widehat{f}(-x)$.
	- (Page 52) Write the solution to the heat equation $\partial_t u = \Delta u$ on $\mathbb{R}^d \times (0, \infty)$ with initial data $u(x, 0) = f(x)$ as a convolution $u(x, t) = (f * W_t)(x)$ using the Weierstrass/heat kernel $W_t(x) = (4\pi t)^{-d/2} e^{-|x|^2/(4t)}$.
	- (Page 53) For a continuous summability kernel $\Phi$, prove why the normalization condition $\Phi(0) = 1$ is necessary for $\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} \phi(x) \, dx = 1$.
	- (Page 53) For $\Phi(\xi) = e^{-2\pi |\xi|}$, compute the Abel means and the explicit inverse Fourier transform to obtain the Poisson kernel for the upper half-space $\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_+$.
	- (Page 54) Define the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing functions $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d) = \{f \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d) : \sup_{x} |x^\beta D^\alpha f(x)| < \infty\}$. Prove that the Fourier transform $\mathcal{F}: \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d) \to \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ is an isomorphism, and show that $\mathcal{F}(f * g) = \widehat{f} \cdot \widehat{g}$.
	- (Page 54) Prove Plancherel's Theorem: the Fourier transform restricted to $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ is an $L^2$-isometry ($\|\widehat{f}\|_{L^2} = \|f\|_{L^2}$), and extends uniquely to a unitary isomorphism on $L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 55) Explain why continuous characters $e_\xi(x) = e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi}$ do not belong to $L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$, and explain how the continuous spectral resolution of the identity replaces discrete orthonormal bases via the formal relation $\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} e^{2\pi i x \cdot (\xi - \eta)} \, dx = \delta(\xi - \eta)$.
	- (Page 55) Compute the kernel $K(x, \xi) = e^{-2\pi i x \cdot \xi}$ and its modulus. Verify the distributional identity $\int_{\mathbb{R}^d} e^{2\pi i x \cdot \xi} \, d\xi = \delta_0(x)$.
	- (Page 56) Let $T: L^2(\mathbb{R}^d) \to L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ be a bounded translation-invariant operator. Prove that $T$ diagonalizes under the Fourier transform: $\widehat{Tf}(\xi) = m(\xi)\widehat{f}(\xi)$ for some $m \in L^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d)$ with $\|T\|_{\text{op}} = \|m\|_{L^\infty}$.
	- (Page 56) Compare the general representation of translation-invariant operators on $L^1(\mathbb{R}^d)$ ($Tf = f * \mu$ for $\mu \in M(\mathbb{R}^d)$) with those on $L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ ($Tf = \mathcal{F}^{-1}(m \cdot \widehat{f})$ for $m \in L^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d)$).
	- (Page 57) Let $P: L^2(\mathbb{R}^d) \to L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ be an orthogonal projection operator commuting with translations. Prove that $P^2 = P = P^*$ and characterize the subspace to which $f - Pf$ is orthogonal.
	- (Page 57) Show that the Fourier multiplier of an orthogonal projection $P$ on $L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ satisfying $P^2 = P$ must be a characteristic function $m(\xi) = \mathbf{1}_E(\xi)$ for some measurable set $E \subseteq \mathbb{R}^d$.
	- (Page 57) State Beurling's theorem on translation-invariant subspaces of $L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$: show that the closed translation-invariant subspace generated by $f \in L^2(\mathbb{R}^d)$ is $\{g \in L^2(\mathbb{R}^d) : \widehat{g}(\xi) = 0 \text{ whenever } \widehat{f}(\xi) = 0\}$.
	- (Page 59) Let $f, g \in L^1_{\text{loc}}(U)$. Prove that if $\int_U f(x)\phi(x)\,dx = \int_U g(x)\phi(x)\,dx$ for all $\phi \in \mathcal{D}(U) = C_c^\infty(U)$, then $f = g$ a.e., establishing the canonical injection $L^1_{\text{loc}}(U) \hookrightarrow \mathcal{D}'(U)$.
	- (Page 59) Define the space of distributions $\mathcal{D}'(U)$ as the topological dual of $\mathcal{D}(U) = C_c^\infty(U)$ equipped with the inductive limit topology.
	- (Page 59) State the continuity criterion for a linear functional $u: \mathcal{D}(U) \to \mathbb{C}$ to be a distribution: for every compact $K \subset U$, there exist $C > 0$ and $N \in \mathbb{N}_0$ such that $|\langle u, \phi \rangle| \le C \sum_{|\alpha| \le N} \sup_K |D^\alpha \phi|$.
	- (Page 60) Define a discrete Dirac comb / measure distribution $u = \sum_{j} c_j \delta_{a_j}$ for a locally finite set of points $\{a_j\} \subset \mathbb{R}^d$.
	- (Page 61) Define the Cauchy principal value distribution $\operatorname{p.v.}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R})$ by $\langle \operatorname{p.v.}(1/x), \phi \rangle = \lim_{\varepsilon \to 0^+} \int_{|x| > \varepsilon} \frac{\phi(x)}{x} \, dx$. Prove that this limit exists for all $\phi \in \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})$, and explain why it is not a locally integrable function or a Radon measure.
	- (Page 62) For a distribution $u \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and $x \in \mathbb{R}^d$, define the translated distribution $\tau_x u$ via the adjoint pairing $\langle \tau_x u, \phi \rangle = \langle u, \tau_{-x} \phi \rangle$.
	- (Page 62) Define the distributional derivative $D^\alpha u$ for $u \in \mathcal{D}'(U)$ by $\langle D^\alpha u, \phi \rangle = (-1)^{|\alpha|} \langle u, D^\alpha \phi \rangle$ for all $\phi \in \mathcal{D}(U)$.
	- (Page 62) Compute the distributional derivative of the Heaviside step function $H(x) = \mathbf{1}_{[0, \infty)}(x)$ on $\mathbb{R}$: show that $\langle H', \phi \rangle = -\int_0^\infty \phi'(x) \, dx = \phi(0) = \langle \delta_0, \phi \rangle$, hence $H' = \delta_0$.
	- (Page 62) Let $f$ be a piecewise $C^1$ function with a jump discontinuity of height $\Delta f(x_0)$ at $x_0$. Compute the distributional derivative $u_f'$ in terms of the classical derivative $f'$ and a Dirac delta mass.
	- (Page 63) Prove in the sense of distributions on $\mathbb{R}$ that $\frac{d}{dx} \ln|x| = \operatorname{p.v.}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)$.
	- (Page 64) Define weak-* convergence $u_n \to u$ in $\mathcal{D}'(U)$ ($\langle u_n, \phi \rangle \to \langle u, \phi \rangle$ for all $\phi \in \mathcal{D}(U)$), and prove that distributional differentiation is continuous under weak-* limits: $u_n \to u \implies D^\alpha u_n \to D^\alpha u$.
	- (Page 64) Evaluate the distributional convergence of the partial sums of the formal trigonometric series $\sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} e^{2\pi i n x}$ in $\mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{T})$.
	- (Page 65) Define the space of compactly supported distributions $\mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ as the dual of $C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d)$. State the continuity condition for $u \in \mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 66) Compute the distributional derivative of the Fourier series representation of the sawtooth function $f(x) = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{i}{2\pi} \sum_{n \ne 0} \frac{e^{2\pi i n x/a}}{n}$ on $\mathbb{R}$.
	- (Page 67) Define the Fourier transform $\widehat{u}$ of a tempered distribution $u \in \mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ via duality: $\langle \widehat{u}, \phi \rangle = \langle u, \widehat{\phi} \rangle$ for all $\phi \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$. Why must $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ be used instead of $\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^d)$?
	- (Page 67) State the seminorm convergence condition $\sup_{x \in \mathbb{R}^d} |x^\beta D^\alpha \phi_n(x)| \to 0$ defining the topology on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, and state the continuity criterion for $u \in \mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 68) Characterize tempered distributions $u \in \mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ as distributions of finite order with polynomial growth: $u = D^\alpha f$ where $f$ is a continuous function satisfying $|f(x)| \le C(1 + |x|)^N$.
	- (Page 70) Compute the Fourier transform of the Dirac delta distribution $\delta_0 \in \mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 70) Compute the Fourier transform of a finite sum of shifted Dirac deltas $\sum_{j=1}^n c_j \delta_{a_j}$, and explicitly compute $\widehat{\delta_1}$ on $\mathbb{R}$.
	- (Page 70) Prove that $\mathcal{F}\left(\operatorname{p.v.}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)\right)(\xi) = -i\pi \operatorname{sgn}(\xi)$ in $\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R})$.
	- (Page 71) State the Paley–Wiener Theorem for smooth functions with compact support: show that $\widehat{f}(\zeta) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^d} f(x) e^{-2\pi i x \cdot \zeta} \, dx$ extends to an entire holomorphic function on $\mathbb{C}^d$ satisfying exponential type bounds $|\widehat{f}(\zeta)| \le C_N (1 + |\zeta|)^{-N} e^{2\pi R |\operatorname{Im} \zeta|}$.
	- (Page 72) State the Paley–Wiener–Schwartz Theorem: prove that $u \in \mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ if and only if $\widehat{u}(\zeta) = \langle u, e^{-2\pi i \langle \cdot, \zeta \rangle} \rangle$ is an entire function on $\mathbb{C}^d$ of exponential growth $|\widehat{u}(\zeta)| \le C (1 + |\zeta|)^N e^{2\pi R |\operatorname{Im} \zeta|}$.
	- (Page 73) For $u \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and $\phi \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d)$, define the convolution $(u * \phi)(x) = \langle u, \tau_x \widetilde{\phi} \rangle$ with $\widetilde{\phi}(y) = \phi(-y)$, and prove that $u * \phi \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 73) Prove the differentiation formula for distributional convolution: $D^\alpha(u * \phi) = (D^\alpha u) * \phi = u * (D^\alpha \phi)$.
	- (Page 73) For $u \in \mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and $\phi \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, compute the Fourier transform $\mathcal{F}(u * \phi) = \widehat{u} \cdot \widehat{\phi}$.
	- (Page 73) For $u \in \mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and $v \in \mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$, compute the Fourier transform $\mathcal{F}(u \cdot v)$ as a convolution of distributions.
	- (Page 74) For $f, g \in L^1(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and $\phi \in \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, express $\langle f * g, \phi \rangle = \iint f(x) g(y) \phi(x+y) \, dx \, dy$, and use this pairing to define the convolution $u * v$ for $u \in \mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and $v \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 74) Compute the convolution $\delta_0 * u$ for any distribution $u \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 74) Prove the Leibniz product rule for distributional derivatives of convolutions: $D^\alpha(u * v) = (D^\alpha u) * v = u * (D^\alpha v)$ for $u \in \mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d), v \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 75) Let $T: \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^d) \to \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ be a continuous translation-invariant linear operator. Prove that $T\phi = \phi * u$ for a unique distribution $u \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$.
	- (Page 76) Let $u \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ and let $\omega \in (\mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d))'$ be a continuous linear functional on distributions. Use the reflexivity of test function spaces to express $\omega(u)$ as a distributional pairing.
	- (Page 76) Prove that $\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^d)$ is dense in $\mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ under the weak-* topology by testing against smooth functions.
	- (Page 77) State Hörmander's theorem on translation-invariant operators on $\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$: show that $T(\phi * \psi) = T\phi * \psi$, deduce that $\mathcal{F}(T\psi) = m \cdot \widehat{\psi}$, and determine the space of multipliers $m \in \mathcal{O}_M(\mathbb{R}^d)$ (smooth functions of polynomial growth).
	- (Page 78) Define a fundamental solution $E \in \mathcal{D}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$ for a constant-coefficient differential operator $P(D)$: $P(D)E = \delta_0$. Show that for any $f \in \mathcal{E}'(\mathbb{R}^d)$, $u = E * f$ satisfies $P(D)u = f$. State the Malgrange–Ehrenpreis Theorem guaranteeing the existence of a fundamental solution for every non-zero $P(D)$.
	- (Page 78) Compute the Fourier multiplier $m(\xi) = -4\pi^2 |\xi|^2$ of the Laplacian $\Delta$ on $\mathbb{R}^d$. Find the fundamental solution $E(x)$ such that $\Delta E = \delta_0$ for $d = 2$ ($E(x) = \frac{1}{2\pi}\ln|x|$) and for $d > 2$ ($E(x) = \frac{1}{(2-d)\omega_d} |x|^{2-d}$), and compute $\Delta(E * f)$.
	- (Page 78) State Weyl's Lemma for the Laplace equation: if $u \in \mathcal{D}'(U)$ satisfies $\Delta u = f$ distributionally with $f \in C^\infty(U)$, prove that $u \in C^\infty(U)$ is a classical smooth solution.
	- (Page 79) State and prove the Poisson Summation Formula: for $\phi \in \mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^d)$, $\sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}^d} \phi(n) = \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}^d} \widehat{\phi}(k)$. Derive the distributional formulation $\sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} \delta_{na} = \frac{1}{a} \sum_{k \in \mathbb{Z}} \delta_{k/a}$ and compute the Fourier transform of $\sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} \phi(x - na)$.
	- (Page 80) Let $f \in L^1_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R})$ be an $a$-periodic function with Fourier expansion $f(x) \sim \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} c_n(f) e^{2\pi i n x/a}$. Compute the Fourier transform of $f$ in the space of tempered distributions $\mathcal{S}'(\mathbb{R})$ as a weighted sum of Dirac deltas: $\widehat{f} = \frac{1}{a} \sum_{n \in \mathbb{Z}} c_n(f) \delta_{n/a}$.

http://mat.msgsu.edu.tr/~aad/2012/Slides/paganin.pdf, class field theory in 33 slides
    - (Slide 2) Define $G$-modules as abelian groups equipped with an action of a group $G$, equivalently modules over the group ring $\mathbb{Z}[G]$.
    - (Slide 5) Define group cohomology $H^n(G, A) = \operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbb{Z}[G]}^n(\mathbb{Z}, A)$ as the right derived functors of the fixed point functor $A \mapsto A^G$.
    - (Slide 7) State the Lyndon-Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence $E_2^{p, q} = H^p(G/H, H^q(H, A)) \implies H^{p+q}(G, A)$ for a normal subgroup $H \triangleleft G$.
    - (Slide 9) State Hilbert's Theorem 90: $H^1(G_K, (K^{\mathrm{sep}})^\times) = 0$, and relate $H^2(G_K, (K^{\mathrm{sep}})^\times) \cong \operatorname{Br}(K)$ to the Brauer group of $K$.
    - (Slide 9) Rewrite Hilbert's Theorem 90 as the following. Suppose $K$ is a finite Galois extension of a field $k$ with Galois group $G$. Regard the general linear group $\mathrm{GL}(n,K)$ as a $G$-module, then compute the first Galois cohomology with coefficients in $\mathrm{GL}(n,K)$.
    - (Slide 9) What is the geometry significance of Hilbert's Theorem 90 and what is the purpose of the first Galois cohomology? (Hint: vector spaces descend uniquely from the finite Galois extension $K$ to the field $k$, and there are no nontrivial $k$-forms of $K^n$. Note that this changes for other algebraic groups. For example, the projective linear group which classifies central simple algebras via the first Galois cohomology. The orthogonal group classifies the quadratic forms. The unitary group classifies the hermitian forms. Therefore, the first Galois cohomology is used to classify forms.)

https://www.ms.u-tokyo.ac.jp/~yasuyuki/yamashita-crest2022-3.pdf, differential cohomology (Yamashita)
	- How does one define holonomy for a $U(1)$-connection? (Hint: $(L, \nabla) \rightarrow X$ be a Hermitian line bundle, with $U(1)$-connection over a manifold. For a closed curve $f: S^1 \rightarrow X$ in $X$, get holonomy in $\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$ which is isomorphic to $U(1)$.
	- Why is the Chern-Simons invariant important?
	- Why is holonomy and the Chern-Simons invariant not topological invariants? Construct a counterexample to the statement that they are topological invariants.
	- Why does it follow that the field strength is integral? What does integral mean?
	- What is Dirac charge quantisation?
	- What values does one recover from holonomy?
	- What is the motivation for the object $\hat{x}$ that recovers to topological terms in the integral $\int_{M^{n-1}} f^{*} \hat{x}$ that lives inside differential cohomology? (Hint: you want it the integral to be a topological action in a field theory. Using ordinary cohomology classes $x$, one loses all geometric information. If one uses only a differential form, one loses global topology and torsion information. The cohomology class $\hat{x}$ has an underlying topological class, a closed differential form that is usually integral, and a flat and holonomic part. This integral therefore produces a gauge-invariant, geometrically refined topological term. This is what Chern-Simons theory needs.)
	- How does one motivate the definition of a connection in differential geometry to detect cohomology classes, in particular the first Chern class $c_1(L) = [i \Omega / 2 \pi]$ is in cohomology $H^2(X, \mathbb{Z})$? (Hint: with a connection, one can define the curvature, and recover it up to torsion. A line bundle is a topological object, to compute characteristics via local invariants, one must differentia locally. The de Rham class only sees the real part of the integer Chern class, torsion is invisible to the curvature alone, however, a connection detects the Chern class up to torsion. The torsion is recorded by the holonomy or the information in the flat line bundle.)
	- How is Dirac charge quantization related to curvature? (Hint: condition on the curvature class. I do not understand this.)
	- How does flat line bundles fail to recover the topology? (Hint: take tensors with a connection such as $F =0$, taking tensor products with real cohomology means the Chern class vanishes. A flat bundle can only see the torsion cohomology. Flatness is valuable locallly, but it is not general enough to detect the topology of all line bundles.)
	- Compute the holonomy of a trivial bundle $\mathbb{C} \times S^2 \rightarrow S^2$ that preserves the trivial connection for $X = \mathbb{RP^2} = S/\mathbb{Z_2}$.
	- How does holonomy $\mathrm{Hol}(L, \nabla)$ remember the isomorphism (= gauge equivalence, recall that picking an isomorphism means one picks a gauge field)?
	- What values do holonomy recover for a flat connection? (Hint: The monodromy representation which is $\pi_1(X) \rightarrow U(1)$, this is an element of $H^1(X, U(1)$. It also recovers the curvature but I am not sure.) 
	- What is the condition for holonomy maps from $C^\infty(S^1, X) \rightarrow \mathbb{R/Z}$?
	- What is the motivation behind the Cheeger-Simons' definition of the second differential cohomology?
	- How does one invent the notion of isomorphism classes of vector bundles? (Hint: allow for bundle isomorphisms, one can quotient out local trivialisations. These isomorphism classes form a semigroup under direct sum. The Grothendieck group is the group completion of the semigroup of isomorphism classes. This allows one to subtract bundles formally, this is useful for say, index theorems.)
	- How does one invent the notion of the relative $K^0$-group of $K^0(X,Y)$?
	- What is the Bunke-Schick axiom of differential cohomology?
	- What is the Hopkins-Singer model of differential cohomology?
	- What is the basic dictionary between ordinary topology and differential cohomology for the first Chern class? (Hint: Line bundles)
	- What is the basic dictionary between ordinary topology and differential cohomology for the closed two form? (Hint: Curvature)
	- What is the basic dictionary between ordinary topology and differential cohomology for the loops around curve? (Hint: Holonomy)
	- What is the basic dictionary between ordinary topology and differential cohomology for the flat bundles? (Hint: Kernel of curvature map. This is because one can think about the curvature map as some classification, those that are killed off and mapped to zero correspond to flat bundles.)
	- What is the basic dictionary between ordinary topology and differential cohomology for the torsion and integrality? (Hint: the periods of the curvature form integrate to integers over cycles, capturing the quantisation of fields in Physics.)
	- What is the basic dictionary between ordinary topology and differential cohomology for the torsion and integrality? (Hint: the periods of the curvature form integrate to integers over cycles, capturing the quantisation of fields in Physics.)
	- How does the first Pontrjagin number of 4-manifolds recover Chern-Simons theory? (Hint: Differential Pontrjagin classes can be used to show that $SO(3)$ or $\mathbb{R}P^3$ with the usual metric of constant curvature does not admit a conformal immersion in $\mathbb{R}^4$. See Chern, Shiing Shen; Simons, James Characteristic forms and geometric invariants. Ann. of Math. (2) 99 (1974), 48–69)

https://math.dartmouth.edu/~lesen/notes/talk_slides.pdf, Shimura varieties slides
	- How does one define multiplication by an imaginary number by defining an endomorphism? (Hint: pick $J$ such that $J^2 = -1$)
	- Fix a standard symplectic form $\psi$. Define $G = \mathrm{GSp}_2g(\mathbb{R})$. Show that the standard Siegel modular variety $M := \{A + Bi \in \mathrm{Sym}_g(\mathbb{C}): B > 0\}$ is identified with the description as a $G(\mathbb{R})$-conjugacy class of homomorphisms $h: \mathbb{S} \rightarrow G_\mathbb{R}$ (from the Deligne torus). Show that with this identification, if the associated $M / \Gamma(N)$ and $D/K(N)$ both parameterise abelian varieties with level $N$ structure for all $N$, then the moduli functors are represented by the same variety.

https://www.preschema.com/lecture-notes/cohdag2022/cohdag2022.pdf, cohomological intersection theory
	- (Page 1) How does one formulate Poincaré duality for a noncompact oriented smooth manifold of dimension $d$? (Hint: There exists a fundamental class $[X] \in H_d^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$ in Borel-Moore homology such that the cap product $-\cap [X] \colon H^i(X) \xrightarrow{\sim} H_{d-i}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$ is an isomorphism for all $i$.)
	- (Page 2) How does one have a fundamental class for submanifolds? (Hint: For a closed oriented submanifold $i \colon Y \hookrightarrow X$ of dimension $m$, define $[Y]_X := i_*([Y]) \in H_m^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$ via the proper pushforward along the inclusion.)
	- (Page 2) How does one define a cohomological Bézout theorem? (Hint: Cup product defines a ring structure on $H^*(X)$. Via Poincaré duality, this induces an intersection product $[Y] \cdot [Z] := \mathrm{PD}^{-1}(\mathrm{PD}([Y]) \cup \mathrm{PD}([Z]))$. If $Y^m$ and $Z^n$ intersect transversely in $X^d$, then $Y \cap Z$ is an oriented submanifold of dimension $m+n-d$ and $[Y] \cdot [Z] = [Y \cap Z]$.)
	- (Page 2) What does Thom's transversality mean in non-transverse case? (Hint: Smooth maps can be homotoped to transverse ones, so non-transverse intersections can be perturbed away. This fails in algebraic geometry because the Zariski topology is rigid and continuous perturbations are not algebraic morphisms; it fails in equivariant geometry because perturbations must respect group actions.)
	- (Page 3) Classify many Borel-Moore type theories? (Hint: (1) Topological: $H_*^{\mathrm{BM}}(X(\mathbb{C})^{\mathrm{an}})$; (2) $\ell$-adic: $H_i^{\mathrm{BM}}(X) = H^{-i}(X, \omega_X)$; (3) Motivic / Chow: $H_{2i}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X, \mathbb{Z}(i)) \cong \mathrm{CH}_i(X)$; (4) $G$-theory: $G_0(X) = K_0(\mathbf{Coh}(X))$; (5) Algebraic cobordism: $\Omega_*(X)$ via Voevodsky's cobordism spectrum $\mathbf{MGL}$, which interpolates between $\mathrm{CH}_*(X)$ and $G_0(X)$.)
	- (Page 3) How does one study $G$-theory? (Hint: As the algebraic $K$-theory of coherent sheaves $K(\mathbf{Coh}(X))$. It behaves as a homology theory with proper pushforwards, whereas $K$-theory of vector bundles $K(\mathbf{Perf}(X))$ behaves like cohomology with arbitrary pullbacks.)
	- (Page 4) Write down the implication of Chow's moving lemma in Chow homology for the intersection product? (Hint: Any two cycles can be moved into proper intersection, yielding $[Y] \cdot [Z] = \sum_\alpha i(W_\alpha, Y \cdot Z; X) [W_\alpha]$, where the multiplicities $i(W_\alpha, Y \cdot Z; X) = \sum_k (-1)^k \mathrm{length}_{\mathcal{O}_{X, W_\alpha}}(\mathrm{Tor}_k^{\mathcal{O}_{X, W_\alpha}}(\mathcal{O}_{Y, W_\alpha}, \mathcal{O}_{Z, W_\alpha}))$ are given by Serre's Tor-formula.)
	- (Page 4) Apart from using Chow's moving lemma, state another result one can using derived algebraic geometry to get non-transverse intersection theory? (Hint: Form the derived intersection $Y \times_X^{\mathbf{R}} Z = \mathbf{Spec}_X(\mathcal{O}_Y \otimes_{\mathcal{O}_X}^{\mathbf{L}} \mathcal{O}_Z)$. It is quasi-smooth and carries a virtual fundamental class $[Y \times_X^{\mathbf{R}} Z]^{\mathrm{vir}}$ such that $[Y] \cdot [Z] = [Y \times_X^{\mathbf{R}} Z]^{\mathrm{vir}}$, recovering Serre's Tor-formula without moving cycles.)
	- (Page 4) What are the sheaf theories of finite type one considers? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Topological $\mathcal{D}(X^{\mathrm{an}})$, étale $D_{\text{ét}}(X, \Lambda)$, and motivic $\mathcal{DM}(X)$. Invented to provide a stable $\infty$-categorical coefficient system $D(X)$ satisfying the full Grothendieck six-operation formalism.)
	- (Page 5) State the pushforward and pullback adjunction in the six operation. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Standard adjunction $f^* \colon D(Y) \rightleftarrows D(X) \colon f_*$ and exceptional adjunction $f_! \colon D(X) \rightleftarrows D(Y) \colon f^!$. Invented because $f^*$ preserves colimits (hence has right adjoint $f_*$) and compactly supported integration $f_!$ preserves colimits (hence has right adjoint $f^!$).)
	- (Page 5) What requirement is needed for any morphism for compactly supported variants? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: The morphism $f$ must be compactifiable/of finite type, factoring as an open immersion followed by a proper map $X \xrightarrow{j} \overline{X} \xrightarrow{p} Y$, so $f_! := p_* \circ j_!$.)
	- (Page 5) What are the compatabilities associated with tensor products and internal hom? State these formulas in full. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Projection formula: $f_!(\mathcal{F} \otimes f^*\mathcal{G}) \simeq f_!(\mathcal{F}) \otimes \mathcal{G}$. Base change formula for Cartesian squares: $g^* f_! \simeq (f')_! (g')^*$ and $f^! g_* \simeq (g')_* (f')^!$. Invented to encode that integration commutes with base change and module actions.)
	- (Page 5) State the condition and definition for the "forget supports" map. (Hint: The canonical transformation $\alpha_f \colon f_! \to f_*$ is defined by compactification; it is an isomorphism if and only if $f$ is proper.)
	- (Page 5) State the definition of a scheme of finite type. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: A scheme covered by finitely many affines $\mathbf{Spec}(A_i)$ with each $A_i$ a finitely generated $k$-algebra. Invented to formalize geometries cut out by finitely many polynomial equations.)
	- (Page 5) State the definition of the derived global sections of an object in the derived category. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: $\mathbf{R}\Gamma(X, \mathcal{F}) := (a_X)_*(\mathcal{F}) \in D(\mathbf{Spec}(k))$, where $a_X \colon X \to \mathbf{Spec}(k)$ is the structure map. Invented as the right adjoint to the constant pullback functor.)
	- (Page 5) State the definition for the cohomology of a scheme of finite type over a field $k$ given $a: X \rightarrow \mathrm{Spec}(k)$ for the structural morphism. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: $C^\bullet(X) := (a_X)_*(\mathbf{1}_X)$, with $H^i(X) = \pi_{-i} C^\bullet(X)$. Invented as derived sections of the monoidal unit.)
	- (Page 6) State the definition of a structural morphism. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: The canonical projection $a_X \colon X \to \mathbf{Spec}(k)$, representing $X$ as a family over the base point.)
	- (Page 6) State the definition of a dualising complex for a scheme of finite type in terms of a structural morphism. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: $\omega_X := a_X^!(\mathbf{1}_{\mathbf{Spec}(k)})$. Invented as the representing object for the Verdier dual of compactly supported sections.)
	- (Page 6) State the definition of Borel-Moore homology. And the relative version. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Absolute: $C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X) := (a_X)_* a_X^!(\mathbf{1}_k) = \mathbf{R}\Gamma(X, \omega_X)$, $H_i^{\mathrm{BM}}(X) = H^{-i}(X, \omega_X)$. Relative to $f \colon X \to S$: $C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X/S) := f_* f^!(\mathbf{1}_S) \in D(S)$.)
	- (Page 6) State the canonical isomorphism in the motivic case using Bloch linearised higher Chow groups. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: $H_{2j}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X_{\mathrm{mot}}, \mathbb{Z}(j)) \cong \mathrm{CH}_j(X)$ and $H_i^{\mathrm{BM}}(X_{\mathrm{mot}}, \Lambda(j)) \cong \mathrm{CH}_j(X, i-2j; \Lambda)$, identifying motivic Borel-Moore homology with Bloch's higher Chow complexes.)
	- (Page 6) Does Borel-Moore homology have proper pushforwards? State the definition of proper pushforwards? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Yes. For proper $f$, $f_! \simeq f_*$, so the counit $\mathrm{Tr}_f \colon f_* f^! \to \mathrm{id}$ induces $f_* \colon (a_X)_* a_X^! \mathbf{1} \simeq (a_Y)_* f_* f^! a_Y^! \mathbf{1} \to (a_Y)_* a_Y^! \mathbf{1}$.)
	- (Page 6) State the definition of a fundamental class for $X$ smooth of relative dimension $d$? Why are these conditions needed? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: By purity, $f^!\mathbf{1}_S \simeq \mathbf{1}_X(d)[2d]$; the fundamental class is the image of $\mathrm{id}$ under $\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbf{1}_X, \mathbf{1}_X) \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathrm{Hom}(\mathbf{1}_X, f^!\mathbf{1}_S(-d)[-2d])$. Smoothness is needed so the cotangent complex is locally free.)
	- (Page 7) State the conditions and requirements for a Gysin map for relative fundamental class. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: For $f \colon X \to Y$ smooth of dimension $d$, purity gives $f^! \omega_Y \simeq f^* \omega_Y(d)[2d]$, inducing $f^! \colon C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(Y)(d)[2d] \to C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$.)
	- (Page 7) State the definition of a relative Poincare duality isomorphism? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: For smooth $f \colon X \to S$ of dimension $d$, the cap product $-\cap [X/S] \colon f_*(\mathbf{1}_X)(d)[2d] \xrightarrow{\sim} f_* f^!(\mathbf{1}_S) = C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X/S)$ is an equivalence in $D(S)$.)
	- (Page 7) State the exact distinguished triangle and the conditions for the property of localisation? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: For closed immersion $i \colon Z \hookrightarrow X$ with complement open immersion $j \colon U \hookrightarrow X$, there is a fiber sequence $i_* \omega_Z \to \omega_X \to j_* \omega_U$, yielding the exact localization triangle $C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(Z) \xrightarrow{i_*} C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X) \xrightarrow{j^*} C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(U) \xrightarrow{+1}$.)
	- (Page 7) State the property of descent for cases of Zariski or Nisnevich covers. What is the homotopy limit diagram? What are the conditions? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Cohomology satisfies descent: for a hypercover $U_\bullet \to X$, $C^\bullet(X) \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathrm{holim}_{[n] \in \mathbf{\Delta}} C^\bullet(U_n)$. Invented to compute global invariants by gluing local sections.)
	- (Page 7) State the property of codescent for cases of Zariski or Nisnevich covers. What is the homotopy colimit diagram? What are the conditions? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Borel-Moore homology satisfies codescent: $\mathrm{hocolim}_{[n] \in \mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}} C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(U_n) \xrightarrow{\sim} C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$ for proper covers or Nisnevich hypercovers. Invented as the homological dual to sheaf descent.)
	- (Page 8) State the definition of a stack? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: A pseudofunctor (or $\infty$-functor) $\mathcal{X} \colon \mathbf{Aff}^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{Grpd}$ satisfying effective descent along a Grothendieck topology. Invented to parametrize geometric objects with nontrivial automorphisms.)
	- (Page 8) State the definition of an Artin stack? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: A stack $\mathcal{X}$ with representable, smooth diagonal $\mathcal{X} \to \mathcal{X} \times \mathcal{X}$, admitting a smooth surjective atlas $U \to \mathcal{X}$ from a scheme $U$. Invented to do differential/algebraic geometry on quotient spaces by non-finite groups.)
	- (Page 8) State the definition of a Deligne-Mumford stack? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: A stack admitting an étale surjective atlas $U \to \mathcal{X}$, equivalently having unramified/discrete automorphism groups. Invented to parametrize objects without infinitesimal automorphisms, such as stable curves $\overline{\mathcal{M}}_g$.)
	- (Page 9) State the definition of a stack for the case of an infinity category? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: An $\infty$-functor $\mathcal{X} \colon \mathbf{CAlg}_k \to \mathcal{S}$ to the $\infty$-category of spaces/$\infty$-groupoids satisfying $\infty$-sheaf descent for the étale topology.)
	- (Page 9) State the definition of the Borel-Moore homology of an Artin stack using infinity stacks? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: For an Artin stack $\mathcal{X}$ presented by a smooth simplicial scheme atlas $U_\bullet \to \mathcal{X}$, $C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(\mathcal{X}) := \mathrm{hocolim}_{[n] \in \mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}} C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(U_n)(-d_n)[-2d_n]$, where $d_n$ is the relative dimension of $U_n \to \mathcal{X}$.)
	- (Page 9) State the definition of the cohomology of an Artin stack using infinity stacks? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: $C^\bullet(\mathcal{X}) := \mathrm{holim}_{[n] \in \mathbf{\Delta}} C^\bullet(U_n) = \mathbf{R}\Gamma(\mathcal{X}, \mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{X}})$.)
	- (Page 10) What can one use to resolve objects in some nonabelian categories? (Hint: Simplicial resolutions / simplicial objects in the category to construct nonabelian derived functors in the sense of Quillen.)
	- (Page 10) What is homotopical algebra? (Hint: Quillen's formalism of model categories and $\infty$-categories that allows doing homological algebra in non-abelian settings by replacing isomorphisms with weak homotopy equivalences.)
	- (Page 10) How does one compute the Dold-Kan equivalence in the abelian case? (Hint: The functor $N \colon s\mathbf{Ab} \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathrm{Ch}_{\ge 0}(\mathbf{Ab})$ sends a simplicial abelian group $A_\bullet$ to its normalized chain complex $N(A)_n = \bigcap_{i=0}^{n-1} \ker(d_i) \subset A_n$ with differential $d = (-1)^n d_n$.)
	- (Page 10) Define the notion of finite projective objects. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: An object $P$ in an $\infty$-category $\mathcal{C}$ such that $\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(P, -)$ commutes with filtered colimits (finite/compact) and with geometric realizations/reflexive coequalizers (projective).)
	- (Page 10) Define rigorously an algebraic category using the definition of enough finite projective objects. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: A category $\mathcal{A}$ generated under filtered colimits and sifted colimits by a small subcategory $\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fp}}$ of finite projective objects.)
	- (Page 10) Define the nonabelian derived category of an algebraic category $\mathcal{A}$ as free generation under? How does one invent the notion? (Hint: $\mathcal{P}_\Sigma(\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fp}})$, the free completion of the finite projective objects $\mathcal{A}_{\mathrm{fp}}$ under sifted colimits / simplicial homotopy colimits.)
	- (Page 10) Compute using the definition for the derived category of sets. (Hint: The finite projective objects in $\mathbf{Set}$ are finite sets; their sifted/simplicial completion is the $\infty$-category of spaces $\mathcal{S} \simeq \mathbf{sSet}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]$, so the nonabelian derived category of sets is the $\infty$-category of homotopy types.)
	- (Page 10) Why is it the "good" or "finite projective objects" are the smooth schemes. Define smooth schemes. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Smooth schemes are locally étale over affine space $\mathbb{A}^n$, meaning their coordinate algebras are retracts of polynomial rings, making them projective generators for derived schemes.)
	- (Page 11) How does one define the conormal complex for a derived Artin stack? (Hint: For an immersion or morphism $f \colon X \to Y$, the conormal complex is the shifted relative cotangent complex $N^*_{X/Y} := L_{X/Y}[-1]$.)
	- (Page 11) How does one define the normal bundle of a derived Artin stack? What does it parameterise? (Hint: The normal bundle/stack is $\mathbb{N}_{X/Y} := \mathbf{Spec}_X(\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_X}(L_{X/Y}[-1])) = \mathbf{V}(N^*_{X/Y})$. It parameterizes derived normal deformations and cosections.)
	- (Page 12) Define what does it mean for something to be of locally finite presentation? (Hint: A morphism $f \colon X \to Y$ is locally of finite presentation if its functor of points commutes with filtered colimits of commutative rings.)
	- (Page 12) Define the relative cotangent complex of perfect Tor amplitude less than or equal to 1? Define each term slowly. then all at once. (Hint: (1) Perfect: $L_{X/Y}$ is locally quasi-isomorphic to a bounded complex of vector bundles; (2) Tor amplitude in $[-1, 0]$: for any quasi-coherent module $M$, $H^i(L_{X/Y} \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} M) = 0$ for $i \notin [-1, 0]$; (3) Together: $L_{X/Y}$ is locally represented by a two-term complex of vector bundles $[E_{-1} \to E_0]$ concentrated in degrees $-1$ and $0$.)
	- (Page 12) Define the notion of a quasi-smooth morphism of derived Artin stacks? (Hint: A morphism $f \colon X \to Y$ locally of finite presentation whose relative cotangent complex $L_{X/Y}$ has Tor-amplitude concentrated in $[-1, 0]$ (a derived local complete intersection).)
	- (Page 13) State the generalised Thom isomorphism and compute the Gysin map for the projection using the virtual rank. (Hint: For a perfect complex $E$ of virtual rank $r$ on $X$, there is a Thom isomorphism $C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(\mathbf{V}(E)) \cong C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)(r)[2r]$, making the Gysin map of the zero section $s_0 \colon X \to \mathbf{V}(E)$ an equivalence shifted by the virtual rank.)
	- (Page 13) Produce the Gysin map for quasi-smooth morphisms of relative virtual dimension using Borel-Moore homology. How does one give rise to the relative virtual fundamental class from this definition? (Hint: Factor $f \colon X \to Y$ through a smooth map followed by the zero section of a vector bundle; the specialized Thom map gives $f^! \colon C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(Y)(d)[2d] \to C_\bullet^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$ where $d = \mathrm{virdim}(f)$. The relative virtual class is $[X/Y]^{\mathrm{vir}} := f^!(\mathbf{1}_Y)$.)
	- (Page 13) Why does the virtual fundamental class not satisfy Poincare duality in general? (Hint: Because quasi-smooth derived stacks are generally singular as classical schemes; $L_X$ is not a locally free sheaf concentrated in degree 0, so the purity isomorphism between $f^!$ and $f^*$ fails.)
	- (Page 14) Compute the tangent bundle of a scheme or a Deligne-Mumford stack. (Hint: $T_X = \mathbf{Spec}_X(\mathrm{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_X}(\Omega_X^1)) = \underline{\mathrm{Spec}}_X(\mathrm{Sym}(L_X))$. For a classical smooth DM stack it is concentrated in degree 0; for a general DM stack it is the dual complex $\mathbb{T}_X = L_X^\vee$.)
	- (Page 14) Define the virtual fundamental class. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: For a quasi-smooth derived stack $X$ with virtual dimension $d$, $[X]^{\mathrm{vir}} := p^!([*]) \in H_{2d}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X, \mathbb{Z}(d))$, where $p \colon X \to \mathbf{Spec}(k)$ is the structure map. Invented to assign an intrinsic cycle class of the expected dimension to moduli spaces with obstruction theories.)
	- (Page 14) State the functoriality theorem of the virtual fundamental class of derived Artin stacks. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: For composable quasi-smooth morphisms $X \xrightarrow{f} Y \xrightarrow{g} Z$, $(g \circ f)^! = f^! \circ g^!$, hence $[X/Z]^{\mathrm{vir}} = f^!([Y/Z]^{\mathrm{vir}})$.)
	- (Page 15) State the base change theorem of the virtual fundamental class of derived Artin stacks. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: Given a derived Cartesian square with $f$ quasi-smooth, the pullback $f'$ is quasi-smooth with the same virtual dimension, and $g^* f^! \simeq (f')^! (g')^*$, so $g^* [X/Y]^{\mathrm{vir}} = [X'/Y']^{\mathrm{vir}}$.)
	- (Page 15) State the excess intersection theorem of the virtual fundamental class of derived Artin stacks. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: If $i \colon Y \hookrightarrow X$ is a classical regular immersion and $Z \hookrightarrow X$ is a submanifold intersecting $Y$ with excess bundle $E = N_{Y/X}|_{Y \cap Z} / N_{Y \cap Z / Z}$, then the derived intersection $Y \times_X^{\mathbf{R}} Z$ has $[Y \times_X^{\mathbf{R}} Z]^{\mathrm{vir}} = c_{\mathrm{top}}(E) \cap [Y \cap Z]$.)
	- (Page 15) State the non-transverse Bézout's theorem of the virtual fundamental class of derived Artin stacks. How does one invent the notion? (Hint: For closed subschemes $Y, Z \subset X$ with $X$ smooth, the derived intersection product satisfies $[Y] \cdot [Z] = \iota_* [Y \times_X^{\mathbf{R}} Z]^{\mathrm{vir}} \in H_*^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$, holding unconditionally without moving lemmas.)

https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11950, Weil-Moore Anima (lecture 2)
	- (1) To organise Galois groups and modules, how does one formulate a classical class formation? (Hint: A class formation is a pair $(\Gamma, A)$ where $\Gamma$ is a profinite group and $A$ is a discrete $\Gamma$-module such that for every open subgroup $H \subset \Gamma$ and open normal subgroup $N \triangleleft H$, $H^1(H/N, A^N) = 0$ and $H^2(H/N, A^N) \cong \frac{1}{[H:N]}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}$ canonically via an invariant map $\mathrm{inv}_{H/N}$.)
	- (2) How does one establish the vanishing of $H^1(H/N, A^N)$ and the cyclicity of $H^2(H/N, A^N)$? (Hint: By studying the Galois fixed-point modules $A^N$ across finite Galois extensions and computing the Herbrand quotient $h(A^N) = [H:N]$ for cyclic steps using Hilbert's Theorem 90 for the $H^1$-vanishing.)
	- (3) What is the purpose of the Nakayama-Tate lemma in class formations? (Hint: Given a class formation with fundamental class $\alpha \in H^2(G, A)$, cup product with $\alpha$ yields an isomorphism $\widehat{H}^i(G, \mathbb{Z}) \xrightarrow{\sim} \widehat{H}^{i+2}(G, A)$ for all $i \in \mathbb{Z}$; taking $i = -2$ gives the Artin reciprocity isomorphism $(G)^{\mathrm{ab}} \cong \widehat{H}^{-2}(G, \mathbb{Z}) \xrightarrow{\sim} \widehat{H}^0(G, A) = A^G / \mathrm{Tr}_G(A)$.)
	- (4) How does one verify that a given Galois module satisfies the Nakayama-Tate condition? (Hint: Check that for every Sylow $p$-subgroup $G_p \subset G$, $H^1(G_p, A) = 0$ and $H^2(G_p, A)$ is cyclic of order $|G_p|$, generated by the restriction of the fundamental class $\alpha$.)
	- (5) How does one construct the Artin reciprocity map with dense image and determine its kernel? (Hint: Taking the projective limit over all open normal subgroups $N \triangleleft H$ of the Nakayama-Tate isomorphisms $(H/N)^{\mathrm{ab}} \cong A^H / \mathrm{Tr}_{H/N}(A^N)$ yields the Artin map $A^H \to H^{\mathrm{ab}}$ with dense image and kernel $\bigcap_{N} \mathrm{Tr}_{H/N}(A^N)$, which is the subgroup of universal norms.)
	- (6) What is the image of the global/local Artin map $A^H \to H^{\mathrm{ab}}$? (Hint: The image is dense in $H^{\mathrm{ab}}$, and the map induces a topological isomorphism $\widehat{A^H} \xrightarrow{\sim} H^{\mathrm{ab}}$ on the appropriate completion (the profinite completion for local fields / idèle class groups).)
	- (7) What are the axiomatic cohomological criteria required for a class formation? (Hint: (i) $H^1(H/N, A^N) = 0$; (ii) there exists an isomorphism $\mathrm{inv}_{H/N} \colon H^2(H/N, A^N) \xrightarrow{\sim} \frac{1}{[H:N]}\mathbb{Z}/\mathbb{Z}$; (iii) these invariant maps commute with inflation and restriction maps across all open subgroups $N \subset H \subset \Gamma$.)
	- (8) How does one reduce the verification of class formation axioms to cyclic extensions? (Hint: By Sylow theory and solvable filtration, any finite group can be decomposed into a tower of cyclic extensions of prime degree; checking the axioms on prime-degree cyclic steps implies them for all finite Galois extensions.)
	- (9) How is Tate cohomology exploited via 2-periodicity for cyclic groups? (Hint: For cyclic $G$, $\widehat{H}^{i}(G, A) \cong \widehat{H}^{i+2}(G, A)$ for all $i$, so the entire cohomology is determined by $\widehat{H}^0(G, A) = A^G / \mathrm{Nm}(A)$ and $\widehat{H}^1(G, A) = \ker(\mathrm{Nm}) / I_G A$, simplifying all calculations to the Herbrand quotient $h(A) = |\widehat{H}^0(A)| / |\widehat{H}^1(A)|$.)
	- (10) What ingredients are required to show a module forms a class formation? (Hint: One needs: (1) vanishing of $H^1$ via Hilbert's Theorem 90 / Kummer theory; (2) computation of Herbrand quotients $h(A^N) = [H:N]$; (3) existence of enough cyclic extensions (especially unramified ones) to normalize and assemble the invariant maps.)
	- (11) How is local class field theory proved using class formations for nonarchimedean local fields? (Hint: Let $\mathcal{C}$ be the Galois category of finite separable extensions of $K$, with coefficient functor $A(L) = L^\times$. (1) $H^1(L/K, L^\times) = 0$ by Hilbert 90; (2) for unramified extensions, $h(\mathcal{O}_L^\times) = 1$ implies $h(L^\times) = [L:K]$; (3) unramified invariant maps extend to all extensions, proving local CFT.)
	- (12) Why is the classical definition of class formations non-canonical, and how does the basepoint problem arise? (Hint: The absolute Galois group $\Gamma$ requires choosing an algebraic closure $\overline{k}$, making $\Gamma$ defined only up to non-unique inner automorphism; the Galois category is an étale/connected groupoid, not an intrinsically based group.)
	- (13) How does one generate enough cyclic extensions to establish class formations when roots of unity are missing? (Hint: Adjoin roots of unity and apply Kummer theory, using the non-degeneracy of the Kummer pairing $K^\times / (K^\times)^n \times \mathrm{Gal}(K(\sqrt[n]{K})/K) \to \mu_n$.)
	- (14) What is the Weil group of a class formation and how does it resolve the canonicity issue? (Hint: The Weil group $W_{L/K}$ is the group extension $1 \to A^L \to W_{L/K} \to \mathrm{Gal}(L/K) \to 1$ classified by the fundamental class $\alpha \in H^2(\mathrm{Gal}(L/K), A^L)$; Clausen refines this into an abelian gerbe on $B\Gamma$ banded by $A$.)
	- (15) How are cocycle classes and 2-morphisms interpreted at the chain level for gerbes? (Hint: A 2-cocycle is $x \in C^2$ with $dx = 0$ representing the extension class; a 1-cochain $\alpha \in C^1$ with $d\alpha = y - x$ defines a 1-isomorphism (2-morphism of extensions) between cocycles $x$ and $y$.)
	- (16) How are isomorphism classes of gerbes defined using 2-groupoids? (Hint: The 2-category of groupoids forms a $(2, 1)$-category where gerbes banded by $A$ over a site $\mathcal{C}$ are classified by $\pi_0 \mathrm{Map}(\mathcal{C}, B^2 A) \cong H^2(\mathcal{C}, A)$, with 1-morphisms being $A$-torsor equivalences and 2-morphisms being $A$-translations.)
	- (17) What are the objects, 1-morphisms, and 2-morphisms in the categorical presentation of the Weil group? (Hint: Objects are inverse systems of group extensions $1 \to A^N \to E_N \to H/N \to 1$; 1-morphisms are morphisms of inverse systems compatible with projection to $H/N$; 2-morphisms are conjugations by elements of the abelian kernel $A^N$.)
	- (18) What is a refined class formation in the sense of Clausen/Scholze? (Hint: A triple $(\mathcal{C}, F, \alpha)$ where $\mathcal{C}$ is a Galois category, $F \colon \mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})$ is a cosheaf valued in the derived $\infty$-category of condensed/discrete abelian groups such that $H_i(F(X)) = 0$ for $i \neq 0, 1$, and $\alpha \colon H_0(F(X)) \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathbb{Z}$ is an isomorphism for every connected object $X \in \mathcal{C}$.)
	- (19) How does one promote a classical class formation to a refined class formation? (Hint: Construct a map $\kappa \colon \mathbb{Z}/\#\mathcal{C} \to \mathbf{R}\Gamma(\mathcal{C}, A)[2]$ in $\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})$—where $\mathbb{Z}/\#\mathcal{C} = \bigcup_{X} \frac{1}{|X|}\mathbb{Z} \subset \mathbb{Q}$—such that $\kappa$ induces an isomorphism $(\mathbb{Z}/\#\mathcal{C})/\mathbb{Z} \xrightarrow{\sim} H^2(\mathcal{C}, A)$.)
	- (20) What are the homotopy groups $\pi_0, \pi_1, \pi_2$ of the mapping space classifying promotions to refined class formations? (Hint: $\pi_0$ is the set of compatible fundamental classes in $\mathrm{Hom}((\mathbb{Z}/\#\mathcal{C})/\mathbb{Z}, H^2(\mathcal{C}, A))$; $\pi_1$ is the torsor under $\mathrm{Ext}^1(\mathbb{Z}/\#\mathcal{C}, A(*))$; $\pi_2$ is $\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}/\#\mathcal{C}, A(*))$.)
	- (21) Why must $F$ be defined as a cosheaf, and how does this relate to its transpose $F^{\mathrm{tr}}$? (Hint: The homology invariant $H_1(F(X)) = A(X)$ assigns to a Galois cover its units/class group via pushforwards/norms, so $F$ is covariant on $\mathcal{C}$ (a cosheaf); its linear dual/transpose $F^{\mathrm{tr}} = \mathbf{R}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(F, \mathbb{Z})$ is a contravariant sheaf on $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}}$.)
	- (22) How does the refined class formation work for the base field $\mathbb{Q}$? (Hint: $H^2(G_\mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{A}_\mathbb{Q}^\times / \mathbb{Q}^\times) \cong \mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}$ has two choices of sign (homology/geometric Frobenius vs. cohomology/arithmetic Frobenius); $\mathrm{Ext}^1(\mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{A}_\mathbb{Q}^\times / \mathbb{Q}^\times) = 0$, and $\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Q}, \mathbb{A}_\mathbb{Q}^\times / \mathbb{Q}^\times) \cong \mathbb{R}_{>0}^\times$, which contracts upon passing to norm-1 idèles, giving a canonical refined class formation.)
	- (23) How does one obtain canonical refined class formations for general number fields from $\mathbb{Q}$? (Hint: By restricting the canonical refined class formation of $\mathbb{Q}$ along the finite étale slice $\mathcal{C}_K \subset \mathcal{C}_\mathbb{Q}$; the lift is unique up to an isomorphism that is unique up to powers of Frobenius.)
	- (24) How are refined class formations constructed for finite fields and nonarchimedean local fields? (Hint: For $k = \mathbb{F}_q$ or a nonarchimedean local field $K$, $\mathrm{Ext}^1(\mathbb{Z}/\#\mathcal{C}, \mathcal{O}_K^\times) \cong \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}/\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathrm{Hom} = 0$; fixing the canonical Frobenius generator gives a canonical lift to a refined class formation.)
	- (25) How does one handle archimedean local fields $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{C}$ in refined class formations? (Hint: For $\mathbb{R}$, the Galois group is $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$; $\mathrm{Ext}^1(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{R}^\times) = 0$ and $\mathrm{Hom}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{R}^\times) \cong \mathbb{R}^\times / \{\pm 1\} \cong \mathbb{R}_{>0}^\times$, which is canonically trivialized by taking positive norms.)
	- (26) How do operations on refined class formations behave under base change? (Hint: Under a morphism of sites/Galois categories $f \colon \mathcal{C}' \to \mathcal{C}$, the pullback of a refined class formation is obtained by derived pushforward of the cosheaf $f_* F$, preserving the axioms.)
	- (27) How does one define class field theory universally for locally compact Hausdorff number rings $R$? (Hint: Define an $\infty$-functor $F^{\mathrm{univ}}$ on the finite étale site of $R$ with $H_0(F^{\mathrm{univ}}(R)) = \mathbb{Z}$ and $H_1(F^{\mathrm{univ}}(R)) = C(R)$, the topological class group of $R$.)
	- (28) What are the classical class groups recovered by $H_1(F^{\mathrm{univ}}(R))$ across different rings $R$? (Hint: (1) Local field: $K^\times$; (2) Local ring of integers $\mathcal{O}_K$: $\mathcal{O}_K^\times$; (3) Finite field $\mathbb{F}_q$: $\mathbb{Z}$; (4) Global ring of integers $\mathcal{O}_K$: $\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{O}_K)$; (5) Global field $K$: idèle class group $C_K = \mathbb{A}_K^\times / K^\times$.)
	- (29) How does $K$-theory of locally compact modules universally produce class field theory? (Hint: The condensed $K$-theory spectrum $K(\mathbf{LCA}_R)$ of locally compact $R$-modules satisfies $K_1(\mathbf{LCA}_R) \cong C(R)$ (the class group), while $K_0(\mathbf{LCA}_R)$ contains topological dimension data that can be normalized to $\mathbb{Z}$.)
	- (30) How does the refined class formation assemble into the global Weil groupoid $\mathcal{W}$? (Hint: The gerbes over finite Galois quotients form an inverse system of topological 2-groupoids indexed by finite étale covers; taking the 2-limit $\mathcal{W} = \varprojlim_N E_N$ yields the Weil groupoid, whose connectedness ($\pi_0 \mathcal{W} = *$) follows from the compactness of the idèle class groups.)

https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.11731, Complex Geometry and Condensed Mathematics
	- (Page 6) How does one characterize the sheaf of holomorphic functions locally and globally on complex domains? (Hint: A holomorphic function is locally given on open polydiscs by power series converging absolutely and uniformly; since holomorphicity is local, it glues to a sheaf $\mathcal{O}_X$ whose higher sheaf cohomology vanishes on Stein domains by Cartan's Theorem B.)
	- (Page 7) How does one establish the existence and cohomological vanishing of the sheaf of holomorphic functions? (Hint: Analyticity follows from the Cauchy integral formula; using smooth bump functions and partitions of unity, one constructs the Dolbeault resolution $0 \to \mathcal{O} \to \Omega^{0,0} \xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}} \Omega^{0,1} \xrightarrow{\bar{\partial}} \dots$, reducing higher cohomology vanishing to the solvability of the $\bar{\partial}$-equation on Stein/polydisc domains.)
	- (Page 8) What is the motivating idea behind an analytic ring structure on a topological ring? (Hint: An analytic ring structure specifies a functorial topological module of "measures" $\mathcal{M}(S)$ for every profinite set $S$, enabling a unique integration/extension map from functions on $S$ to summable series and integrals in the module category.)
	- (Page 8) Why is the one-point compactification $\mathbb{N} \cup \{\infty\}$ relevant as a test space? (Hint: $\mathbb{N} \cup \{\infty\}$ is a countable cofiltered limit of finite discrete sets (hence profinite), serving as the universal test space for convergent sequences and summability.)
	- (Page 8) Why does the naive topological module of Radon measures fail over $\mathbb{R}$? (Hint: The space of Radon measures on a profinite set is not complete under general algebraic operations and lacks good categorical exactness properties because topological abelian groups do not form an abelian category.)
	- (Page 8) Why do topological abelian groups fail to form an abelian category? (Hint: A continuous bijective homomorphism between topological abelian groups (e.g., from discrete $\mathbb{R}$ to standard $\mathbb{R}$) has trivial kernel and cokernel but is not an open isomorphism, so the First Isomorphism Theorem fails.)
	- (Page 9) How does condensed mathematics resolve the failure of the First Isomorphism Theorem for topological groups? (Hint: By embedding topological groups into condensed abelian groups via their functor of points on profinite sets; the quotient of standard $\mathbb{R}$ by discrete $\mathbb{R}$ becomes a non-trivial condensed abelian group sheaf.)
	- (Page 10) What is the rigorous preliminary definition of an analytic ring? (Hint: An analytic ring is a condensed ring $\mathcal{A}$ equipped with a family of condensed $\mathcal{A}$-modules $\mathcal{M}(S)$ of "measures" for profinite sets $S$, together with Dirac-delta maps $S \to \mathcal{M}(S)$ such that the category of $\mathcal{M}$-complete $\mathcal{A}$-modules forms an abelian subcategory/heart of $\mathbf{Cond}(\mathcal{A})$ stable under limits, colimits, and extensions.)
	- (Page 11) Why promote the classical sheaf of holomorphic functions to an abelian tensor category? (Hint: To obtain a category of modules with well-behaved completed tensor products and homological algebra (derived categories) that unifies functional analysis with algebraic geometry.)
	- (Page 11) How are profinite sets characterized as a cofiltered limit of finite sets? (Hint: Every compact Hausdorff totally disconnected space $S$ is canonically isomorphic to $\varprojlim_{i} S_i$, where $S_i$ runs over all finite partitions of $S$ into clopen subsets ordered by refinement.)
	- (Page 11) What is the finitary Grothendieck topology on the category of $\kappa$-small profinite sets $\mathbf{Prof}_\kappa$? (Hint: A covering of a profinite set $S$ is a finite family of continuous maps $\{f_i \colon S_i \to S\}$ such that the induced map $\coprod_{i=1}^n S_i \to S$ is surjective.)
	- (Page 12) What is the category of $\kappa$-condensed sets and how does it behave under cardinal cutoffs? (Hint: $\mathbf{Cond}_\kappa(\mathbf{Set})$ is the category of sheaves on $(\mathbf{Prof}_\kappa, \text{finitary étale})$, i.e., contravariant functors $X \colon \mathbf{Prof}_\kappa^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{Set}$ sending finite coproducts to finite products and preserving covers; taking the colimit over strong limit cardinals $\kappa$ yields the fully faithful category $\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Set})$.)
	- (Page 13) What is a quasi-compact condensed set? (Hint: A condensed set $X$ such that for every collection of open coverings, any covering surjection $\coprod_{i} U_i \to X$ admits a finite sub-surjection; equivalently, $X$ admits a surjection from a single profinite set.)
	- (Page 13) Why is a condensed set quasi-compact if and only if it is a quotient of a profinite set? (Hint: Profinite sets are quasi-compact projective generators; every condensed set is a canonical colimit of profinite sets, so finite subcoverings correspond precisely to surjections $S \twoheadrightarrow X$ from a profinite set $S$.)
	- (Page 13) What is a quasi-separated condensed set? (Hint: A condensed set $X$ whose diagonal morphism $\Delta \colon X \to X \times X$ is quasi-compact, meaning for any two maps from profinite sets $S, T \to X$, the fiber product $S \times_X T$ is quasi-compact.)
	- (Page 13) How are compact Hausdorff spaces embedded into condensed sets? (Hint: The subcategory of quasi-compact quasi-separated (qcqs) condensed sets is equivalent to the category of compact Hausdorff topological spaces via $X \mapsto \underline{X}(S) = C(S, X)$, representing closed equivalence relations on profinite sets.)
	- (Page 14) What does it mean for a condensed set $X$ to be projective? (Hint: For every surjective map of condensed sets $Y \twoheadrightarrow Z$, the induced map $\mathrm{Hom}(X, Y) \to \mathrm{Hom}(X, Z)$ is surjective; equivalently, any surjection $Y \twoheadrightarrow X$ admits a section.)
	- (Page 14) Why does the Stone-Čech compactification $\beta I$ serve as a projective generator for condensed sets? (Hint: For any discrete set $I$, the Stone-Čech compactification $\beta I$ is an extremally disconnected profinite set, and Gleason's theorem implies $\beta I$ is a projective object in the category of compact Hausdorff spaces and condensed sets.)
	- (Page 15) Why does the category of condensed abelian groups $\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})$ possess good homological properties? (Hint: $\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})$ is a Grothendieck abelian category with enough compact projective objects given by the free condensed abelian groups $\mathbb{Z}[S]$ for extremally disconnected profinite sets $S$.)
	- (Page 15) Why is $\mathbb{Z}[S]$ compact projective for an extremally disconnected profinite set $S$? (Hint: The functor $\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})}(\mathbb{Z}[S], -) \cong \underline{(-)}(S)$ preserves all colimits and all exact sequences because $S$ is extremally disconnected and compact.)
	- (Page 16) What is the concept of a liquid vector space over $\mathbb{R}$? (Hint: A condensed $\mathbb{R}$-vector space $V$ that is complete with respect to $p$-summable measures, meaning for every $p \in (0, 1]$, infinite sums of $p$-convex combinations converge uniquely in $V$.)
	- (Page 16) How are signed Radon measures with bounded $p$-variation defined algebraically on profinite sets? (Hint: For a profinite set $S = \varprojlim S_i$, the condensed module of $p$-measures is $\mathcal{M}_{\le p}(S) = \bigcup_{C > 0} \varprojlim_{i} \{ (a_s) \in \mathbb{R}[S_i] : \sum |a_s|^p \le C \}$, forming the unit balls of $\ell^p(S)$ under non-convex $p$-norms.)
	- (Page 18) What is the condensed ring module of $p$-Radon measures $\mathcal{M}_{<p}(S)$? (Hint: $\mathcal{M}_{<p}(S) := \varinjlim_{p' < p} \mathcal{M}_{\le p'}(S)$, defined as the filtered colimit of bounded $\ell^{p'}$-variation measure balls to ensure exactness and monoidal properties.)
	- (Page 19) State the fundamental definition of a $p$-liquid vector space. (Hint: A condensed abelian group $V$ is $p$-liquid if for every profinite set $S$, the natural evaluation map $\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})}(\mathcal{M}_{<p}(S), V) \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathrm{Map}(S, V)$ is an isomorphism of condensed abelian groups.)
	- (Page 19) Why are $p$-Banach spaces and their inverse limits $p$-liquid? (Hint: A $p$-Banach space $(B, \|\cdot\|)$ is sequentially complete under its $p$-norm ($\|x+y\| \le \|x\| + \|y\|$), ensuring all series with $\sum \|x_n\|^p < \infty$ converge uniquely, which matches the integration axiom against $\mathcal{M}_{<p}(S)$.)
	- (Page 20) Why is $\mathcal{M}_{<p}(S)$ not Banach, and how is its internal dual characterized? (Hint: $\mathcal{M}_{<p}(S)$ is a strict inductive limit of non-convex metric spaces; its internal continuous dual is $\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M}_{<p}(S), \mathbb{R}) \cong C(S, \mathbb{R})$, recovering the classical Banach space of continuous real functions on $S$.)
	- (Page 22) How is a condensed set characterized via extremally disconnected test spaces? (Hint: A condensed set is uniquely determined as a contravariant functor from the category of extremally disconnected profinite sets $\mathbf{ExtrProf}^{\mathrm{op}}$ to $\mathbf{Set}$ that preserves finite products.)
	- (Page 22) What is the algebraic duality between profinite sets and Boolean algebras? (Hint: Stone duality: $\mathbf{Prof}^{\mathrm{op}} \simeq \mathbf{Bool}$ (Boolean algebras via clopen subsets) and $\mathbf{ExtrProf}^{\mathrm{op}} \simeq \mathbf{CBool}$ (complete Boolean algebras).)
	- (Page 23) Why can the free condensed abelian group $\mathbb{Z}[S]$ be written as a countable union of profinite sets? (Hint: For $S$ profinite, $\mathbb{Z}[S] = \bigcup_{n \ge 1} (\sum_{i=1}^n \pm S)$, which is a countable union of compact Hausdorff/profinite subspaces inside the condensed group.)
	- (Page 24) When is a condensed abelian group flat? (Hint: $M \in \mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})$ is flat if and only if for every extremally disconnected profinite set $S$, the evaluation $M(S)$ is a torsion-free discrete abelian group.)
	- (Page 25) How are compact projective condensed abelian groups characterized? (Hint: An object is compact projective in $\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})$ if and only if it is a retract of $\mathbb{Z}[\beta I]$ for some set $I$, where $\beta I$ is the Stone-Čech compactification of discrete $I$.)
	- (Page 26) Why are internal Ext groups non-zero in condensed mathematics? (Hint: Because $\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})$ contains topological data; for example, $\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^1(\underline{\mathbb{R}}, \underline{\mathbb{Z}}) \cong \underline{\mathbb{R}}/\underline{\mathbb{Z}} \cong \mathbb{S}^1 \neq 0$, capturing non-trivial extensions of topological groups.)
	- (Page 26) Why is the projective tensor product $\mathbb{Z}[\beta I] \otimes \mathbb{Z}[\beta J]$ not projective? (Hint: The product of two extremally disconnected compact spaces $\beta I \times \beta J$ is generally not extremally disconnected, destroying projectivity under naive tensor products.)
	- (Page 27) State Theorem 3.11 on the structure of the category $\mathbf{Liq}_p$ of $p$-liquid vector spaces. (Hint: $\mathbf{Liq}_p$ is an abelian subcategory of $\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})$ stable under all limits, colimits, extensions, and internal $\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}$ and $\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}$; the inclusion derived functor $\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Liq}_p) \hookrightarrow \mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab}))$ is fully faithful and admits a left adjoint $p$-liquification functor $(-)_{{\mathrm{liq}}_p}$ that is symmetric monoidal with unit $\mathbb{R}$.)
	- (Page 35) What are gaseous and solid vector spaces over $\mathbb{Q}_p$? (Hint: Over nonarchimedean fields like $\mathbb{Q}_p$, the measures $\mathcal{M}(S) = \mathbb{Z}_p[[S]]$ produce the abelian category of solid vector spaces $\mathbf{Solid}_{\mathbb{Q}_p}$; intermediate growth rate conditions yield $p$-liquid / gaseous structures.)
	- (Page 38) How is the liquid tensor product defined? (Hint: The liquid tensor product $V \otimes_{\mathbf{Liq}_p}^{\mathbf{L}} W := (V \otimes_{\mathbf{Cond}(\mathbf{Ab})}^{\mathbf{L}} W)_{{\mathrm{liq}}_p}$ is the liquification of the condensed derived tensor product, completing it with respect to $p$-summable measures.)
	- (Page 40) How are $p$-measures defined on locally profinite topological spaces? (Hint: For a locally profinite space $X = \bigcup U_i$, a $p$-measure is a finitely additive measure of bounded $p$-variation on compact open subsets, forming a condensed $\mathbb{R}$-module $\mathcal{M}_{<p}(X) = \varinjlim_{K \subset X} \mathcal{M}_{<p}(K)$.)
	- (Page 44) How does Poincaré duality motivate liquid analytic geometry? (Hint: Liquid modules allow the formulation of a six-operation formalism $(f^*, f_*, f_!, f^!, \otimes, \underline{\mathrm{Hom}})$ on non-archimedean and complex analytic spaces, realizing Poincaré and Serre duality via derived trace maps.)
	- (Page 48) What role does the one-point compactification play in functional analysis on liquid spaces? (Hint: It compactifies non-compact domains (like $\mathbb{C}$ or $\mathbb{R}$), transforming compactly supported smooth/analytic distributions into global sections on a compact base.)
	- (Page 50) How do nuclear spaces resolve the non-isomorphism of sequence spaces? (Hint: Nuclear spaces are projective/inductive limits of Hilbert spaces with trace-class transition maps; their tensor products are uniquely defined without completing differently under projective vs. injective topologies.)
	- (Page 52) What is a locale and how is it used in analytic geometry? (Hint: A locale is a lattice/point-free space formalizing the frame of open sets; it avoids point-set pathologies when taking fiber products and spectrum constructions in non-archimedean/complex geometry.)
	- (Page 52) What is the Berkovich spectrum $\mathcal{M}(A)$ in terms of locales? (Hint: For a Banach/liquid algebra $A$, the Berkovich spectrum $\mathcal{M}(A)$ is the compact Hausdorff locale of bounded multiplicative semi-norms on $A$.)
	- (Page 59) How is the categorified locale $S(C)$ defined for a symmetric monoidal category $C$? (Hint: $S(C)$ is the locale whose closed sublocales correspond to idempotent algebras/monoids $A \in C$ such that tensoring with $A$ defines a localization of $C$.)
	- (Page 63) What is an analytic space viewed as a categorified locale? (Hint: A categorified locale $(X, \mathcal{C}_X)$ over $(* , \mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Liq}_p))$ equipped with a structure sheaf of $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-algebras $\mathcal{O}_X := \underline{\mathrm{End}}_{\mathcal{C}_X}(\mathbf{1}_X)$ that is locally equivalent to an affinoid model.)
	- (Page 85) State the liquid GAGA theorem for proper complex schemes. (Hint: For a proper scheme $X$ over $\mathbb{C}$, the analytification functor induces an equivalence between the derived category of coherent algebraic sheaves $\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Coh}}^b(X)$ and the category of discrete/nuclear liquid coherent analytic sheaves $\mathcal{D}_{\mathbf{Coh}}^b(X^{\mathrm{an}})$.)
	- (Page 90) How does the relation "Finite = Compact + Discrete" characterize coherent sheaves? (Hint: In the liquid category, coherent sheaves are precisely the objects that are simultaneously compact (dualizable/finitely presented) and discrete (algebraic), bridging functional analysis and algebraic geometry.)
	- (Page 95) What is a nuclear module in liquid complex geometry? (Hint: A $p$-liquid vector space $V$ isomorphic to a filtered colimit of Banach/Hilbert spaces with nuclear/trace-class transition maps (e.g., spaces of overconvergent holomorphic functions $\mathcal{O}(D)$ on closed discs).)
	- (Page 98) What is a trace-class / nuclear morphism in a closed symmetric monoidal category? (Hint: A morphism $f \colon X \to Y$ is nuclear if it lies in the image of the canonical evaluation map $X^\vee \otimes Y \to \underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(X, Y)$.)
	- (Page 100) What is a dual nuclear Fréchet (DNF) space? (Hint: A condensed vector space that is a sequential filtered colimit of Hilbert spaces $H_0 \to H_1 \to H_2 \to \dots$ along injective transition maps that are of $p$-Schatten class.)
	- (Page 101) What is a $p$-Schatten operator between Hilbert spaces? (Hint: A compact operator $T \colon H_1 \to H_2$ whose singular values $(\sigma_n)_{n \ge 0}$ satisfy $\sum_{n=0}^\infty \sigma_n^p < \infty$.)
	- (Page 102) When is an object in a compactly generated stable $\infty$-category $C$ called discrete? (Hint: An object $M \in C$ is discrete if it lies in the essential image of the base change functor $\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{End}(1)) \to C$ generated by colimits and shifts of the unit object $\mathbf{1}$.)
	- (Page 102) When is an object in $C$ a perfect complex? (Hint: An object $V \in C$ is a perfect complex if and only if it is both compact and discrete, i.e., in the thick subcategory generated by the unit object under finite colimits, shifts, and retracts.)
	- (Page 103) How are compact nuclear objects characterized via trace-class maps? (Hint: An object is compact and nuclear if and only if it is dualizable in the liquid category, which occurs precisely when it is a retract of the fiber/cone of $\mathrm{id} - f \colon V \to V$ for a nuclear operator $f$.)
	- (Page 103) When is a liquid category Fredholm? (Hint: A category is Fredholm if every dualizable object is discrete, ensuring that index theorems and finite-dimensionality of cohomology hold.)
	- (Page 103) What is the precise definition of an affinoid algebra in liquid complex geometry? (Hint: A commutative algebra $A \in \mathbf{CAlg}(\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Liq}_p))$ that is quasi-isomorphic to a quotient of the algebra of overconvergent power series $\mathbb{C}\{T_1, \dots, T_n\}^\dagger$ on a polydisc by a finitely generated ideal.)
	- (Page 105) State the global definition of a complex analytic space. (Hint: A complex analytic space is a categorified locale $(X, \mathcal{C}_X)$ over $(* , \mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Liq}_p))$ that is locally isomorphic to the categorified spectrum $\mathrm{Spec}(A, \mathcal{M}_{<p})$ of an affinoid liquid algebra $A$.)
	- (Page 111) How are open and closed immersions defined for liquid complex analytic spaces? (Hint: An open immersion is a local isomorphism of categorified locales corresponding to restriction to an open sublocale; a closed immersion is a morphism corresponding to a surjective quotient of structure sheaves $A \twoheadrightarrow A/I$ in $\mathbf{Liq}_p$.)

http://cmtp.uniroma2.it/13MQP/slides/Connes.pdf, Introduction to the Spectral Action Principle
	- (Slide 2) How did Newton historically introduce operator-theoretic ideas into mechanics? (Hint: By replacing static geometric variables with fluxions and differential operators acting on functions, anticipating the operational formalism of quantum observables.)
	- (Slide 5) Why does quantum mechanics provide a more subtle answer to the nature of space and the reduction of the wave packet? (Hint: The collapse of the wave packet is an irreversible thermodynamic phenomenon; observables are non-commuting operators on a Hilbert space $\mathcal{H}$, meaning quantum states interact non-trivially with the algebra of observables.)
	- (Slide 8) How does physical time emerge from quantum thermodynamics and thermal equilibrium? (Hint: A thermal equilibrium state at inverse temperature $\beta$ is given by the Gibbs state $\varphi(A) = \frac{\mathrm{Tr}(A e^{-\beta H})}{\mathrm{Tr}(e^{-\beta H})}$; time evolution $A \mapsto \sigma_t(A) = e^{i t H} A e^{-i t H}$ is uniquely determined by the algebraic KMS condition satisfied by $\varphi$.)
	- (Slide 10) State the Kubo-Martin-Schwinger (KMS) condition at inverse temperature $\beta$. (Hint: A faithful state $\varphi$ on a von Neumann algebra $M$ satisfies the $\mathrm{KMS}_\beta$ condition with respect to a 1-parameter automorphism group $\sigma_t$ if for all $x, y \in M$, there exists a bounded holomorphic function $F(z)$ on the strip $0 \le \mathrm{Im}(z) \le \beta$ such that $F(t) = \varphi(x \sigma_t(y))$ and $F(t + i\beta) = \varphi(\sigma_t(y) x)$.)
	- (Slide 12) State the Tomita-Takesaki Theorem for von Neumann algebras. (Hint: Let $M$ be a von Neumann algebra and $\varphi$ a faithful normal state on $M$. There exists a unique 1-parameter group of automorphisms $\sigma_t^\varphi \in \mathrm{Aut}(M)$ (the modular automorphism group) fulfilling the $\mathrm{KMS}_1$ condition with respect to $\varphi$.)
	- (Slide 13) How did Connes' thesis establish that a von Neumann algebra possesses a canonical thermodynamic time evolution? (Hint: Connes proved the Radon-Nikodym cocycle theorem: for any two faithful normal states $\varphi, \psi$, the modular automorphism groups $\sigma_t^\varphi$ and $\sigma_t^\psi$ differ only by inner automorphisms $\mathrm{Ad}(u_t)$, yielding a canonical outer automorphism homomorphism $\delta \colon \mathbb{R} \to \mathrm{Out}(M) = \mathrm{Aut}(M)/\mathrm{Inn}(M)$ independent of the state.)
	- (Slide 15) How is time evolution interpreted algebraically in quantum gravity? (Hint: Time is not an external geometric parameter but the canonical 1-parameter group of outer automorphisms $\delta \colon \mathbb{R} \to \mathrm{Out}(\mathcal{M})$ of the non-commutative von Neumann algebra of gravitational observables.)
	- (Slide 17) Why does the spectrum of the Laplacian fail to characterize a compact Riemannian manifold? (Hint: John Milnor constructed non-isometric 16-dimensional flat tori that are isospectral ($\mathrm{Spec}(\Delta_1) = \mathrm{Spec}(\Delta_2)$), proving that one cannot "hear the shape of a drum" from the eigenvalue spectrum alone.)
	- (Slide 19) What missing operator-theoretic information is needed beyond the spectrum to reconstruct a manifold? (Hint: The relative position (commutation relations) of two abelian von Neumann algebras acting on the Hilbert space $L^2(M)$: the algebra of smooth coordinates $C^\infty(M)$ and the algebra of momentum operators/differential operators generated by the Dirac operator $D$.)
	- (Slide 21) How does von Neumann's theorem characterize the algebra of bounded measurable functions on Hilbert space? (Hint: By von Neumann's uniqueness theorem, the maximal abelian $*$-algebra $L^\infty(M)$ acting by multiplication on $L^2(M)$ is uniquely isomorphic to the standard measure algebra of $M$, containing purely measure-theoretic, non-metric data.)
	- (Slide 23) Why is the Dirac operator $D$ used instead of the scalar Laplacian $\Delta$? (Hint: The Dirac operator $D$ is a first-order differential operator providing a square root of the Laplacian ($D^2 = \Delta + \frac{1}{4}R$ via Lichnerowicz), whose inverse $D^{-1}$ represents the fermion propagator and directly encodes the metric and spin structure.)
	- (Slide 25) What are the CKM invariant, $\mathrm{Spec}(D)$, and the relative spectrum $\mathrm{Spec}_N(M)$? (Hint: $\mathrm{Spec}(D)$ gives the energy levels/frequencies of the system, while the relative spectrum $\mathrm{Spec}_N(M)$ measures the relative modular position of $M$ inside $\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})$, encoding cross-correlations and transition probabilities between frequencies.)
	- (Slide 27) How is the Riemannian geodesic distance defined spectrally in noncommutative geometry? (Hint: Connes' spectral distance formula replaces the infimum of arc lengths by the dual operator formula: $d(p, q) = \sup \{ |f(p) - f(q)| : f \in C^\infty(M), \|[D, f]\| \le 1 \}$, where the infinitesimal line element is $ds = D^{-1}$.)
	- (Slide 29) How does noncommutative geometry describe spacetime unification in the Standard Model? (Hint: Spacetime is modeled as an almost-commutative spectral triple $(\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{H}, D)$ with algebra $\mathcal{A} = C^\infty(M) \otimes \mathcal{A}_F$, where $\mathcal{A}_F = \mathbb{C} \oplus \mathbb{H} \oplus M_3(\mathbb{C})$ corresponds to the finite non-commutative geometry of the Standard Model.)
	- (Slide 31) Why is the group of inner automorphisms $\mathrm{Inn}(\mathcal{A})$ of $\mathcal{A} = C^\infty(M) \otimes M_n(\mathbb{C})$ identified with gauge transformations?
