(Hint: The automorphism group splits as a semi-direct product $\mathrm{Aut}(\mathcal{A}) = \mathrm{Inn}(\mathcal{A}) \rtimes \mathrm{Out}(\mathcal{A})$, where $\mathrm{Out}(\mathcal{A}) \cong \mathrm{Diff}(M)$ gives gravity (diffeomorphisms) and $\mathrm{Inn}(\mathcal{A}) \cong C^\infty(M, \mathrm{SU}(n))$ gives the local internal gauge group.)
	- (Slide 34) How does the shift from 4 to 10 dimensions arise in noncommutative geometry compared to string theory compactifications? (Hint: In string theory, 6 extra dimensions are compactified on an unnaturally small Calabi-Yau manifold; in NCG, the KO-dimension of the finite spectral triple $\mathcal{A}_F$ is $6 \pmod 8$, making the total metric/KO-dimension of spacetime $4 + 6 = 10 \equiv 2 \pmod 8$ without requiring geometric compactification.)
	- (Slide 37) How does the Spectral Action Principle unify gravity with the Standard Model forces? (Hint: The full bosonic action is given by the spectral action $\mathrm{Tr}(f(D_A / \Lambda)) + \frac{1}{2}\langle J\psi, D_A \psi\rangle$, where $D_A = D + A + \epsilon J A J^{-1}$ is the Dirac operator with gauge fluctuations; its asymptotic heat kernel expansion automatically produces the Einstein-Hilbert action, the Yang-Mills gauge actions, and the Higgs potential with unified coupling relations at the unification scale $\Lambda$.)

http://www.tac.mta.ca/tac/volumes/11/4/11-04.pdf, six functor formalisms in Wirthmueller contexts.
	- What is the formal categorical goal of Fausk-Hu-May in axiomatic six-functor formalisms? (Hint: To systematically isolate the formal categorical machinery of the projection formula and duality isomorphisms from specific geometric contexts, showing how right adjoints $f^!$ or $f_*$ can be identified as twists/shifts of left adjoints $f_!$ or $f^*$ via an intrinsic dualizing object.)
	- Why is the right adjoint generally harder to understand than the left adjoint? (Hint: The left adjoint (e.g., free construction, scalar extension, pullback $f^*$, or extension-by-zero $j_!$) is explicitly defined by generators and preserves colimits; its right adjoint (e.g., coextension, pushforward $f_*$, or exceptional pullback $f^!$) is given abstractly via the adjoint functor theorem and requires deep descent/duality theorems to compute.)
	- How do Neeman's techniques establish the formal nature of Verdier and Grothendieck isomorphisms? (Hint: Neeman proved that in triangulated and derived categories with Brown representability, the existence of right adjoints $f^!$ and their duality isomorphisms with twisted left adjoints are purely formal consequences of closed symmetric monoidal structures and compactly generated categories.)
	- How is the standard pullback-pushforward pair $(f^*, f_*)$ structured in a closed symmetric monoidal category? (Hint: Let $f \colon \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ be a geometric morphism; the pullback functor $f^* \colon \mathcal{B} \to \mathcal{A}$ is strong symmetric monoidal ($f^*(Y \otimes_{\mathcal{B}} Z) \cong f^*Y \otimes_{\mathcal{A}} f^*Z$ and $f^*\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{B}} \cong \mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{A}}$), and its right adjoint $f_* \colon \mathcal{A} \to \mathcal{B}$ is lax monoidal with an adjunction $\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{A}}(f^*Y, X) \cong \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{B}}(Y, f_*X)$.)
	- What is the categorical relationship between $(f^*, f_*, f_!, f^!)$ when $f$ is a proper morphism? (Hint: When $f$ is proper, the exceptional pushforward coincides with the ordinary pushforward ($f_! \cong f_*$); consequently, the exceptional pullback $f^!$ is the right adjoint to the right adjoint of pullback, $f^! \colon \mathcal{B} \to \mathcal{A}$ being right adjoint to $f_* \cong f_!$.)
	- What is the Grothendieck context versus the Verdier-Grothendieck context? (Hint: The **Grothendieck context** is an adjoint triple $(f^* \dashv f_* \dashv f^!)$ where $f_*$ has a right adjoint $f^!$ (modeling proper morphisms or global module duality); the **Verdier-Grothendieck context** includes the full four-functor setup $(f_! \dashv f^!)$ and $(f^* \dashv f_*)$ with an isomorphism $f_! \xrightarrow{\sim} f_*$ on proper subcategories.)
	- How do extension and coextension of scalars model $f_!$, $f^*$, and $f_*$ in module categories? (Hint: For a ring homomorphism $\phi \colon R \to S$, the forgetful/restriction functor is $f^*$; its left adjoint is extension of scalars $f_!(M) = S \otimes_R M$ (left adjoint/induction), and its right adjoint is coextension of scalars $f_*(M) = \mathrm{Hom}_R(S, M)$ (right adjoint/coinduction). Notice that here $f^*$ is generally not strong monoidal unless $S$ is flat/étale.)
	- What is the Wirthmüller context and how did it originate in equivariant topology? (Hint: Originating from Wirthmüller's theorem for compact Lie groups $G/H$, a **Wirthmüller context** is an adjoint triple $(f_! \dashv f^* \dashv f_*)$ where the strong symmetric monoidal pullback $f^*$ has both a left adjoint $f_!$ and a right adjoint $f_*$, together with a natural twist isomorphism $f_!(X) \cong f_*(X \otimes D)$ for a dualizing object $D$.)
	- How does strong symmetric monoidality of $f^*$ yield the internal hom adjunction and projection maps? (Hint: Since $f^*$ commutes with $\otimes$, the $(f^* \dashv f_*)$ adjunction formally induces a natural morphism $\pi \colon f_*(X) \otimes Y \to f_*(X \otimes f^*Y)$ and a dual morphism on internal homs $f^* \underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{B}}(Y, Z) \to \underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{A}}(f^*Y, f^*Z)$ via the Barr-Beck/monad comparison apparatus.)
	- State Definition 4.6 of the Projection Formula in the abstract axiomatic context. (Hint: In an adjoint setup $(f^* \dashv f_*)$, the **projection formula** is the assertion that the canonical morphism $\pi \colon f_!(X) \otimes_{\mathcal{B}} Y \xrightarrow{\sim} f_!(X \otimes_{\mathcal{A}} f^*Y)$ is a natural isomorphism for all $X \in \mathcal{A}$ and $Y \in \mathcal{B}$.)
	- When does a Wirthmüller context arise in algebra and representation theory? (Hint: When $f^* \colon \mathbf{Mod}_S \to \mathbf{Mod}_R$ is the restriction of scalars along a Frobenius algebra/finite flat map $\phi \colon R \to S$, where extension of scalars $f_!(-) = S \otimes_R -$ is isomorphic to coextension of scalars $f_*(-) = \mathrm{Hom}_R(S, -)$ up to tensoring with the dualizing module $\omega_{S/R}$.)
	- How does the dualizing object interact with the Chu construction and star-autonomous categories? (Hint: An intrinsic dualizing object $D \in \mathcal{A}$ defines a duality functor $\mathbb{D}(-) = \underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(-, D)$; the Chu construction formally completes a closed symmetric monoidal category into a $*$-autonomous category where the Wirthmüller and Grothendieck contexts become dual via $\mathbb{D} \circ f^* \cong f^! \circ \mathbb{D}$.)
	- Why is the Projection Formula recognized categorically as Frobenius reciprocity? (Hint: The projection formula is the tensor-categorical generalization of classical Frobenius reciprocity $\mathrm{Ind}(V \otimes \mathrm{Res}(W)) \cong \mathrm{Ind}(V) \otimes W$ for representations; categorically, it states that $f_!$ is an enriched $\mathcal{B}$-module functor.)
	- How does the Projection Formula allow moving target objects and computing Reynolds operators/Haar integrals? (Hint: It allows pullbacks of base objects $f^*Y$ to be factored outside the pushforward integration $f_!(f^*Y \otimes X) \cong Y \otimes f_!(X)$; for $X = \mathbf{1}$, $f_!(f^*Y) \cong Y \otimes f_!(\mathbf{1})$, which identifies the pushforward with a generalized Reynolds averaging operator / integration against the Haar measure $f_!(\mathbf{1})$.)

https://arxiv.org/pdf/2010.00681, foundations of categorical measures	
	- What is the primary objective of Jamneshan and Tao's framework for uncountable measure theory? (Hint: To develop measure theory, functional analysis, and ergodic theory without countability, separability, metrizability, or standard Borel/Lebesgue hypotheses, using category-theoretic dualities and canonical models.)
	- How does the Gelfand duality operate between compact Hausdorff spaces and commutative C*-algebras? (Hint: The functor $C \colon \mathbf{CH} \to \mathbf{uCC^*Alg}^{\mathrm{op}}$ sending $X \mapsto C(X)$ is an equivalence of categories, with quasi-inverse spectrum functor $\mathrm{Spec} \colon \mathbf{uCC^*Alg}^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{CH}$ sending $A \mapsto \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{uCC^*Alg}}(A, \mathbb{C})$.)
	- How is Gelfand duality extended to locally compact Hausdorff (LCH) spaces and non-unital C*-algebras? (Hint: The functor $C_0 \colon \mathbf{LCH}_p \to \mathbf{CC^*Alg}_{nd}^{\mathrm{op}}$ (proper continuous maps and non-degenerate $*$-homomorphisms) is an equivalence; for non-proper continuous maps, morphisms map into the multiplier algebra $M(A)$.)
	- What are the minimal and maximal compactifications of an LCH space in this categorical framework? (Hint: The Alexandroff one-point compactification $X^+$ is the minimal compactification corresponding to the unitization $A^+ = C_0(X) \oplus \mathbb{C}$, while the Stone-Čech compactification $\beta X$ is the maximal compactification corresponding to the multiplier algebra $M(C_0(X)) = C_b(X)$.)
	- Why does uncountable measure theory adopt a Baire-centric rather than Borel-centric philosophy? (Hint: In non-metrizable spaces, the Borel $\sigma$-algebra is too large and points/compact sets may fail to be $G_\delta$; the Baire $\sigma$-algebras $C_0\text{-}\mathrm{Baire}(X)$ and $C_b\text{-}\mathrm{Baire}(X)$ are generated by continuous functions and interact naturally with functional analysis.)
	- How is a Radon probability measure defined via inner regularity on an LCH space? (Hint: A probability measure $\mu$ on $C_0\text{-}\mathrm{Baire}(X)$ or $\mathrm{Borel}(X)$ is Radon if it is inner regular with respect to compact $G_\delta$ sets, meaning $\mu(E) = \sup \{ \mu(K) : K \subset E, K \text{ compact } G_\delta \}$.)
	- What is $\tau$-additivity ($\tau$-smoothness) and why does it eliminate pathological measure behavior? (Hint: A measure $\mu$ is $\tau$-additive if $\mu(\bigcup U_\alpha) = \sup \mu(U_\alpha)$ for every upward-directed net of open sets; on Baire sets, $\tau$-smoothness corresponds to $\lim \lambda(f_\alpha) = 0$ for decreasing nets $f_\alpha \downarrow 0$, making Radon hypotheses automatic on compact spaces.)
	- How do Riesz dualities augment Gelfand dualities in the presence of measures and traces? (Hint: The Riesz representation theorem establishes an equivalence between categories of compact/LCH probability spaces $(\mathbf{CHPrb}, \mathbf{LCHPrb})$ with Radon measures and categories of tracial commutative $C^*$-algebras $(\mathbf{uCC^*Alg}^\tau, \mathbf{CC^*Alg}^\tau)$ equipped with faithful positive states/traces.)
	- What is an abstract probability algebra and what is the abstraction functor? (Hint: A probability algebra is a pair $(\mathcal{B}, \mu)$ where $\mathcal{B}$ is a Dedekind $\sigma$-complete Boolean algebra and $\mu$ is a strictly positive, countably additive measure; the abstraction functor $\mathbf{Alg} \colon \mathbf{CncPrb} \to \mathbf{PrbAlg}$ quotients out the ideal of null sets $\Sigma_X / \mathcal{N}_\mu$.)
	- How is the category of probability algebras dual to commutative tracial von Neumann algebras? (Hint: By probability duality, $\mathbf{PrbAlg}$ is equivalent to $(\mathbf{CvNAlg}^\tau)^{\mathrm{op}}$ via the functor assigning $(\mathcal{B}, \mu)$ to its $L^\infty(\mathcal{B}, \mu)$ space equipped with the integral trace $\tau(f) = \int f\, d\mu$.)
	- What is the canonical model functor $\mathtt{Conc} \colon \mathbf{PrbAlg} \to \mathbf{CHPrb}$? (Hint: Defined by $\mathtt{Conc}(X) := \mathtt{Riesz}(L^\infty(X)_{\mathbf{uCC^*Alg}^\tau}) = \mathrm{Spec}(L^\infty(X))$; it assigns to every abstract probability algebra $X$ an extremally disconnected compact Hausdorff Stone probability space modeling $X$.)
	- What universal properties does the canonical model $\mathtt{Conc}(X)$ satisfy? (Hint: $\mathtt{Conc}(X)$ is an extremally disconnected Stone space satisfying the strong Lusin property (every bounded Baire-measurable function equals a unique continuous function almost everywhere) and acts as a universal concrete representative for $X$ analogous to the Stone-Čech compactification.)
	- How does the canonical model provide canonical disintegration of probability measures? (Hint: Given a morphism of probability algebras $T \colon Y \to X$, the functoriality of $\mathtt{Conc}$ induces a unique continuous measure-preserving map $\pi \colon \mathtt{Conc}(X) \to \mathtt{Conc}(Y)$ whose fiber measures $y \mapsto \mu_y$ provide a canonical, point-everywhere defined disintegration without exceptional null sets.)
	- How does the Loomis-Sikorski theorem realize abstract $\sigma$-algebras as concrete quotients? (Hint: The Loomis-Sikorski functor $\mathtt{LS} = \mathtt{Meager} \circ \mathtt{Stone}$ represents any abstract $\sigma$-algebra $\mathcal{B}$ as the concrete quotient $\mathrm{Baire}(S(\mathcal{B})) / \mathcal{M}$, where $S(\mathcal{B})$ is the Stone space and $\mathcal{M}$ is the $\sigma$-ideal of meager Baire sets.)
	- How does the Loomis-Sikorski theorem yield an abstract Kolmogorov Extension Theorem? (Hint: Combining Stone duality with the Riesz representation theorem on product Stone spaces allows projective systems of probability algebras to admit projective limits without requiring Polish or topological regularity assumptions.)
	- How does the Baire Category Theorem provide a qualitative analogue of measure theory? (Hint: BCT implies that in LCH or completely metrizable spaces, meager sets (countable unions of nowhere dense sets) model "negligible/null sets" and comeager sets model "full measure sets", preserving the intuition that countable intersections of dense open sets remain dense.)

https://opus.bibliothek.uni-augsburg.de/opus4/files/42513/notes.pdf, Blechschmidt's thesis and slides
	- (Page 11) Compute the internal meaning of a sheaf of $\mathcal{O}_X$-modules to be of finite type. (Hint: An $\mathcal{O}_X$-module $\mathcal{F}$ is locally of finite type externally if and only if internally in $\mathbf{Sh}(X)$ it is a finitely generated $\mathcal{O}_X$-module: $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \exists n \in \mathbb{N},\, \exists x_1, \dots, x_n \in \mathcal{F},\, \forall x \in \mathcal{F},\, \exists a_1, \dots, a_n \in \mathcal{O}_X,\, x = \sum_{i=1}^n a_i x_i$.)
	- (Page 11) Compute using a commutative algebra fact the finite generation of the middle term in a short exact sequence of modules, and deduce the sheaf version. (Hint: In ordinary algebra, if $0 \to M' \to M \to M'' \to 0$ is exact and $M', M''$ are finitely generated, then $M$ is finitely generated; since the internal logic of $\mathbf{Sh}(X)$ validates constructive module algebra, if $\mathcal{F}'$ and $\mathcal{F}''$ are locally of finite type, then $\mathcal{F}$ is locally of finite type.)
	- (Page 11) Compute the external meaning of a plain ring internal to $\mathbf{Sh}(X)$. (Hint: The structure sheaf of rings $\mathcal{O}_X$ itself.)
	- (Page 12) Compute the functor of points from $A$ to the category of Sets. (Hint: The functor $h_A = \mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{CAlg}}(A, -) \colon \mathbf{CAlg} \to \mathbf{Set}$, evaluating affine schemes on test algebras.)
	- (Page 12) Compute the internal meaning of Grothendieck's generic freeness lemma: $\mathcal{F}$ is locally free on some dense open subset. (Hint: Any finitely generated vector space over a field is not not free: $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \neg\neg(\mathcal{F} \text{ is free})$, since double negation $\neg\neg$ corresponds externally to validity on a dense open subset.)
	- (Page 12) Compute the modal translation of a statement $\phi$, denoted $\phi^\square$. (Hint: $\phi^\square$ means the evaluation/validity of $\phi$ over all stalks/germs or over every open neighborhood of a point.)
	- (Page 12) Compute the modal translation of the statement $\square\phi$. (Hint: $\square\phi$ means $\phi$ holds over an open neighborhood of the given locus/point.)
	- (Warm Up) Compute the hierarchy of points, infinitesimals, formals, germs/local functions/stalkwise, open neighborhoods of finite sets. (Hint: Points $\subset$ Infinitesimals $\mathbb{D} = \mathrm{Spec}(k[\epsilon]/\epsilon^2) \subset$ Formal neighborhoods $\mathrm{Spf}(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{X, x}) \subset$ Germs/stalks $\mathcal{O}_{X, x} = \varinjlim_{U \ni x} \mathcal{O}_X(U) \subset$ Étale/open neighborhoods $U \subset X$.)
	- (Page 13) Compute the internalisation of an affine line. (Hint: The affine line $\mathbb{A}^1$ is the internal scalar field/ring object $\mathcal{O}_X$ in $\mathbf{Zar}(S)$.)
	- (Page 13) Compute the parameter space of the irreducible closed subsets using the classical set theory approach to schemes. (Hint: The topological space of points of $\mathrm{Spec}(A)$, where closed points correspond to maximal ideals and generic points correspond to non-maximal prime ideals.)
	- (Page 14) Compute the equaliser diagram of a cover via a functor as the external logic. (Hint: For an open cover $U = \coprod U_i \to X$, the sheaf condition is the equalizer $\mathcal{F}(X) \to \prod_i \mathcal{F}(U_i) \rightrightarrows \prod_{i, j} \mathcal{F}(U_i \cap U_j)$.)
	- (Page 14) Construct the big Zariski topos using ideas from this condition. (Hint: The big Zariski topos $\mathbf{Zar}(S) = \mathbf{Sh}(\mathbf{Sch}/S, \text{Zariski})$ is the topos of sheaves on the category of all $S$-schemes with the Zariski Grothendieck topology.)
	- (Page 14) Compute the value of the object $\mathbb{A}^1$ on an $X$-scheme, its ring object structure in $\mathbf{Zar}(X)$, and its internal meaning. (Hint: Externally, $\mathbb{A}^1(Y) = \Gamma(Y, \mathcal{O}_Y)$; as a ring object it is the affine line scheme $\mathbb{A}_X^1$; internally, $\mathbb{A}^1$ is the base field/continuum of scalars.)
	- (Page 15) Compute the synthetic spectrum of an $\mathbb{A}_X^1$-algebra $A$. (Hint: $\mathrm{Spec}_{\mathrm{synth}}(A) = \underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbf{CAlg}(\mathbb{A}^1)}(A, \mathbb{A}^1)$, the internal scheme/space of homomorphisms into the base line.)
	- (Page 15) Compute the internalisation of a sheaf of modules to be quasi-coherent. (Hint: An $\mathcal{O}_X$-module $\mathcal{F}$ is quasi-coherent iff internally it is presented as the cokernel of a morphism between free $\mathcal{O}_X$-modules: $\mathcal{O}_X^{\oplus I} \to \mathcal{O}_X^{\oplus J} \to \mathcal{F} \to 0$.)
	- (Page 15) Compute the implication of a scheme with internal language having a weak form of disjunction property in mathematical logic. (Hint: If $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \phi \lor \psi$ and $X$ is connected, then either $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \phi$ or $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \psi$; for disconnected schemes it forces a partition of $X$ into open loci where each disjunct holds.)
	- (Page 16) Compute the external meaning of $[X, Y]$ (the internal mapping object). (Hint: The sheaf of local morphisms $\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(X, Y)$ whose sections over $U$ are $\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{Sh}(U)}(X|_U, Y|_U)$.)
	- (Page 17) Compute the separator of the category of Sets. (Hint: The singleton set $1 = \{*\}$, since $\mathrm{Hom}(1, X) \cong X$ distinguishes all elements and morphisms.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash (s = t)$ for sections $s, t \in \mathcal{F}$. (Hint: $s|_U = t|_U \in \mathcal{F}(U)$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash (s \in \mathcal{G})$ for a subsheaf $\mathcal{G} \subset \mathcal{F}$. (Hint: $s|_U \in \mathcal{G}(U) \subset \mathcal{F}(U)$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash \top$. (Hint: Always holds for any open $U \subset X$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash \bot$. (Hint: Holds if and only if $U = \emptyset$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the internal meaning of $U \Vdash \bigwedge_{j \in J} \phi_j$. (Hint: $U \Vdash \phi_j$ holds for all $j \in J$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash \phi$ for $U = \bigcup_i U_i$. (Hint: $U \Vdash \phi \iff \forall i,\, U_i \Vdash \phi$ (sheaf locality).)
	- (Page 19) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash \bigvee_{j \in J} \phi_j$. (Hint: There exists an open cover $U = \bigcup_{i} U_i$ and a choice $j(i) \in J$ such that $U_i \Vdash \phi_{j(i)}$ for each $i$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash (\phi \implies \psi)$. (Hint: For all open subsets $V \subset U$, if $V \Vdash \phi$ then $V \Vdash \psi$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the meaning of $U \Vdash \forall s \in \mathcal{F},\, \phi(s)$. (Hint: For all open $V \subset U$ and all sections $s \in \mathcal{F}(V)$, $V \Vdash \phi(s)$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the meaning of $U \Vdash \exists s \in \mathcal{F},\, \phi(s)$. (Hint: There exists an open cover $U = \bigcup_i U_i$ and sections $s_i \in \mathcal{F}(U_i)$ such that $U_i \Vdash \phi(s_i)$ for all $i$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the meaning of $U \Vdash \forall \mathcal{F},\, \phi(\mathcal{F})$. (Hint: For all open $V \subset U$ and all sheaves $\mathcal{F}$ over $V$, $V \Vdash \phi(\mathcal{F})$.)
	- (Page 19) Compute the meaning of $U \Vdash \exists \mathcal{F},\, \phi(\mathcal{F})$. (Hint: There exists an open cover $U = \bigcup_i U_i$ and sheaves $\mathcal{F}_i \in \mathbf{Sh}(U_i)$ such that $U_i \Vdash \phi(\mathcal{F}_i)$.)
	- (Page 20) Compute the external meaning of $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \mathcal{F} \text{ is finite free}$. (Hint: $\mathcal{F}$ is locally free of finite rank: there exists an open cover $X = \bigcup_i U_i$ such that $\mathcal{F}|_{U_i} \cong \mathcal{O}_{U_i}^{n_i}$ for $n_i \in \mathbb{N}$.)
	- (Page 20) Compute the external meaning of $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \neg\neg(\mathcal{F} \text{ is finite free})$. (Hint: There exists a dense open subset $U \subset X$ such that $\mathcal{F}|_U$ is locally free of finite rank.)
	- (Page 21) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash \forall s \in \mathcal{F},\, \forall t \in \mathcal{G},\, \phi(s, t)$. (Hint: Step 1: Germ equivalence; Step 2: For all open $V \subset U$; Step 3: For all $s \in \mathcal{F}(V)$ and $t \in \mathcal{G}(V)$, $V \Vdash \phi(s, t)$.)
	- (Page 21) Compute the internalisation of $\phi$ holding nowhere. (Hint: $X \Vdash \neg\phi$, equivalently $\phi \implies \bot$.)
	- (Page 21) Compute the externalisation of "$\mathbf{Sh}(X) \not\models (f \in \mathcal{O}_X^\times)$" vs. "$\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \neg(f \in \mathcal{O}_X^\times)$". (Hint: $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \not\models (f \in \mathcal{O}_X^\times)$ means $f$ is not globally invertible; $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \neg(f \in \mathcal{O}_X^\times)$ means $f$ is nowhere invertible (i.e., $f$ vanishes on all stalks / is nilpotent on reduced schemes).)
	- (Page 21) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash \forall s \in \mathcal{F},\, (\phi(s) \implies \psi(s))$. (Hint: For all open $V \subset U$ and all $s \in \mathcal{F}(V)$, if $V \Vdash \phi(s)$ then $V \Vdash \psi(s)$.)
	- (Page 21) Compute the external meaning of $U \Vdash \exists ! s \in \mathcal{F},\, \phi(s)$. (Hint: There exists a unique global section $s \in \mathcal{F}(U)$ such that $U \Vdash \phi(s)$.)
	- (Page 21) Compare the strength of $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \neg\phi$ vs. $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \not\models \phi$, and identify when they are equivalent. (Hint: $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \neg\phi$ implies $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \not\models \phi$ (for $X \neq \emptyset$); the converse holds if $X$ is a point $*$, where $\mathbf{Sh}(*) \simeq \mathbf{Set}$ is Boolean.)
	- (Page 21) Compute the isomorphism under $X = *$ (one-point space) with constant sheaves. (Hint: $\mathbf{Sh}(*) \simeq \mathbf{Set}$, recovering standard classical logic and set theory.)
	- (Page 21) Compute the external meaning of categorical products and coproducts in $\mathbf{Sh}(X)$. (Hint: Products are computed sectionwise $\left(\prod \mathcal{F}_i\right)(U) = \prod \mathcal{F}_i(U)$; coproducts require sheafifying the presheaf coproduct $U \mapsto \coprod \mathcal{F}_i(U)$.)
	- (Page 22) Compute the power set construction $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})$ of a sheaf. (Hint: $\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{F})(U)$ is the set of all subsheaves of $\mathcal{F}|_U$ on $U$, isomorphic to $\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbf{Sh}(U)}(\mathcal{F}|_U, \Omega|_U)$ where $\Omega(U) = \mathcal{O}(U)$.)
	- (Page 22) Compute for a formula $\phi(s)$ with free variable $s \in \mathcal{F}$ whether $U \mapsto \{s \in \mathcal{F}(U) : U \Vdash \phi(s)\}$ is a sheaf. (Hint: Yes, it is automatically a subsheaf of $\mathcal{F}$ due to the locality and gluing axioms of Kripke-Joyal semantics.)
	- (Page 22) State the Principle of Unique Choice in topoi and its application to morphisms. (Hint: If $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \forall s \in \mathcal{F},\, \exists ! t \in \mathcal{G},\, \phi(s, t)$, there exists a unique morphism of sheaves $\alpha \colon \mathcal{F} \to \mathcal{G}$ such that $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models \forall s,\, \phi(s, \alpha(s))$; two sheaves are isomorphic if there is an internally bijective relation between them.)
	- (Page 22) Why are geometric morphisms and geometric formulas $(\exists, \top, \bot, \wedge, \bigvee)$ preserved under pullbacks? (Hint: Because inverse image functors $f^*$ preserve all finite limits and all arbitrary colimits (hence preserve $\exists, \top, \bot, \wedge, \bigvee$).)
	- (Page 22/23) Compute the stalkwise substitution of a formula and state the condition for a geometric implication to hold. (Hint: For a geometric implication $\phi \implies \psi$, $\mathbf{Sh}(X) \models (\phi \implies \psi) \iff \forall x \in X,\, \mathcal{F}_x \models (\phi_x \implies \psi_x)$.)
	- (Page 24) Infer a standard sheaf fact from injectivity and surjectivity being geometric implications. (Hint: A morphism of sheaves $f \colon \mathcal{F} \to \mathcal{G}$ is injective (resp. surjective) if and only if the induced map on stalks $f_x \colon \mathcal{F}_x \to \mathcal{G}_x$ is injective (resp. surjective) for all $x \in X$.)
	- (Page 24) Why are arbitrary products $\prod$, power sets $\mathcal{P}$, and internal homs $\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}$ not geometric? (Hint: Because inverse image functors $f^*$ do not generally preserve infinite products, power objects, or internal homs.)
	- (Page 24) Is finite product $\mathcal{F} \times \mathcal{G}$ geometric? (Hint: Yes, $f^*$ preserves finite limits.)
	- (Page 24) Is finite coproduct $\mathcal{F} \amalg \mathcal{G}$ geometric? (Hint: Yes, $f^*$ preserves finite colimits.)
	- (Page 24) Is arbitrary coproduct $\coprod_{i \in I} \mathcal{F}_i$ geometric? (Hint: Yes, $f^*$ commutes with all colimits.)
	- (Page 24) Is set comprehension $\{x \in \mathcal{F} : \phi(x)\}$ for geometric $\phi$ geometric? (Hint: Yes, it is formed by equalizers and pullbacks of geometric formulas.)
	- (Page 24) Is the condition of being a free module geometric? (Hint: No, because freeness involves universal quantification $\forall$ over linear combinations and non-geometric infinite products.)
	- (Page 24) Is the localization of a module geometric? (Hint: Yes, localization $S^{-1}M$ is a filtered colimit of module operations, which commutes with $f^*$.)
	- (Page 24) Does the failure of both $p$ and $q$ determine which one holds intuitionistically? (Hint: No; intuitionistically $\neg(p \land q)$ does not imply $\neg p \lor \neg q$ without the Law of Excluded Middle.)
	- (Page 25) Compute the geometric meaning of double negation $\neg\neg$ in Kripke-Joyal semantics. (Hint: $U \Vdash \neg\neg\phi$ iff $\phi$ holds on a dense open subset of $U$, because $\neg\neg U = \mathrm{Int}(\overline{U}) = X \iff U$ is dense.)
	- (Page 25) What happens if every open $U \subset X$ has no proper dense open subsets (e.g., $X$ is a 0-dimensional Noetherian/irreducible scheme)? (Hint: The double negation modality collapses ($\neg\neg\phi \iff \phi$), the internal logic becomes Boolean, and existential/disjunctive statements hold globally without passing to covers.)
	- (Page 25) How does Barr's Theorem justify using the Axiom of Choice to prove geometric statements intuitionistically (e.g., Nullstellensatz)? (Hint: Barr's Theorem states that if a geometric implication is provable in classical logic with Choice, it is provable intuitionistically without Choice, making classical proofs of the Nullstellensatz internally valid.)

https://arxiv.org/pdf/2510.26269, Scholze's six functor formalisms (mini)
	- What are the classical analogues of the six operations in Schapira's warm-up of ordinary functions? (Hint: The tensor product $\otimes$ corresponds to the pointwise product of functions $(f \cdot g)(x) = f(x)g(x)$; the pullback $f^*$ corresponds to precomposition $f^*(g) = g \circ f$; the compactly supported pushforward $f_!$ corresponds to integration along fibers $\int_{f^{-1}(y)} \phi(x) dx$.)
	- How does the exceptional pullback $f^!$ arise from Poincaré duality in Schapira's warm-up? (Hint: Poincaré duality identifies homology (integration/compact supports) with cohomology; the exceptional pullback $f^!$ is the right adjoint to derived pushforward with compact supports $f_!$, serving as the pullback on distribution/dualizing complexes that exists only in derived categories.)
	- How is proper base change formulated for a Cartesian diagram in étale cohomology? (Hint: For a Cartesian square with $f \colon X \to Y$ proper and $g \colon Y' \to Y$, the base change morphism $g^* f_* \xrightarrow{\sim} f'_* g'^*$ is a natural isomorphism in $D_{\text{ét}}(Y', \Lambda)$.)
	- What is one concrete setting for the geometric category $\mathcal{C}$ and class of admissible maps $E$? (Hint: The category of $p$-adic analytic spaces / $p$-adic Lie groups, or schemes with $E$ being morphisms of finite type / compactifiable morphisms.)
	- What is the right adjoint to the lax symmetric monoidal tensor functor $\otimes \colon \mathcal{D}(X) \times \mathcal{D}(X) \to \mathcal{D}(X)$? (Hint: The internal hom functor $\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(-, -) \colon \mathcal{D}(X)^{\mathrm{op}} \times \mathcal{D}(X) \to \mathcal{D}(X)$.)
	- How is the projection formula formulated in étale cohomology? (Hint: For any morphism $f \colon X \to Y$, $A \in D(X)$, and $B \in D(Y)$, the canonical map $f_!(A \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} f^*B) \xrightarrow{\sim} f_!(A) \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} B$ is an isomorphism in $D(Y)$.)
	- What are the objects and morphisms in the category of correspondences $\mathrm{Corr}(\mathcal{C}, E)$? (Hint: Objects are objects of $\mathcal{C}$; a morphism from $X$ to $Y$ is a correspondence diagram $X \xleftarrow{g} W \xrightarrow{f} Y$ where the forward arrow $f$ belongs to the distinguished class $E$.)
	- Where must the backward and forward arrows belong in a correspondence $X \leftarrow W \to Y$ in $\mathrm{Corr}(\mathcal{C}, E)$? (Hint: The backward arrow $g \colon W \to X$ is an arbitrary morphism in $\mathcal{C}$ (inducing pullback $g^*$), while the forward arrow $f \colon W \to Y$ must belong to the class $E$ (inducing pushforward $f_!$).)
	- Why is the category of correspondences $\mathrm{Corr}(\mathcal{C}, E)$ endowed with a symmetric monoidal structure via Cartesian products $X \times Y$? (Hint: Cartesian products define symmetric monoidal structures on both $\mathcal{C}$ and its correspondences, ensuring the assignment $X \mapsto \mathcal{D}(X)$ is a symmetric monoidal functor preserving external products $\mathcal{D}(X) \boxtimes \mathcal{D}(Y) \simeq \mathcal{D}(X \times Y)$.)
	- How is the composition of two correspondences defined in $\mathrm{Corr}(\mathcal{C}, E)$? (Hint: Given $X \xleftarrow{g} W \xrightarrow{f} Y$ and $Y \xleftarrow{g'} Z \xrightarrow{f'} T$, their composite is formed by the pullback/fiber product $W \times_Y Z$ in the middle: $X \leftarrow W \times_Y Z \to T$, where $W \times_Y Z \to T$ remains in $E$ because $E$ is stable under base change and composition.)
	- How are derived global sections related to the constant sheaf $\underline{\mathbb{Z}}$? (Hint: The derived global section functor is $\mathbf{R}\Gamma(X, -) := (a_X)_*(-)$; evaluating on the constant sheaf $\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_X = a_X^*(\mathbb{Z})$ computes the Betti/cohomology complex $\mathbf{R}\Gamma(X, \underline{\mathbb{Z}}_X)$, whose homology groups give $H^i(X, \mathbb{Z})$.)
	- What functor provides functorial composition in $\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{Ab})$ and what is its right adjoint? (Hint: The derived tensor product $-\otimes^{\mathbf{L}} -$ provides composition/monoidal product; its right adjoint is the derived internal hom $\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(-, -)$.)
	- How is the stalk $(f_* A)_y$ computed via proper base change for a proper map $f \colon X \to Y$? (Hint: Let $i_y \colon \{y\} \hookrightarrow Y$ and $i_{X_y} \colon X_y = X \times_Y \{y\} \hookrightarrow X$; proper base change gives $(f_* A)_y \cong i_y^* f_* A \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathbf{R}\Gamma(X_y, i_{X_y}^* A)$.)
	- What is the natural proper base change isomorphism for a Cartesian square $X' \xrightarrow{g'} X$, $X \xrightarrow{f} Y$, $Y' \xrightarrow{g} Y$, $X' \xrightarrow{f'} Y'$? (Hint: $g^* f_* \xrightarrow{\sim} f'_* g'^*$ (or $g^* f_! \xrightarrow{\sim} f'_! g'^*$ in general).)
	- How is the derived tensor product computed via right derived functors under $\mathbb{Z}$-coefficients? (Hint: Via the derived tensor product $\mathcal{F} \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} \mathcal{G}$, which resolves sheaves by flat/projective complexes and satisfies $\mathbf{R}\Gamma(X, \mathcal{F} \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} \mathcal{G})$ via the Künneth formula.)
	- What is the right adjoint to $-\otimes^{\mathbf{L}} \mathcal{F}$ in $\mathcal{D}(X)$? (Hint: The derived internal hom $\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{F}, -)$.)
	- How is $f_!(A \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} f^*B) \cong f_!(A) \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} B$ deduced using the projection formula? (Hint: By identifying $\mathcal{D}(X)$ as a module category over $\mathcal{D}(Y)$ via the symmetric monoidal structure of the correspondence functor $f_!$.)
	- How is the dual of derived global sections $\mathbf{R}\Gamma(X, \underline{\mathbb{Z}}_X)^\vee$ computed via Poincaré-Verdier duality? (Hint: $\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathbf{R}\Gamma_c(X, \underline{\mathbb{Z}}), \mathbb{Z}) \cong \mathbf{R}\Gamma(X, \omega_X)$, where $\omega_X = a_X^! \mathbb{Z}$ is the dualizing complex.)
	- How is the right adjoint $f^!$ of a smooth manifold bundle $f \colon X \to Y$ computed locally? (Hint: Locally, $X \cong Y \times \mathbb{R}^d$, so $f^! B \cong f^*B \otimes \omega_{X/Y} \cong f^*B[d]$ by the Thom isomorphism / Poincaré duality.)
	- How is $\mathcal{D}(X, \mathbb{Z})$ related to Betti cohomology for a finite-dimensional LCH space $X$? (Hint: $\mathcal{D}(X, \mathbb{Z})$ is the derived category of sheaves of $\mathbb{Z}$-modules on $X$; its morphisms $\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(X)}(\underline{\mathbb{Z}}_X, \underline{\mathbb{Z}}_X[i])$ compute the classical singular/Betti cohomology $H^i(X, \mathbb{Z})$.)
	- What conditions are captured by the functor $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{CMon}(\mathbf{Cat}_\infty)$? (Hint: It assigns to each space $X$ a symmetric monoidal $\infty$-category $(\mathcal{D}(X), \otimes, \mathbf{1})$ with functorial pullbacks $f^*$ compatible with symmetric monoidal structures.)
	- What is the central coherence difficulty in defining a six-functor formalism? (Hint: Ensuring that all natural isomorphisms (base change, projection formula, unit/associativity constraints, and adjoint equivalences) satisfy higher coherence homotopies without conflicting cocycle choices.)
	- What are the objects and morphisms of the simplex category $\mathbf{\Delta}$? (Hint: Objects are non-empty finite linearly ordered sets $[n] = \{0, 1, \dots, n\}$ for $n \ge 0$; morphisms are order-preserving maps $f \colon [n] \to [m]$.)
	- What is the geometric interpretation of the 2-simplices $\Delta^2$ in an $\infty$-category? (Hint: A 2-simplex represents a composable pair of 1-morphisms $f \colon x \to y$ and $g \colon y \to z$ together with a specified composite $h \colon x \to z$ and a 2-morphism/homotopy $\alpha \colon g \circ f \Rightarrow h$.)
	- What is the coproduct $\Delta^1 \amalg_{\Delta^0} \Delta^1$ and what does it naturally embed into? (Hint: The boundary spine $\Lambda_1^2 \subset \Delta^2$ consisting of two composable edges $\{0 \to 1\}$ and $\{1 \to 2\}$.)
	- What lifting property defines a trivial Kan fibration and what is its prototypical example? (Hint: A map $p \colon X \to Y$ having the right lifting property with respect to all boundary inclusions $\partial\Delta^n \hookrightarrow \Delta^n$; the prototypical example is the evaluation/projection map from a space of paths/equivalences.)
	- How is an ordinary simplicial category $\mathcal{D}$ mapped to an $\infty$-category $\mathcal{C}$? (Hint: Via the simplicial nerve functor $\mathrm{N}_{\Delta}(\mathcal{D}) \in \mathbf{sSet}$, which is an $\infty$-category (quasicategory) if $\mathcal{D}$ is fibrant.)
	- What is the horn filling condition for a Kan complex $C$? (Hint: Every horn inclusion $\Lambda_k^n \hookrightarrow \Delta^n$ ($0 \le k \le n$) admits a filler $\Delta^n \to C$, ensuring all morphisms are invertible up to homotopy.)
	- How is the space of projective $R$-modules represented in $p$-adic $K$-theory? (Hint: As the groupoid/classifying space $\mathbf{Proj}_R \simeq \coprod_{[P]} B\mathrm{Aut}_R(P)$, decomposed as a disjoint union of classifying spaces of automorphism groups over isomorphism classes of finitely generated projective $R$-modules.)
	- How is $\mathbf{Proj}_R$ viewed as a commutative $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-monoid in spaces? (Hint: The direct sum of projective modules $\oplus$ endows $\mathbf{Proj}_R$ with the structure of an $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-space ($\mathbb{E}_\infty$-monoid in the $\infty$-category of spaces $\mathcal{S}$).)
	- What is a space in the context of $\infty$-categories? (Hint: A Kan simplicial set / $\infty$-groupoid in the $\infty$-category $\mathcal{S} \simeq \mathbf{sSet}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]$.)
	- How is algebraic $K$-theory $K(R)$ defined as an $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-group completion? (Hint: $K(R)$ is the $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-group completion (left adjoint to the forgetful functor from $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-groups to $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-monoids) of $\mathbf{Proj}_R$, whose homotopy groups are $\pi_i K(R) = K_i(R)$.)
	- State the Thomason-Trobaugh theorem on $K$-theory as a sheaf of spaces. (Hint: The presheaf $X \mapsto K(X) = K(\mathbf{Perf}(X))$ satisfies Nisnevich descent and Zariski descent on qcqs schemes, but generally fails étale descent.)
	- How is the $K$-theory of a scheme defined via derived global sections? (Hint: $K(X) \simeq \mathbf{R}\Gamma(X_{\mathrm{Zar}}, \mathcal{K}) \simeq K(\mathbf{Perf}(X))$; its étale version is the étale hypersheafification $K_{\text{ét}}(X) \simeq \mathbf{R}\Gamma(X_{\text{ét}}, \mathcal{K})$.)
	- How is the $\ell$-adic completion of an $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-ring spectrum $A$ constructed? (Hint: By taking the homotopy limit $A_{\ell}^\wedge := \varprojlim_n (A \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} \mathbb{Z}/\ell^n\mathbb{Z})$ over powers $\ell^n$.)
	- What are the homotopy groups of the $\ell$-adic completed $K$-theory of a strictly Henselian local ring $R$ (with $\ell \in R^\times$)? (Hint: Gabber-Suslin rigidity shows $\pi_i K(R)_{\ell}^\wedge \cong \pi_i K(k)_{\ell}^\wedge \cong \mathbb{Z}_\ell(j)$ for $i = 2j$ even, and $0$ for $i$ odd (matching topological complex $K$-theory).)
	- What is the local-to-global spectral sequence relating étale cohomology to $\ell$-adic $K$-theory? (Hint: The Quillen-Lichtenbaum / motivic spectral sequence $E_2^{p, q} = H^{p-q}(X_{\text{ét}}, \mathbb{Z}_\ell(-q)) \implies \pi_{-(p+q)} K(X)_{\ell}^\wedge$ for $p \le q \le 0$.)
	- How is a six-functor formalism encoded as a lax symmetric monoidal functor on correspondences? (Hint: As a lax symmetric monoidal $\infty$-functor $\mathcal{D} \colon \mathrm{Corr}(\mathcal{C}, E) \to \mathbf{Cat}_\infty$, where 1-morphisms encode $(g^*, f_!)$, right adjoints produce $f^!$ and $g_*$, and monoidality encodes the projection formula and Künneth isomorphisms.)
	- How do Grothendieck's Cartesian fibrations and Lurie's straightening/unstraightening equivalence work? (Hint: The straightening/unstraightening theorem provides an equivalence of $\infty$-categories between Cartesian/coCartesian fibrations over $\mathcal{C}$ and functors $\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{Cat}_\infty$ (resp. $\mathcal{C} \to \mathbf{Cat}_\infty$).)
	- How is a symmetric monoidal $\infty$-category defined via coCartesian fibrations over $\mathbf{Fin}_*$? (Hint: As a coCartesian fibration $C^\otimes \to \mathbf{Fin}_*$ such that for every finite pointed set $\langle n \rangle$, the induced maps $\rho^i \colon \langle n \rangle \to \langle 1 \rangle$ induce an equivalence $C_{\langle n \rangle}^\otimes \xrightarrow{\sim} (C_{\langle 1 \rangle}^\otimes)^n$.)
	- How does the combinatorics of correspondences automatically produce the projection formula? (Hint: The composition of the diagonal correspondence and the product correspondence in $\mathrm{Corr}(\mathcal{C}, E)$ yields $f_!(A \otimes f^*B) \cong f_!(A) \otimes B$ as a formal consequence of functoriality.)
	- What are the minimal geometric axioms on $(I, P)$ needed to construct a six-functor formalism? (Hint: $I$ (open immersions) and $P$ (proper maps) must be stable under compositions, base change, and diagonals, satisfy $I \cap P = \mathrm{Isom}$, and ensure every $f \in E$ factors as $f = p \circ j$ with $j \in I$ and $p \in P$ (Nagata compactification).)
	- What is the definition of cohomological smoothness for a morphism $f \colon X \to Y$? (Hint: A morphism $f$ is cohomologically smooth if $f_!$ admits a right adjoint $f^!$, the relative dualizing object $\omega_{f} := f^!(\mathbf{1}_Y)$ is $\otimes$-invertible in $\mathcal{D}(X)$, and the canonical map $f^*(-) \otimes \omega_f \xrightarrow{\sim} f^!(-)$ is an isomorphism compatible with arbitrary base change.)
	- Why is cohomological smoothness more general than classical geometric smoothness? (Hint: Because it is defined purely through the existence of Poincaré-Verdier duality and invertible dualizing sheaves, holding for rigid analytic and perfectoid spaces without requiring smooth charts.)

https://arxiv.org/pdf/2112.10456, Gallauer, six functor formalisms
	- How did Emmy Noether conceptualize Betti numbers in the development of homology? (Hint: Noether showed that Betti numbers are numeric shadows of the underlying homology groups $H_n(X)$, which are abelian groups measuring the difference between closed cycles and exact boundaries; homology is a strictly richer algebraic invariant that cannot be recovered from Betti numbers alone.)
	- How does the Weil zeta function of a variety over a finite field motivate cohomology theories in arithmetic geometry? (Hint: The zeta function $\zeta_X(T) = \exp\left(\sum_{n \ge 1} \frac{\#X(\mathbb{F}_{q^n})}{n} T^n\right)$ packages point counts over all finite extensions; Weil's proof for curves and his conjectures for higher-dimensional varieties predicted that $\zeta_X(T)$ is a rational function whose zeroes and poles are governed by a well-behaved Weil cohomology theory ($\ell$-adic étale cohomology).)
	- What is the categorification principle from numbers to abelian groups and cohomology? (Hint: While numbers have few interesting maps between them, abelian groups and chain complexes possess rich morphism spaces and derived invariants; passing from numbers to derived categories of sheaves categorifies numerical invariants into six-functor formalisms.)
	- Why does Grothendieck's relative point of view require the six operations? (Hint: Rather than studying isolated varieties $X$, one studies families and morphisms $f \colon X \to Y$; on non-proper varieties, global invariants fail to behave well without compactly supported pushforwards $f_!$ and exceptional pullbacks $f^!$, completing the six operations $(f^*, f_*, f_!, f^!, \otimes, \underline{\mathrm{Hom}})$.)
	- What are the classical sheaf operations and how does the exceptional pushforward $f_!$ relate to its right adjoint $f^!$? (Hint: $f^*$ (inverse image) and $f_*$ (direct image) form a standard adjoint pair; on locally compact Hausdorff spaces / schemes, $f_!$ (compactly supported pushforward) preserves colimits and admits a right adjoint $f^!$ (exceptional pullback/twist) only in the derived $\infty$-category setting.)
	- How are cohomology, compactly supported cohomology, Borel-Moore homology, and homology expressed in terms of the six operations on the tensor unit $\mathbf{1}$? (Hint: Let $p \colon X \to *$ be the structure map. (1) Cohomology: $C^*(X) = p_* p^*(\mathbf{1})$; (2) Compactly supported cohomology: $C_c^*(X) = p_! p^*(\mathbf{1})$; (3) Borel-Moore homology: $C_*^{\mathrm{BM}}(X) = p_* p^!(\mathbf{1})$; (4) Homology with compact supports: $C_*(X) = p_! p^!(\mathbf{1})$.)
	- How does Verdier duality operate between these four theories via the dualizing object $\omega_X = p^!\mathbf{1}$? (Hint: The adjunctions induce a canonical pairing $\mathbf{R}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(p_! p^* \mathbf{1}, \mathbf{1}) \simeq p_* \mathbf{R}\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(p^* \mathbf{1}, p^! \mathbf{1}) = p_* p^! \mathbf{1}$, identifying the dual of compactly supported cohomology $C_c^*(X)^\vee$ with Borel-Moore homology $C_*^{\mathrm{BM}}(X)$.)
	- How do different geometric coefficient systems determine specific cohomology theories? (Hint: (1) Constructible $\ell$-adic sheaves $\mathcal{D}_c^b(X, \mathbb{Q}_\ell) \implies$ étale cohomology; (2) Constructible topological sheaves $\mathcal{D}_c^b(X^{\mathrm{an}}, \Lambda) \implies$ Betti cohomology; (3) Holonomic $\mathcal{D}$-modules $\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{hol}}^b(\mathcal{D}_X) \implies$ algebraic de Rham cohomology; (4) Voevodsky motives $\mathcal{DM}(X) \implies$ motivic cohomology.)
	- How are vanishing/nearby cycle functors, Chern classes, and regulator maps viewed in the six-functor context? (Hint: Nearby/vanishing cycles are specialized gluing functors across degenerations; Chern classes and cycle/regulator maps are natural transformations between different six-functor coefficient systems (e.g., from motivic cohomology to étale or de Rham cohomology).)
	- What does proper pushforward mean for the transformation $f_! \to f_*$? (Hint: The canonical "forget supports" natural transformation $\alpha_f \colon f_! \to f_*$ is an isomorphism if and only if $f$ is proper.)
	- How does Grothendieck duality specialize to Poincaré and Serre dualities? (Hint: For a smooth morphism $f \colon X \to *$, relative purity computes $f^!\mathbf{1} \simeq \mathbf{1}(d)[2d]$; for $\ell$-adic coefficients, this yields $H^n(X, \mathbb{Q}_\ell)^\vee \cong H_c^{2d-n}(X, \mathbb{Q}_\ell(d))$ (Poincaré duality), and for coherent sheaves on proper $X$, it yields $\mathrm{Ext}^i(\mathcal{F}, \omega_X) \cong H^{n-i}(X, \mathcal{F})^\vee$ (Serre duality).)
	- What is the proper projection formula and what does it assert? (Hint: For any morphism $f \colon X \to Y$, there is a natural isomorphism $f_!(A \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} f^*B) \xrightarrow{\sim} f_!(A) \otimes^{\mathbf{L}} B$, asserting that $f_!$ is an enriched $\mathcal{D}(Y)$-linear module functor.)
	- What is relative purity and how is it measured by the relative dualizing object? (Hint: For a smooth morphism $f$ of relative dimension $d$, relative purity is the natural equivalence $f^!(-) \simeq f^*(-) \otimes f^!(\mathbf{1}) \simeq f^*(-)(d)[2d]$, identifying $f^!$ as a Tate-twisted shift of $f^*$.)
	- How do open covers give rise to Mayer-Vietoris exact triangles in six-functor formalisms? (Hint: For an open cover $X = U \cup V$ with intersection $W = U \cap V$, the localization sequences for open immersions glue to produce the distinguished triangle $C^*(X) \to C^*(U) \oplus C^*(V) \to C^*(W) \xrightarrow{+1}$.)
	- How is the homology of a closed subscheme $Z$ relative to $X$ defined? (Hint: As the Borel-Moore homology of the closed immersion $i \colon Z \hookrightarrow X$, computed by $C_*^{\mathrm{BM}}(Z) \simeq p_* i_* i^! p^!\mathbf{1} \simeq p_* i_! i^! \omega_X$.)
	- Why can the localization axiom be difficult to establish in non-topological contexts (e.g., Kashiwara's lemma)? (Hint: Localization requires that for a closed immersion $i$, $i_*$ is fully faithful with left adjoint $i^*$; for $\mathcal{D}$-modules, this full faithfulness is Kashiwara's equivalence, which requires deep analytic/algebraic regularity theorems.)
	- How does the abstract blow-up square encode blow-up excision in cohomology via proper base change? (Hint: For a blow-up $\widetilde{X} \to X$ with center $Z$ and exceptional divisor $E$, the Cartesian square is an abstract cdh-distinguished square; proper base change and localization formally produce the excision fiber sequence $C^*(X) \to C^*(\widetilde{X}) \oplus C^*(Z) \to C^*(E) \xrightarrow{+1}$.)
	- What is the Beck-Chevalley condition for a Cartesian square? (Hint: For a Cartesian square with projections $f, g, f', g'$, the natural transformation $g^* f_* \to f'_* g'^*$ (or $g^* f_! \to f'_! g'^*$) formed by adjunction units and counits is an isomorphism.)
	- What is proper base change vs. smooth base change? (Hint: Proper base change states $g^* f_* \xrightarrow{\sim} f'_* g'^*$ when $f$ is proper; smooth base change states $g'_! f'^! \xrightarrow{\sim} f^! g_!$ (and $g^* f_* \xrightarrow{\sim} f'_* g'^*$) when $g$ is smooth.)
	- Why does the pullback along a smooth morphism admit a left adjoint $p_\#$? (Hint: By relative purity, $p^*(-) \simeq p^!(-)(-d)[-2d]$; since $p^!$ is right adjoint to $p_!$, $p^*$ admits the left adjoint $p_\#(-) := p_!(-)(d)[2d]$.)
	- What is a Nisnevich-Mayer-Vietoris square? (Hint: An elementary distinguished square where $U \hookrightarrow X$ is an open immersion, $V \to X$ is an étale morphism, and $(V \setminus V \times_X U) \to (X \setminus U)$ is an isomorphism; coefficient systems satisfy descent on these squares.)
	- What are the axiomatic conditions defining a coefficient system in Gallauer's framework? (Hint: A functor $\mathcal{D} \colon \mathbf{Sch}_S^{\mathrm{op}} \to \mathbf{CAlg}(\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{\mathrm{L}})$ assigning presentable stable symmetric monoidal $\infty$-categories satisfying: (1) smooth base change; (2) smooth projection formula; (3) localization; (4) $\mathbb{A}^1$-homotopy invariance; and (5) Tate stability ($\mathbf{1}(1)$ is $\otimes$-invertible).)
	- State Gallauer's main theorem (Theorem 2.13) on the extension of coefficient systems to six-functor formalisms. (Hint: Any coefficient system $\mathcal{D}$ uniquely extends to a full six-functor formalism equipped with $(f^*, f_*, f_!, f^!, \otimes, \underline{\mathrm{Hom}})$, Grothendieck duality, proper base change, and the projection formula; morphisms of coefficient systems induce morphisms of six-functor formalisms.)
	- How does Ind-completion resolve presentability issues for small coefficient systems? (Hint: By replacing a small category of constructible/dualizable sheaves $\mathcal{D}_c(X)$ with its Ind-completion $\mathrm{Ind}(\mathcal{D}_c(X)) \simeq \mathcal{D}(X)$, ensuring all limits, colimits, and adjoints exist via the Adjoint Functor Theorem.)
	- Why can Grothendieck/Verdier duality only be expected on constructible or dualizable subcategories? (Hint: The infinite-dimensional unit $\mathbf{1}$ is not compact in $\mathrm{Ind}$-categories; the biduality isomorphism $M \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathbb{D}\mathbb{D}(M)$ holds only for dualizable/constructible objects, where $\mathbb{D}(-) = \underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(-, \omega_X)$.)
	- How did Voevodsky establish that exceptional functoriality $f_!$ arises from localization and $\mathbb{A}^1$-homotopy? (Hint: Voevodsky proved that compactifications and purity formulas can be reconstructed purely from the localization sequences of open/closed immersions combined with $\mathbb{A}^1$-invariance, defining $f_!$ as a localized homotopy pushforward without manual compactifications.)
	- What are the three conceptual perspectives on the Stable Motivic Homotopy Category $\mathcal{SH}(S)$ (Remark 2.25)? (Hint: (1) The universal presentable stable $\infty$-category generated by smooth $S$-schemes with $\mathbb{A}^1$-invariance and Nisnevich descent; (2) The $\mathbb{P}^1$-stabilization (Tate spectrum inversion) of motivic spaces; (3) The category of motivic $\mathbb{E}_\infty$-sheaves of spectra on $\mathbf{Sm}_S$.)
	- How does cdh-descent allow extending coefficient systems from schemes to algebraic stacks? (Hint: Coefficient systems with cdh-descent and smooth/étale descent extend to Artin stacks by computing homotopy limits over simplicial scheme atlases $U_\bullet \to \mathcal{X}$.)
	- What is a linear coefficient system and how does scalar extension apply? (Hint: A coefficient system enriched over a base ring/spectrum $\Lambda$ (e.g., $\mathbf{Mod}_\Lambda$-linear); scalar extension along $\Lambda \to \Lambda'$ preserves the six-functor axioms via $\mathcal{D}(X) \otimes_{\mathcal{D}(*)} \mathcal{D}'(*)$.)
	- What characterizes an orientable coefficient system? (Hint: A coefficient system where every vector bundle $E \to X$ of rank $d$ has a Thom class trivializing the Thom space $\mathrm{Th}(E) \cong \mathbf{1}_X(d)[2d]$, making all geometric twists isomorphic to standard Tate twists.)
	- What is an exponential coefficient system (exponential motives) and its 7th operation? (Hint: A coefficient system enriched with exponential motives $e^f$ for regular functions $f \colon X \to \mathbb{A}^1$; it possesses a 7th geometric operation: the **Fourier-Deligne transform**, exchanging additive convolution with tensor products.)
	- Why does exceptional functoriality require the support property? (Hint: To guarantee that $f_!$ is independent of the choice of compactification $X \xrightarrow{j} \overline{X} \xrightarrow{p} Y$, the pushforward must vanish on the boundary complement, which is guaranteed by proper base change and localization.)
	- How does the pullback lift structures while the pushforward quantifies local-to-global obstructions? (Hint: $p^*(\mathbf{1})$ equips the space with constant coefficients; $p_*$ collects global sections, and its higher derived cohomology groups $R^i p_*$ quantify the homological obstructions to gluing local sections into global ones.)
	- How are integral transforms and logical quantifiers realized in six-functor formalisms? (Hint: An integral kernel transform along a correspondence $X \xleftarrow{g} W \xrightarrow{f} Y$ is the functor $\Phi_K(-) = f_!(g^*(-) \otimes K)$; in logic, $f_!$ acts as the existential quantifier $\exists_f$ and $f_*$ acts as the universal quantifier $\forall_f$.)
	- Why does cohomology possess a ring structure (cup product) while homology possesses a coalgebra structure? (Hint: The diagonal map $\Delta \colon X \to X \times X$ pulls back under the contravariant functor $p_* p^*$, yielding the product $C^*(X) \otimes C^*(X) \xrightarrow{\Delta^*} C^*(X)$ (cup product); conversely, homology is covariant under $\Delta_*$, which pushes forward classes to $C_*(X) \xrightarrow{\Delta_*} C_*(X) \otimes C_*(X)$, splitting classes apart into a coalgebra.)

https://sites.math.rutgers.edu/~ctw/quotients.pdf, Geometric Invariant Theory and Symplectic Quotients
	- (1) Formulate the finite-dimensional versions of Harder–Narasimhan and Jordan–Hölder filtrations in geometric invariant theory. (Hint: One technique is to exploit the convexity of Kempf–Ness functions along non-compact directions).
	- (2) Compute the moment polytope of a Hamiltonian group action. (Hint: Consider the relationship between moment polytope computations and geometric quantization).
	- (3) Compute the time-1 flow at the identity on a Lie group $G$ where multiplication is a smooth map. (Hint: Use the exponential map $\exp: \mathfrak{g} \to G$ of the Lie algebra, generated by left-invariant vector fields acting on the Lie group).
	- (4) Define a smooth left action of a Lie group $K$ on a manifold $M$ satisfying associativity and identity (forming a $K$-manifold), and define what it means for a map between such spaces to be $K$-equivariant. (Hint: Make the Lie group act on vector spaces analogously to linear algebra so that maps preserve the group action).
	- (5) Explain the sign convention of the Lie bracket $[\cdot, \cdot]$ when taking infinitesimal generators. (Hint: Left-invariant vector fields generate a right action, whereas group actions are conventionally defined on the left, which introduces a sign change).
	- (6) For a point $x \in M$, define its stabilizer subgroup $K_x = \operatorname{Stab}_K(x)$ and its orbit $\mathcal{O}_x = K \cdot x$. (Hint: The stabilizer is the subgroup for which the orbit is constant/fixed at $x$).
	- (7) Define the de Rham differential operators on differential forms. (Hint: They are graded derivations satisfying the graded product rule $D(\alpha_0 \alpha_1) = (D\alpha_0)\alpha_1 + (-1)^{|D||\alpha_0|} \alpha_0 (D\alpha_1)$).
	- (8) Distinguish complex linear transformations from classical groups of linear symplectomorphisms on a symplectic manifold $(M, \omega)$. (Hint: A symplectic manifold requires a closed, non-degenerate 2-form $\omega$ satisfying $d\omega = 0$).
	- (9) Define a conserved quantity in a Hamiltonian dynamical system with an energy function $H \in C^\infty(X)$. (Hint: Apply Noether's theorem relating continuous symmetries of the Hamiltonian to conserved quantities).
	- (10) Explain how Lagrangian correspondences generalize symplectomorphisms. (Hint: Symplectomorphisms require the domain and codomain to have the same dimension, whereas Lagrangian submanifolds $L \subset M_1^- \times M_2$ remove this dimension restriction).
	- (11) Explain how Poisson geometry generalizes symplectic geometry. (Hint: Note that Poisson geometry is not generally compatible with Lagrangian correspondences as morphisms).
	- (12) Define ordinary momentum on a symplectic manifold using a moment map $\mu: M \to \mathfrak{k}^*$. (Hint: Use Hamiltonian vector fields $X_H$ and lift them to an equivariant Lie algebra homomorphism via the co-moment map $\mu^*: \mathfrak{k} \to C^\infty(M)$).
	- (13) Compute the surface area of the two-sphere $S^2$ using a moment map calculation for the $S^1$-action. (Hint: Show that this computation directly recovers the area of the cylinder between two $z$-values, $z_1$ and $z_2$).
	- (14) Define a Hamiltonian $K$-manifold. (Hint: Combine the machinery of linear algebra, exterior algebra, pullbacks, and interior products $\iota_{\xi_M} \omega$).
	- (15) Generalize Darboux's theorem using Morse theory and equivariantly closed differential forms. (Hint: Use the equivariant cohomology framework introduced by Atiyah and Bott).
	- (16) Interpret the moment map as an infinitesimal linearization of a group action. (Hint: Think of the moment map as providing a lift of the action to an equivariant vector bundle or prequantum line bundle).
	- (17) Define the symplectic quotient (Marsden–Weinstein reduction) $M /\!\!/ K = \mu^{-1}(0)/K$ for a Hamiltonian $K$-manifold. (Hint: Free actions on the zero level set $\mu^{-1}(0)$ ensure that the quotient admits a smooth and well-defined symplectic structure).
	- (18) Compute the symplectic quotient for the product of unit two-spheres $(S^2)^n$ under the diagonal action of $K = \mathrm{SO}(3)$. (Hint: Show that this quotient is the moduli space of closed spatial $n$-gons with side lengths rescaled by $\lambda$/eigenvalues, where the moment map sends each configuration to a vector sum/trace).
	- (19, Page 18) Describe the compatibility of a symplectic 2-form $\omega$ with an almost complex structure $J$. (Hint: Compatibility and integrability yield the definition of a Kähler manifold).
	- (20, Page 3) Define the orbits $\mathcal{O}_x$ and stabilizers $\operatorname{Stab}_K(x)$ of points under a smooth group action. (Hint: Examine how the partition of points into orbits relates to their stabilizer subgroups).
	- (21) Let $(X, \omega)$ be a symplectic manifold with energy function $H \in C^\infty(X)$, and let $K \in C^\infty(X)$ be an observable (such as a component of angular momentum). 	- (Section 1) Explain how the Freudenthal suspension theorem motivates the construction of the stable homotopy category. (Hint: The sequence of pointed homotopy classes $[ \Sigma^k X, \Sigma^k Y ]_+$ is eventually constant for finite-dimensional CW-complexes, suggesting the construction of a localized category where the suspension functor $\Sigma = S^1 \wedge -$ is formally inverted).
	- (Section 1) How does Brown's representability theorem connect generalized reduced cohomology theories to spectra? (Hint: Every generalized reduced cohomology theory $H^*$ on pointed spaces is stably representable by a spectrum $E$ such that $\widetilde{H}^n(Z) \cong [Z, E_n]_+$).
	- (Section 1) List the four key properties that define a "good" category of spectra. (Hint: It should be an elementary model category whose homotopy category is the stable homotopy category, it must possess a compatible closed symmetric monoidal smash product, and spectra arising from generalized cohomology theories like $KO$ and $HA$ should be easily described).
	- (Section 2.1) Review the category of pointed simplicial sets $\mathcal{S}_*$. Show that $\mathcal{S}_*$ is a closed symmetric monoidal category under the smash product $X \wedge Y = (X \times Y)/(X \times * \cup * \times Y)$. (Hint: The unit object is $S^0 = \Delta[0]_+$, and the right adjoint to $-\wedge X$ is the mapping simplicial set $\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{S}_*}(X, Y)_n = \mathcal{S}_*(X \wedge \Delta[n]_+, Y)$).
	- (Section 2.2) Define the category of ordinary (naive) spectra $\mathrm{Sp}^{\mathbb{N}}$ in the simplicial context. (Hint: An object is a sequence of pointed simplicial sets $(X_n)_{n \ge 0}$ equipped with structure maps $\sigma: S^1 \wedge X_n \to X_{n+1}$, where $S^1 = \Delta[1]/\partial\Delta[1]$).
	- (Section 2.2) Define the category of symmetric spectra $\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma$. (Hint: In addition to the structure maps $\sigma$, each $X_n$ is equipped with a basepoint-preserving left action of the symmetric group $\Sigma_n$ such that the iterated structure maps $\sigma^p: S^p \wedge X_n \to X_{p+n}$ are $\Sigma_p \times \Sigma_n$-equivariant for all $p \ge 1, n \ge 0$).
	- (Section 2.2) Explain why verifying equivariance for $\sigma$ and $\sigma^2$ suffices in the definition of a symmetric spectrum. (Hint: The transpositions / 2-cycles generate the symmetric group $\Sigma_n$).
	- (Section 2.2) Describe the canonical forgetful functor $U: \mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma \to \mathrm{Sp}^{\mathbb{N}}$. (Hint: The functor forgets the $\Sigma_n$-actions while preserving the underlying sequence of pointed simplicial sets and structure maps).
	- (Section 2.3) Define the sphere spectrum $\mathbb{S}$ as a symmetric spectrum. (Hint: Set $\mathbb{S}_n = S^n = (S^1)^{\wedge n}$ with $\Sigma_n$ acting by permuting the $n$ smash factors of $S^1$, and canonical isomorphisms $\sigma: S^1 \wedge S^n \to S^{n+1}$).
	- (Section 2.3) Define the suspension spectrum functor $\Sigma^\infty: \mathcal{S}_* \to \mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma$ and the Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum $HA$ for an abelian group $A$. (Hint: For $\Sigma^\infty K$, set $(\Sigma^\infty K)_n = S^n \wedge K$; for $HA$, set $(HA)_n = A \otimes_\mathbb{Z} \mathbb{Z}(S^n)$, where $\mathbb{Z}(S^n)$ is the free simplicial abelian group generated by the non-basepoint simplices).
	- (Section 3.1) Explain the technique of prolongation for a functor $R: \mathcal{S}_* \to \mathcal{S}_*$. Use it to prove that $\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma$ is complete and cocomplete. (Hint: Extend $R$ levelwise by $(RX)_n = R(X_n)$; limits and colimits in $\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma$ are computed levelwise from $\mathcal{S}_*$).
	- (Section 3.2) Describe the simplicial action on symmetric spectra and the mapping space $\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma}(X, Y)$. (Hint: Define the tensor $X \wedge K$ and power spectrum $X^K$ by prolongation, yielding an enrichment of $\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma$ over pointed simplicial sets via $\operatorname{Map}_{\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma}(X, Y)_n = \mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma(X \wedge \Delta[n]_+, Y)$).
	- (Section 3.3) Define the category of symmetric sequences $\mathcal{S}_*^\Sigma = \operatorname{Fun}(\Sigma, \mathcal{S}_*)$ and its convolution tensor product $\otimes$. (Hint: $(X \otimes Y)_n = \bigvee_{p+q=n} (\Sigma_n)_+ \wedge_{\Sigma_p \times \Sigma_q} (X_p \wedge Y_q)$, with unit $(S^0, *, *, \dots)$ and twist isomorphism given by the block transposition $\rho_{q,p}$).
	- (Section 3.3) Prove that the sphere spectrum $\mathbb{S}$ is a commutative monoid in the category of symmetric sequences $(\mathcal{S}_*^\Sigma, \otimes)$. (Hint: The structure maps $S^p \wedge S^q \to S^{p+q}$ assemble into an associative multiplication $m: \mathbb{S} \otimes \mathbb{S} \to \mathbb{S}$, and the symmetric group action absorbs the block permutation twist $\rho_{p,q}$ to guarantee commutativity).
	- (Section 3.3) Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a bicomplete closed symmetric monoidal category and let $R$ be a commutative monoid in $\mathcal{C}$. Formulate the monoidal product $M \otimes_R N$ for $R$-modules as a coequalizer and define the internal hom $\operatorname{Hom}_R(M, N)$ as an equalizer. (Hint: $M \otimes_R N$ is the coequalizer of $M \otimes R \otimes N \rightrightarrows M \otimes N$).
	- (Section 3.3) Prove that the category of symmetric spectra $\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma$ is equivalent to the category of $\mathbb{S}$-modules in $(\mathcal{S}_*^\Sigma, \otimes)$. (Hint: The $\Sigma_p \times \Sigma_q$-equivariant structure maps $m_{p,q}: S^p \wedge X_q \to X_{p+q}$ assemble via the universal property of Day convolution into a left $\mathbb{S}$-action map $m: \mathbb{S} \otimes X \to X$).
	- (Section 3.3) Deduce that $\mathrm{Sp}^\Sigma$ possesses a closed symmetric monoidal smash product $\wedge = \otimes_\mathbb{S}$. Why does this construction fail for ordinary spectra $\mathrm{Sp}^{\mathbb{N}}$? (Hint: In ordinary spectra, the sphere spectrum is not a commutative monoid because the twist map $\tau: S^1 \wedge S^1 \to S^1 \wedge S^1$ has degree $-1$ and is not the identity; symmetric spectra fix this by incorporating the $\Sigma_n$-actions).

	- (Page 9) Define what it means for a prestack or derived affine scheme to be locally of finite type (lft). 	- (Page 10) Define flatness for morphisms of derived affine schemes $S' \to S$. (Hint: A morphism $\operatorname{Spec} B \to \operatorname{Spec} A$ is flat if $\pi_0(B)$ is flat over $\pi_0(A)$ in the classical sense and the natural base change map $\pi_i(A) \otimes_{\pi_0(A)} \pi_0(B) \xrightarrow{\sim} \pi_i(B)$ is an isomorphism for all $i$).
	- (Page 17) State the descent and locality properties of morphisms (flat, smooth, étale, open immersion) under base change. (Hint: Morphism properties are stable under derived base change and local with respect to the chosen topology; note that open embeddings are not Zariski-local on the source, and the Zariski topology is not étale-local on the target).
	- (Page 18) Formulate the Čech descent condition for a prestack $\mathcal{Y}$ with respect to a flat/étale covering $S' \to S$. 	- (Page 18) Explain why the inclusion of stacks into prestacks $\mathbf{Stk} \hookrightarrow \mathbf{PreStk}$ admits a left adjoint. (Hint: The left adjoint is the sheafification/stackification functor with respect to the chosen Grothendieck topology, making $\mathbf{Stk}$ a left-exact localization / reflective subcategory of $\mathbf{PreStk}$).
	- (Page 19) Formulate the sheafification functor on prestacks. (Hint: Define stackification as the composite left adjoint $\mathbf{PreStk} \to \mathbf{Stk} \hookrightarrow \mathbf{PreStk}$ enforcing the Čech descent condition).
	- (Page 20) Analyze the relationship between descent conditions and Postnikov connectivity. (Hint: Examine how étale and flat descent interact with the connectivity of truncated derived affine schemes $\tau_{\le n} S$).
	- (Page 20) Define a schematic morphism of prestacks $\mathcal{Y} \to \mathcal{Z}$. (Hint: A morphism is schematic if for every derived affine scheme $S$ and morphism $S \to \mathcal{Z}$, the derived fiber product $\mathcal{Y} \times_{\mathcal{Z}} S$ is representable by a derived scheme).

	- (1, Page 3) Why does classical gluing of triangulated or derived categories fail, and how do limits of diagrams resolve this? (Hint: Constructions defined strictly on chain complexes fail to glue canonically except up to homotopy so one requires homotopy colimits, whereas in -categories gluing is canonically defined via diagrams of affine schemes)
	- (2, Page 7) How is a monoid object encoded in an $\infty$-category $\mathcal{C}$? (Hint: Formulate it as a functor from the opposite category of simplicial sets $\Delta^{\text{op}} \to \mathcal{C}$ satisfying the Segal induction condition from $0$ to $i-1$ and $i$ to $1$).
	- (3, Page 7) Define what it means for an object in a monoidal $\infty$-category to be dualizable. (Hint: Dualizability mirrors the classical dual of an object or module, characterized by evaluation and coevaluation maps fixing an adjoint 1-morphism).
	- (4, Page 8) Explain why stability is an intrinsic property of an $\infty$-category rather than extra structure. (Hint: Stable $\infty$-categories are automatically enriched over the category of spectra $\mathbf{Sp}$ without needing to specify spectrum mapping spaces by hand).
	- (5, Page 8) Define a $t$-structure on a stable $\infty$-category $\mathcal{C}$ and characterize its heart. (Hint: A $t$-structure is a substructure of connective chain complexes whose intersection $\mathcal{C}^{\le 0} \cap \mathcal{C}^{\ge 0}$ satisfies the axioms of an abelian category).
	- (6, Page 8) Describe the Lurie tensor product on stable presentable $\infty$-categories. (Hint: The tensor product of module categories satisfies $\operatorname{Mod}_A \otimes \operatorname{Mod}_B \simeq \operatorname{Mod}_{A \otimes B}$, naturally endowing presentable stable categories with a symmetric monoidal structure).
	- (7, Page 9) How is the category of spectra $\mathbf{Sp}$ characterized as a unit object? (Hint: $\mathbf{Sp}$ is the unit object in $\mathbf{1}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}_{\text{cont}}^{\text{St}}$, meaning every cocomplete stable $\infty$-category is automatically an $\mathbf{Sp}$-module possessing a relative internal hom).
	- (8, Page 8–9) Explain why compactly generated stable $\infty$-categories are dualizable in $\mathbf{Pr}_{\text{st}}^L$. (Hint: Every compactly generated category is the ind-completion $\operatorname{Ind}(\mathcal{C}^c)$, which can be realized as the category of exact functors $(\mathcal{C}^c)^{\text{op}} \to \mathbf{Sp}$, making its dual its own ind-completion).
	- (9, Page 9–10) Describe Verdier duality on the compact objects of $\mathcal{D}$-modules $(\mathcal{D}\text{-}\mathbf{Mod}(X))^c$. (Hint: Compact objects have finitely many non-zero cohomology sheaves with respect to the $t$-structure and consist of coherent $\mathcal{D}$-modules; Verdier duality gives an auto-equivalence on compact objects where compact pushforward and exceptional pushforward act as Verdier conjugates).
	- (10, Page 10) What is the motivation behind defining rigid monoidal categories? (Hint: Rigidity imposes finiteness conditions on objects, ensuring every object is dualizable).
	- (11, Page 14) Define the Yoneda embedding functor for $\infty$-categories into spaces. (Hint: Construct the functor $j: \mathcal{C} \hookrightarrow \operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{\text{op}}, \mathcal{S})$ sending each object to its representable space of morphisms).
	- (12, Page 16) What is the primary motivation for the Grothendieck construction in higher category theory? (Hint: It provides the bridge between fibrations over a base category and functors taking values in categories).
	- (13, Page 17) How are the Yoneda functor, adjoints, and smash products constructed via straightening and unstraightening? (Hint: Straightening and unstraightening converts Cartesian and coCartesian fibrations into diagrammatic functors to $\mathbf{Cat}_\infty$, resolving the coherence difficulties of defining monoidal smash products in stable homotopy theory).
	- (14, Page 22) How are limits and colimits formulated as Kan extensions, and what do geometric realizations and totalizations look like? (Hint: Colimits over $\Delta^{\text{op}}$ are geometric realizations, while limits over the category $\Delta$ are totalizations).
	- (15, Page 22) State the role of cofinality in computing limits and colimits. (Hint: Cofinality allows one to replace a limit or colimit over a large indexing diagram with a limit or colimit over a smaller sub-diagram).
	- (16, Page 29) How is a monoidal category represented as a simplicial object? (Hint: A monoidal category is encoded as a functor from $\Delta^{\text{op}}$ to $\mathbf{1}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}$ satisfying Segal-type product equivalences).
	- (17, Page 32) How does replacing $\Delta^{\text{op}}$ with finite pointed sets yield symmetric monoidal categories? (Hint: Symmetric monoidal categories correspond to operadic fibrations over the category of finite pointed sets $\operatorname{Fin}_*$).
	(18, Page 38) What is the role of the Barr–Beck–Lurie theorem in higher algebra? (Hint: It characterizes when an $\infty$-category of modules over a monad is equivalent to the original category via monadic descent. More precisely, you do not need to check Cech-cocycles. The basic example is to prove the category of modules is equivalent to descent data for modules over $B$, for $A \rightarrow B$ to be a fiathfully flat ring map, formally one can consider the pullback and erxtension of scalars adjunction of categories of modules. Pushforward is forgetful or restriction, pullback is extension off scalars. Then one wants to show that $Mod(A)$ is equivalent to comodules over the comonad, show that the adjunction exists, the right adjoint is conservative i.e. maps of $B$-modules is equivalence iff maps of $A$-modules is equivalence using faithful flatness. Flatness ensure exactness, faithfulness means no nonzero module maps are sent to $0$. $Mod(B)$ and $f_*$ preserve totalisations of cosimplicial objects, to see Khan's notes for a review of totalisation. Barr-Beck-Lurie is then used to prove this.)
	- (19, Page 57) What is the motivation for the $\operatorname{Ind}$-completion functor on a non-cocomplete stable category? (Hint: It canonically produces a cocomplete category by formally adjoining all filtered colimits).
	- (20, Page 59) How does $\operatorname{Ind}$-completion yield a categorical version of Verdier duality? (Hint: The pairing on compact objects $\mathcal{C}^c \times (\mathcal{C}^c)^{\text{op}} \to \mathbf{Sp}$ extends by ind-completion to identify the dual of a compactly generated category).
	- (21, Page 71) What distinguishes rigid monoidal categories in Gaitsgory's framework? (Hint: Rigid monoidal categories act as categorical analogues of Frobenius algebras in the stable setting).
	- (22, Page 79) Give a concrete example of the heart of a $t$-structure on a stable derived category. (Hint: For the derived category of a ring $\mathcal{D}(R)$ with its standard $t$-structure, the heart $\mathcal{D}^{\le 0} \cap \mathcal{D}^{\ge 0}$ recovers the abelian category of $R$-modules).
	- (23, Page 4) Why can the $G$-equivariant derived category of sheaves not be constructed purely from the ordinary derived category of sheaves? (Hint: Group equivariance requires higher homotopies and chain-level constructions that are lost in the homotopy category).
	- (24, Page 4) Why does derived base change for quasicoherent sheaves require connective commutative differential graded algebras? (Hint: Ordinary base change fails without flatness; derived tensor products require CDGAs concentrated in non-positive degrees, which define derived affine schemes).
	- (25, Page 71) How do rigid monoidal categories relate to Frobenius algebras and continuous dg-categories? (Hint: Rigid monoidal categories behave as Frobenius algebras in stable categories, forming the foundation of continuous dg-categories $\mathbf{DGCat}_{\text{cont}}$ in the Gaitsgory–Rozenblyum program).
	- (26, Page 4) Why did Lurie develop quasicategories as the foundational model for higher algebra? (Hint: Quasicategories provide an explicit simplicial syntax where higher homotopies and coherences can be checked using horn-filling conditions).
	- (27, Page 4) Why is the language of $(\infty, 2)$-categories required to guarantee canonicity? (Hint: Categorical dualities, adjunctions between $\infty$-categories, and correspondences require 2-morphisms to be formulated canonically).
	- (28, Page 8) Explain how stable $\infty$-categories are enriched over spectra and how this simplifies $t$-structures. (Hint: Mapping spaces automatically lift to spectrum objects, allowing triangulated structures and $t$-structures to be defined intrinsically).
	- (29, Page 9) How does Gaitsgory's ind-completion construct compactly generated stable categories? (Hint: $\operatorname{Ind}$-completion is necessary to describe all colimit-preserving functors out of the category of compact objects).

	- (Subanalytic Sets) Define a semi-analytic subset $S \subseteq M$ of a real analytic manifold $M$ as a locally closed subset defined locally by finitely many analytic inequalities $\{f_i(x) < 0, g_j(x) \le 0\}$. Explain why the family of semi-analytic sets is inadequate for geometric operations and how the family of subanalytic sets $\operatorname{SbA}(M)$ resolves this. (Hint: Semi-analytic sets are not stable under proper direct images except when $\dim M \le 2$; $\operatorname{SbA}(M)$ is the smallest family containing semi-analytic sets that is stable under proper direct images, preimages, finite unions/intersections, complements, interiors, and closures).
	- (Topological Regularity of Subanalytic Sets) State the topological properties of compact subanalytic sets. (Hint: Every compact subanalytic set is homeomorphic to a finite simplicial complex / CW-complex, and in particular has only finitely many connected components, ensuring well-behaved homology).
	- (Lojasiewicz Inequalities on Coverings) Let $U = \bigcup_{j \in J} U_j$ be a finite covering of relatively compact open subanalytic sets. State the Łojasiewicz inequality relating distances to the boundaries. (Hint: There exist constants $C > 0$ and $N \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $\operatorname{dist}(x, M \setminus U)^N \le C \cdot \max_{j \in J} \operatorname{dist}(x, M \setminus U_j)$).
	- (Sites and Generalizing Local Properties) Explain why constructing sheaf theories for analysis does not require taking all topological open sets or all arbitrary coverings. (Hint: Sheaf axioms only require a notion of open subobjects and a collection of covering families satisfying Grothendieck topology axioms, allowing one to restrict to tame subanalytic opens and finite covers).
	- (R-Constructible Sheaves) Define an $\mathbb{R}$-constructible sheaf $\mathcal{F}$ on a real analytic manifold $M$. (Hint: $\mathcal{F}$ admits a stratification $M = \bigsqcup_\alpha M_\alpha$ into locally closed subanalytic strata such that each restriction $\mathcal{F}|_{M_\alpha}$ is a locally constant sheaf of finite rank; the category $\mathbb{R}\text{-}\mathbf{C}(k_M)$ forms an abelian subcategory of $\operatorname{Mod}(k_M)$).
	- (The Subanalytic Site) Define the subanalytic site $M_{\text{sa}}$ and the canonical morphism of sites $\rho_{\text{sa}}: M \to M_{\text{sa}}$. (Hint: The category $\operatorname{Op}_{M_{\text{sa}}}$ consists of relatively compact, open subanalytic subsets with inclusion morphisms, and coverings are families $\{U_i\}_{i \in I}$ admitting a finite subcover $\bigcup_{j \in J} U_j = U$. This yields an adjoint pair $(\rho_{\text{sa}}^{-1}, \rho_{\text{sa}*})$ between $\operatorname{Mod}(k_M)$ and $\operatorname{Mod}(k_{M_{\text{sa}}})$).
	- (Temperate Functions and Distributions on M_sa) Let $U \in \operatorname{Op}_{M_{\text{sa}}}$.
