Define what it means for a smooth function $f \in \mathcal{C}^\infty(U)$ to have polynomial growth of order $N$ at a boundary point $p \in M$, and define temperate functions $\mathcal{C}_{M_{\text{sa}}}^{\infty, \text{tp}}(U)$ and temperate distributions $\mathcal{D}b_{M_{\text{sa}}}^{\text{tp}}(U)$. (Hint: Polynomial growth requires $\sup_{x \in K \cap U} \operatorname{dist}(x, K \setminus U)^N |f(x)| < \infty$ for a compact neighborhood $K$; a function is temperate if all its derivatives have polynomial growth).
	- (Sheaf Property via Lojasiewicz Inequalities) Prove that the presheaves $U \mapsto \mathcal{C}_{M_{\text{sa}}}^{\infty, \text{tp}}(U)$ and $U \mapsto \mathcal{D}b_{M_{\text{sa}}}^{\text{tp}}(U)$ form genuine sheaves on the subanalytic site $M_{\text{sa}}$. (Hint: The gluing condition and separation on overlapping charts are guaranteed by the Łojasiewicz inequality for finite subanalytic coverings).
	- (Fiber Integration of Distributions) For a projection $f: N \times \mathbb{R}^d \to N$ and a closed subanalytic set $Z$ proper over $N$, show that fiber integration of temperate distribution forms induces a quasi-isomorphism with $\mathbf{R}\mathcal{H}om(R f_* \mathbb{C}_Z, \mathcal{D}b_N^{\text{tp}})$. (Hint: This reduces the analytic characterization of pushforwards of distributions to the purely topological computation of $R f_* \mathbb{C}_Z$).
	- (Temperate Holomorphic Functions and Algebraic Cohomology) On a complex manifold $X$, define the sheaf of temperate holomorphic functions $\mathcal{O}_{X_{\text{sa}}}^{\text{tp}}$ via the Dolbeault complex with coefficients in $\mathcal{C}_{X_{\text{sa}}}^{\infty, \text{tp}}$. For a closed complex analytic subset $Z \subset X$, compute $\rho_{\text{sa}}^{-1} \mathbf{R}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathbb{C}_{X_{\text{sa}}}}(\mathbb{C}_Z, \mathcal{O}_{X_{\text{sa}}}^{\text{tp}})$. (Hint: The result is isomorphic to the algebraic local cohomology $R\Gamma_{[Z]}(\mathcal{O}_X)$; for a hypersurface, this recovers the sheaf of meromorphic functions with poles along $Z$).
	- (Distribution Theory without Functional Analysis) For a real analytic manifold $M$ and complexification $X$, show that $\rho_{\text{sa}}^{-1} \mathbf{R}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathbb{C}_{X_{\text{sa}}}}(\mathcal{D}'_X \mathbb{C}_M, \mathcal{O}_{X_{\text{sa}}}^{\text{tp}}) \cong \mathcal{D}b_M$. (Hint: Schwartz distributions $\mathcal{D}b_M$ and Sato hyperfunctions $\mathcal{B}_M$ are constructed in parallel cohomological frameworks without relying on topological vector spaces or functional analysis).
	- (The Linear Subanalytic Site) Define the linear subanalytic site $M_{\text{sal}}$ and 1-regularly situated coverings. (Hint: A covering $\{U_i\}_{i \in I_0}$ is 1-regularly situated if $\operatorname{dist}(x, M \setminus \bigcup_{i} U_i) \le C \cdot \max_{i} \operatorname{dist}(x, M \setminus U_i)$; linear coverings refine the subanalytic Grothendieck topology by imposing uniform linear boundary behavior).
	- (Acyclicity and Brown Representability on M_sal) Formulate the exact sequence characterizing sheaves on $M_{\text{sal}}$ and explain the construction of the right adjoint $\rho_{\text{sal}}^!$. (Hint: A presheaf $\mathcal{F}$ is a sheaf on $M_{\text{sal}}$ if $0 \to \mathcal{F}(U_1 \cup U_2) \to \mathcal{F}(U_1) \oplus \mathcal{F}(U_2) \to \mathcal{F}(U_1 \cap U_2)$ is exact for linear coverings; by Brown representability, $\mathbf{R}\rho_{\text{sal}*}: \mathcal{D}^+(k_{M_{\text{sa}}}) \to \mathcal{D}^+(k_{M_{\text{sal}}})$ admits a right adjoint $\rho_{\text{sal}}^!$).
	- (Open Sets with Lipschitz Boundary) Let $U \in \operatorname{Op}_{M_{\text{sa}}}$ have Lipschitz boundary. Prove that $\mathbf{R}\rho_{\text{sal}*} k_U \cong k_U$ and compute $\mathbf{R}\Gamma(U; \rho_{\text{sal}}^! \mathcal{F})$ for a $\Gamma$-acyclic sheaf $\mathcal{F}$ on $M_{\text{sal}}$. (Hint: Parusinski's theorem implies $\mathbf{R}\Gamma(U; \rho_{\text{sal}}^! \mathcal{F}) \cong \mathcal{F}(U)$ for Lipschitz opens, meaning derived objects $\rho_{\text{sal}}^! \mathcal{F} \in \mathcal{D}^+(k_{M_{\text{sa}}})$ coincide with classical sections on Lipschitz domains).
	- (Temperate Functions of Precise Order s) Define the sheaf $\mathcal{C}_{M_{\text{sal}}}^{\infty, s}$ of smooth functions tempered of order $s \ge 0$ on the linear subanalytic site $M_{\text{sal}}$. (Hint: A function has growth of order $\le s$ if $\sup_{x \in K \cap U} \operatorname{dist}(x, K \setminus U)^s |f(x)| < \infty$; $\mathcal{C}_{M_{\text{sal}}}^{\infty, 0}$ is a sheaf of rings, $\mathcal{C}_{M_{\text{sal}}}^{\infty, s}$ is a $\mathcal{C}_{M_{\text{sal}}}^{\infty, 0}$-module, and partitions of unity guarantee $\Gamma$-acyclicity).
	- (Sobolev Sheaves after Gilles Lebeau) For $s \le 0$, state Lebeau's theorem constructing Sobolev sheaves $\mathcal{H}_{M_{\text{sal}}}^s$ on the linear subanalytic site $M_{\text{sal}}$ and their derived realization $\mathcal{H}_{M_{\text{sa}}}^s := \rho_{\text{sal}}^! \mathcal{H}_{M_{\text{sal}}}^s \in \mathcal{D}^+(\mathbb{C}_{M_{\text{sa}}})$. (Hint: For any Lipschitz open set $U$, $\mathcal{H}_{M_{\text{sal}}}^s(U) = H^s(U)$ coincides with the classical Sobolev space of distributions extendable to $H^s(\mathbb{R}^n)$; for non-Lipschitz intersections $U_1 \cap U_2$, the derived complex $\mathcal{H}^s(U_1 \cup U_2) \to \mathcal{H}^s(U_1) \oplus \mathcal{H}^s(U_2)$ provides the canonical replacement for $H^s(U_1 \cap U_2)$).

https://indico.math.cnrs.fr/event/10843/contributions/12868/, Class Field Theory and Zeta Spectral Triples by Alain Connes
	- (Philosophy of Class Field Theory & Yoneda Lemma) How does class field theory realize the principle that an algebraic number field "possesses within itself the elements of its own surpassing"? (Hint: Abelian extensions are determined entirely by internal invariants of the base field $K$, mirroring the Yoneda lemma; trace the historical progression from Kummer's ideal numbers and Dedekind's ideal classes to Chevalley's idèle class group $C_K = \mathrm{GL}_1(\mathbb{A}_K)/K^\times$, whose quotient by the connected component of the identity yields $\operatorname{Gal}(K^{\text{ab}}/K)$).
	- (Galois Theory as Group Actions on Spaces) Why is it insufficient to view Galois groups merely as abstract groups? (Hint: Galois originally formulated solvability via primitive equations of prime power degree acting transitively on pairs of roots; Galois groups must be understood through their concrete actions on spaces, where the idèle class group plays the fundamental geometric role).
	- (Adeles and Global Fields) Define the adèle ring $\mathbb{A}_K$ of a global field $K$ as introduced by André Weil. (Hint: $\mathbb{A}_K$ is the restricted direct product of all local completions $K_v$ with respect to the integer subrings $\mathcal{O}_v$, where $K$ embeds discretely and cocompactly).
	- (The Adele Class Space as a Noncommutative Space) Define the adèle class space $X_K = K^\times \backslash \mathbb{A}_K$ introduced by Alain Connes in 1996. Why does classical measure theory fail on this space? (Hint: The action of $K^\times$ on $\mathbb{A}_K$ is ergodic with respect to the Haar measure, meaning any measurable invariant function is almost everywhere constant; $X_K$ behaves as the leaf space of a foliation, serving as a prototype noncommutative space).
	- (Local-to-Global Class Field Theory via Isotropy) How does the adèle class space $X_K$ bridge local and global class field theory? (Hint: Local class field theory for a completion $K_v$ is simpler; in the adèle class space, the local multiplicative group $K_v^\times$ embeds canonically as the isotropy group of the local slice at $v$, realizing the local-to-global transition geometrically).
	- (Riemann-Weil Explicit Formulas as a Trace Formula) Explain how the local terms $\int_{K_v^\times} \frac{f(u)}{|1-u|}\,d^*u$ in the Riemann–Weil explicit formula arise from the adèle class space. (Hint: The action of the scaling operator $u \in K_v^\times$ on the transverse space has Schwartz kernel $\delta(y - ux)$, whose diagonal trace $\int \delta(x - ux)\,dx = \frac{1}{|1-u|}$ converts Weil's explicit formula into a geometric trace formula).
	- (Spectral Realization of Zeros as an Absorption Spectrum) How does the action of the idèle class group on the adèle class space realize the zeros of the Riemann zeta function and $L$-functions? (Hint: The zeros appear spectrally as an absorption spectrum / missing spectral lines, which geometrically explains the minus sign appearing in front of the sum of local orbital integrals in the explicit formula).
	- (The Scaling Site without Adeles) Define the scaling site $X_{\mathbb{Q}} = \mathbb{Q}^\times \backslash \mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}} / \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^\times$ and describe its presentation in terms of rank-1 subgroups of $\mathbb{R}$. (Hint: $X_{\mathbb{Q}}$ can be realized without adèles as the space of rank-1 additive subgroups of $\mathbb{R}$, such as $\mathbb{Z}[1/p]$; the visible generic orbit is $\mathbb{R}_+^*$, and the periodic orbits have lengths $\log p$ corresponding to prime numbers $p$).
	- (Constructing Finite Covers of the Scaling Site) Let $G$ be a finite abelian group and let $\chi: \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^\times \twoheadrightarrow G$ be a surjective continuous homomorphism. How is the corresponding finite cover of the scaling site constructed? (Hint: Take the balanced product $X_{\mathbb{Q}} \times_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^\times} G$, yielding a space where $G$ acts transitively on the fibers over the double quotient).
	- (Ramification and Periodic Orbits) How does ramification of a number field extension manifest in the finite cover of the scaling site? (Hint: For an unramified prime $p$, the preimage of the periodic orbit $C_p$ (of length $\log p$) decomposes into connected circles corresponding to the places lying over $p$, each acting as the mapping torus of the Frobenius automorphism).
	- (Artin Reciprocity and the Kronecker-Weber Theorem) How does the mapping torus structure of periodic orbits in the scaling site embody the Kronecker–Weber theorem and Artin reciprocity? (Hint: Every finite abelian extension of $\mathbb{Q}$ is cyclotomic; on residue fields $\mathbb{F}_p \hookrightarrow \mathbb{F}_q$, the Galois group is generated by the Frobenius automorphism $x \mapsto x^p$, which matches the monodromy around the periodic orbit $C_p$ in the fiber bundle).
	- (Dense Orbits and the 3D Geometry of Spec Z) How does the generic dense orbit in the adèle class space relate to the étale fundamental group $\pi_1^{\text{ét}}$ and the 3D topology of $\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}$? (Hint: In the Mazur–Mumford–Artin analogy, $\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}$ behaves as a 3-manifold where primes are knots; the generic point $\eta$ is everywhere dense, corresponding directly to the generic orbit of the idèle class group that is dense and intersects every periodic orbit $C_p$).
	- (The Scaling Site Topos and Frequencies) Formulate the scaling site as a semiringed topos over the multiplicative semigroup of natural numbers. (Hint: It is described by the semidirect product $[0, \infty) \rtimes \mathbb{N}^\times$, where $[0, \infty)$ represents continuous scaling/frequencies and $\mathbb{N}^\times$ acts by multiplication/harmonics, evaluating over the tropical semifield $\mathbb{R}_{\max}$ associated with $\mathbb{F}_1$).

https://www.math.stonybrook.edu/~mmovshev/MAT570Spring2008/BOOKS/atiyahbott_moment.pdf, The Moment Map and Equivariant Cohomology by Atiyah and Bott
	- (The Borel Construction and Equivariant Cohomology) Define the equivariant cohomology $H_G^*(M)$ of a $G$-space $M$ using the Borel homotopy quotient construction. (Hint: Form the mixed space $M_G = EG \times_G M = (EG \times M)/G$, where $G$ acts freely on the right of the contractible universal space $EG$ and on the left of $M$. Define $H_G^*(M) := H^*(M_G)$ as the ordinary cohomology of the fiber bundle $M_G \to BG$ with fiber $M$ over the classifying space $BG$).
	- (Equivariant Coefficient Rings) What is the coefficient ring of equivariant cohomology when $M = \{*\}$ is a single point, and how does this enlarge the coefficient structure for general $G$-spaces? (Hint: $H_G^*(\{*\}) \cong H^*(BG)$; for a compact Lie group $G$ with maximal torus $T$ and Weyl group $W$, show that $H_G^*(\{*\} ; \mathbb{R}) \cong S(\mathfrak{t}^*)^W$ is the polynomial ring of $W$-invariant polynomials on the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$, turning $H_G^*(M)$ into a module over $H^*(BG)$).
	- (The Cartan Model and Equivariant de Rham Complex) Formulate the de Rham model of equivariant cohomology for a smooth $G$-manifold $M$ with compact Lie group $G$. (Hint: Define the space of equivariant differential forms $\Omega_G^*(M) = (S(\mathfrak{g}^*) \otimes \Omega^*(M))^G$ as polynomial maps $\alpha: \mathfrak{g} \to \Omega^*(M)$ invariant under the diagonal adjoint/$G$-action. Define the Cartan differential $d_G \alpha(X) = d(\alpha(X)) - \iota_{X_M}(\alpha(X))$ for $X \in \mathfrak{g}$, and prove that $d_G^2 = 0$ on invariant forms).
	- (Equivariantly Closed Forms and the Moment Map) Let $(M, \omega)$ be a symplectic manifold equipped with a Hamiltonian action of a compact Lie group $G$ with equivariant moment map $\mu: M \to \mathfrak{g}^*$. Show that the modified symplectic 2-form $\widetilde{\omega}(X) = \omega + \langle \mu, X \rangle$ is an equivariantly closed form in the Cartan model. (Hint: Compute $d_G \widetilde{\omega}(X) = (d - \iota_{X_M})(\omega + \langle \mu, X \rangle) = d\omega - \iota_{X_M}\omega + d\langle \mu, X \rangle$; verify that this vanishes identically using $d\omega = 0$ and Hamilton's equation $d\langle \mu, X \rangle = \iota_{X_M}\omega$).
	- (The Atiyah-Bott / Berline-Vergne Localization Formula) State and prove the Atiyah–Bott localization theorem in equivariant de Rham cohomology for an equivariantly closed form $\alpha \in \Omega_G^*(M)$ on a compact oriented manifold $M$. (Hint: For isolated fixed points $p \in M^G$, the integral over $M$ localizes completely to the fixed-point set: $\int_M \alpha(X) = \sum_{p \in M^G} \frac{\alpha(X)(p)}{\det\left(\frac{1}{2\pi} L_p(X)\right)}$, where $L_p(X) \in \mathfrak{so}(T_p M)$ is the infinitesimal rotation induced by $X \in \mathfrak{g}$ on the tangent space).
	- (Duistermaat-Heckman Theorem and Exact Stationary Phase) Explain how the localization formula proves the Duistermaat–Heckman theorem, establishing that the stationary phase approximation for oscillatory Hamiltonian path integrals is exact. (Hint: For an $S^1$-action on a symplectic manifold $(M, \omega)$ with moment map $H$, the Fourier transform of the Liouville pushforward measure $\int_M e^{i t H} \frac{\omega^n}{n!}$ is evaluated exactly by the localized sum over the critical points of $H$, which are the fixed points of the circle action).
	- (Algebraic Mechanism of Localization: Inverting Primes in H*(BG)) Detail the commutative algebra underpinning the localization theorem in the case of a circle group $G = S^1$. (Hint: The coefficient ring is $H^*(BS^1; \mathbb{Q}) \cong \mathbb{Q}[u]$ (a PID). Every finitely generated module over $\mathbb{Q}[u]$ decomposes into a free part and a torsion part; localizing by inverting the polynomial generator $u$ kills all $\mathbb{Q}[u]$-torsion supported outside the fixed-point set $M^{S^1}$, establishing an isomorphism $H_{S^1}^*(M) \otimes_{\mathbb{Q}[u]} \mathbb{Q}[u, u^{-1}] \cong H^*(M^{S^1}) \otimes \mathbb{Q}[u, u^{-1}]$).
	- (Moment Maps, Morse Theory, and Norm-Square Functional) Let $\mu: M \to \mathfrak{g}^*$ be an equivariant moment map. How did Atiyah and Bott utilize the norm-square functional $f = \|\mu\|^2: M \to \mathbb{R}$ to compute the topology of moduli spaces? (Hint: $f$ is a $G$-invariant, minimally degenerate Morse–Bott function whose critical points correspond to the stratification of $M$ into unstable and semistable loci in Geometric Invariant Theory; this provides equivariant Morse inequalities that compute the Betti numbers of the symplectic quotient $M /\!\!/ G = \mu^{-1}(0)/G$).
	- (Moduli Spaces of Bundles and the Narasimhan-Seshadri Theorem) How does the Atiyah–Bott framework link infinite-dimensional equivariant localization to Donaldson's proof of the Narasimhan–Seshadri theorem on Riemann surfaces? (Hint: Consider the infinite-dimensional affine space of unitary connections $\mathcal{A}$ on a principal bundle over a Riemann surface $\Sigma$, acted on by the gauge group $\mathcal{G}$. The curvature $F_A$ acts as the moment map for the gauge action, and the symplectic reduction $\mathcal{A} /\!\!/ \mathcal{G} = \mu^{-1}(0)/\mathcal{G}$ yields the moduli space of flat/stable holomorphic vector bundles).
	- (Equivariant Index Theory and the Atiyah-Segal Fixed Point Formula) How does the de Rham localization theorem mirror the Atiyah–Segal equivariant index theorem in $K$-theory? (Hint: The index of an equivariant elliptic operator $D$ on a $G$-manifold is expressed as a character of $G$, and its evaluation at an element $g \in G$ localizes to an integral of topological characteristic classes over the fixed-point submanifold $M^g$, divided by the equivariant Euler class of the normal bundle).

https://www.its.caltech.edu/~matilde/EntropySlides2021Ma148.pdf, Entropy, Information Algebras, and the Field with One Element by Matilde Marcolli
	- (Information, Shannon Entropy, and Khinchin Axioms) Define Shannon information $I(p_i) = -\log p_i$ and the Shannon entropy $S(P) = -\sum_{i=1}^n p_i \log p_i$ for a probability distribution $P = (p_1, \dots, p_n)$. State the Khinchin axioms (continuity, maximality at the uniform distribution, expandability by zero-probability states, and additivity/extensivity for independent subsystems: $S(P \otimes Q) = S(P) + S(Q)$). (Hint: Prove the uniqueness theorem showing that any functional satisfying the Khinchin axioms is a positive constant multiple of the Shannon entropy).
	- (Rényi Entropy, Escort Distributions, and Thermodynamics) Define the Rényi entropy of order $\alpha \ge 0$ ($\alpha \ne 1$) by $S_\alpha(P) = \frac{1}{1-\alpha} \log \left( \sum_{i=1}^n p_i^\alpha \right)$. Show that $\lim_{\alpha \to 1} S_\alpha(P) = S(P)$. Define the escort distribution $P^{(\beta)}$ with partition function $Z(\beta) = \sum_{i=1}^n p_i^\beta$, and relate Rényi entropy to the thermodynamic Helmholtz free energy $F(\beta) = -\frac{1}{\beta} \log Z(\beta)$ and the Gibbs equilibrium relation $S = \beta(E - F)$. (Hint: Rényi entropy relaxes strong additivity to additivity for independent subsystems; its derivative with respect to $\alpha$ connects to the Kullback–Leibler divergence $D_{\text{KL}}(P^{(\alpha)} \parallel P) \ge 0$).
	- (Tsallis Entropy and q-Deformations) Define the Tsallis non-extensive entropy $S_q(P) = \frac{1}{q-1}\left(1 - \sum_{i=1}^n p_i^q\right) = -\sum_{i=1}^n p_i^q \ln_q(p_i)$, where $\ln_q(x) = \frac{x^{1-q}-1}{1-q}$ is the $q$-logarithm. Show that $\lim_{q \to 1} S_q(P) = S(P)$, and formulate the non-extensive pseudo-additivity rule for independent subsystems: $S_q(A \star B) = S_q(A) + S_q(B) + (1-q)S_q(A)S_q(B)$. (Hint: Tsallis entropy is inspired by Jackson $q$-derivatives and governs non-extensive statistical mechanics).
	- (Asymptotics of Multinomial Coefficients and Types) For an alphabet of $N$ symbols and an empirical frequency distribution $P = (k_1/n, \dots, k_N/n)$ of words of length $n = \sum k_i$, evaluate the asymptotic limit of the multinomial coefficient via Stirling's approximation: $\lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{1}{n} \log \binom{n}{k_1, \dots, k_N} = S(P)$. (Hint: In the method of types, the number of sequences of a given type $P$ scales exponentially as $e^{n S(P)}$; maximizing the multinomial coefficient corresponds to the maximum entropy principle).
	- (Combinatorics over F1 vs. Fq-Geometry) Detail the dictionary comparing classical information theory to geometry over the field with one element $\mathbb{F}_1$ and its $q$-deformation over finite fields $\mathbb{F}_q$:
	1. Messages / Words of length $n \longleftrightarrow$ Basis elements of vector spaces over $\mathbb{F}_1$ vs. $\mathbb{F}_q^n$.
	2. Number of 1s (Hamming weight) $\longleftrightarrow$ Vector subspace dimension $k$.
	3. Number of messages $\binom{n}{k} \longleftrightarrow$ Gaussian binomial coefficients (Grassmannians) $\left[ \begin{matrix} n \\ k \end{matrix} \right]_q = \#\operatorname{Gr}(k, n)(\mathbb{F}_q)$, where $\lim_{q \to 1} \left[ \begin{matrix} n \\ k \end{matrix} \right]_q = \binom{n}{k}$.
	4. Projective spaces $\mathbb{P}^{n-1}(\mathbb{F}_1) = \{1, \dots, n\}$ as finite sets of cardinality $n$.
	(Hint: Show how Tsallis $q$-deformed entropy corresponds to counting subspaces in $\mathbb{F}_q$-geometry, while Shannon entropy recovers the classical limit as $q \to 1$ over $\mathbb{F}_1$).
	- (Finite Logarithms and Finite Polylogarithms) On a finite field $\mathbb{F}_p$, define the finite logarithm as a solution to the functional equation $\phi(xy) = \phi(x) + \phi(y)$ for $x, y \in \mathbb{F}_p^\times$. Define the finite polylogarithm $\operatorname{Li}_n^{(p)}(x) = \sum_{k=1}^{p-1} \frac{x^k}{k^n} \in \mathbb{F}_p[x]$, and compute its mod-$p$ differential and Frobenius periodicity relations. (Hint: Regard the functional equation cohomologically as the condition that $\phi$ is an additive 1-cocycle in group cohomology $H^1(\mathbb{F}_p^\times, \mathbb{F}_p)$).
	- (Categorical Characterization of Entropy: Baez-Fritz-Leinster) Let $\mathbf{FinProb}$ be the category of finite probability spaces $(X, P)$, where morphisms $f: (X, P) \to (Y, Q)$ are measure-preserving maps ($Q(y) = \sum_{x \in f^{-1}(y)} P(x)$). State the Baez–Fritz–Leinster theorem characterizing information loss $L(f) = S(P) - S(Q)$: prove that any functor $L: \mathbf{FinProb} \to (\mathbb{R}_{\ge 0}, +)$ satisfying convex linearity $L(\lambda f \oplus (1-\lambda) g) = \lambda L(f) + (1-\lambda) L(g)$ and continuity is a constant non-negative multiple of the Shannon information loss. (Hint: Adapt the axioms to Tsallis entropy by replacing convex linearity with $q$-deformed non-extensive convex combinations).
	- (Information Geometry and the Fisher-Rao Metric) On a statistical manifold $\mathcal{M} = \{P_\theta = (p_1(\theta), \dots, p_n(\theta))\}$, define the Fisher–Rao information metric $g_{ij}(\theta) = \sum_{k=1}^n \frac{1}{p_k(\theta)} \frac{\partial p_k}{\partial \theta^i} \frac{\partial p_k}{\partial \theta^j} = \mathbb{E}_\theta \left[ \frac{\partial \log p}{\partial \theta^i} \frac{\partial \log p}{\partial \theta^j} \right]$. Show that the coordinate transformation $X_k = \sqrt{p_k}$ embeds $\mathcal{M}$ isometrically into a positive Euclidean sphere $S^{n-1}$ with metric $ds^2 = 4 \sum dX_k^2$. (Hint: Show that the Fisher–Rao metric is the Hessian of the Kullback–Leibler divergence: $g_{ij}(\theta) = \left. \frac{\partial^2}{\partial \theta_1^i \partial \theta_2^j} D_{\text{KL}}(P_{\theta_1} \parallel P_{\theta_2}) \right|_{\theta_1 = \theta_2 = \theta}$).
	- (Frobenius Manifolds and Statistical Quantum Cohomology) Define a Frobenius manifold $(M, g, \Phi)$ following Dubrovin and Manin, where $g$ is a flat metric and $\Phi$ is a potential function satisfying the WDVV (Witten–Dijkgraaf–Verlinde–Verlinde) associativity equations: $c_{ijk} = \frac{\partial^3 \Phi}{\partial t^i \partial t^j \partial t^k}$ defines an associative, commutative multiplication $\circ$ on the tangent bundle $TM$. Explain how statistical manifolds equipped with Amari–Chentsov cubic tensors $C(\partial_i, \partial_j, \partial_k) = \mathbb{E}\left[\partial_i \log p \cdot \partial_j \log p \cdot \partial_k \log p\right]$ connect to Frobenius structures in Gromov–Witten theory.
	- (Hochschild Cohomology of Information Structures: Vigneaux's Theorem) Let $\mathcal{S}$ be the category of observables (random variables on finite probability spaces) with morphisms given by joint measurements / refinements. Define the Hochschild cochain complex $C^n(\mathcal{S}, \mathcal{F}) = \operatorname{Hom}(\mathcal{S}^{\otimes n}, \mathcal{F})$ for an information structure. State Pierre Baudot and Juan-Pablo Vigneaux's theorem:
	1. The 0-th Hochschild cohomology $H^0(\mathcal{S}, \mathbb{R}) \cong \mathbb{R}$ when $\alpha = 1$, and vanishes otherwise.
	2. The 1-cocycles $Z^1(\mathcal{S}, \mathbb{R})$ satisfying the cocycle condition $F(X \vee Y) = F(X) + F(Y \mid X)$ (the entropy chain rule) are spanned uniquely by the Shannon entropy (for extensive additivity) and by the Tsallis entropy $S_\alpha$ (for non-extensive deformations).
	(Hint: The entropy chain rule is the 1-cocycle condition in the Hochschild complex of the information algebra).

https://people.math.harvard.edu/~lurie/papers/Conceptual.pdf, Makkai Conceptual Completeness and Ultracategories by Jacob Lurie
	- (From Stone Duality to Conceptual Completeness) Review classical Stone duality for Boolean algebras: the contravariant functor $\operatorname{Spec}: \mathbf{Bool}^{\text{op}} \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathbf{Stone}$ establishes an anti-equivalence between Boolean algebras (propositional syntax) and compact totally disconnected Hausdorff spaces (Stone spaces of models/points). Explain how Michael Makkai generalized this duality from propositional logic to first-order coherent logic by replacing Boolean algebras with small pretopoi $\mathcal{E}$ and Stone spaces with ultracategories of models $\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{E})$. (Hint: Points/models in $\operatorname{Spec}(B)$ are 2-valued homomorphisms $B \to \{0, 1\}$; in coherent logic, models of a pretopos $\mathcal{E}$ are coherent/left-exact functors $\mathcal{E} \to \mathbf{Set}$ preserving finite disjoint coproducts and effective quotients).
	- (Definition of Ultracategories and Ultraproducts) Define an ultracategory $\mathcal{C}$ in the sense of Makkai and Lurie. (Hint: An ultracategory is a category $\mathcal{C}$ equipped with an ultrastructure functor $\int_{\mathcal{U}}: \mathcal{C}^I \to \mathcal{C}$ for every set $I$ and every ultrafilter $\mathcal{U}$ on $I$, specifying ultraproducts $\prod_{\mathcal{U}} X_i$ that satisfy transitivity/coherence under Fubini-type iterations of ultrafilters and principal ultrafilter trivializations $\int_{\delta_i} (X_j)_{j \in I} \cong X_i$).
	- (Ultrafunctors and Symmetries of Ultraproducts) Define an ultrafunctor $F: \mathcal{C} \to \mathcal{D}$ between ultracategories. (Hint: $F$ is a functor equipped with coherent natural isomorphisms $F\left(\int_{\mathcal{U}} X_i\right) \cong \int_{\mathcal{U}} F(X_i)$ commuting with all ultraproduct operations).
	- (Makkai's Conceptual Completeness Theorem) State Makkai's Conceptual Completeness Theorem as formulated by Lurie: for any small pretopos $\mathcal{E}$, let $\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{E}) = \operatorname{Coh}(\mathcal{E}, \mathbf{Set})$ be its category of models equipped with its canonical ultracategory structure via pointwise ultraproducts in $\mathbf{Set}$. Prove that the evaluation functor
	$$\operatorname{ev}: \mathcal{E} \xrightarrow{\sim} \mathbf{Ult}(\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{E}), \mathbf{Set})$$
	is an equivalence of categories between the small pretopos $\mathcal{E}$ and the category of ultrafunctors from $\operatorname{Mod}(\mathcal{E})$ to $\mathbf{Set}$. (Hint: This proves that a coherent first-order theory $\mathcal{E}$ is entirely and uniquely recoverable from its category of models together with the ultraproduct operations on those models).
	- (Ultrastructures as Categorical Integration / Stokes' Formula) Detail Lurie's formulation of ultrastructures as a family of categorical integration operations. (Hint: Think of ultrafilters as $\{0, 1\}$-valued finitely additive measures $\mathcal{U}: \mathcal{P}(I) \to \{0, 1\}$; the ultraproduct functor $\int_{\mathcal{U}}: \mathcal{C}^I \to \mathcal{C}$ acts as an integration operator with respect to $\mathcal{U}$, and the coherence axioms mirror Fubini's theorem and Stokes'-type boundary-compatibility conditions on ultracategories).
	- (Stone Duality as a Chu Construction / *-Autonomous Duality) Explain how Stone duality and Gödel's completeness theorem can be viewed through the lens of the Chu construction over $\mathbf{Set}$ with dualizing object $2 = \{0, 1\}$. (Hint: A Chu space $(\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{X}, e)$ pairs a syntactic object $\mathcal{A}$ with a semantic space $\mathcal{X}$ via an evaluation pairing $e: \mathcal{A} \times \mathcal{X} \to \{0, 1\}$; in Stone duality, $\mathcal{A}$ is a Boolean algebra, $\mathcal{X} = \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Bool}}(\mathcal{A}, 2)$ is the set of prime filters/models, and the bidual evaluation $\mathcal{A} \xrightarrow{\sim} \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{Stone}}(\operatorname{Spec}(\mathcal{A}), 2)$ is an isomorphism of *-autonomous systems).
	- (Ultrafilters as Codensity Monads and Leinster's Heuristic) State Tom Leinster's theorem characterizing the ultrafilter monad $\beta: \mathbf{Set} \to \mathbf{Set}$ as the codensity monad of the inclusion functor $\mathbf{FinSet} \hookrightarrow \mathbf{Set}$. (Hint: The Stone–Čech compactification $\beta(X)$ is the maximal compactification of a discrete set $X$; integrating a bounded or finite-image function $f: X \to \mathbb{R}$ against an ultrafilter $\mathcal{U} \in \beta(X)$ picks out the unique value $r \in \mathbb{R}$ such that $f^{-1}(r) \in \mathcal{U}$, acting as an ultra-mode / point-evaluation on large sets).
	- (Connections to Condensed Mathematics and Liquid Vector Spaces) How does Makkai's ultracategory framework relate to Clausen–Scholze condensed mathematics? (Hint: Condensed sets are sheaves on the pro-étale site of a point, testable against extremally disconnected compact Hausdorff spaces $\beta I$; in Makkai's framework, ultraproducts $\prod_{\mathcal{U}} X_i$ handle infinite limits and compact projections without topological pathologies, providing an alternate categorical path to measure theory and liquid vector spaces via ultrafilter integration).

https://www.uni-muenster.de/IVV5WS/WebHop/user/nikolaus/Papers/sheaves-on-manifolds.pdf, Sheaves on Manifolds, Six Functors, and Efimov K-Theory by Thomas Nikolaus
	- (Sheaves with Values in the Derived Category vs. Derived Category of Sheaves) Let $X$ be a locally compact Hausdorff space. Define the $\infty$-category $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}))$ as the $\infty$-category of functors $F: \operatorname{Open}(X)^{\text{op}} \to \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})$ satisfying descent: for any open cover $U \cup V$, the Mayer–Vietoris square is a pullback in $\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})$, and more generally $F(\bigcup_i U_i) \xrightarrow{\sim} \lim_{I} F(U_i)$. (Hint: Contrast $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}))$ with the ordinary derived category of sheaves of abelian groups $\mathcal{D}(\operatorname{Shv}(X, \mathbf{Ab}))$. Show that the latter is the Bousfield localization of $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}))$ at hypercovers, i.e. the $\infty$-category of hypersheaves, and that both coincide when $X$ is paracompact with finite covering dimension).
	- (Pushforward, Pullback, and Global Sections) Let $f: Y \to \{*\}$ be the projection to a point. Show that $\operatorname{Shv}(\{*\}, \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})) \simeq \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z})$. For $F \in \operatorname{Shv}(Y; \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}))$, verify that the pushforward $f_* F \simeq F(Y)$ recovers global sections, and describe the pullback functor $f^*: \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}) \to \operatorname{Shv}(Y; \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}))$ assigning constant sheaves. (Hint: Formulate $f^*$ and $f_*$ as an adjoint pair $(f^* \dashv f_*)$ on presentable stable $\infty$-categories).
	- (Presentable Stable Categories and the Category Pr^L) Define the $\infty$-category $\mathbf{Pr}^L$ whose objects are presentable $\infty$-categories and whose morphisms are left adjoints (colimit-preserving functors). (Hint: Explain how the Adjoint Functor Theorem ensures that any colimit-preserving functor between presentable categories is uniquely determined by its restriction to the subcategory of $\kappa$-compact generators via the Yoneda embedding, and discuss the Beck–Chevalley condition for commutative squares).
	- (Compactly Assembled Infinity-Categories) Define what it means for a morphism $f: C \to D$ in an $\infty$-category to be compact, and define a compactly assembled $\infty$-category. (Hint: A morphism $f$ is compact if $\operatorname{Map}(D, -)$ maps filtered colimits along $f$ to colimits in spaces. An object $X$ is weakly/strongly compact if $\operatorname{id}_X$ satisfies the compact morphism condition; this generalizes compact generation to categories lacking enough compact objects, such as $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}))$ for non-compact $X$).
	- (Compact Assembly and Continuous Posets) Explain why a poset viewed as a category is compactly assembled if and only if it is a continuous poset / continuous domain. (Hint: In a continuous poset, every element is the directed supremum of elements that are "way below" it, mirroring how objects in a compactly assembled category are filtered colimits of compact morphisms).
	- (Limits, Colimits, and Tensor Products in Pr_ca^L) Describe the limits, colimits, and Lurie symmetric monoidal tensor products $\mathcal{C} \otimes \mathcal{D}$ in the $\infty$-category $\mathbf{Pr}_{\text{ca}}^L$ of compactly assembled presentable categories. (Hint: Limits in $\mathbf{Pr}^L$ are computed on underlying categories, whereas colimits pass to limits of right adjoints; discuss how $\mathbf{Pr}_{\text{ca}}^L$ inherits a closed symmetric monoidal structure where dualizable objects exhibit monoidal duality).
	- (Verdier Sequences and Verdier Quotients) Define a Verdier sequence in the $\infty$-category $\mathbf{Cat}_\infty^{\text{ex}}$ of small idempotent-complete stable $\infty$-categories:
	$$\mathcal{A} \hookrightarrow \mathcal{B} \twoheadrightarrow \mathcal{C}.$$
	(Hint: A Verdier sequence is both a fiber and cofiber sequence in $\mathbf{Cat}_\infty^{\text{ex}}$; the quotient $\mathcal{C} \simeq \mathcal{B}/\mathcal{A}$ is obtained via Dwyer–Kan localization / Verdier localization at the morphisms whose cones lie in $\mathcal{A}$).
	- (Connective vs. Non-Connective Algebraic K-Theory) Recall Quillen's connective algebraic $K$-theory functor $K: \mathbf{Cat}_\infty^{\text{perf}} \to \mathbf{Sp}_{\ge 0}$. Why does connective $K$-theory fail to send Verdier sequences to fiber sequences of spectra? (Hint: The negative $K$-groups $K_{-n}$ are non-trivial for non-regular schemes or non-smooth algebras; the Eilenberg swindle can kill positive $K$-theory on infinite-dimensional Calkin-type categories without vanishing homotopically).
	- (Efimov Continuous K-Theory) Define Alexander Efimov's continuous (non-connective) $K$-theory functor $K^{\text{cont}}: \mathbf{Pr}_{\text{dual}}^L \to \mathbf{Sp}$ on the $\infty$-category of dualizable presentable stable $\infty$-categories. (Hint: Efimov $K$-theory is the universal localizing invariant preserving filtered colimits and mapping Verdier/fiber sequences of dualizable categories to fiber/cofiber sequences of spectra, extending the Blumberg–Gepner–Tabuada framework of noncommutative motives $\mathbf{NcMot}$).
	- (Local Rigidity and Frobenius Algebras) Define a locally rigid symmetric monoidal presentable category $\mathcal{C}$. (Hint: A category is locally rigid if its compact objects are dualizable; explain how local rigidity turns $\mathcal{C}$ into a commutative Frobenius algebra object in $\mathbf{Pr}^L$, equipped with a non-degenerate evaluation/coevaluation pairing).
	- (String Diagrams and Poincaré Duality of Morphisms) How does the Frobenius algebra structure on a locally rigid category $\mathcal{C}$ enable a string diagram calculus for the six operations? (Hint: Morphisms and adjoints $(f^* \dashv f_*)$ and $(f_! \dashv f^!)$ are represented as 2D string diagrams / bordisms, where Poincaré–Verdier duality corresponds to rotating strings by $180^\circ$).
	- (Verdier Self-Duality of Sheaves of Spectra) Formulate the Verdier self-duality theorem for the $\infty$-category $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathbf{Sp})$ of sheaves of spectra on a locally compact manifold $X$. (Hint: Show that $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathbf{Sp})$ is a dualizable, locally rigid presentable category whose categorical dual is canonically equivalent to $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathbf{Sp})$ itself, exchanging compact pushforward $f_!$ with exceptional pullback $f^!$).
	- (Cohomology with Compact Support and Borel-Moore Homology via Six Functors) Let $t: X \to \{*\}$ be the unique terminal map. Express standard topological invariants in terms of the six operations on the tensor unit $\mathbf{1} = \mathbb{S} \in \operatorname{Shv}(\{*\}, \mathbf{Sp})$:
	1. Ordinary cohomology: $H^*(X) \simeq t_* t^* \mathbf{1}$.
	2. Cohomology with compact support: $H_c^*(X) \simeq t_! t^* \mathbf{1}$.
	3. Locally finite (Borel–Moore) homology: $H_*^{\text{BM}}(X) \simeq t_* t^! \mathbf{1}$.
	4. Standard homology: $H_*(X) \simeq t_! t^! \mathbf{1}$.
	(Hint: Use the adjunctions $(t^* \dashv t_*)$ and $(t_! \dashv t^!)$ together with the self-duality of $\operatorname{Shv}(X; \mathbf{Sp})$ to prove Poincaré duality $H_c^*(X)^\vee \cong H_*(X)$ and $H^*(X)^\vee \cong H_*^{\text{BM}}(X)$).

https://arxiv.org/pdf/2605.11950, Weil-Moore anima (paper)
	- (Page 1) Define the Weil group of the rational number field $\mathbb{Q}$. (Hint: a locally compact Hausdorff group that refines the absolute Galois group by a surjective homomorphism with a connected kernel)
	- (Page 1) How does the Weil group of the rational number field $\mathbb{Q}$ determine an analogous Weil group for an arbitrary number field? (Hint: take a pullback along an inclusion.)
	- (Page 2) How does one do unite Artin L-functions attached to complex Galois representations, and Hecke L-functions attached to Hecker characters? (Hint: agree one a one dimensional representation of the Weil group, to be composed with the Artin map to get a Hecke character. So there is a one to one correspondence between 1 dimensional Galois representations and Hecke characters OF FINITE IMAGE. Artin reciprocity says that the corresponding Artin and Hecke L-functions are equal.)
	- (Page 2) What are the possible generalisations of the Weil group? (Hint: not all Artin representations are 1D and built from direct sums and inductions, not all Hecke characters have finite image.)
	- (Page 2) What is the key idea of the absolute Galois group? (Hint: absolute Galois group of inverse limit of coarser terms, $\mathrm{Gal}(K^ab/\mathbb{Q})$ in short exact sequences of groups with finite quotient and abelian kernel. These are classified up to isomorphism by a group cohomology classes and cohomological calass field theory produces canonical such classes.)
	- (Page 2) What is Clausen thinking of the Weil group? (Hint: number field should not be a $K(\pi,1)$ and the Weil group should not be the fundamental group, there should be higher homotopy groups. But the problem is that you do not want topological groups, you want condensed anima.)
	- (Page 3) Why use the definition condensed? How does one invent condensed sets? (Hint: Start with a set. Do not specify opens, specifies families of points that "condense" to form a compact Hausdorff space, since this is what you want. Using the fact that every compact Hausdorff space is a quotient of a profinite set by a profinite equivalence relation, you can explode these compact Hausdorff spaces into profinite sets)
	- (Page 3) What is an anima? (Hint: it is a homotopy type viewed as an object in the natural ambient infinity-category, Beilison describes set as static as unable to move around. Consider an anima $A$, with its underlying set $\pi_0 A$, the point is free to move around within its connected component. If we have a $CW-complex$, the anima is what remains when you remove the point-set incarnation.)
	- (Page 4) What is the important point in the Weil group definition? (Hint: it must have an Artin map from the domain of Hecke characters $\mathbb{A}^\times_K / K^\times$ to the absolute Galois group, this is phrased as a cohomology calculation. The first cohomology of the classifying space $BW_\mathbb{K}$ with its coefficients in a torus $\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$ is Pontryagin dual to idele class group $\mathbb{A}_K^*/K^*$. The higher cohomology groups are not to vanish. Add higher homotopy groups to make the abelisation better cvrrespond to idele class group of a global field$\mathbb{A}_K^*/K^*$. This Pontryagin duality is of course the starting point of doing harmonic analysis here.)
	- (Page 4) Summarise Theorem 1.2 in the Weil-Moore anima paper. (Hint: condensed anima $X_\mathbb{Q}$ exist such that $\pi_0 X_\mathbb{Q}$ as a condensed set is a point, $\pi_1 X_\mathbb{Q}$ as a condensed set is a Weil group of the rational numbers, $\pi_n X_\mathbb{Q}$ as a condensed set for $n > 1$ is compact Hausdorff, and for any number field if $X_K \rightarrow X_\mathbb{Q}$ denotes the corresponding finite etale cover, then the higher cohomology groups vanish. This last property essentially is to force canonical isomorphisms to refine the Artin map.)
	- (Page 4) How does one build spheres using Eilenberg-Maclane spaces by homotopy? (Hint: inductive procedure to build up the Postnikov tower of $S^n$.)
	- (Page 4) How does one build the Weil-Moore anima using this idea of Serre with building spheres using Eilenberg-Maclane spaces by homotopy? (Hint: inductive procedure to build up the Postnikov tower of $S^n$, instead, build the Weil-Moore anima $X_\mathbb{Q}$ from $BW_\mathbb{Q} = K(W_\mathbb{Q}, 1)$)
	- (Page 4) How is the Fargues-Fontaine curve related to this? (Hint: it should be the condensed homotopy type underlying the space of closed points in the global analogue of the Fargues-Fontaine curve.)
	- (Page 5) How does the cohomology of the Weil-Moore anima improve Galois cohomology (imprecisely)? (Hint: simpler version of Tate's global Euler characteristic formula similar to that of function fields, versions of global and local Poitou-Tate duality, cohomological dimensions)
	- (Page 7) What is the "fundamental class" for Poitou-Tate duality? (Hint: calculation of the Brauer group of a number field, specifically the kernel of the sum of the invariant maps from the local Brauer groups.)
	- (Page 7) According to Fontaine, how much information does the $p$-adic representations of the absolute Galois group of $\mathbb{Q_p}$ have? (Hint: a lot of $p$-adic Hodge theory information)
	- (Page 7) Summarise where does Galois cohomology appear? (Hint: (1) reformulation of the Dirichlet class number formula, Kato's generalised Iwasawa main conjecture. Reformulate Kato's conjecture as having trivialisations using the non-compactly supported cohomology of the Weil-Moore anima. Replace Galois representation with a Weil-Moore representation, for a $p$-adic, discrete coefficient ring it gives Galois representations, but one can try to find a "spectral decomposition" of the compactly supported cohomology of Shimura varieties.)
	- (Page 10) What is a twistor line? (Hint: nontrivial real form of $\mathbb{P}^1$).
	- (Page 23) What is a definition of a almost compact, locally compact Hausdorff group? Note that the words locally compact is omitted for brevity usually. (Hint: admits compact normal subgroup that is i.e. itself compact, the quotient with the compact normal subgroup is the additive integers, or the quotient with the compact normal subgroup is the additive reals.)
	- (Page 38) How does one produce the Moore anima? (Hint: obstruction theory from homotopy theory based on the Postnikov tower.)
	- (Page 45) How does one motivate the notion of class formations? (Hint: give a collection of generators for the cyclic group of  the second cohomology, one can then use the Tate-Nakayama to deduce the cup product induces an isomorphism in Tate cohomology. Reformulate it as a Galois category instead, one can then use Grothendieck-Galois theory and the theory of fibre functors. One also needs cocycle-level version of the data from the $H^2$.)
	- (Page 70) How does one associate Galois groupoids to Galois categories? (Hint: $2$-category of Galois categories where objects are Galois categories, $1$-morphisms are exact functors is a $(2,1)$-category identifying with the opposite whose objects are the profinite groups, morphisms are continuous morphisms. Now, use this topological category to be realised as the full subcategory of condensed anima spanned by the objects of the form $BG$, where $G$ is profinite group.)

https://stacks.math.columbia.edu/download/algebra.pdf, Commutative Algebra in the Stacks Project
	- (Notational Conventions for Contraction of Primes) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be a ring map and let $\mathfrak{q} \in \operatorname{Spec}(S)$ be a prime ideal. Explain the Stacks Project convention of writing the contraction $\phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})$ as $R \cap \mathfrak{q}$. (Hint: This notation is standardly used even when $R$ is not a subring of $S$ and $\phi$ is not injective; ideals geometrically correspond to kernels of maps to integral domains).
	- (The Snake Lemma and Connecting Homomorphisms) State the Snake Lemma for commutative diagrams of $R$-modules with exact rows:
	$$\begin{matrix} & M_1 & \xrightarrow{f_1} & M_2 & \xrightarrow{g_1} & M_3 & \to 0 \\ 0 \to & N_1 & \xrightarrow{f_2} & N_2 & \xrightarrow{g_2} & N_3 & \end{matrix}$$
	Prove that the connecting homomorphism $\delta: \ker(\gamma) \to \operatorname{coker}(\alpha)$ factors as $i' \circ \beta \circ s'$, where $s'$ is a preimage lift from $M_3$ to $M_2$ via surjectivity (unique modulo $\ker(g_1) = \operatorname{im}(f_1)$), $\beta$ is the central vertical map, and $i'$ is the projection into $\operatorname{coker}(\alpha)$. (Hint: Show that $\beta(s'(z))$ maps to $0$ in $N_3$ and therefore lifts uniquely to $N_1$, proving that $\delta$ is well-defined, yields a 6-term exact sequence, and characterizes abelian categories as categories where the Snake Lemma holds).
	- (Presentations and Lemma 10.5.2) Let $M$ be an $R$-module with a presentation $R^{\oplus m} \to R^{\oplus n} \to M \to 0$, where $n$ represents the number of generators and $m$ the number of relations. Prove Lemma 10.5.2: if $\alpha: R^{\oplus n} \to M$ and $\beta: N \to M$ are $R$-module maps such that $\operatorname{im}(\alpha) \subseteq \operatorname{im}(\beta)$, then $\alpha$ lifts to a map $\gamma: R^{\oplus n} \to N$ such that $\alpha = \beta \circ \gamma$. (Hint: Express the image of each standard basis element $e_i \in R^{\oplus n}$ as a linear combination over the generators of $N$).
	- (Finiteness Conditions and Lemma 10.5.3) Prove Lemma 10.5.3: for a short exact sequence of $R$-modules $0 \to M_1 \to M_2 \to M_3 \to 0$,
	1. If $M_2$ is finitely generated, then $M_3 \cong M_2/M_1$ is finitely generated.
	2. If $M_1$ and $M_3$ are finitely generated, then $M_2$ is finitely generated.
	3. If $M_2$ is finitely presented and $M_1$ is finitely generated, then $M_3$ is finitely presented.
	4. If $M_1$ and $M_3$ are finitely presented, then $M_2$ is finitely presented.
	5. If $M_2$ is finitely presented and $M_3$ is finitely generated, then $M_1$ is finitely generated.
	(Hint: Use the Snake Lemma and presentations of modules to prove each clause).
	- (Descending Induction on Finite Modules: Lemma 10.5.4) Let $M$ be a finitely generated $R$-module. Prove Lemma 10.5.4: there exists a finite filtration $0 = M_0 \subseteq M_1 \subseteq \dots \subseteq M_r = M$ such that each quotient $M_i/M_{i-1}$ is a cyclic $R$-module isomorphic to $R/I_i$ for some ideal $I_i \subseteq R$. (Hint: Induct on the minimal number of generators of $M$; quotient out by $r-1$ generators and use the first isomorphism theorem on the cyclic kernel).
	- (Module Generators under Base Change: Lemma 10.5.5) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be a ring map and let $M$ be an $S$-module. Prove that if $M$ is generated as an $R$-module by a subset $E \subset M$, then $E$ also generates $M$ as an $S$-module. (Hint: Regard the $R$-module structure on $M$ as the restriction of scalars along $\phi$ into the endomorphism ring $\operatorname{End}(M)$).
	- (Finite Type and Finitely Presented Algebras: Lemma 10.6.2) Let $S$ be an $R$-algebra. Distinguish between $S$ being of finite type and of finite presentation over $R$. (Hint: $S$ is of finite type if there is a surjection $R[x_1, \dots, x_n] \twoheadrightarrow S$ (analogous to existence conditions in Stone–Čech compactifications); $S$ is of finite presentation if in addition the kernel ideal of relations is finitely generated. Prove Lemma 10.6.2: compositions of finite type/presentation maps are of finite type/presentation).
	- (Directed Systems, Colimits, and Homology: Lemma 10.8.8) Let $(C_\bullet^{(i)})_{i \in I}$ be a directed system of chain complexes of $R$-modules over a filtered poset $I$, with $C_\bullet^{(i)} = (L_i \to M_i \to N_i)$ and homology $H(C_\bullet^{(i)})$. Prove Lemma 10.8.8: the colimit of the homology groups is canonically isomorphic to the homology of the colimit complex: $\varinjlim H(C_\bullet^{(i)}) \cong H(\varinjlim C_\bullet^{(i)})$. (Hint: Filtered colimits are exact in $\operatorname{Mod}_R$, but explain why colimits fail to be exact in general non-abelian categories).
	- (Localization as a Directed System: Lemma 10.9.9) Let $S \subset R$ be a multiplicative subset. Define the localization $S^{-1}R$ via its universal property. Prove Lemma 10.9.9: $S^{-1}M$ is canonically isomorphic to the directed colimit of principal localizations $\varinjlim_{f \in S} M_f$ along transition maps $\frac{m}{f'} \mapsto \frac{m(f'')^e}{f^e}$ where $f = f' f''$. (Hint: Show that localization preserves quotients and is an exact functor $S^{-1}(-) \cong - \otimes_R S^{-1}R$ as in Lemma 10.9.12).
	- (Internal Hom Functors and Filtered Colimits: Lemma 10.10.1 & 10.11.4) Define the internal hom $\operatorname{Hom}_R(M, N)$ as an $R$-module. Prove that $\operatorname{Hom}_R(M, -)$ is a left exact additive functor. Prove Lemma 10.11.4: an $R$-module $M$ is finitely presented if and only if the canonical map $\varinjlim \operatorname{Hom}_R(M, N_i) \to \operatorname{Hom}_R(M, \varinjlim N_i)$ is an isomorphism for all filtered directed systems $(N_i)_{i \in I}$. (Hint: Apply the Yoneda lemma).
	- (Multilinear Maps and Bimodule Associativity: Lemma 10.12.7) Let $M$ be an $(R, S)$-bimodule, $N$ an $(S, T)$-bimodule, and $P$ a $(T, U)$-bimodule. Construct the tensor product $M \otimes_S N$ and prove Lemma 10.12.7: there is a canonical associativity isomorphism $(M \otimes_S N) \otimes_T P \cong M \otimes_S (N \otimes_T P)$. (Hint: Show that tensor products factor multilinear maps through universal bilinear maps).
	- (Tensor-Hom Adjunction and Left Exactness: Lemma 10.12.8 & 10.12.11) Prove the tensor-hom adjunction $\operatorname{Hom}_S(M \otimes_R N, P) \cong \operatorname{Hom}_R(M, \operatorname{Hom}_S(N, P))$ (Lemma 10.12.8). Show that $-\otimes_R N$ preserves all colimits and is right exact. Give a counterexample (Lemma 10.12.11) showing that $-\otimes_R N$ is not left exact in general, and define $N$ to be flat if $-\otimes_R N$ is exact. (Hint: Consider $0 \to \mathbb{Z} \xrightarrow{\times 2} \mathbb{Z}$ tensored with $\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}$).
	- (Tensor, Symmetric, and Exterior Algebras) For an $R$-module $M$, define the non-commutative tensor algebra $T(M) = \bigoplus_{n \ge 0} M^{\otimes n}$, the symmetric algebra $\operatorname{Sym}(M) = T(M)/\langle x \otimes y - y \otimes x \rangle$, and the exterior algebra $\bigwedge(M) = T(M)/\langle x \otimes x \rangle$. Formulate their universal properties, and compute $\bigwedge^r(M)$ for a finite free module $M \cong R^{\oplus n}$. (Hint: Use induction on tensor power components).
	- (Base Change and the Adjoint Triple) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be a ring map and $M$ an $S$-module. Prove that restriction of scalars $\phi_*: \operatorname{Mod}_S \to \operatorname{Mod}_R$ forms an adjoint triple $(\phi^* \dashv \phi_* \dashv \phi^!)$, where base change $\phi^*(N) = N \otimes_R S$ is the left adjoint and $\phi^!(N) = \operatorname{Hom}_R(S, N)$ is the right adjoint. (Hint: Compare this with pushforward and pullback of sheaves on open sets).
	- (Prime Ideals and Ideal Products) Let $I, J \subseteq R$ be ideals and $\mathfrak{p} \subseteq R$ a prime ideal. Prove that if $IJ \subseteq \mathfrak{p}$, then $I \subseteq \mathfrak{p}$ or $J \subseteq \mathfrak{p}$. (Hint: Prove by contradiction: assume there exist $x \in I \setminus \mathfrak{p}$ and $y \in J \setminus \mathfrak{p}$, and consider $xy \in IJ$).
	- (The Prime Avoidance Lemma) State and prove the Prime Avoidance Lemma: if an ideal $I \subseteq R$ is contained in a finite union of prime ideals $\bigcup_{i=1}^n \mathfrak{p}_i$, then $I \subseteq \mathfrak{p}_j$ for some $j$. (Hint: Induct on $n$; if $I \not\subseteq \mathfrak{p}_i$ for each $i$, construct an element $x = y_k + \prod_{j \ne k} y_j \in I \setminus \bigcup \mathfrak{p}_i$ to reach a contradiction).
	- (Chinese Remainder Theorem as Geometric Decomposition) State the Chinese Remainder Theorem: if $I_1, \dots, I_n \subseteq R$ are pairwise comaximal ideals ($I_j + I_k = R$ for $j \ne k$), then $\bigcap_{j=1}^n I_j = \prod_{j=1}^n I_j$ and $R/\bigcap I_j \cong \prod R/I_j$. (Hint: Interpret this geometrically as decomposing a ring into disconnected spatial points / components, and show by induction that $R$ contains $2^n$ idempotents corresponding to subsets of comaximal components).
	- (Cayley-Hamilton Theorem over Rings) Let $R$ be a commutative ring, $M$ an $R$-module generated by $n$ elements, and $\phi: M \to M$ an $R$-linear endomorphism with $\phi(M) \subseteq IM$ for an ideal $I \subseteq R$. Prove the Cayley–Hamilton identity: there exists a monic polynomial $P(T) = T^n + a_1 T^{n-1} + \dots + a_n \in R[T]$ with $a_j \in I^j$ such that $P(\phi) = 0$ on $M$. (Hint: Represent $\phi$ as an $n \times n$ matrix $A$ over $R$, multiply by the adjugate matrix $\operatorname{adj}(T \cdot I_n - A)$, and apply Cramer's rule; reduce general rings to polynomial rings over $\mathbb{Z}$).
	- (The Zariski Topology, Distinguished Opens, and Quasicompactness) Define the Zariski topology on $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ via closed varieties $V(I) = \{\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Spec}(R) : I \subseteq \mathfrak{p}\}$ and distinguished open sets $D(f) = \{\mathfrak{p} : f \notin \mathfrak{p}\}$. Prove that $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ is quasicompact and that $\{D(f)\}_{f \in R}$ forms a basis of quasicompact open sets satisfying $D(f) \cong \operatorname{Spec}(R_f)$ and $D(fg) = D(f) \cap D(g)$. (Hint: Use posets and radical ideal inclusions to analyze finite subcovers).
	- (Local Rings and the Stacks Fundamental Fiber Square: Remark 10.18.5) Define a local ring $(R, \mathfrak{m}, \kappa)$ and a local homomorphism $\phi: R \to S$. For a prime $\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Spec}(R)$, prove that the fiber of $\operatorname{Spec}(S) \to \operatorname{Spec}(R)$ over $\mathfrak{p}$ is canonically homeomorphic to $\operatorname{Spec}(S \otimes_R \kappa(\mathfrak{p}))$, where $\kappa(\mathfrak{p}) = R_\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}R_\mathfrak{p}$. (Hint: Analyze the commutative diagram of Remark 10.18.5 identifying the fiber square of spectra).
	- (The Jacobson Radical and Nakayama's Lemma) Define the Jacobson radical $\operatorname{Rad}(R) = \bigcap_{\mathfrak{m} \text{ max}} \mathfrak{m}$. State Nakayama's Lemma: if $M$ is a finitely generated $R$-module and $I \subseteq \operatorname{Rad}(R)$ is an ideal such that $IM = M$, prove that $M = 0$. (Hint: Apply Cayley–Hamilton to an $n$-element generating set to find $r \in I$ such that $(1-r)M = 0$, and observe that elements of $I$ act as infinitesimal scalars making $1-r$ invertible in $R$).
	- (Idempotents and Clopen Sets of Spec R) Establish the 1-to-1 correspondence between orthogonal idempotents $e^2 = e \in R$ and clopen (closed and open) subsets $D(e) = V(1-e) \subseteq \operatorname{Spec}(R)$. Prove that $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ is connected as a topological space if and only if $R$ contains no non-trivial idempotents (i.e. only $0$ and $1$). (Hint: Decompose $R \cong Re \times R(1-e)$).
	- (Minimal Primes, Nilpotents, and Irreducible Components: Section 10.26) Prove that the irreducible components of $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ are precisely the closed sets $V(\mathfrak{p})$ where $\mathfrak{p}$ is a minimal prime ideal of $R$. Prove that in the local ring $R_\mathfrak{p}$ at a minimal prime $\mathfrak{p}$, the maximal ideal $\mathfrak{p} R_\mathfrak{p}$ consists entirely of nilpotents, and deduce that if $R$ is reduced, $R_\mathfrak{p} \cong \kappa(\mathfrak{p})$ is a field. (Hint: Show that if $R$ is reduced and $\mathfrak{p}$ is minimal, the localization $R_\mathfrak{p}$ has no non-zero zero divisors, so $\mathfrak{p}R_\mathfrak{p} = 0$).
	- (Oka Families and Primes Disjoint from Multiplicative Sets) Let $S \subset R$ be a multiplicative subset. Prove that any ideal $\mathfrak{p} \subset R$ that is maximal with respect to the property $\mathfrak{p} \cap S = \emptyset$ is a prime ideal. (Hint: Show that $\mathfrak{p} = R \cap \mathfrak{m}$ for some maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m} \subset S^{-1}R$, and apply Zorn's lemma).
	- (Chevalley's Constructibility Theorem) State Chevalley's Theorem: let $\phi: R \to S$ be a ring map of finite presentation. Prove that the induced continuous map $f: \operatorname{Spec}(S) \to \operatorname{Spec}(R)$ is a constructible map, sending constructible subsets of $\operatorname{Spec}(S)$ to constructible subsets of $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$. (Hint: A subset is constructible if it is a finite union of locally closed subsets $U \cap V^c$).
	- (Noetherian Rings and Absolute Noetherian Reduction) State the equivalent definitions of a Noetherian ring (ACC on ideals, every ideal is finitely generated, every prime ideal is finitely generated). State Hilbert's Basis Theorem ($R$ Noetherian $\implies R[x_1, \dots, x_n]$ Noetherian) and prove that formal power series rings $R[[x]]$ are Noetherian. Explain the principle of absolute Noetherian reduction: why every finite-type algebra over a field or over $\mathbb{Z}$ is Noetherian. (Hint: Exploit $\mathbb{Z}$ as the initial object in rings and $\operatorname{Spec}(\mathbb{Z})$ as the terminal scheme).
	- (Locally Nilpotent Ideals and Idempotent Lifts) Let $I \subset R$ be a locally nilpotent ideal (every $x \in I$ satisfies $x^n = 0$ for some $n$). Prove that the reduction map $R \to R/I$ induces a bijection on idempotents: $\operatorname{Idemp}(R) \xrightarrow{\sim} \operatorname{Idemp}(R/I)$. (Hint: Use successive polynomial lifts $e_{k+1} = 3e_k^2 - 2e_k^3$).
	- (Jacobson Rings and Finite-Type Algebras) Define a Jacobson ring as a ring in which every radical ideal is the intersection of maximal ideals. Prove that if $k$ is a field (not necessarily algebraically closed), the polynomial ring $k[x_1, \dots, x_n]$ is a Jacobson ring, and every maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}$ has a finite residue field extension $\kappa(\mathfrak{m})/k$. (Hint: Use induction via Euclidean division / Noether normalization to prove that finite-type algebras over Jacobson rings are Jacobson).
	- (Integral Extensions, Lying-Over, and Going-Up) Let $R \subseteq S$ be an integral ring extension. State the Lying-Over Theorem ($\operatorname{Spec}(S) \to \operatorname{Spec}(R)$ is surjective) and the Going-Up Theorem for chains of prime ideals $\mathfrak{p}_0 \subsetneq \dots \subsetneq \mathfrak{p}_n$. (Hint: Show that $\dim(R) = \dim(S)$ and that finite morphism maps between schemes are closed).
	- (Normal Domains and the Almost Integral Criterion) Define a normal domain as an integral domain that is integrally closed in its field of fractions $K = \operatorname{Frac}(R)$. Define an element $x \in K$ to be almost integral over $R$ if there exists a non-zero $r \in R$ such that $r x^n \in R$ for all $n \ge 1$. Prove that if $R$ is Noetherian, an element is almost integral if and only if it is integral. (Hint: $R[x] \subseteq r^{-1}R$ is a finitely generated $R$-module).
	- (Going-Down for Normal Domains) State the Going-Down Theorem: let $R$ be an integrally closed normal domain, $S$ an integral domain containing $R$ such that $S$ is integral over $R$, and assume no non-zero element of $R$ is a zero-divisor in $S$. For any chain of primes $\mathfrak{p}_1 \supsetneq \mathfrak{p}_2$ in $R$ and $\mathfrak{q}_1 \in \operatorname{Spec}(S)$ lying over $\mathfrak{p}_1$, prove there exists $\mathfrak{q}_2 \subsetneq \mathfrak{q}_1$ lying over $\mathfrak{p}_2$. (Hint: Use Galois theory / field extensions of fraction fields).
	- (Equational Criterion of Flatness: Relations in Modules) Let $M$ be an $R$-module. Prove that $M$ is flat if and only if every linear relation $\sum_{i=1}^n r_i x_i = 0$ in $M$ ($r_i \in R, x_i \in M$) factors through trivial relations: there exist elements $y_1, \dots, y_m \in M$ and coefficients $a_{ij} \in R$ such that $x_i = \sum_{j=1}^m a_{ij} y_j$ and $\sum_{i=1}^n r_i a_{ij} = 0$ for all $j$. (Hint: Show that flatness over a PID is equivalent to torsion-freeness).
	- (Support and Annihilators of Modules) For an $R$-module $M$, define the support $\operatorname{Supp}(M) = \{\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Spec}(R) : M_\mathfrak{p} \ne 0\}$ and the annihilator ideal $\operatorname{Ann}_R(M) = \{r \in R : rM = 0\}$. Prove that if $M$ is finitely generated, $\operatorname{Supp}(M) = V(\operatorname{Ann}_R(M))$ is a closed Zariski subset. (Hint: For an element $x \in M$, $\operatorname{Ann}(x) = \ker(R \xrightarrow{\cdot x} M)$).
	- (Geometrically Reduced, Irreducible, and Connected Algebras) Let $S$ be an algebra of finite type over a field $k$. Formulate the definitions for $S$ to be geometrically reduced, geometrically irreducible, and geometrically connected over $k$. (Hint: These require $S \otimes_k K$ to remain reduced, have a unique minimal prime, and have only trivial idempotents for every field extension $K/k$; use the exactness of tensor products with filtered colimits to reduce to finite extensions).
	- (Valuation Rings and Domination of Local Rings) Define a valuation ring $A \subset K$ of a field $K$ via the domination of local rings ($A \subseteq B \subset K$ and $\mathfrak{m}_A = A \cap \mathfrak{m}_B$). Prove that $A$ is a valuation ring if and only if for every $x \in K^\times$, either $x \in A$ or $x^{-1} \in A$. (Hint: Show that a valuation ring is a local domain whose ideals are totally ordered by inclusion, making every finitely generated ideal principal).
	- (Artin-Rees Lemma and Exactness of Completions) State the Artin–Rees Lemma for an ideal $I$ in a Noetherian ring $R$ and a submodule $N \subseteq M$: there exists $k \ge 0$ such that $I^n M \cap N = I^{n-k}(I^k M \cap N)$ for all $n \ge k$. Use this to prove that the $I$-adic completion functor $\widehat{(-)} = \varprojlim (-)/I^n(-)$ is exact on finitely generated modules over Noetherian rings. (Hint: Use the graded Rees algebra $R[It] = \bigoplus_{n \ge 0} I^n t^n$).
	- (Length of Modules and Structure of Artinian Rings) Define the length $\operatorname{length}_R(M)$ of an $R$-module $M$ via composition series. State the structure theorem for Artinian rings: a commutative ring $R$ is Artinian if and only if it is Noetherian of Krull dimension 0, in which case $R \cong \prod_{i=1}^k R_{\mathfrak{m}_i}$ is a finite product of Artinian local rings with nilpotent maximal ideals $\mathfrak{m}_i$. (Hint: Show that descending chains of ideals stabilize).
	- (Grothendieck Groups K0 and Module Ranks) Define the Grothendieck group $K_0(R)$ of finitely generated $R$-modules modulo short exact sequences $0 \to M' \to M \to M'' \to 0$ ($[M] = [M'] + [M'']$). Prove that if $k$ is a field, $K_0(k) \cong \mathbb{Z}$ via the dimension function $\dim_k$, and if $R$ is a PID, $K_0(R) \cong \mathbb{Z}$ via the free rank. (Hint: Apply the structure theorem for finitely generated modules over a PID).
	- (Graded Rings and the Proj Construction) Let $S = \bigoplus_{d \ge 0} S_d$ be a graded ring with irrelevant ideal $S_+ = \bigoplus_{d > 0} S_d$. Define the homogeneous spectrum $\operatorname{Proj}(S)$ as the set of homogeneous prime ideals $\mathfrak{p} \not\supseteq S_+$. Show that $\operatorname{Proj}(S)$ is covered by affine charts $D_+(f) \cong \operatorname{Spec}((S_f)_0)$ for homogeneous elements $f \in S_+$. (Hint: Relate this to the geometry of projective schemes).
	- (Hilbert Functions and Polynomials) Let $S = k[x_0, \dots, x_n]$ be standard graded and $M = \bigoplus_{d \ge 0} M_d$ a finitely generated graded $S$-module. Define the Hilbert function $h_M(d) = \dim_k(M_d)$. Prove that for $d \gg 0$, $h_M(d) = P_M(d)$ is a polynomial in $d$ of degree $\dim(\operatorname{Supp}(M)) - 1$. (Hint: Use exact sequences $0 \to K_d \to M_d \xrightarrow{x_n} M_{d+1} \to C_{d+1} \to 0$ and induction on the dimension).
	- (Krull Dimension and Regular Local Rings) Define the Krull dimension $\dim(R)$ as the supremum of lengths of chains of prime ideals $\mathfrak{p}_0 \subsetneq \dots \subsetneq \mathfrak{p}_n$. For a Noetherian local ring $(R, \mathfrak{m}, \kappa)$, prove the fundamental inequality $\dim(R) \le \dim_\kappa(\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^2)$. Define $R$ to be a regular local ring if $\dim(R) = \dim_\kappa(\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^2)$, and prove that every regular local ring is an integral domain. (Hint: Use Nakayama's lemma on minimal generating sets of $\mathfrak{m}$).
	- (Associated Primes and Symbolic Powers) For an $R$-module $M$, define an associated prime $\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Ass}_R(M)$ as a prime ideal of the form $\mathfrak{p} = \operatorname{Ann}_R(x)$ for some $x \in M \setminus \{0\}$. Define the $n$-th symbolic power of a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$ as $\mathfrak{p}^{(n)} = \ker(R \to R_\mathfrak{p}/\mathfrak{p}^n R_\mathfrak{p}) = \mathfrak{p}^n R_\mathfrak{p} \cap R$. (Hint: Show that associated primes isolate minimal primes (irreducible components) and embedded primes (nilpotent fuzz)).
	- (Regular Sequences, Depth, and Ext Functors) Let $M$ be an $R$-module. Define an $M$-regular sequence $x_1, \dots, x_r \in R$. Define the depth $\operatorname{depth}_I(M)$ of an ideal $I \subseteq R$ on $M$, and prove that $\operatorname{depth}_I(M) = \min \{i \ge 0 : \operatorname{Ext}_R^i(R/I, M) \ne 0\}$. (Hint: Show that regular sequences successively cut down the dimension of $M$ by 1 without introducing zero-divisors).
	- (Rees Blow-Up Algebras and Tangent Cones) Define the Rees algebra (blow-up algebra) $R[It] = \bigoplus_{n \ge 0} I^n t^n$ and the associated graded ring $\operatorname{gr}_I(R) = \bigoplus_{n \ge 0} I^n/I^{n+1}$. (Hint: Show that $\operatorname{Proj}(R[It])$ represents the blow-up $\operatorname{Bl}_V(X)$ of a scheme along the subscheme defined by $I$, replacing a point with the projective space of all incoming tangent lines).
	- (Tor Groups, Filtered Colimits, and Flatness) Define $\operatorname{Tor}_i^R(M, N)$ as the $i$-th homology group of $P_\bullet \otimes_R N$, where $P_\bullet \to M$ is a projective resolution. Prove that $\operatorname{Tor}_i^R(-, N)$ commutes with filtered colimits, and show that an $R$-module $M$ is flat if and only if $\operatorname{Tor}_1^R(M, N) = 0$ for all $R$-modules $N$. (Hint: Use the fact that tensoring preserves direct sums).
	- (Lazard's Theorem on Flat Modules and Universal Invariance) State Lazard's Theorem: an $R$-module $M$ is flat if and only if $M$ is a filtered colimit of finitely generated free $R$-modules. Define universally exact sequences: prove that $M$ is flat if and only if every short exact sequence $0 \to K \to P \to M \to 0$ ending in $M$ is universally exact under tensoring with any $R$-module. (Hint: Use equational criteria to factor relations through free modules).
	- (Descent of Projectivity and Mittag-Leffler Modules: Section 10.87 & 10.88) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be a faithfully flat ring map. State the criteria under which projectivity of $M \otimes_R S$ descends to projectivity of $M$ over $R$. Define an $R$-module $M$ to be a Mittag–Leffler module (Lemma 10.88.6), and prove that $M$ is projective if and only if it is flat, Mittag–Leffler, and a direct sum of countably generated modules via Kaplansky devissage. (Hint: The Mittag–Leffler condition ensures that inverse limits of dual modules are exact and non-empty, replacing topological compactness in algebra).
	- (Coherent Modules and Coherent Rings) Define an $R$-module $M$ to be coherent if it is finitely generated and every finitely generated submodule of $M$ is finitely presented. Define a ring $R$ to be coherent if it is coherent as a module over itself. Prove that $R$ is coherent if and only if every finitely generated ideal of $R$ is finitely presented, if and only if the product $\prod_{i \in I} M_i$ of any family of flat $R$-modules is flat. (Hint: Show that Noetherian rings and valuation rings are coherent).
	- (Completeness Criteria and Acyclicity over Artinian Local Rings) Let $(R, \mathfrak{m})$ be an Artinian local ring. Prove that any exact complex of free modules $R^{\oplus n_e} \to \dots \to R^{\oplus n_1} \to R^{\oplus n_0} \to 0$ splits into a direct sum of trivial complexes, because $R$ has depth 0. (Hint: Show that the rank of maps of finite free modules is determined by the vanishing of $r \times r$ determinantal minors).
	- (Cohen-Macaulay Rings and Modules) Define an $R$-module $M$ over a Noetherian local ring $(R, \mathfrak{m})$ to be Cohen–Macaulay if $\operatorname{depth}_\mathfrak{m}(M) = \dim(\operatorname{Supp}(M))$. Prove that polynomial rings over Cohen–Macaulay rings are Cohen–Macaulay, and state the Auslander–Buchsbaum formula $\operatorname{pd}_R(M) + \operatorname{depth}_\mathfrak{m}(M) = \operatorname{depth}_\mathfrak{m}(R)$ for modules of finite projective dimension. (Hint: Depth and codimension are complementary in Cohen–Macaulay rings).
	- (Catenary and Universally Catenary Rings) Define a commutative ring $R$ to be catenary if for any pair of prime ideals $\mathfrak{p} \subseteq \mathfrak{q}$, all maximal chains of prime ideals between $\mathfrak{p}$ and $\mathfrak{q}$ have the same finite length. Define $R$ to be universally catenary if every finitely generated $R$-algebra is catenary. (Hint: Show that Cohen–Macaulay rings and regular rings are universally catenary).
	- (Pure Ideals and Closed Subsets of Spec R) Define an ideal $I \subseteq R$ to be a pure ideal if $R/I$ is flat as an $R$-module. Prove that $I$ is pure if and only if $V(I) \subseteq \operatorname{Spec}(R)$ is a closed subset that is also closed under generalizations (i.e. if $\mathfrak{p} \in V(I)$ and $\mathfrak{q} \subseteq \mathfrak{p}$, then $\mathfrak{q} \in V(I)$). (Hint: Connect pure ideals to direct limits of idempotent elements).
	- (Schanuel's Lemma and Global Dimension) State Schanuel's Lemma: given two exact sequences $0 \to K_1 \to P_1 \to M \to 0$ and $0 \to K_2 \to P_2 \to M \to 0$ with $P_1, P_2$ projective, prove there is an isomorphism $K_1 \oplus P_2 \cong K_2 \oplus P_1$. Define the global dimension $\operatorname{gl.dim}(R)$ as the supremum of projective dimensions of all $R$-modules. (Hint: Construct a fiber product / pullback module $P_1 \times_M P_2$).
	- (Local Rings of Fibers and Dimension Formulas) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be a ring map, $\mathfrak{q} \in \operatorname{Spec}(S)$, and $\mathfrak{p} = \phi^{-1}(\mathfrak{q})$. Define the local ring of the fiber at $\mathfrak{q}$ as $S_\mathfrak{q}/\mathfrak{p} S_\mathfrak{q} \cong (S \otimes_R \kappa(\mathfrak{p}))_\mathfrak{q}$. If $\phi$ satisfies Going-Up and Going-Down, prove the dimension relation $\dim(S_\mathfrak{q}) = \dim(R_\mathfrak{p}) + \dim(S_\mathfrak{q}/\mathfrak{p} S_\mathfrak{q})$. (Hint: Analyze maximal chains of prime ideals passing through $\mathfrak{q}$).
	- (Generic Flatness) State the Generic Flatness Theorem: let $R$ be an integral domain and $S$ an $R$-algebra of finite type. Prove there exists a non-zero element $f \in R \setminus \{0\}$ such that the localized algebra $S_f$ is a free (and hence flat) $R_f$-module. (Hint: Use Noether normalization and induction on the transcendence degree).
	- (Krull-Akizuki Theorem on 1-Dimensional Domains) State the Krull–Akizuki Theorem: let $R$ be a 1-dimensional Noetherian integral domain with fraction field $K$, and let $L/K$ be a finite field extension. For any subring $A \subseteq L$ containing $R$, prove that $A$ is a 1-dimensional Noetherian domain and for every non-zero ideal $I \subseteq A$, $A/I$ is a finite-length $R$-module. (Hint: Apply this to show that integral closures of 1D Noetherian domains are Dedekind domains).
	- (Unique Factorization Domains and Nagata's Criterion) Define a Unique Factorization Domain (UFD / factorial domain). State Nagata's Criterion for Factoriality: let $R$ be a Noetherian domain and $S \subset R$ a multiplicative subset generated by prime elements. If $S^{-1}R$ is a UFD, prove that $R$ is a UFD. (Hint: Show that an integral domain is a UFD if and only if every height-1 prime ideal is principal).
	- (Dedekind Domains and Order of Vanishing) Define a Dedekind domain as a Noetherian, integrally closed domain of Krull dimension 1. For a prime ideal $\mathfrak{p}$, define the discrete valuation (order of vanishing) $\operatorname{ord}_\mathfrak{p}(x) = \operatorname{length}_{R_\mathfrak{p}}(R_\mathfrak{p}/x R_\mathfrak{p})$ for $x \in K^\times$, and show that every non-zero ideal factors uniquely as $\mathfrak{a} = \prod \mathfrak{p}_i^{\operatorname{ord}_{\mathfrak{p}_i}(\mathfrak{a})}$. (Hint: Use the fact that localizations $R_\mathfrak{p}$ are DVRs).
	- (Zariski's Main Theorem for Quasifinite Morphisms) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be an algebra of finite type. Define $\phi$ to be quasifinite at $\mathfrak{q} \in \operatorname{Spec}(S)$ if $\mathfrak{q}$ is an isolated point in its fiber $\operatorname{Spec}(S \otimes_R \kappa(\mathfrak{p}))$. State Zariski's Main Theorem: prove that if $\phi$ is quasifinite at $\mathfrak{q}$, there exists an integral closure factorization $R \to S' \to S$ and an element $g \in S' \setminus \mathfrak{q}'$ such that $S'_g \cong S_g$. (Hint: Deduce that the locus of points where a morphism is quasifinite is an open subset of $\operatorname{Spec}(S)$).
	- (Openness of the Flat and Cohen-Macaulay Loci) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be a ring map of finite presentation. State the theorems establishing that the flat locus $\{\mathfrak{q} \in \operatorname{Spec}(S) : S_\mathfrak{q} \text{ is flat over } R\}$ and the Cohen–Macaulay locus $\{\mathfrak{q} \in \operatorname{Spec}(S) : S_\mathfrak{q} \text{ is Cohen-Macaulay}\}$ are open subsets of $\operatorname{Spec}(S)$. (Hint: Use constructibility and stability under generalizations).
	- (Derivations, Kähler Differentials, and the de Rham Complex) For an $R$-algebra $S$, define an $R$-derivation $D: S \to M$ satisfying the Leibniz rule $D(ab) = a D(b) + b D(a)$ and $D(R) = 0$. Construct the module of relative Kähler differentials $\Omega_{S/R}^1 \cong I/I^2$, where $I = \ker(S \otimes_R S \xrightarrow{\text{mult}} S)$, and construct the universal de Rham complex $(\Omega_{S/R}^\bullet, d)$ with $d^2 = 0$. (Hint: Show that $\operatorname{Hom}_S(\Omega_{S/R}^1, M) \cong \operatorname{Der}_R(S, M)$).
	- (Local Complete Intersections and Syntomic Morphisms) Define a Noetherian local ring $(R, \mathfrak{m})$ to be a local complete intersection (LCI) if its $\mathfrak{m}$-adic completion $\widehat{R}$ is isomorphic to $P/(f_1, \dots, f_c)$, where $P$ is a regular local ring and $f_1, \dots, f_c$ is a regular sequence. Define a ring map $\phi: R \to S$ to be syntomic if it is flat, of finite presentation, and all its fibers are local complete intersections. (Hint: Show that syntomic morphisms are stable under base change and composition).
	- (Smooth, Étale, and Formally Unramified Morphisms) Let $\phi: R \to S$ be a ring map of finite presentation. Formulate the differential criteria for:
	1. **Smooth:** $\phi$ is flat and the naive cotangent complex $NL_{S/R}$ is quasi-isomorphic to a finite projective $S$-module placed in degree 0 (so $\Omega_{S/R}^1$ is locally free).
	2. **Étale:** $\phi$ is smooth of relative dimension 0 ($\Omega_{S/R}^1 = 0$ and $H_1(NL_{S/R}) = 0$).
	3. **Formally Unramified:** For every ideal $I \subset A$ with $I^2 = 0$, there exists at most one lifting morphism $\operatorname{Spec}(A) \to \operatorname{Spec}(S)$ commuting over $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ (i.e. $\Omega_{S/R}^1 = 0$).
	(Hint: An étale map of local rings induces isomorphisms on residue fields and completions).
	- (Henselian Rings, Strict Henselization, and Etale Colimits) Define a local ring $(R, \mathfrak{m}, \kappa)$ to be Henselian if Hensel's Lemma holds for monic polynomials. Define the Henselization $R^h$ (resp. strict Henselization $R^{\text{sh}}$) as the filtered colimit of all étale (resp. strictly étale) $R$-algebras. Prove that $R^{\text{sh}}$ is a local ring with separably closed residue field $\kappa^{\text{sep}}$, representing the étale stalk of the structure sheaf of a scheme. (Hint: Show that roots of polynomials with simple reduction modulo $\mathfrak{m}$ lift uniquely to $R^h$).
	- (Serre's Criterion for Normality: R_k and S_k) State Serre's Criterion for Normality: a Noetherian ring $R$ is normal if and only if it satisfies properties $(R_1)$ (regular in codimension 1: $R_\mathfrak{p}$ is a regular local ring for all $\operatorname{height}(\mathfrak{p}) \le 1$) and $(S_2)$ (Serre depth condition: $\operatorname{depth}_{\mathfrak{p} R_\mathfrak{p}}(R_\mathfrak{p}) \ge \min(2, \operatorname{height}(\mathfrak{p}))$ for all $\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Spec}(R)$). (Hint: Deduce that normal domains have no embedded prime components).
	- (Cohen Structure Theorem for Complete Local Rings) State the Cohen Structure Theorem: let $(R, \mathfrak{m}, \kappa)$ be a complete Noetherian local ring. Prove that $R$ contains a coefficient ring $\Lambda$ (where $\Lambda \cong \kappa$ if $\operatorname{char}(R) = \operatorname{char}(\kappa)$, or $\Lambda$ is a complete DVR with uniformizer $p$ if $\operatorname{char}(\kappa) = p$ and $\operatorname{char}(\operatorname{Frac} R) = 0$), and prove that $R \cong \Lambda[[x_1, \dots, x_n]]/I$ is a quotient of a formal power series ring. (Hint: Construct coefficient lifts via successive Henselian approximations).
	- (Japanese and Nagata Rings: Condition N-2) Define an integral domain $R$ with fraction field $K$ to be Japanese (condition $N\text{-}2$) if for every finite field extension $L/K$, the integral closure of $R$ in $L$ is a finitely generated $R$-module. Define a Nagata ring as a Noetherian ring $R$ such that for every prime ideal $\mathfrak{p} \in \operatorname{Spec}(R)$, the quotient domain $R/\mathfrak{p}$ is Japanese. (Hint: Prove that fields, complete Noetherian local rings, $\mathbb{Z}$, and finite-type algebras over Nagata rings are Nagata).

https://math.berkeley.edu/~reb/courses/261/all.pdf, Lie Theory and Lie Groups by Richard Borcherds
	- (Foundations of Lie Groups) Define a Lie group $G$ as a smooth manifold equipped with a group structure such that multiplication $m: G \times G \to G$ and inversion $i: G \to G$ are smooth maps. Explain why $\mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb{R})$ and any finite-dimensional real vector space under addition $(\mathbb{R}^n, +)$ serve as prototypical examples. (Hint: Lie groups represent continuous, differentiable symmetries of geometric objects).
	- (Identity Components and Discrete Quotients) Let $G$ be a Lie group. Show that the connected component of the identity $G_{\text{conn}} = G_0$ is a connected, open, and closed normal subgroup, and that the quotient $G/G_{\text{conn}}$ is a discrete group fitting into the exact sequence $1 \to G_{\text{conn}} \to G \to G_{\text{discrete}} \to 1$. (Hint: Connected components of topological groups are always cosets of the identity component).
