	- (Classical Groups, Projective Quotients, and Extensions) Define the classical Lie groups as subgroups of $\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{R})$ or $\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{C})$ preserving bilinear, quadratic, or Hermitian forms. 
	1. Define the special linear group $\mathrm{SL}_n(\mathbb{R}) = \ker(\det)$.
	2. Define the projective general linear group $\mathrm{PGL}_n(\mathbb{R}) = \mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{R}) / Z(\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{R}))$ by quotienting out the center of scalar matrices.
	3. Define the general symplectic group $\operatorname{GSp}_{2n}(\mathbb{R})$ as the group of matrices scaling a symplectic form $\omega$ by a non-zero scalar.
	(Hint: Projective groups act faithfully on projective space $\mathbb{P}^{n-1}$; central extensions enlarge the center, as seen in Spin double covers $\operatorname{Spin}(n) \twoheadrightarrow \mathrm{SO}(n)$).
	- (Solvable and Nilpotent Lie Groups) Define solvable and nilpotent Lie groups in terms of derived series and lower central series. Give the standard matrix models: the group of upper triangular invertible matrices $B$ (solvable) and strictly upper triangular matrices with ones on the diagonal $N$ (nilpotent/unipotent). (Hint: In a nilpotent group, repeatedly quotienting by the center terminates at the trivial group).
	- (Erlangen Program vs. Riemannian Geometry) Compare Felix Klein's Erlangen Program (defining geometries via homogeneous spaces $G/H$ and transformation groups) with Riemannian geometry. (Hint: Klein's program classifies spaces by global continuous symmetries, which fails for generic Riemannian manifolds whose curvature tensors destroy all non-trivial continuous isometry groups).
	- (The Solvable Radical and Structure of General Lie Groups) Define the solvable radical $\operatorname{Rad}(G)$ as the unique maximal connected normal solvable subgroup of a connected Lie group $G$. Establish the canonical structural filtration:
	$$1 \subseteq G_{\text{nil}} \subseteq G_{\text{sol}} \subseteq G_{\text{conn}} \subseteq G,$$
	where $G_{\text{nil}} = [G_{\text{sol}}, G_{\text{sol}}]$ is nilpotent, $G_{\text{sol}} = \operatorname{Rad}(G)$, and $G_{\text{conn}}/G_{\text{sol}}$ is semisimple. Analyze this filtration for the Euclidean isometry group of $\mathbb{R}^4$: $G = \mathbb{R}^4 \rtimes \mathrm{GO}_4(\mathbb{R})$. (Hint: Translations form the abelian/solvable normal part, and orthogonal rotations form the reductive/semisimple quotient).
	- (Dynkin Diagrams and Classification) Describe the role of Dynkin diagrams in classifying complex simple Lie algebras. Explain why nodes represent $\mathfrak{sl}_2$-subalgebras, lines represent non-trivial commutation relations, and multiple edges represent root length ratios. (Hint: Dynkin diagrams classify diverse mathematical objects: finite 3D rotation groups, crystallographic reflection groups, du Val surface singularities, Macdonald polynomials, and singular fibers of elliptic surfaces).
	- (Infinite-Dimensional Lie Groups) Give foundational examples of infinite-dimensional Lie groups:
	1. Diffeomorphism groups $\operatorname{Diff}(M)$ of smooth manifolds.
	2. Gauge groups $\operatorname{Map}(M, K)$ of smooth maps into a compact Lie group $K$.
	3. The Virasoro algebra as the unique non-trivial 1-dimensional central extension $0 \to \mathbb{C} \cdot c \to \operatorname{Vir} \to \operatorname{Vect}(S^1) \to 0$ of vector fields on the circle.
	4. Affine Kac–Moody algebras as central extensions of loop groups $LK = \operatorname{Map}(S^1, K)$.
	(Hint: Infinitesimal generators of gauge transformations and circle diffeomorphisms require smooth loop/vector field algebras).
	- (Accidental Isomorphisms) Analyze accidental isomorphisms between low-order finite groups of Lie type, such as $\operatorname{PSL}_2(\mathbb{F}_4) \cong \operatorname{PSL}_2(\mathbb{F}_5) \cong A_5$. (Hint: Compare the permutation actions of $\operatorname{PSL}_2(\mathbb{F}_4)$ on the 5 points of $\mathbb{P}^1(\mathbb{F}_4)$ and $\operatorname{PSL}_2(\mathbb{F}_5)$ on the 6 points of $\mathbb{P}^1(\mathbb{F}_5)$ with the alternating group on 5 letters).
	- (Algebraic Groups vs. Lie Groups) Compare linear algebraic groups (algebraic varieties over $\mathbb{R}$ with polynomial group operations) with smooth Lie groups. 
	1. Contrast unipotent and semisimple abelian algebraic groups (e.g. $(\mathbb{R}, +) \cong \mathbb{G}_a$ vs. $(\mathbb{R}_{>0}, \times) \cong \mathbb{G}_m$), which are isomorphic as real Lie groups but not as algebraic groups.
	2. Explain why abelian varieties (compact projective algebraic groups) differ from affine algebraic groups.
	3. Give examples of Lie groups that do not arise as algebraic groups (e.g., the Heisenberg group with non-trivial central action, or the universal covering group $\widetilde{\mathrm{SL}}_2(\mathbb{R})$).
	(Hint: Algebraic representations must be given by regular rational functions, while Lie group representations require only smoothness).
	- (The Heisenberg Group in Quantum Mechanics) Define the 3-dimensional continuous Heisenberg group and its Lie algebra $\mathfrak{h}_3 = \operatorname{span}\{X, Y, Z\}$ with commutation relations $[X, Y] = Z$ and $[X, Z] = [Y, Z] = 0$.
	1. Realize the generators as quantum operators on $L^2(\mathbb{R})$: $X = x$ (position), $Y = \frac{d}{dx}$ (momentum), and $Z = 1$ (identity) via the Leibniz rule $[x, \frac{d}{dx}] = -1$.
	2. State the Stone–von Neumann theorem and prove that $\mathfrak{h}_3$ admits no finite-dimensional unitary representations where $Z$ acts as the non-zero identity scalar $\operatorname{Id}$.
	(Hint: For finite-dimensional matrices, the trace of a commutator $\operatorname{Tr}([X, Y]) = \operatorname{Tr}(XY - YX) = 0$, whereas $\operatorname{Tr}(Z) = \operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Id}) = n \ne 0$).
	- (The Iwasawa Decomposition: G = KAN) State the Iwasawa decomposition theorem for a connected semisimple Lie group $G$: there exist closed subgroups $K$ (compact), $A$ (abelian and isomorphic to $\mathbb{R}^r$), and $N$ (nilpotent/unipotent) such that the multiplication map
	$$K \times A \times N \xrightarrow{\sim} G$$
	is a surjective diffeomorphism. For $G = \mathrm{SL}_n(\mathbb{R})$, identify $K = \mathrm{SO}(n)$, $A = \{ \operatorname{diag}(a_1, \dots, a_n) : a_i > 0, \prod a_i = 1 \}$, and $N = \{ (u_{ij}) : u_{ii} = 1, u_{ij} = 0 \text{ for } i > j \}$. (Hint: Show that $G = KAN$ is the geometric matrix reformulation of the Gram–Schmidt orthonormalization process, and deduce that $\pi_1(G) \cong \pi_1(K)$).
	- (The Metaplectic Double Cover) Define the metaplectic group $\mathrm{Mp}_2(\mathbb{R})$ as the unique connected double cover of $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})$, fitting into $1 \to \mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z} \to \mathrm{Mp}_2(\mathbb{R}) \to \mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R}) \to 1$. Explain why $\mathrm{Mp}_2(\mathbb{R})$ is essential for defining modular forms of half-integral weight (such as the Dedekind $\eta$-function and theta series). (Hint: Compute the fundamental group $\pi_1(\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})) \cong \pi_1(\mathrm{SO}(2)) \cong \mathbb{Z}$).
	- (p-Adic Lie Groups and Galois Representations) Define a $p$-adic Lie group as a topological group equipped with an analytic manifold structure over $\mathbb{Q}_p$. Explain how continuous Galois representations $\rho: \operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/K) \to \mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{Q}_p)$ arising from the étale cohomology $H_{\text{ét}}^m(X_{\overline{K}}, \mathbb{Q}_p)$ of algebraic varieties take values in $p$-adic Lie groups. (Hint: Compact $p$-adic Lie groups possess open normal subgroups that are pro-$p$ groups).
	- (The Lie Bracket from Group Commutators) Let $G = \mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{R})$ with Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g} = \mathfrak{gl}_n(\mathbb{R}) = M_n(\mathbb{R})$. For small $A, B \in \mathfrak{g}$, compute the Taylor expansion of the group commutator:
	$$(I + A)(I + B)(I + A)^{-1}(I + B)^{-1} = I + [A, B] + \mathcal{O}(\|(A, B)\|^3),$$
	where $[A, B] = AB - BA$. Prove that $[A, B] = -[B, A]$ and deduce the Jacobi identity $[A, [B, C]] + [B, [C, A]] + [C, [A, B]] = 0$. (Hint: The Lie bracket measures the leading-order obstruction to the commutativity of the group multiplication).
	- (Vector Fields, Derivations, and the Adjoint Map) Show that the space of smooth vector fields $\mathfrak{X}(M)$ is isomorphic to the space of derivations $\operatorname{Der}(C^\infty(M))$.
	1. Prove that the commutator of two derivations $[D_1, D_2] = D_1 \circ D_2 - D_2 \circ D_1$ is a derivation of order 1.
	2. Define the adjoint map $\operatorname{ad}_x(y) = [x, y]$ for $x \in \mathfrak{g}$, and prove that $\operatorname{ad}_x \in \operatorname{Der}(\mathfrak{g})$ is an inner derivation of the Lie algebra, acting as the infinitesimal counterpart of conjugation $g x g^{-1}$.
	(Hint: Second-order partial derivatives cancel in the commutator $[D_1, D_2]$ via Schwarz's theorem).
	- (Lie Groups vs. Formal Groups vs. Lie Algebras) Compare Lie groups, formal group laws, and Lie algebras:
	$$\mathbf{LieGrp} \longrightarrow \mathbf{FormalGrp} \longrightarrow \mathbf{LieAlg}.$$
	1. Explain why Lie algebras fail to capture global topological properties (e.g., $\mathrm{SO}(n)$ and $\mathrm{O}(n)$ have the same Lie algebra $\mathfrak{so}(n)$, and $G$ and $G/Z$ share the same Lie algebra for any discrete central subgroup $Z$).
	2. Define a 1-dimensional formal group law over a ring $R$ as a formal power series $F(X, Y) = X + Y + \sum_{i, j \ge 1} c_{ij} X^i Y^j \in R[[X, Y]]$ satisfying associativity $F(X, F(Y, Z)) = F(F(X, Y), Z)$ and identity $F(X, 0) = X$.
	(Hint: Formal groups retain the infinitesimal formal neighborhood of the identity without requiring analytic convergence).
	- (Classification of Low-Dimensional Connected Lie Groups) Classify all connected real Lie groups up to dimension 3:
	1. **Dimension 1:** The abelian groups $(\mathbb{R}, +)$ (simply connected) and the circle group $S^1 = \mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$ (compact).
	2. **Dimension 2:** The abelian groups $\mathbb{R}^2, \mathbb{R} \times S^1, S^1 \times S^1$, and the non-abelian affine group of the line $\operatorname{Aff}^+(\mathbb{R}) = \{ \left(\begin{matrix} a & b \\ 0 & 1 \end{matrix}\right) : a > 0, b \in \mathbb{R} \} \cong \mathbb{R} \rtimes \mathbb{R}_{>0}$.
	3. **Dimension 3:** $\mathrm{SU}(2) \cong S^3 \cong \operatorname{Spin}(3)$ (compact and simply connected), $\mathrm{SL}_2(\mathbb{R})$ (simple, non-compact, $\pi_1 \cong \mathbb{Z}$), the 3D Heisenberg group $\mathbb{H}_3$, the Euclidean motion group $\mathrm{SE}(2) = \mathbb{R}^2 \rtimes \mathrm{SO}(2)$, and the Bianchi classification of 3D solvable Lie algebras.
	(Hint: Relate the 3D Lie groups to Thurston's eight 3-manifold geometries: $\mathbb{R}^3, S^3, \mathbb{H}^3, S^2 \times \mathbb{R}, \mathbb{H}^2 \times \mathbb{R}, \widetilde{\mathrm{SL}}_2(\mathbb{R}), \text{Nil}$, and $\text{Sol}$).
	- (Differential Operators and Universal Enveloping Algebras) Define a linear differential operator of order $\le n$ on a commutative ring $R$: an operator $D$ such that for every $r \in R$, the commutator $[D, r] = D \circ r - r \circ D$ is a differential operator of order $\le n-1$, with order $\le 0$ being multiplication by elements of $R$.
	1. Prove that the composition of differential operators of order $\le m$ and $\le n$ has order $\le m+n$.
	2. Define the Universal Enveloping Algebra $U(\mathfrak{g}) = T(\mathfrak{g}) / \langle x \otimes y - y \otimes x - [x, y] \rangle$ as the initial associative algebra receiving Lie algebra homomorphisms from $\mathfrak{g}$.
	(Hint: $U(\mathfrak{g})$ is canonically isomorphic to the algebra of left-invariant differential operators on the Lie group $G$).
	- (The Poincaré-Birkhoff-Witt Theorem) State the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt (PBW) Theorem: let $\mathfrak{g}$ be a Lie algebra over a field $k$ with ordered basis $(x_1, x_2, \dots, x_n)$. Prove that the set of ordered monomials
	$$\{ x_1^{i_1} x_2^{i_2} \cdots x_n^{i_n} : i_j \ge 0 \}$$
	forms a vector space basis for the universal enveloping algebra $U(\mathfrak{g})$, and that the associated graded algebra is isomorphic to the symmetric algebra: $\operatorname{gr}(U(\mathfrak{g})) \cong \operatorname{Sym}(\mathfrak{g})$. (Hint: Represent $U(\mathfrak{g})$ on the polynomial algebra $\operatorname{Sym}(\mathfrak{g})$ using the PBW filtration to prove linear independence).
	- (Hopf Algebra Structure on U(g)) Equip the universal enveloping algebra $U(\mathfrak{g})$ with the structure of a cocommutative Hopf algebra:
	1. Define the coproduct $\Delta: U(\mathfrak{g}) \to U(\mathfrak{g}) \otimes U(\mathfrak{g})$ by $\Delta(x) = x \otimes 1 + 1 \otimes x$ for $x \in \mathfrak{g}$.
	2. Define the counit $\epsilon: U(\mathfrak{g}) \to k$ by $\epsilon(x) = 0$ for $x \in \mathfrak{g}$ and $\epsilon(1) = 1$.
	3. Define the antipode $S: U(\mathfrak{g}) \to U(\mathfrak{g})$ by $S(x) = -x$ for $x \in \mathfrak{g}$, satisfying $S(xy) = S(y)S(x)$.
	(Hint: State the Milnor–Moore theorem: every connected, cocommutative graded Hopf algebra over a field of characteristic 0 is isomorphic to the universal enveloping algebra of its Lie algebra of primitive elements $P(H) = \{x \in H : \Delta(x) = x \otimes 1 + 1 \otimes x\}$).
	- (Matrix Exponentials and the Exponential Map) For a matrix $A \in M_n(\mathbb{R})$, define the matrix exponential $\exp(A) = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{A^k}{k!}$.
	1. Prove that the series converges absolutely for any matrix norm.

	- (The Campbell-Baker-Hausdorff Formula) State the Campbell–Baker–Hausdorff (CBH) formula: in the completion of the free Lie algebra generated by formal variables $A$ and $B$,
	$$\log(\exp(A)\exp(B)) = A + B + \frac{1}{2}[A, B] + \frac{1}{12}[A, [A, B]] - \frac{1}{12}[B, [A, B]] + \dots$$
	Prove that all terms in the series are Lie polynomials built purely from iterated Lie brackets. Explain how the CBH formula proves that the Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$ uniquely determines the local multiplication law of the Lie group $G$. (Hint: Show that $\exp(A)\exp(B) = \exp(C)$ has $C$ as a primitive element in the completed Hopf algebra $\widehat{U(\mathfrak{g})}$).
	- (Lower Central Series and Graded Lie Rings) Let $G$ be a group with descending central series $G = G_1 \supseteq G_2 \supseteq G_3 \supseteq \dots$, where $G_{i+1} = [G, G_i]$. Construct the associated graded Lie ring:
	$$L(G) = \bigoplus_{i=1}^\infty G_i / G_{i+1},$$
	where addition is the abelian group operation in each quotient and the Lie bracket is induced by the group commutator $[x G_{i+1}, y G_{j+1}] = (x y x^{-1} y^{-1}) G_{i+j+1}$. 	- (Engel's Theorem on Nilpotent Lie Algebras) State and prove Engel's Theorem: let $\mathfrak{g} \subset \mathfrak{gl}(V)$ be a Lie algebra of nilpotent endomorphisms on a non-zero finite-dimensional vector space $V$. Deduce that there exists a complete flag $0 = V_0 \subset V_1 \subset \dots \subset V_n = V$ such that $\mathfrak{g} \cdot V_i \subseteq V_{i-1}$, meaning $\mathfrak{g}$ is conjugate to an algebra of strictly upper-triangular matrices. 	- (Nilpotence, p-Groups, and Zassenhaus's Theorem)

	- (Solvable Lie Algebras and Lie's Theorem) State Lie's Theorem: let $\mathfrak{g}$ be a solvable Lie algebra over an algebraically closed field $k$ of characteristic 0, and let $V$ be a non-zero finite-dimensional $\mathfrak{g}$-module. 	- (Haar Measures on Nilpotent vs. Solvable Groups) Define the left and right Haar measures on a locally compact Lie group $G$. 	- (Picard-Vessiot Theory and Differential Galois Theory) Formulate Picard–Vessiot theory for linear differential equations: let $k$ be a differential field and let $L(y) = y^{(n)} + a_{n-1}y^{(n-1)} + \dots + a_0 y = 0$ be a linear differential equation.

	- (Additive and Multiplicative Jordan Decompositions) Let $V$ be a finite-dimensional vector space over a perfect field $k$.

	2. **Multiplicative Jordan Decomposition:** For any invertible $g \in \mathrm{GL}(V)$, prove there exists a unique decomposition $g = g_s g_u = g_u g_s$ where $g_s$ is semisimple and $g_u$ is unipotent (all eigenvalues equal to 1).

	- (Cartan's Criteria for Solvability and Semisimplicity) Define the Killing form $B(x, y) = \operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{ad}_x \circ \operatorname{ad}_y)$ on a finite-dimensional Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$.

	- (Cartan Subalgebras, Root Decompositions, and Maximal Tori) Define a Cartan subalgebra $\mathfrak{h} \subset \mathfrak{g}$ as a nilpotent subalgebra that is equal to its own normalizer: $N_\mathfrak{g}(\mathfrak{h}) = \mathfrak{h}$.
	1. For a complex semisimple Lie algebra $\mathfrak{g}$, construct the root space decomposition $\mathfrak{g} = \mathfrak{h} \oplus \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi} \mathfrak{g}_\alpha$, where $\mathfrak{g}_\alpha = \{ x \in \mathfrak{g} : [h, x] = \alpha(h)x \text{ for all } h \in \mathfrak{h} \}$.

	- (The Bruhat Decomposition and Schubert Cells) State the Bruhat decomposition for $G = \mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{C})$:

	- (Real Forms of Orthogonal Groups and Pythagorean Triples)

	- (Clifford Algebras and Their Z/2-Grading) Let $V$ be a vector space over a field $k$ equipped with a quadratic form $N: V \to k$. Define the Clifford algebra $\operatorname{Cl}(V, N) = T(V) / \langle v \otimes v - N(v)\mathbf{1} \rangle$.

	- (Bott Periodicity Modulo 8 and the Super Brauer Group) Describe the 8-fold Bott periodicity of real Clifford algebras $\operatorname{Cl}_{p, q}(\mathbb{R}) \cong \operatorname{Cl}_{p-1, q-1}(\mathbb{R}) \otimes M_2(\mathbb{R})$:
	$$\operatorname{Cl}_0 \cong \mathbb{R}, \; \operatorname{Cl}_1 \cong \mathbb{C}, \; \operatorname{Cl}_2 \cong \mathbb{H}, \; \operatorname{Cl}_3 \cong \mathbb{H} \oplus \mathbb{H}, \; \operatorname{Cl}_4 \cong M_2(\mathbb{H}), \; \operatorname{Cl}_5 \cong M_4(\mathbb{C}), \; \operatorname{Cl}_6 \cong M_8(\mathbb{R}), \; \operatorname{Cl}_7 \cong M_8(\mathbb{R}) \oplus M_8(\mathbb{R}), \; \operatorname{Cl}_8 \cong M_{16}(\mathbb{R}).$$

	- (Spin Groups, Spinor Norms, and Galois Cohomology) Define the Clifford group (Lipschitz group) $\Gamma_V = \{ g \in \operatorname{Cl}(V)^\times : g V \alpha(g)^{-1} \subseteq V \}$, the spinor norm $N(g) = g \cdot \alpha(g) \in k^\times$, and the Spin group $\operatorname{Spin}(V) = \{ g \in \operatorname{Cl}^0(V) : g \alpha(g) = 1 \text{ and } g V g^{-1} \subseteq V \}$.

	- (Triality in Dimension 8 and String Groups) Explain the triality automorphism of the Dynkin diagram $D_4$ (corresponding to $\mathfrak{so}_8(\mathbb{C})$), which permutes the vector representation $V \cong \mathbb{R}^8$, the positive half-spinor representation $S^+ \cong \mathbb{R}^8$, and the negative half-spinor representation $S^- \cong \mathbb{R}^8$. 	- (Kummer's Ideal Numbers and Failure of Unique Factorization) Explain Kummer's motivation for introducing ideal numbers using the ring of integers $\mathcal{O}_K = \mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{-5}]$ in $K = \mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-5})$. (Hint: Consider the non-unique factorization $6 = 2 \times 3 = (1 + \sqrt{-5})(1 - \sqrt{-5})$; Dedekind resolved this by replacing numbers with prime ideals $\mathfrak{p}_2 = (2, 1+\sqrt{-5})$, $\mathfrak{p}_3 = (3, 1+\sqrt{-5})$, and $\mathfrak{p}_3' = (3, 1-\sqrt{-5})$, where factorization is restored via Legendre symbols).
	- (Intrinsic Characterization of Global Fields via Adeles) Formulate André Weil's analytic definition of a global field $K$. 	- (Ergodicity of K-Cross on the Adeles and Noncommutative Spaces) Let $\mathbb{A}_K$ be equipped with its additive Haar measure. Explain why the quotient space $X_K = K^\times \backslash \mathbb{A}_K$ must be treated as a noncommutative space. (Hint: The multiplicative action of $K^\times$ on $\mathbb{A}_K$ is ergodic, so every measurable $K^\times$-invariant function is almost everywhere constant; no non-trivial measurable injection into $\mathbb{R}$ exists, requiring $X_K$ to be treated as the leaf space of a foliation or a crossed-product $C^*$-algebra).
	- (Local-to-Global Principle via Isotropy Subgroups) How does the adèle class space $X_K = K^\times \backslash \mathbb{A}_K$ geometrically encode the Hasse local-to-global principle in class field theory? 	- (Schwartz Kernels, Scaling Operators, and Orbital Traces) Compute the trace of the scaling operator $x \mapsto \lambda x$ on the local field $K_v$. (Hint: The scaling operator on $K_v$ is represented by the Schwartz distribution kernel $\delta(y - \lambda x)$; its distributional trace on the diagonal is given by $\int_{K_v} \delta(x - \lambda x)\,dx = \frac{1}{|1 - \lambda|}$, which directly recovers the local terms in the Riemann–Weil explicit formula).
	- (Spectral Realization of L-Function Zeros as an Absorption Spectrum) Explain why the zeros of $L$-functions appear as an absorption spectrum in the action of the idèle class group on the adèle class space. (Hint: The scaling module $C_K \to \mathbb{R}_+^*$ has a compact kernel parameterizing characters; the zeros of the $L$-functions appear as missing spectral lines/absorption lines, explaining the minus sign in the explicit formula $-\sum_v \int \frac{f(u)}{|1-u|}\,d^*u$).
	- (The Scaling Site without Adeles: Rank-1 Subgroups of R) Define the scaling site $X_{\mathbb{Q}} = \mathbb{Q}^\times \backslash \mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}} / \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^\times$ and describe its realization purely in terms of real subgroups. (Hint: $X_{\mathbb{Q}}$ is isomorphic to the noncommutative space of rank-1 additive subgroups of $\mathbb{R}$, such as $H = \mathbb{Z}[1/p]$; the visible generic orbit is $\mathbb{R}_+^*$, and the periodic orbits $C_p$ have length $\log p$ corresponding to prime numbers $p$).
	- (The Mapping Torus of Frobenius and Periodic Orbits) For a prime $p$, consider the localized ring $\mathbb{Z}_{(p)}$ where all primes $q \ne p$ are inverted. Show that the preimage of the periodic orbit $C_p$ in the adèle class space is the mapping torus of the Frobenius endomorphism in the abelianized étale fundamental group $\pi_1^{\text{ét}}(\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}_{(p)})^{\text{ab}}$. (Hint: The circle $C_p$ represents the Frobenius automorphism acting on the fiber, linking the geometry of $X_{\mathbb{Q}}$ directly to Grothendieck's étale fundamental group).
	- (Dense Orbits and Intersections across Multiple Primes) Explain how the single generic orbit $\mathbb{R}_+^*$ in the adèle class space winds densely around every periodic orbit $C_p$. (Hint: In the product of $p$-adic spaces $\prod_p \mathbb{Q}_p$, the diagonally embedded rationals produce a single dense orbit that intersects all periodic orbits $C_2, C_3, C_5, \dots$, modeled noncommutatively via matrix algebras / groupoid equivalence relations).
	- (Refined Kronecker-Weber Theorem and Minkowski's Discriminant Bound) State the refined Kronecker–Weber theorem without increasing ramification. (Hint: Every finite abelian extension $L/\mathbb{Q}$ is contained in a cyclotomic field $\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_m)$ ramified at the exact same primes; if an extension of $\mathbb{Q}$ is everywhere unramified, Minkowski's discriminant bound $|\Delta_K| > 1$ proves that $L = \mathbb{Q}$).
	- (Diagonal Embedding of Frobenius and Artin Reciprocity) In the abelianized fundamental group $\pi_1^{\text{ét}}(\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z} \setminus S)^{\text{ab}} \cong \prod_{q \in S} \mathbb{Z}_q^\times$, explain how the image of the Frobenius at $p$ is represented. (Hint: The prime $p$ embeds diagonally into $\prod_{q \ne p} \mathbb{Z}_q^\times$ as an invertible element; since $p$ is non-trivial in every component, the periodic orbit circle $C_p$ is topologically linked to every other prime, establishing the global Artin reciprocity law).
	- (Finite Covers of the Scaling Site and Splitting of Primes) For a finite abelian group $G$ and a surjective character $\chi: \widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^\times \twoheadrightarrow G$, construct the finite covering space $X_{\mathbb{Q}} \times_{\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}^\times} G$. (Hint: Over an unramified prime $p$, the lift of the periodic orbit $C_p$ splits into a disjoint union of circles labeled by the places $\mathfrak{P} \mid p$ in the field extension, with the Galois group $G$ permuting the components transitively).
	- (The Role of the Generic Point Eta and Cartier Divisors on Spec Z) Consider the pushforward of the sheaf $\mathbb{G}_m$ from the generic point $\eta = \operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Q}$ to $\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}$. Show how this exact sequence defines Cartier divisors on $\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}$ and connects étale cohomology $H_{\text{ét}}^2(\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{G}_m)$ to the Brauer group $\operatorname{Br}(\mathbb{Q})$ and Artin–Verdier duality $H_{\text{ét}}^3(\operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}, \mathbb{G}_m) \cong \mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}$. (Hint: In local division algebras $\mathbb{Q}_p$, the Frobenius is implemented by the division algebra; local invariants sum to $0 \pmod 1$ in the global Brauer–Hasse–Noether theorem).
	- (Topos Theory and Semidirect Products) Explain why topos theory is necessary to define the scaling site as $[0, \infty) \rtimes \mathbb{N}^\times$. (Hint: Classical topological quotients of non-group monoid actions fail; topos theory allows semidirect products by monoids $\mathbb{N}^\times$, where $[0, \infty)$ represents continuous scaling/frequencies and $\mathbb{N}^\times$ represents discrete harmonics/octaves).
	- (Characteristic One and the Boolean Semifield) Define the Boolean semifield $\mathbb{B} = \{0, 1\}$ with $1 + 1 = 1$. Prove that the Frobenius endomorphisms $x \mapsto x^n$ for $n \in \mathbb{N}^\times$ are injective endomorphisms on semifields of characteristic 1. (Hint: The role of finite fields $\mathbb{F}_q$ is played by $\mathbb{B}$; the abelianized Weil group at the Archimedean place is $\mathbb{R}_+^{\max} = ([0, \infty), \max, \times)$, whose automorphisms $\operatorname{Aut}_{\mathbb{B}}(\mathbb{R}_+^{\max}) \cong \mathbb{R}_+^\times$ are given by $\operatorname{Fr}_\lambda(x) = x^\lambda$).
	- (The Characteristic 1 Witt Formula and Entropy) State Connes' characteristic 1 deformation formula for addition:
	$$\log(x + y) = \max_{\alpha \in [0, 1]} \big( \alpha \log x + (1 - \alpha) \log y + S(\alpha) \big),$$
	where $S(\alpha) = -\alpha \log \alpha - (1 - \alpha) \log(1 - \alpha)$ is the binary Shannon entropy. Show that exponentiating yields $x \oplus y = \max_{\alpha \in [0, 1]} \big( e^{S(\alpha)} x^\alpha y^{1-\alpha} \big)$, and compare this with the classical Witt vector addition formula and the Legendre–Fenchel transform. (Hint: As the base $q \to 1$, the $L^p$-norm $(x^p + y^p)^{1/p}$ converges to $\max(x, y)$, and the Legendre transform replaces the Fourier transform).
	- (The Arithmetic Site as a Semiringed Topos) Define the arithmetic site $(\widehat{\mathbb{N}^\times}, \mathcal{O})$ as the presheaf topos on the multiplicative monoid $\mathbb{N}^\times$ endowed with the structure sheaf of tropical integers $\mathcal{O} = \mathbb{Z}_{\max} = (\mathbb{Z} \cup \{-\infty\}, \max, +)$. (Hint: The structure sheaf $\mathcal{O}$ prevents arbitrary permutations of prime numbers by locking the Frobenius action $n \mapsto \operatorname{Fr}_n$ to the integers).
	- (Function Fields and Cartier Divisors on the Scaling Site) Describe the structure sheaf $\mathcal{O}(Z)$ on the scaling site as the semiring of continuous, convex, piecewise affine functions on $\mathbb{R}_+$ with integer slopes and finitely many singularities of the derivative. (Hint: The function field consists of differences of convex functions (general piecewise affine functions with rational slopes); Cartier divisors are global sections of $\mathcal{K}^\times / \mathcal{O}^\times$).
	- (Tropical Elliptic Curves and the Riemann-Roch Theorem) For the scaling site viewed as a tropical analogue of a Jacobi elliptic curve, define the degree map $\operatorname{deg}: \operatorname{Div} \to \mathbb{R}$ and the character invariant $\chi \in \mathbb{Z}/(p-1)\mathbb{Z}$. (Hint: Principal divisors lie in the kernel of the map $(\operatorname{deg}, \chi)$, yielding a tropical Riemann–Roch theorem with theta functions on $\mathbb{Q}_p \times \mathbb{R}$).

	- (The Circle as an Algebraic Group and Weierstrass Substitution) Realize the unit circle $S^1 \subset \mathbb{R}^2$ as an affine algebraic variety defined by $x^2 + y^2 = 1$, and equip it with an algebraic group law. Use the stereographic/Weierstrass substitution $t = \tan(\theta/2)$ to construct a rational parametrization $t \mapsto \left(\frac{1-t^2}{1+t^2}, \frac{2t}{1+t^2}\right)$. (Hint: Show that this birational map from $\mathbb{A}^1$ to $S^1$ parametrizes all rational points on the circle and connects Pythagorean triples to the geometry of algebraic groups).
	- (Resolving the Nodal Singularity of the Cubic Curve) Consider the nodal cubic curve $y^2 = x^3 + x^2$ in $\mathbb{A}^2$, which has a singular node at the origin $(0, 0)$. Show that the map $t \mapsto (t^2 - 1, t(t^2 - 1))$ is a polynomial map from $\mathbb{A}^1$ to the curve that is $2:1$ at the origin ($t = \pm 1 \mapsto (0, 0)$) and $1:1$ elsewhere. (Hint: This normalization map is a birational isomorphism and provides the simplest example of a resolution of singularities in characteristic 0).
	- (Birational Maps vs. Isomorphisms) Define a rational map $f: X \dashrightarrow Y$ and a birational map between irreducible algebraic varieties. Explain why birational maps can fail to be regular isomorphisms only on subvarieties of codimension $\ge 1$. (Hint: Contrast this with differential topology, where every smooth bijective map between manifolds with non-vanishing Jacobian is a global diffeomorphism).
	- (Abelian Varieties and Projective Group Varieties) Explain how adding points at infinity compactifies smooth affine cubic curves into elliptic curves (smooth projective curves of genus 1 with a group law). State the theorem that any connected projective algebraic group is an abelian variety (a commutative projective group variety), and contrast this with affine linear algebraic groups (such as $\mathrm{GL}_n$), which can be non-abelian.
	- (Schemes vs. Vector Bundles and Tangent Sheaves) Explain why schemes and sheaves are necessary when studying singular varieties. (Hint: On a singular algebraic variety $X$, the tangent/cotangent bundle does not exist as a locally trivial vector bundle, whereas the cotangent sheaf $\Omega_{X/k}^1$ and the module of Kähler differentials are well-defined everywhere).
	- (The Non-Hausdorff Zariski Topology and Separated Schemes) Show that the Zariski topology on a product variety $X \times Y$ is strictly finer than the product topology of the Zariski topologies on $X$ and $Y$. Explain why algebraic varieties are almost never Hausdorff, and how Grothendieck's definition of a separated scheme (the diagonal $\Delta: X \to X \times X$ is a closed immersion) replaces the Hausdorff condition to ensure uniqueness of limits and intersections.
	- (Determinantal Varieties) Define determinantal varieties in the affine space $M_{m, n}(k) \cong \mathbb{A}^{mn}$ of $m \times n$ matrices as the zero loci of all $(r+1) \times (r+1)$ determinantal minors. (Hint: Determinantal varieties parameterize linear maps $k^n \to k^m$ of rank $\le r$).
	- (Short Proof of Hilbert's Basis Theorem) State and prove Hilbert's Basis Theorem: if $R$ is a Noetherian ring, then $R[x]$ is Noetherian. (Hint: For any ideal $I \subseteq R[x]$, consider the ideal $J \subseteq R$ of leading coefficients of polynomials in $I$; since $R$ is Noetherian, $J$ is finitely generated, and an inductive degree argument shows $I$ is finitely generated. Adapt the same argument to formal power series rings $R[[x]]$ by considering the lowest-degree non-zero coefficients).
	- (Noetherian Topological Spaces and Irreducible Components) Define a Noetherian topological space as a space satisfying the descending chain condition on closed subsets. Prove that every closed subset of a Noetherian space decomposes uniquely as a finite union of irreducible closed subvarieties $Y = Y_1 \cup \dots \cup Y_m$. (Hint: Use Noetherian induction on a minimal counterexample).
	- (Failure of the Nullstellensatz over Non-Algebraically Closed Fields) Consider the maximal ideal $\langle x^2 + 1 \rangle \subset \mathbb{R}[x]$. Explain why this maximal ideal does not correspond to any point in $\mathbb{A}^1(\mathbb{R})$, demonstrating that the classical Nullstellensatz requires the base field to be algebraically closed. (Hint: The residue field $\mathbb{R}[x]/\langle x^2 + 1 \rangle \cong \mathbb{C}$ is a non-trivial algebraic extension of $\mathbb{R}$).
	- (Proof of the Weak Nullstellensatz via Zariski's Lemma) Prove the Weak Nullstellensatz: if $k$ is an algebraically closed field and $\mathfrak{m} \subset k[x_1, \dots, x_n]$ is a maximal ideal, then the quotient field $K = k[x_1, \dots, x_n]/\mathfrak{m}$ is isomorphic to $k$. (Hint: Use Zariski's Lemma: if a field $K$ is finitely generated as an algebra over $k$, then $K$ is a finite algebraic extension of $k$; since $k$ is algebraically closed, $K = k$).
	- (The Strong Nullstellensatz and the Rabinowitsch Trick) State the Strong Nullstellensatz: for any ideal $I \subseteq k[x_1, \dots, x_n]$ over an algebraically closed field $k$, $\mathbf{I}(\mathbf{V}(I)) = \sqrt{I}$. Prove this from the Weak Nullstellensatz using the Rabinowitsch trick. (Hint: Introduce an auxiliary variable $y$ and apply the Weak Nullstellensatz to the ideal $J = \langle I, 1 - y f \rangle \subset k[x_1, \dots, x_n, y]$ for $f \in \mathbf{I}(\mathbf{V}(I))$).
	- (Lasker-Noether Primary Decomposition and Nilpotent Fuzz) State the Lasker–Noether theorem: every ideal $I$ in a Noetherian ring $R$ admits a primary decomposition $I = \mathfrak{q}_1 \cap \dots \cap \mathfrak{q}_m$, where each $\mathfrak{q}_i$ is a primary ideal ($\sqrt{\mathfrak{q}_i} = \mathfrak{p}_i$ is prime). Distinguish between isolated/minimal primes (representing geometric irreducible components) and embedded primes (representing non-reduced nilpotent fuzz supported on lower-dimensional loci). (Hint: Primary decomposition generalizes the prime factorization of integers in $\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$).
	- (Nagata's Counterexample to Finite Generation of Invariants) Explain David Mumford's geometric invariant theory (GIT) setup for constructing moduli spaces as quotients $X/\!\!/G$. 	- (Topological Dimension vs. Krull Dimension) Compare the Lebesgue covering dimension of topological spaces with the Krull dimension of algebraic varieties. 	- (Moduli Spaces and Hironaka's Non-Projective Smooth Variety) Define the Hilbert scheme parameterizing subschemes of $\mathbb{P}^n$ with a fixed Hilbert polynomial, and the Picard scheme parameterizing invertible sheaves. 	- (The Segre Embedding of Product Varieties) Construct the Segre embedding $\sigma: \mathbb{P}^m \times \mathbb{P}^n \hookrightarrow \mathbb{P}^{(m+1)(n+1)-1}$ given in homogeneous coordinates by $([x_0 : \dots : x_m], [y_0 : \dots : y_n]) \mapsto [x_0 y_0 : x_0 y_1 : \dots : x_m y_n]$. 	- (The Ax-Grothendieck Theorem and the Lefschetz Principle) State the Ax–Grothendieck theorem: every injective polynomial endomorphism $f: \mathbb{C}^n \to \mathbb{C}^n$ is surjective. 	- (Blowing Up and Resolution of Singularities) Define the blow-up $\operatorname{Bl}_p(X)$ of an algebraic variety $X$ at a point $p$. 	- (Zariski Tangent Space as a Dual Vector Space) For a point $p$ on an algebraic variety $X$ with local ring $\mathcal{O}_{X, p}$ and maximal ideal $\mathfrak{m}_p$, define the intrinsic Zariski tangent space as $T_p X = (\mathfrak{m}_p / \mathfrak{m}_p^2)^* = \operatorname{Hom}_k(\mathfrak{m}_p / \mathfrak{m}_p^2, k)$. 	- (Hensel's Lemma as Power Series Lifting) State Hensel's Lemma for a complete local ring $(R, \mathfrak{m})$: if a polynomial $f(x) \in R[x]$ has a root $a_0 \in R/\mathfrak{m}$ such that $f'(a_0) \ne 0 \pmod{\mathfrak{m}}$, then $a_0$ lifts uniquely to a root $a \in R$ such that $f(a) = 0$. 	- (Weil Divisors, Cartier Divisors, and the Picard Group)

	2. Define a Cartier divisor as a global section of the quotient sheaf $\mathcal{K}_X^\times / \mathcal{O}_X^\times$.
	3. Formulate the Picard group $\operatorname{Pic}(X)$ as the group of isomorphism classes of invertible sheaves under tensor product, and prove that on smooth varieties, Cartier divisor classes coincide with Weil divisor classes modulo linear equivalence.
	(Hint: Associate an invertible sheaf $\mathcal{O}_X(D)$ to every divisor $D$, and formulate the Riemann–Roch problem as computing $\dim H^0(X, \mathcal{O}_X(D))$).

	Explain how the Betti numbers $b_{2k}(\mathbb{P}^n(\mathbb{C})) = 1$ and $b_{2k+1}(\mathbb{P}^n(\mathbb{C})) = 0$ are read off from the point counts over finite fields via the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula in the Weil conjectures.

https://pbelmans.ncag.info/notes/etale-cohomology.pdf, Properties of Weil Cohomology Theories and Etale Cohomology by Pieter Belmans
	- (Definition of a Grothendieck Topology and Sites: Page 11) Formulate the precise definition of a Grothendieck topology on a category $\mathcal{C}$ via covering families. (Hint: A Grothendieck topology specifies a collection of covering families $\{U_i \to U\}_{i \in I}$ such that:
	1. Any isomorphism $\{V \xrightarrow{\sim} U\}$ is a covering family.
	2. If $\{U_i \to U\}$ is a covering family and $V \to U$ is any morphism, all fiber products $U_i \times_U V$ exist and the induced projections $\{U_i \times_U V \to V\}$ form a covering family.
	3. If $\{U_i \to U\}_{i \in I}$ is a covering family and for each $i$, $\{V_{ij} \to U_i\}_{j \in J_i}$ is a covering family, then the composite family $\{V_{ij} \to U\}_{i \in I, j \in J_i}$ is a covering family.
	Illustrate this with the prototypical category of open sets $\operatorname{Open}(X)$ where morphisms are inclusions, fiber products are intersections $U_i \cap V$, and coverings are jointly surjective open covers $\bigcup U_i = U$).
	- (Comparison of Grothendieck Topologies on Schemes) Define and compare the standard Grothendieck sites on schemes:
	1. **Small Zariski Site $(X)_{\text{Zar}}$:** Full subcategory of $\mathbf{Sch}/X$ whose objects are open immersions $U \hookrightarrow X$, with coverings given by jointly surjective families of open immersions.
	2. **Big Zariski Site $(\mathbf{Sch}/X)_{\text{Zar}}$:** Category of all $X$-schemes with jointly surjective open covers.
	3. **Small Étale Site $(X)_{\text{ét}}$:** Full subcategory of $\mathbf{Sch}/X$ whose objects are étale morphisms $U \to X$ (flat, unramified, locally of finite presentation), with coverings given by jointly surjective families of étale morphisms.
	4. **Big fppf Site $(\mathbf{Sch}/X)_{\text{fppf}}$:** Jointly surjective families of flat morphisms locally of finite presentation.
	5. **Big fpqc Site $(\mathbf{Sch}/X)_{\text{fpqc}}$:** Jointly surjective families of faithfully flat and quasi-compact morphisms.
	(Hint: Define a topology to be subcanonical if every representable functor $h_Y = \operatorname{Hom}(-, Y)$ is a sheaf; explain why the fpqc and fppf topologies are subcanonical, and why topologies involving proper morphisms can fail to be subcanonical).
	- (Failure of the Zariski Topology for Non-Coherent Cohomology) Why does the Zariski topology fail to produce the correct cohomology groups for constant sheaves or smooth group schemes? (Hint: The inverse function theorem fails algebraically in the Zariski topology; on an irreducible variety $X$, every constant sheaf $\underline{A}$ is flasque, forcing $H_{\text{Zar}}^i(X, \underline{A}) = 0$ for all $i \ge 1$. For example, the Frobenius map $x \mapsto x^p$ on $\mathbb{A}^1$ is étale away from $0$ but is nowhere a local isomorphism in the Zariski topology. Étale morphisms formally add local algebraic inverses, resolving this failure).
	- (Sheaves, Topoi, and the Six-Functor Formalism: Page 14) Define presheaves, separated presheaves, and sheaves on a Grothendieck site. Define a Grothendieck topos as a category equivalent to the category of sheaves on some site. For a scheme morphism $f: X \to Y$, construct the adjoint pair of functors $(f^* \dashv f_*)$: show that the inverse image $f^*$ is exact, whereas the direct image $f_*$ is only left exact. Explain how derived functors $R^i f_*$ and the Grothendieck spectral sequence approximate higher direct images via composition: $R^n(g \circ f)_* \Leftarrow R^p g_* (R^q f_*)$.
	- (Galois Cohomology as a Special Case of Étale Cohomology) Let $K/k$ be a finite Galois extension with Galois group $G = \operatorname{Gal}(K/k)$. Show that $\operatorname{Spec}(K) \to \operatorname{Spec}(k)$ is an étale covering.
	1. Prove that the fiber product decomposes as $\operatorname{Spec}(K) \times_{\operatorname{Spec}(k)} \operatorname{Spec}(K) \cong \coprod_{\sigma \in G} \operatorname{Spec}(K)$ via the map $x \otimes y \mapsto (x \sigma(y))_{\sigma \in G}$.
	2. Deduce that the Čech complex for the étale cover $\operatorname{Spec}(K) \to \operatorname{Spec}(k)$ coincides with the standard algebraic bar resolution computing group cohomology, establishing the canonical isomorphism $H_{\text{ét}}^n(\operatorname{Spec}(k), \mathcal{F}) \cong H^n(\operatorname{Gal}(k^{\text{sep}}/k), M)$.
	(Hint: Higher intersections in the Čech nerve correspond to iterated products indexed by $G^n$).
	- (Axioms of a Weil Cohomology Theory) Define a Weil cohomology theory $H^*$ as a contravariant functor from the category of smooth projective varieties over a field $k$ to finite-dimensional graded-commutative $K$-algebras (where $K$ is a field of characteristic 0), satisfying:
	1. **Finite Dimensionality:** Each $H^i(X)$ is a finite-dimensional $K$-vector space.
	2. **Vanishing:** If $\dim(X) = n$, then $H^i(X) = 0$ for $i \notin [0, 2n]$.
	3. **Orientation & Trace Map:** A canonical isomorphism $\operatorname{Tr}: H^{2n}(X) \xrightarrow{\sim} K$.
	4. **Poincaré Duality:** The cup product pairing $H^i(X) \times H^{2n-i}(X) \to H^{2n}(X) \xrightarrow{\operatorname{Tr}} K$ is a non-degenerate bilinear form.
	5. **Künneth Formula:** The exterior cup product $H^*(X) \otimes_K H^*(Y) \xrightarrow{\sim} H^*(X \times Y)$ is an isomorphism.
	6. **Cycle Class Maps:** A functorial cycle map $\gamma: \operatorname{CH}^r(X) \to H^{2r}(X)$ compatible with intersections and Chern classes.
	(Hint: List the four classical Weil cohomology theories: $\ell$-adic étale cohomology, singular cohomology of the analytification $X(\mathbb{C})$, algebraic de Rham cohomology in characteristic 0, and crystalline cohomology over perfect fields).
	- (Constructible Sheaves and l-Adic Cohomology) Explain why constant $\mathbb{Q}_\ell$-sheaves are insufficient and how constructible sheaves resolve the failure of pushforwards. (Hint: Locally constant sheaves are not stable under pushforward along open immersions or closed embeddings. A sheaf $\mathcal{F}$ on $X_{\text{ét}}$ is constructible if $X$ can be stratified into a finite disjoint union of locally closed subschemes $X = \coprod X_i$ such that $\mathcal{F}|_{X_i}$ is a finite locally constant sheaf. $\ell$-adic cohomology is defined via the projective system of constructible torsion sheaves: $H^i(X, \mathbb{Q}_\ell) := (\varprojlim_n H_{\text{ét}}^i(X, \mathbb{Z}/\ell^n\mathbb{Z})) \otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_\ell} \mathbb{Q}_\ell$).
	- (Compactification and Cohomology with Compact Support: Page 14) By Nagata's Compactification Theorem, every separated scheme $X$ of finite type over a field $k$ admits an open immersion $j: X \hookrightarrow \overline{X}$ into a proper scheme $\overline{X}$.
	1. For an open immersion $j: U \hookrightarrow X$, define the extension by zero functor $j_!$ (exceptional pushforward) as the exact left adjoint to $j^*$.
	2. Define the cohomology with compact support $H_c^i(X, \mathcal{F}) := H_{\text{ét}}^i(\overline{X}, j_! \mathcal{F})$, and prove that this definition is independent of the choice of compactification $\overline{X}$.
	(Hint: Show that $j_! \mathcal{F}$ has stalks $(j_!\mathcal{F})_x = \mathcal{F}_x$ for $x \in U$ and $(j_!\mathcal{F})x = 0$ for $x \notin U$).
	- (Proper Base Change Theorem: Theorem 27) State the Proper Base Change Theorem (SGA 4, Artin): let $f: Y \to X$ be a proper morphism of schemes and let $g: X' \to X$ be any morphism. For any constructible sheaf $\mathcal{F}$ on $Y_{\text{ét}}$, prove that the canonical base change map
	$$g^* R^i f_* \mathcal{F} \xrightarrow{\sim} R^i f'_* (g'^* \mathcal{F})$$
	is an isomorphism of constructible sheaves on $X'_{\text{ét}}$ for all $i \ge 0$. (Hint: When $X' = \operatorname{Spec}(\kappa(\overline{x}))$ is a geometric point, this reduces the stalk $(R^i f_* \mathcal{F})_{\overline{x}}$ to the étale cohomology of the geometric fiber $H_{\text{ét}}^i(Y_{\overline{x}}, \mathcal{F})$).
	- (Torsion Prime to Characteristic and Smooth Specialization) Let $X$ be a scheme and $\mathcal{F}$ a torsion sheaf on $X_{\text{ét}}$. What does it mean for the torsion of $\mathcal{F}$ to be prime to the characteristic of $X$? (Hint: The characteristic $\operatorname{char}(X)$ is the image of $X \to \operatorname{Spec} \mathbb{Z}$; the torsion of $\mathcal{F}$ is prime to $\operatorname{char}(X)$ if multiplication by $p$ on $\mathcal{F}$ is an isomorphism for every prime $p \in \operatorname{char}(X)$. Under this hypothesis, smooth base change and the smooth specialization theorem hold, ensuring that the cohomology of affine spaces $H_{\text{ét}}^i(\mathbb{A}_X^n, \mathcal{F}) \cong H_{\text{ét}}^i(X, \mathcal{F})$ is acyclic).
	- (Cohomological Purity and Gysin Sequences: Page 21) State the Cohomological Purity Theorem: let $i: Z \hookrightarrow X$ be a closed immersion of smooth schemes over a field $k$ of pure codimension $c$, and let $n$ be an integer prime to $\operatorname{char}(k)$. Prove that $R^q i^! (\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}) = 0$ for $q \ne 2c$, and $R^{2c} i^! (\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}) \cong \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}(-c)$ (Tate twist). (Hint: Deduce the long exact Gysin sequence
	$$\dots \to H_{\text{ét}}^{i-2c}(Z, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}(-c)) \xrightarrow{i_*} H_{\text{ét}}^i(X, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}) \xrightarrow{j^*} H_{\text{ét}}^i(X \setminus Z, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}) \to H_{\text{ét}}^{i-2c+1}(Z, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}(-c)) \to \dots$$
	generalizing the topological Thom isomorphism and Gysin sequence).
	- (Poincaré-Verdier Duality in the Derived Category) Formulate Poincaré–Verdier duality for étale cohomology: for a smooth morphism $f: X \to S$ of pure relative dimension $d$ and an integer $n$ invertible on $S$, prove that the exceptional pullback is a twisted shift of the ordinary pullback:
	$$f^! \mathcal{F} \cong f^* \mathcal{F}(d)[2d].$$
	(Hint: Use derived categories $\mathcal{D}_c^b(X_{\text{ét}}, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z})$ and the adjunction $(R f_! \dashv f^!)$ to deduce the trace map $R f_! \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}(d)[2d] \to \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$ and the global Poincaré duality pairing $H_c^i(X, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}) \times H^{2d-i}(X, \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}(d)) \to \mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}$).

	- (Hausdorff Spaces and the Closed Diagonal: Section 5.3) Prove that a topological space $X$ is Hausdorff if and only if the diagonal $\Delta_X = \{(x, x) : x \in X\}$ is a closed subset of $X \times X$. (Hint: Show that $(X \times X) \setminus \Delta_X$ is open by finding disjoint open neighborhoods $U_x, U_y \subset X$ for $x \ne y$ such that $U_x \times U_y \subseteq (X \times X) \setminus \Delta_X$; conversely, if $\Delta_X$ is closed, any basic open neighborhood in the complement yields disjoint open sets).
	- (Closed Graphs and Separated Morphisms: Section 5.3) For a continuous map $f: X \to Y$, define its graph $\Gamma_f = \{(x, f(x)) : x \in X\} \subset X \times Y$ as the preimage of $\Delta_Y$ under $f \times \operatorname{id}_Y$. Prove that if $Y$ is Hausdorff, $\Gamma_f$ is closed. Define a continuous map $f: X \to Y$ to be separated if the relative diagonal $\Delta_{X/Y}: X \to X \times_Y X$ is a closed immersion, and prove that if $X$ is Hausdorff, every map $f: X \to Y$ is separated. (Hint: Show that separatedness is stable under base change and composition).
	- (Bases of Topologies and Intersection Properties: Section 5.5) Let $\mathcal{B}$ be a collection of subsets of a set $X$ such that $X = \bigcup_{B \in \mathcal{B}} B$ and for any $B_1, B_2 \in \mathcal{B}$ and $x \in B_1 \cap B_2$, there exists $B_3 \in \mathcal{B}$ such that $x \in B_3 \subseteq B_1 \cap B_2$. Prove that there exists a unique topology on $X$ for which $\mathcal{B}$ is a basis of open sets.
	- (Subspace, Quotient, and Submersive/Strict Topologies: Section 5.6) Compare the subspace topology (weakest/coarsest topology making the inclusion continuous) with the quotient topology on $Y = X/\sim$ (strongest/finest topology making the projection $\pi: X \twoheadrightarrow Y$ continuous). Define a continuous map $f: X \to Y$ to be submersive (strict) if $f$ is surjective and $Y$ carries the quotient topology ($V \subseteq Y$ is open if and only if $f^{-1}(V)$ is open in $X$). (Hint: Note that this topological definition of submersive is purely quotient-theoretic and distinct from differential submersions with surjective differentials).
	- (Connectedness and Total Disconnectedness: Section 5.7) Define a topological space $X$ to be totally disconnected if every connected component is a singleton $\{x\}$. Contrast discrete spaces (which are totally disconnected) with $\mathbb{Q} \subset \mathbb{R}$ (totally disconnected but not discrete), and define $X$ to be locally connected if every point admits a fundamental neighborhood system of connected open sets.
	- (Irreducible Sets and Irreducible Components: Section 5.8) Define a non-empty topological space $X$ to be irreducible if $X$ cannot be written as the union $X = Z_1 \cup Z_2$ of two proper closed subsets, or equivalently if every non-empty open subset is dense. Prove that the closure $\overline{Y}$ of an irreducible set $Y$ is irreducible, and that every space $X$ decomposes into a union $X = \bigcup Y_\alpha$ of maximal irreducible subsets called the irreducible components of $X$.
	- (Kolmogorov, Quasisober, and Sober Spaces: Section 5.8 & 5.9) Define the separation axioms:
	1. $T_0$ (Kolmogorov): For any $x \ne y$, there is an open set containing one but not the other.
	2. Quasisober: Every irreducible closed subset $Z \subseteq X$ has at least one generic point $\eta \in Z$ such that $\overline{\{\eta\}} = Z$.
	3. Sober: Every irreducible closed subset $Z \subseteq X$ has a unique generic point $\eta \in Z$.
	(Hint: Show that sobriety embodies the Yoneda lemma for topology; state Lemma 5.8.16 constructing the canonical soberification functor $\operatorname{Sober}(X) \to X$ reflecting sober spaces as a reflective subcategory).
	- (Noetherian Spaces and Krull Dimension: Section 5.9 & 5.10) Define a topological space $X$ to be Noetherian if it satisfies the descending chain condition on closed subsets (DCC). Define the Krull dimension $\dim(X)$ as the supremum of lengths of strict chains of irreducible closed subsets $Z_0 \subsetneq Z_1 \subsetneq \dots \subsetneq Z_n \subseteq X$. For a closed subset $Y \subseteq X$, define the codimension $\operatorname{codim}(Y, X) = \inf_{y \in Y} \sup \{n : Z_0 \subsetneq \dots \subsetneq Z_n \subseteq X \text{ with } Z_0 \subseteq Y\}$.
	- (Quasicompactness vs. Compactness: Section 5.12) State the Stacks Project convention: a space $X$ is quasicompact if every open cover has a finite subcover, and compact if it is both quasicompact and Hausdorff. Define a continuous map $f: X \to Y$ to be quasicompact if the preimage $f^{-1}(U)$ of every quasicompact open $U \subseteq Y$ is quasicompact.
	- (Baire Category Theorem for Locally Quasicompact Hausdorff Spaces: Section 5.12) State and prove the Baire Category Theorem (Lemma 5.12.6): if $X$ is a locally quasicompact Hausdorff space and $\{U_n\}_{n=1}^\infty$ is a countable collection of dense open subsets of $X$, then the intersection $\bigcap_{n=1}^\infty U_n$ is dense in $X$. (Hint: For any non-empty open $V \subseteq X$, construct a nested sequence of non-empty closed quasicompact neighborhoods $\overline{E_n} \subseteq E_{n-1} \cap U_n \cap V$ using Hausdorff separation (Lemma 5.12.4); apply the finite intersection property of quasicompact spaces to show $\bigcap E_n \ne \emptyset$).
	- (Tychonoff's Theorem and Product Quasicompactness: Section 5.14) State Tychonoff's Theorem: the product $\prod_{i \in I} X_i$ of any family of quasicompact topological spaces is quasicompact with respect to the product topology. (Hint: Formulate the product topology via the universal property as the categorical limit of the forgetful functor).
	- (Constructible Sets and Retrocompact Opens: Section 5.15) Let $X$ be a topological space. An open subset $U \subseteq X$ is retrocompact if the inclusion $j: U \hookrightarrow X$ is a quasicompact map (i.e. $U \cap K$ is quasicompact for every quasicompact open $K \subseteq X$). Define a constructible subset of $X$ as a finite union of subsets of the form $U \cap V^c$, where $U, V \subseteq X$ are open and retrocompact.
	- (Proper Maps and Universally Closed Morphisms: Section 5.17) Define a continuous map $f: X \to Y$ to be universally closed if for every topological space $Z$, the fiber product map $f \times \operatorname{id}_Z: X \times_Y Z \to Z$ is closed. Define $f: X \to Y$ to be proper if $f$ is separated and universally closed. (Hint: Explain how this definition of properness ensures that proper base change and Verdier duality hold in sheaf theory).
	- (Specialization Orders and Nowhere Dense Sets: Section 5.19 & 5.20) Define the specialization preorder on $X$ by $x \le y \iff x \in \overline{\{y\}}$. Show that $\le$ is a partial order if and only if $X$ is $T_0$. Define a subset $E \subset X$ to be nowhere dense if its closure has empty interior ($\operatorname{Int}(\overline{E}) = \emptyset$).
	- (Profinite Spaces and Stone Duality: Section 5.22) Define a profinite space as a topological space homeomorphic to a cofiltered limit $\varprojlim X_i$ of finite discrete spaces. Prove that a topological space $X$ is profinite if and only if it is compact (quasicompact and Hausdorff) and totally disconnected. (Hint: Use Stone duality: every profinite space is the Stone space of the Boolean algebra of its clopen subsets).
	- (Spectral Spaces after Hochster: Section 5.23) Define a spectral space (in the sense of Melvin Hochster) as a topological space $X$ satisfying:
	1. $X$ is sober.
	2. $X$ is quasicompact.
	3. The collection of quasicompact open subsets $\mathcal{B} = \operatorname{Op}_{\text{qc}}(X)$ forms a basis of the topology and is closed under finite intersections.
	(Hint: State Hochster's theorem: a space is spectral if and only if it is homeomorphic to $\operatorname{Spec}(R)$ for some commutative ring $R$, if and only if it is a cofiltered limit $\varprojlim X_i$ of finite $T_0$ spaces).
	- (The Constructible Topology on a Spectral Space: Section 5.23) Let $X$ be a spectral space. Define the constructible (or patch) topology $X_{\text{cons}}$ on the underlying set of $X$ by declaring all constructible subsets to be clopen. Prove that $X_{\text{cons}}$ is a compact, totally disconnected Hausdorff space (a profinite space) and that $\pi_0(X)$ is a profinite space.
	- (Stone-Cech Compactification and Extremal Disconnectedness: Section 5.25) Construct the Stone–Čech compactification $\beta(X)$ of a topological space $X$ as the universal map $j: X \to \beta(X)$ into a compact Hausdorff space satisfying $\operatorname{Map}(\beta(X), Y) \cong \operatorname{Map}(X, Y)$ for all compact Hausdorff $Y$. Prove Lemma 5.25.2: if $X$ is locally quasicompact and Hausdorff, $X$ embeds as an open dense subspace of $\beta(X)$. Define an extremally disconnected space (a space where the closure of every open set is open), and show that extremally disconnected compact spaces are the projective objects in the category of compact Hausdorff spaces.

Define a stratification of $X$ as a partition such that the closure of every stratum $\overline{X_i}$ is a union of strata $\bigcup_{j \in J} X_j$.

	- (Homotopy Groups of Spheres and the Adams-Novikov Spectral Sequence) Trace the classification of the stable homotopy groups of spheres $\pi_*^s(\mathbb{S}) = \varinjlim \pi_{n+k}(S^n)$, originating from Hurewicz's higher homotopy groups $\pi_m(X)$. Explain why computing $\pi_*^s(\mathbb{S})$ directly is challenging, and how the Adams–Novikov Spectral Sequence (ANSS) based on complex cobordism $\operatorname{MU}$ provides a systematic algebraic approach.
