	- (Quillen's Theorem and the Brown-Peterson Spectrum BP) State Daniel Quillen's theorem identifying the complex cobordism ring $\operatorname{MU}_*$ with the Lazard ring $L$ classifying 1-dimensional commutative formal group laws $F(x, y) = x + y + \sum a_{ij} x^i y^j$. At a fixed prime $p$, describe the $p$-local Brown–Peterson spectrum $\operatorname{BP}$ with coefficient ring:
	$$\pi_*(\operatorname{BP}) \cong \mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[v_1, v_2, \dots], \quad |v_n| = 2(p^n - 1),$$
	and explain its connection to Lubin–Tate formal group laws and $p$-typical formal groups. (Hint: The generators $v_n$ correspond to the Hazewinkel/Araki generators of $p$-typical formal groups).
	- (Morava K-Theories and Height Classification) Define the Morava $K$-theory spectra $K(n)$ at a prime $p$ for $n \ge 0$, with coefficients:
	$$\pi_*(K(n)) \cong \mathbb{F}_p[v_n^{\pm 1}], \quad |v_n| = 2(p^n - 1) \quad (\text{for } n \ge 1), \quad \text{and} \quad K(0) = H\mathbb{Q}.$$
	Explain how $K(n)$ corresponds to the unique 1-dimensional formal group of height $n$ over $\overline{\mathbb{F}}_p$, acting as categorical "prime fields" in stable homotopy theory. (Hint: By the Landweber–Morava classification, Morava $K$-theories are field objects satisfying a Künneth isomorphism $K(n)_*(X \times Y) \cong K(n)_*(X) \otimes_{K(n)_*} K(n)_*(Y)$).
	- (The Chromatic Spectral Sequence and Astronomical Analogy) Describe the chromatic spectral sequence constructed by Miller, Ravenel, and Wilson. Explain why each column displays $v_n$-periodic families and how the $n$-th chromatic layer is controlled by the continuous cohomology of the $n$-th Morava stabilizer group $\mathbb{S}_n = \operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{G}_n) \cong \mathcal{O}_{D_{1/n}}^\times$. (Hint: The name "chromatic" comes from the decomposition of the stable homotopy of spheres into periodic monochromatic components, analogous to decomposing light into spectral lines).
	- (The Hopf Algebroid of BP and the Chromatic Resolution) Define the Hopf algebroid $(A, \Gamma) = (\operatorname{BP}_*, \operatorname{BP}_*(\operatorname{BP}))$ representing the moduli of $p$-typical formal group laws and their strict isomorphisms. Show that the $E_2$-term of the ANSS is given by algebraic $\operatorname{Ext}$ groups:
	$$E_2^{s, t} = \operatorname{Ext}_{\operatorname{BP}_*(\operatorname{BP})}^{s, t}(\operatorname{BP}_*, \operatorname{BP}_*).$$
	Explain how the chromatic resolution of $\operatorname{BP}_*$ decomposes this $\operatorname{Ext}$ group into long exact sequences of comodules. (Hint: The chromatic resolution successively inverts the generators $v_n$ and computes cohomology on monochromatic quotients).
	- (Bousfield Localization and E-Local Spectra) Define an $E$-local spectrum and the Bousfield localization functor $L_E: \mathbf{Sp} \to \mathbf{Sp}$ for a generalized homology theory $E$. (Hint: A spectrum $Z$ is $E$-local if every $E_*$-equivalence $f: A \to B$ induces an isomorphism $f^*: [B, Z] \to [A, Z]$; the canonical map $X \to L_E X$ is the universal $E_*$-equivalence into an $E$-local spectrum. For connective spectra, show that $L_E$ reduces to arithmetic operations like inverting a set of primes or $p$-adic completion).
	- (Johnson-Wilson Spectra and Chromatic Layers) Define the Johnson–Wilson spectrum $E(n)$ with $\pi_*(E(n)) \cong \mathbb{Z}_{(p)}[v_1, \dots, v_{n-1}, v_n^{\pm 1}]$ and the Lubin–Tate/Morava $E$-theory spectrum $E_n$. Show that $L_{E(n)}$ and $L_{E_n}$ define the exact same Bousfield localization functor $L_n$, isolating the $n$-th chromatic stage of the stable homotopy category. (Hint: The localization $L_n X$ captures all $v_i$-periodic information for $i \le n$).
	- (Bousfield Equivalence Classes and the Lattice of Spectra) Define the Bousfield equivalence class $\langle E \rangle$ of a spectrum $E$, where $\langle E \rangle = \langle F \rangle$ if they have the same acyclic spectra ($E_* X = 0 \iff F_* X = 0$).
	1. Define the partial order $\langle E \rangle \ge \langle F \rangle$, with maximal class $\langle \mathbb{S} \rangle$ and minimal class $\langle * \rangle$.
	2. Prove that for a cofiber sequence $W \to X \to Y \xrightarrow{f} \Sigma W$, $\langle X \rangle \le \langle W \rangle \vee \langle Y \rangle$, with equality holding if and only if $f$ is smash-nilpotent ($f^{\wedge k} = 0$).
	3. Verify the Bousfield decompositions:
		$$\langle \mathbb{S} \rangle = \langle H\mathbb{Q} \rangle \vee \bigvee_{p \text{ prime}} \langle S/p \rangle, \quad \langle \operatorname{BP} \rangle \ge \langle H\mathbb{F}_p \rangle \vee \bigvee_{n=1}^\infty \langle K(n) \rangle, \quad \langle E(n) \rangle = \bigvee_{i=0}^n \langle K(i) \rangle.$$
	(Hint: Show that $\langle K(i) \wedge K(j) \rangle = \langle * \rangle$ for $i \ne j$, meaning Morava $K$-theories are mutually orthogonal).
	- (Harmonic vs. Dissonant Spectra) Define the harmonic localization $L_\infty X = \operatorname{holim}_n L_n X$ of a $p$-local spectrum $X$. A spectrum $X$ is harmonic if $X \xrightarrow{\sim} L_\infty X$ is an equivalence, and dissonant if $L_\infty X \simeq *$, or equivalently if $K(n)_* X = 0$ for all $n \ge 0$. Prove that:
	1. There are no non-trivial maps from any dissonant spectrum to any harmonic spectrum: $[D, H] = 0$.
	2. Every finite $p$-local spectrum is harmonic (Ravenel's Theorem).
	3. The mod-$p$ Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum $H\mathbb{F}_p$ is dissonant, as is any spectrum with bounded torsion homotopy groups.
	(Hint: Harmonic spectra are built from the chromatic tower, whereas dissonant spectra are invisible to all Morava $K$-theories).
	- (The Devinatz-Hopkins-Smith Nilpotence and Periodicity Theorems) State the fundamental theorems of chromatic homotopy theory:
	1. **Nilpotence Theorem:** $\langle \operatorname{MU} \rangle = \langle \mathbb{S} \rangle$. A ring spectrum map or self-map $f: \Sigma^d X \to X$ on a finite $p$-local complex is smash-nilpotent if and only if $\operatorname{MU}_*(f) = 0$ (or equivalently $\operatorname{BP}_*(f) = 0$).
	2. **Periodicity Theorem:** Every finite $p$-local spectrum $X$ of chromatic type $n$ (meaning $K(n-1)_* X = 0$ and $K(n)_* X \ne 0$) admits a $v_n$-periodic self-map $v: \Sigma^{d} X \to X$ such that $K(n)_*(v)$ is an isomorphism and $K(m)_*(v) = 0$ for $m \ne n$.
	(Hint: Constructing finite complexes of arbitrary chromatic type $n$ was first achieved by Mitchell via the Steinberg idempotent on flag manifolds).
	- (Smith-Toda Complexes and Finite Realizations) Define the Smith–Toda complexes $V(n)$ as finite $p$-local spectra characterized by $\operatorname{BP}_*(V(n)) \cong \operatorname{BP}_*/(p, v_1, \dots, v_n)$.
	1. Show that $V(0) = S/p$ is the mod-$p$ Moore spectrum (cofiber of $p: \mathbb{S} \to \mathbb{S}$).
	2. Show that $V(1)$ is the cofiber of the Adams self-map $v_1: \Sigma^{2p-2} V(0) \to V(0)$.
	(Hint: Explain why $V(n)$ exists only for small heights depending on the prime $p$, as higher $v_n$-maps can fail to be ring maps).
	- (The Smash Product Conjecture and the Telescope Conjecture) 
	1. **Smash Product Conjecture:** State the conjecture asserting that localization commutes with smash products: $L_n X \simeq X \wedge L_n \mathbb{S}$.
	2. **Telescope Conjecture:** For a finite type-$n$ spectrum $X$ with $v_n$-periodic self-map $v$, compare the telescope localization $v^{-1}X = \operatorname{hocolim}(X \xrightarrow{v} \Sigma^{-d}X \to \dots)$ with the chromatic localization $L_{K(n)} X$. State the recent resolution of the Telescope Conjecture (disproven for $n \ge 2$ at all primes by Burklund, Hahn, Levy, and Schlank).
	(Hint: The telescope localization $v^{-1}X$ detects unstable algebraic phenomena that are completed away in the Morava $K$-theory localization $L_{K(n)} X$).
	- (Representation Theory in Mixed Characteristic and Height Stratification) Contrast the representation theory of finite groups in characteristic 0 (where Maschke's theorem ensures every representation is semisimple and extensions vanish) with modular representation theory in characteristic $p$ (where projective covers are non-trivial and extensions abound). Explain how chromatic homotopy theory organizes this behavior by stratifying the moduli stack of formal groups $\mathcal{M}_{\text{fg}}$ by height, with height 0 corresponding to rational semisimple representations and height $\infty$ to $p$-modular phenomena. (Hint: Formal groups of height $n$ over $\mathbb{F}_p$ correspond to the geometric points of $\mathcal{M}_{\text{fg}}$, with the Morava stabilizer groups $\mathbb{S}_n$ acting as their automorphism groups).

https://iwoat.github.io/2018/slides/Ravenel.pdf, Foundations of Stable Homotopy Theory by Hill, Hopkins, and Ravenel
	- (Classical Spectra and Maps of Spectra) Define an ordinary spectrum $E$ as a sequence of pointed spaces (or pointed simplicial sets) $(E_n)_{n \ge 0}$ equipped with structure maps $\sigma_n: \Sigma E_n = S^1 \wedge E_n \to E_{n+1}$.
	1. Define a strict map of spectra $f: E \to F$ as a family of pointed continuous maps $f_n: E_n \to F_n$ commuting with the structure maps: $f_{n+1} \circ \sigma_n^E = \sigma_n^F \circ (\operatorname{id}_{S^1} \wedge f_n)$.
	2. Define a strict equivalence of spectra as a map $f$ where each level map $f_n: E_n \xrightarrow{\sim} F_n$ is a weak homotopy equivalence of spaces.
	(Hint: Structure maps specify how higher-dimensional cells suspend into the next level of the spectrum).
	- (Stable Equivalences: Homotopy Groups vs. Mapping Telescopes) Contrast the two classical definitions of a stable equivalence $f: E \to F$:
	1. **Homotopy Groups:** $f$ induces an isomorphism on all stable homotopy groups $\pi_k(f): \pi_k(E) \xrightarrow{\sim} \pi_k(F)$, where $\pi_k(E) = \varinjlim_n \pi_{n+k}(E_n)$.
	2. **Spectrifying / Spectrification Functor:** The spectrification map $\Lambda f: \Lambda E \to \Lambda F$ is a strict equivalence, where $(\Lambda E)_n = \operatorname{hocolim}_k \Omega^k E_{n+k}$ is the homotopy mapping telescope.
	(Hint: Explain why these two definitions coincide for classical CW-spectra but bifurcate in generalized diagram categories like symmetric spectra without extra fibrancy hypotheses).
	- (Axioms of Quillen Model Categories) State the five foundational axioms (MC1–MC5) of a Quillen model category $\mathcal{M}$ equipped with three distinguished classes of morphisms: weak equivalences ($\mathcal{W}$), fibrations ($\mathcal{F}\text{ib}$), and cofibrations ($\mathcal{C}\text{of}$):
	1. **MC1 (Bicompleteness):** $\mathcal{M}$ has all small limits and colimits (products, coproducts, pullbacks, pushouts, initial object $\emptyset$, and terminal object $*$).
	2. **MC2 (Two-out-of-Three):** If any two of $f, g, g \circ f$ are weak equivalences, so is the third.
	3. **MC3 (Retracts):** If a morphism $f$ is a retract of a morphism $g$ and $g$ is a weak equivalence (resp. fibration, cofibration), then so is $f$.
	4. **MC4 (Lifting Axiom):** Given a commutative square with $i \in \mathcal{C}\text{of}$ and $p \in \mathcal{F}\text{ib}$:
		$$\begin{matrix} A & \xrightarrow{u} & X \\ i \downarrow & & \downarrow p \\ B & \xrightarrow{v} & Y \end{matrix}$$
		a lift $h: B \to X$ ($h \circ i = u, p \circ h = v$) exists if either $i$ is a trivial cofibration ($i \in \mathcal{C}\text{of} \cap \mathcal{W}$) or $p$ is a trivial fibration ($p \in \mathcal{F}\text{ib} \cap \mathcal{W}$).
	5. **MC5 (Functorial Factorization):** Every morphism $f: X \to Y$ factors functorially as $f = p \circ i$ where $i \in \mathcal{C}\text{of} \cap \mathcal{W}$ and $p \in \mathcal{F}\text{ib}$, and as $f = q \circ j$ where $j \in \mathcal{C}\text{of}$ and $q \in \mathcal{F}\text{ib} \cap \mathcal{W}$.
	(Hint: Factorization is the model-categorical analogue of the first isomorphism theorem, replacing arbitrary objects with weakly equivalent fibrant-cofibrant replacements).
	- (Classical Model Structures on Top and sSet) In the category of compactly generated weakly Hausdorff topological spaces $\mathbf{Top}_*$ (or pointed simplicial sets $\mathbf{sSet}_*$):
	1. Show that weak equivalences are maps inducing isomorphisms on all homotopy groups $\pi_*$.
	2. Show that fibrations are Serre fibrations (possessing the right lifting property with respect to horn inclusions / disk inclusions).
	3. Show that cofibrations are retracts of relative cell complexes (left lifting property with respect to trivial fibrations).
	(Hint: Every space in $\mathbf{Top}_*$ is fibrant ($\emptyset \to X \to *$ has $X \to *$ a fibration); cofibrant objects are precisely spaces that have the homotopy type of retracts of CW-complexes).
	- (Fibrant and Cofibrant Replacements via Factorization) For any object $X \in \mathcal{M}$, use axiom MC5 to factor the unique maps $\emptyset \to X$ and $X \to *$:
	$$\emptyset \xrightarrow{\in \mathcal{C}\text{of}} QX \xrightarrow{\in \mathcal{W} \cap \mathcal{F}\text{ib}} X \quad \text{and} \quad X \xrightarrow{\in \mathcal{W} \cap \mathcal{C}\text{of}} RX \xrightarrow{\in \mathcal{F}\text{ib}} *.$$
	(Hint: $QX$ is the cofibrant replacement (CW approximation) and $RX$ is the fibrant replacement; the homotopy category $\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{M}) = \mathcal{M}[\mathcal{W}^{-1}]$ has mapping sets $\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Ho}(\mathcal{M})}(X, Y) \cong \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{M}}(QX, RY)/\sim_{\text{homotopy}}$).
	- (Bousfield Localization of Model Categories) Formulate left Bousfield localization on a model category $\mathcal{M}$ with respect to a class of morphisms $S$:
	1. **Enlarging Weak Equivalences:** Define the new weak equivalences ($S$-local equivalences) as maps $f: A \to B$ such that $\operatorname{Map}(B, Z) \xrightarrow{\sim} \operatorname{Map}(A, Z)$ is a weak equivalence for all $S$-local objects $Z$.
	2. **Cofibrations:** Keep the class of cofibrations unchanged: $\mathcal{C}\text{of}_{L_S \mathcal{M}} = \mathcal{C}\text{of}_{\mathcal{M}}$.
	3. **Fibrations:** Define the new fibrations ($S$-local fibrations) as maps having the right lifting property with respect to trivial cofibrations in the localized model structure.
	(Hint: Localizing enlarges weak equivalences, adds more trivial cofibrations, and produces fewer fibrant objects; the fibrant replacement functor becomes the localization functor $L_S$).
	- (Concrete Examples of Bousfield Localization: Slide 121) Describe the fibrant replacement functor $L_S X$ for standard choices of localizing classes $S$ on $\mathbf{Top}_*$:
	1. **Postnikov Sections:** Let $S = \{S^n \to *\}$. The local objects are spaces with vanishing homotopy groups $\pi_k(X) = 0$ for $k \ge n$, and the fibrant replacement is the $n$-th Postnikov section $P_n X = \tau_{\le n-1} X$.
	2. **p-Adic / Homological Localization:** Let $S$ be the class of maps inducing isomorphisms on mod-$p$ homology $H_*(-; \mathbb{F}_p)$. For simply connected spaces, the fibrant replacement $L_{\mathbb{F}_p} X$ is the $p$-adic completion $X_p^{\wedge}$.
	3. **Generalized Homology Localization:** Let $E$ be a generalized homology theory and let $S$ be the class of $E_*$-equivalences. The fibrant replacement is the Bousfield $E$-localization $L_E X$.
	(Hint: Whenever one needs to force homological equivalences into isomorphisms in homotopy theory, apply left Bousfield localization).
	- (Enriched Categories and Closed Symmetric Monoidal Categories) Let $(\mathcal{V}, \otimes, \mathbf{1})$ be a closed symmetric monoidal category with internal hom $[-, -]_{\mathcal{V}}$.
	1. Define a $\mathcal{V}$-enriched category $\mathcal{C}$ with mapping objects $\operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{C}}(X, Y) \in \mathcal{V}$ and associative composition morphisms $\operatorname{Map}(Y, Z) \otimes \operatorname{Map}(X, Y) \to \operatorname{Map}(X, Z)$.
	2. Explain what it means for $\mathcal{C}$ to be closed (enriched over itself), and give the standard examples: pointed topological spaces $\mathbf{Top}_*$ enriched over $\mathbf{Top}_*$, and simplicial sets $\mathbf{sSet}_*$ enriched over $\mathbf{sSet}_*$.
	(Hint: In a closed symmetric monoidal category, tensor products and internal homs satisfy the adjunction $\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}}(X \otimes Y, Z) \cong \operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{V}}(X, [Y, Z]_{\mathcal{V}})$).
	- (The Naive Indexing Category $J^{\mathbb{N}}$ and Yoneda Spectra) Define the naive indexing category $J^{\mathbb{N}}$ for spectra:
	1. Objects are natural numbers $n \in \mathbb{N}_0$.
	2. Morphism objects are spheres: $\operatorname{Map}_{J^{\mathbb{N}}}(m, n) = S^{n-m}$ for $n \ge m$, and $*$ otherwise.
	3. Show that a classical spectrum $E$ is precisely a pointed enriched functor $E: J^{\mathbb{N}} \to \mathbf{Top}_*$, interpreting spectra as diagrams of pointed spaces.
	4. Define the Yoneda representable spectrum $S^{-m} = \operatorname{Map}_{J^{\mathbb{N}}}(m, -)$ (where $S^{-0} = \mathbb{S}$ is the sphere spectrum, and $S^{-m}$ is the formal $m$-fold desuspension of the sphere).
	(Hint: While $\mathbf{Top}_*$ is symmetric monoidal under $\wedge$, the indexing category $J^{\mathbb{N}}$ is monoidal under addition of integers but lacks a symmetric twist isomorphism, because the coordinate-swap map $\tau: S^1 \wedge S^1 \to S^1 \wedge S^1$ has degree $-1$).
	- (Failure of the Smash Product on Classical Spectra) Explain why the category of naive spectra $\operatorname{Fun}(J^{\mathbb{N}}, \mathbf{Top}_*)$ does not admit a symmetric monoidal smash product. (Hint: The naive indexing category $J^{\mathbb{N}}$ lacks symmetric group actions; coordinate swaps on $S^p \wedge S^q \cong S^{p+q}$ introduce sign permutations $(p, q) \mapsto (q, p)$ of degree $(-1)^{pq}$ that cannot be absorbed without symmetric group equivariance on the diagram).
	- (The Symmetric Indexing Category $J^\Sigma$ and Symmetric Spectra: Hovey-Shipley-Smith) Construct the symmetric indexing category $J^\Sigma$:
	1. Objects are natural numbers $n \in \mathbb{N}_0$.
	2. Morphism objects are $\Sigma_n$-equivariant wedges of spheres: $\operatorname{Map}_{J^\Sigma}(m, n) = (\Sigma_n)_+ \wedge_{\Sigma_{n-m}} S^{n-m}$ for $n \ge m$, and $*$ otherwise.
	3. Show that $J^\Sigma$ is a symmetric monoidal category enriched over $\mathbf{Top}_*$, where the symmetric group actions absorb the permutation signs of the smash product.
	4. Define a symmetric spectrum as an enriched functor $E: J^\Sigma \to \mathbf{Top}_*$.
	(Hint: Symmetric spectra incorporate the symmetric group actions $\Sigma_n \curvearrowright E_n$ from the ground up, making the diagram category itself symmetric monoidal).
	- (Day Convolution and the Symmetric Monoidal Smash Product) Let $(\mathcal{V}, \otimes, \mathbf{1})$ be a closed symmetric monoidal category and let $\mathcal{J}$ be a small symmetric monoidal $\mathcal{V}$-category.
	1. Construct the Day convolution tensor product on the functor category $\operatorname{Fun}(\mathcal{J}, \mathcal{V})$ via the left Kan extension along the monoidal product $\oplus: \mathcal{J} \times \mathcal{J} \to \mathcal{J}$:
		$$(F \otimes_{\text{Day}} G)(n) = \int^{p, q \in \mathcal{J}} \operatorname{Map}_{\mathcal{J}}(p \oplus q, n) \otimes F(p) \otimes G(q).$$
	2. Deduce that the category of symmetric spectra $\mathbf{Sp}^\Sigma = \operatorname{Fun}(J^\Sigma, \mathbf{Top}_*)$ inherits a strictly associative, commutative, and closed symmetric monoidal smash product $\wedge = \otimes_{\text{Day}}$ with unit the sphere spectrum $\mathbb{S} = \operatorname{Map}_{J^\Sigma}(0, -)$.
	(Hint: Day convolution on enriched functor categories solves the decades-old problem of defining a symmetric monoidal category of spectra before passing to the homotopy category).

https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.07082, Survey on Modern Representation Theory by Geordie Williamson
	- (Galois's Philosophy of Groups and the Monster) How does Galois's perspective define a group not just abstractly by its axioms, but through its actions on sets ("what are you made of and how do you behave")? (Hint: Subgroups of $\mathrm{GL}_n$ are linear groups, while subgroups of the symmetric group $S_n$ are finite permutation groups; discuss how the Monster group—the largest sporadic simple group—connects finite group theory to modular functions and Monstrous Moonshine).
	- (GL(n) as a Quantum Deformation of S(n) and Tits' F1-Philosophy) Explain Jacques Tits' heuristic that the symmetric group is the general linear group over the field with one element: $S_n = \mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{F}_1)$. (Hint: For a Chevalley group scheme $G$ over $\mathbb{Z}$, the point count $\#G(\mathbb{F}_q)$ polynomial in $q$ evaluates at $q = 1$ to the order of its Weyl group $W$; show that the determinant map $\det: \mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{F}_q) \to \mathbb{F}_q^\times$ is a $q$-deformation of the signature homomorphism $\operatorname{sgn}: S_n \to \{\pm 1\}$, and that the alternating group $A_n = \ker(\operatorname{sgn})$ corresponds to $\operatorname{PSL}_n(\mathbb{F}_q)$).
	- (The Barratt-Priddy-Quillen Theorem and F1-Homotopy) State the Barratt–Priddy–Quillen (BPQ) theorem in the context of $\mathbb{F}_1$-geometry: $K_i(\mathbb{F}_1) \cong \pi_i^s(\mathbb{S})$. (Hint: Quillen's plus construction on the classifying space of the infinite symmetric group $B\Sigma_\infty^+$ yields the sphere spectrum $\mathbb{S}$, identifying the higher algebraic $K$-theory of $\mathbb{F}_1$ with the stable homotopy groups of spheres).
	- (The Bruhat Decomposition as Categorified Gaussian Elimination) State the Bruhat decomposition for $\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{F}_q)$ (and $\mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{C})$):
	$$\mathrm{GL}_n = \bigsqcup_{w \in S_n} B w B,$$
	where $B$ is the Borel subgroup of upper-triangular matrices and $w \in S_n$ is represented by a permutation matrix. (Hint: Show that the Bruhat decomposition is the coordinate-free geometric formulation of Gaussian elimination with row/column permutations, reducing structural questions about $\mathrm{GL}_n$ to the Weyl group $S_n$).
	- (Schur-Weyl Duality and Group Algebras) State classical Schur–Weyl duality on tensor space $V^{\otimes n}$, where $V = \mathbb{C}^d$:
	1. The natural action of $\mathrm{GL}(V)$ commutes with the permutation action of the symmetric group $S_n$.
	2. The canonical maps $\mathbb{C}[\mathrm{GL}(V)] \twoheadrightarrow \operatorname{End}_{S_n}(V^{\otimes n})$ and $\mathbb{C}[S_n] \twoheadrightarrow \operatorname{End}_{\mathrm{GL}(V)}(V^{\otimes n})$ are surjective with centralizer properties.
	(Hint: Decompose $V^{\otimes n} \cong \bigoplus_\lambda L_{\mathrm{GL}(V)}(\lambda) \otimes S^\lambda$ as a multiplicity-free module over $\mathrm{GL}(V) \times S_n$, where $S^\lambda$ are Specht modules and $L(\lambda)$ are irreducible polynomial representations).
	- (Specht Modules and Flag Varieties) Contrast Maschke's theorem for $\mathbb{C}[S_n]$ (semisimplicity in characteristic 0) with modular representations. Explain how Specht modules $S^\lambda$ are constructed geometrically via the action of $S_n$ on the cohomology of partial flag varieties rather than naive $n!$-dimensional permutation representations.
	- (Steinberg's Theorem and Modular Representations of SL(n, Fq)) State Robert Steinberg's theorem on the representations of finite groups of Lie type: every irreducible representation of $\mathrm{SL}_n(\mathbb{F}_q)$ in characteristic $p$ is obtained by restricting an irreducible algebraic representation of the algebraic group $\mathrm{SL}_n(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_p)$ whose highest weights are $p$-restricted ($0 \le \langle \lambda, \alpha^\vee \rangle < q$).
	- (Coxeter Systems and Bruhat Order) Define a Coxeter system $(W, S)$ as a group $W$ with generating set $S = \{s_i\}$ and presentation:
	$$(s_i s_j)^{m(s_i, s_j)} = 1, \quad m(s_i, s_i) = 1, \quad m(s_i, s_j) \ge 2 \text{ for } i \ne j.$$
	Define the length function $\ell(w)$ via reduced expressions $w = s_{i_1} \dots s_{i_k}$, and define the Bruhat partial order $u \le w$ on $W$. (Hint: The affine Weyl group $\widetilde{W} = W \ltimes Q^\vee$ is obtained by adjoining the affine reflection $s_0$).
	- (The Affine Grassmannian and Geometric Satake) Define the affine Grassmannian $\operatorname{Gr}_G = G(\mathcal{K})/G(\mathcal{O})$, where $\mathcal{O} = \mathbb{C}[[t]]$ is the ring of formal power series and $\mathcal{K} = \mathbb{C}((t))$ is the field of formal Laurent series.
	1. Define the Iwahori subgroup $\mathcal{I} \subset G(\mathcal{O})$ as the preimage of the Borel subgroup $B \subset G(\mathbb{C})$ under the evaluation map $t \mapsto 0$.
	2. State the Geometric Satake Equivalence of Mirković and Vilonen: the category of $G(\mathcal{O})$-equivariant perverse sheaves on $\operatorname{Gr}_G$ equipped with the convolution product is equivalent as a symmetric monoidal category to the representation category of the Langlands dual group:
		$$\left( \operatorname{Perv}_{G(\mathcal{O})}(\operatorname{Gr}_G), \star \right) \cong (\operatorname{Rep}(G^\vee), \otimes).$$
	(Hint: Perverse sheaves provide the natural abelian category to compute intersection forms and geometric weight spaces via Mirković–Vilonen cycles).
	- (Iwahori-Hecke Algebras and Braid Deformations) Define the Iwahori–Hecke algebra $\mathcal{H}(W, S)$ of a Coxeter system as the $\mathbb{Z}[v, v^{-1}]$-algebra with standard basis $\{H_w\}_{w \in W}$ satisfying:
	1. **Braid Relations:** $H_s H_t H_s \dots = H_t H_s H_t \dots$ for $m(s, t)$ factors.
	2. **Deformed Quadratic Relations:** $(H_s - v^{-1})(H_s + v) = 0$, or equivalently $H_s^2 = (v^{-1} - v)H_s + 1$.
	(Hint: Show that setting $v = 1$ recovers the group algebra of the Coxeter group $\mathbb{Z}[W]$; the braid relations remain undeformed while the involution $s^2 = 1$ is deformed quadratically).
	- (Shimura's Double Coset Hecke Rings) Review Goro Shimura's definition of the Hecke ring $\mathcal{H}(G, \Gamma)$ for a discrete group $G$ and an almost-normal subgroup $\Gamma$ (such that $[\Gamma : \Gamma \cap g \Gamma g^{-1}] < \infty$ for all $g \in G$). Show how taking $G = \mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb{Q})$ and $\Gamma = \mathrm{GL}_2(\mathbb{Z})$ constructs the classical Hecke operators $T_p, T_{p,p}$ acting on modular forms.
	- (Kazhdan-Lusztig Polynomials and Canonical Bases) State the Kazhdan–Lusztig theorem: there exists a unique canonical self-dual basis $\{C_w\}_{w \in W}$ in the Hecke algebra $\mathcal{H}(W, S)$ invariant under the bar-involution $\overline{v} = v^{-1}, \overline{H_s} = H_s^{-1}$, expressed as:
	$$C_w = v^{\ell(w)} \sum_{y \le w} P_{y, w}(v^{-2}) H_y,$$
	where $P_{y, w}(q) \in \mathbb{Z}[q]$ are the Kazhdan–Lusztig polynomials. (Hint: The coefficients of $P_{y, w}$ compute the local intersection cohomology dimensions of Schubert varieties).
	- (Vaughan Jones, Braid Groups, and the Jones Polynomial) Explain how representations of the Artin braid group $B_n = \langle \sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_{n-1} \mid \sigma_i \sigma_{i+1} \sigma_i = \sigma_{i+1} \sigma_i \sigma_{i+1} \rangle$ factoring through the Temperley–Lieb quotient of the Hecke algebra led Vaughan Jones to construct the Jones knot polynomial via Markov traces on $C^*$-algebras.
	- (Soergel Bimodules and Categorification of the Hecke Algebra) Let $R = \operatorname{Sym}(\mathfrak{h}^*)$ be the polynomial ring on the reflection representation of $W$. Define the category of Soergel bimodules $\mathcal{B}\operatorname{im}$ as the additive Karoubian category of graded $R$-bimodules generated by the Bott–Samelson bimodules $B_s = R \otimes_{R^s} R(1)$ for $s \in S$. State Soergel's Categorification Theorem: the split Grothendieck group $K_0(\mathcal{B}\operatorname{im})$ is canonically isomorphic to the Iwahori–Hecke algebra $\mathcal{H}(W, S)$, sending indecomposable Soergel bimodules $B_w$ to the Kazhdan–Lusztig basis elements $C_w$.
	- (Quantum Groups and Roots of Unity Analogies) Compare the quantum group $U_q(\mathfrak{sl}_n)$ at a root of unity with the modular representation theory of $\mathrm{SL}_n(\mathbb{F}_p)$.
	$$\begin{matrix} S_n & \longleftrightarrow & \mathrm{GL}_n(\mathbb{F}_q) \\ U_q(\mathfrak{sl}_n) \text{ at } q = e^{2\pi i/p} & \longleftrightarrow & \mathrm{SL}_n(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_p) \text{ in char } p \end{matrix}$$
	(Hint: Both settings exhibit non-semisimplicity, linkage principles, and tilting modules governed by the same affine Weyl group combinatorics).
	- (Categorification of Lie Algebras via 2-Kac-Moody Categories) Explain how passing from sets to categories enables the categorification of Lie algebras (Khovanov–Lauda and Rouquier): replace the vector space $\mathfrak{sl}_n$ with a 2-category $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_n)$ where 1-morphisms are generating functors $\mathcal{E}_i, \mathcal{F}_i$ and 2-morphisms are string diagrams (diagrammatic Hecke algebras). Show that the action of $\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{sl}_n)$ on derived categories categorifies irreducible highest-weight modules.
